Module 0 — Orientation

Files covered: the whole repository at a glance. This module was written last, after Modules 1–18, so every statement here is a summary of something verified in a later module, with a pointer to it.

Where we are: This is the front door. It assumes nothing. By the end you should know what the system does, the one sentence that frames it, the twenty words you need before Module 1, the stack, the numbers to know cold, and a two-minute pitch you can give today.

The one-line thesis: vla-finetune is a thin, provenance-obsessed experiment layer that fine-tunes a robot vision-language-action model on versioned data and reports results honestly. Its most important result is a tie with a nearest-neighbour lookup, and its most important flaw is that its own interventions aren't yet scored on a shared test set.

0.1 Prerequisites — the absolute basics

The minimum vocabulary to start Module 1. Each term is defined fully in the module shown.

TermOne-line meaningFull treatment
Episodeone recorded demonstration of a robot task, a sequence of framesM1
Frameone timestep: camera images, robot state (joint angles), commanded actionM1
Actionthe vector sent to the robot: 6 numbers (SO-100), 14 (ALOHA)M1
Policya function from observation to action; the trained modelM1
VLAvision-language-action model: images plus an instruction plus state in, actions outM1
SmolVLAthe small VLA used here (lerobot/smolvla_base); predicts action chunks with flow matching, so its outputs are random from call to callM1
Fine-tuningcontinuing to train a pretrained model on new dataM1
Behaviour cloningtraining a policy to copy demonstrated actions (supervised regression)M1
Offline evaluationcomparing predicted vs recorded actions on held-out data, with the robot never moving. The only evaluation hereM1, M7
Closed-loop rolloutletting the policy drive a robot or simulator and measuring task success. Not available; recorded as "unavailable"M1, M12
Train / val / test splitdisjoint sets of episodes: train to learn, val to pick a checkpoint, test to report onceM1, M5
Baselinea trivial model to beat: mean-action (predict the average) and nearest-neighbour (copy the action of the closest training robot state)M7
MSE (normalized)mean squared action error after per-dimension standardization; mean-action ≈ 1.0M1, M7
Dataset versionan immutable, named snapshot in the data engine: raw/v1, clean/v1, training/v6M1, M4
Lineagethe recorded chain of operations that produced a versionM4, M18
Data interventiona deliberate change to the training data (reduce, imbalance, rebalance) to test what data contributesM2, M4
The engine (rde)the sibling repository Episodic Data Engine: owns data, versions, the evaluator, failure detection and the SmolVLA training stepM1, M18
Run directoryruns/<date>/<name>/seed_<n>/, holding everything one experiment producedM3
Checkpointsaved model weights plus pre- and post-processors at a training stepM6, M9
"Unavailable" markeran explicit record that something wasn't measured, and why; never a fabricated valueM5, M10

0.2 What the system does, in one paragraph

Given a YAML experiment file, mtrain train asks the data engine to build a new, lineage-tracked training dataset version (optionally with an intervention such as "half the episodes" or "upweight short episodes"). It re-checks the train/val/test split, profiles the data, and fine-tunes SmolVLA, validating only on the val split and keeping the best verified checkpoint. It then scores that checkpoint and two baselines on the test split through the same evaluator, decomposes the error by tail, episode, phase of the episode and slice, runs the engine's failure detector to produce a recommended next data intervention for a human, and writes a report, model card, per-frame outcomes and index rows. Everything it can't measure is written down as unavailable (M11).

0.3 The framing sentence

"The model layer never owns the data." Every dataset this package trains on is an immutable, catalog-resolved version produced by the engine, and even a data intervention is executed as a lineage-tracked transform, never a local copy (README; verified in M4).

And the honest counterweight to carry with it:

"The data layer owns the model." The SmolVLA adapter and its gradient step (fit) live in the engine; this repository owns the procedure around them (M1 §1.3).

0.6 The numbers to know cold

Data: SO-100: 50 raw → 48 after QC (dropped 9, 31) → 38/5/5, 1,908 test frames, 6-dimensional action, 2 cameras at 30 fps, one task, 326–475 frames per episode. ALOHA: 40 → 32/4/4, 4,400 test frames, 14-dimensional action, 4 cameras at 50 fps, test episodes 1,100 frames each.

Results (test MSE, normalized):

SmolVLANearest-neighbourMean-actionRatio to mean
SO-100, 1,000 steps0.02900.02390.9083.2%
SO-100, 2,000 steps0.02390.02390.9082.6%
ALOHA, 1,000 steps0.21570.10621.01021.4%

Training: batch 4, lr 1e-4 (3e-5 when resumed), warmup 50 (20 when resumed), checkpoint and validation every 100 steps, validation on 32 frames. 324 / 385 / 576 s. Peak GPU 2.2–3.0 GB of 8.

The derived numbers that change the story:

  • val minimum 0.0018 vs test 0.0239, about 13× (M6)
  • ≈ 0.26 epoch (SO-100) and ≈ 0.11 epoch (ALOHA) per 1,000 steps (M15)
  • re-scoring noise up to 5% vs the 18% continued-training gain (M7)
  • evaluation ≈ 45–57% of run time, about half of it redundant (M15)
  • failure rule flags 191 / 1,908 (10%), max episode 13.8% vs the 0.5 cut (M8)

Code: about 2,700 lines in src/; 4 layers; 11 CLI commands; 56 tests; about 200 logged gaps → 7 P0 / 26 P1 / 28 doc corrections / about 60 P2 (M16).

0.7 The two-minute pitch

"This is the experiment and evaluation layer for fine-tuning SmolVLA, a small vision-language-action robot policy, on real LeRobot datasets: SO-100 pick-and-place and bimanual ALOHA coffee.

The design rests on one rule: the model layer never owns the data. Every data change, like halving the training set or dropping a regime, is executed as a lineage-tracked version in a separate data engine, so any checkpoint traces back to the exact raw episodes it learned from, including which ones QC removed.

One command runs an experiment end to end. It builds the dataset version, re-checks the episode-level splits, fine-tunes with checkpoint selection on validation only, and scores the result against a mean-action and a nearest-neighbour baseline through the same evaluator. It breaks error down by tail, episode, phase and slice, produces a feedback artifact recommending the next data change, and writes a report where anything it can't measure, like closed-loop success, is recorded as unavailable rather than invented.

The results are honest rather than flattering. On SO-100, fine-tuning reached 2.6% of the mean-action error, and continued training gave an 18% gain on an identical test split, but it exactly tied a lookup over robot state. On ALOHA, retrieval was twice as good. Since that run saw about a ninth of an epoch, I read that as undertrained before I read it as retrieval being better.

Auditing the harness itself, the main thing I'd fix is that interventions re-split the data, so each one is scored on a different test set. Freezing one test split, propagating real seeds and adding bootstrap intervals come before any new infrastructure."

(About 2 minutes at a normal pace. Adjust "I" and "we" to your actual role. See the M17 authorship note.)

0.8 Honest gaps to own before an interviewer finds them

The five that matter most, each owned by a later module:

  1. Interventions are scored on different test sets (filter-then-split). → M4, P0-1
  2. --seed doesn't seed; replicates are fake. → M11, P0-2
  3. Checkpoint selection uses 32 validation frames (val about 13× below test). → M6, P0-7
  4. The feedback loop runs on test, and can't say "no action". → M8, P0-5
  5. mtrain test overwrites published results. → M12, P0-6

And the scope facts: offline only, single seed, 5 and 4 test episodes, no CI, the engine unpinned and not in this repo, run artifacts not in git (M1, M18).

The shape to carry into Module 1

   YAML ─▶ mtrain train ─▶ [ engine: version + lineage ] ─▶ train (val-only) ─▶ test vs 2 baselines
                                                                   │
           report · model card · per-frame outcomes · feedback ◀──┘   (rollout: "unavailable")

   principle: the model layer never owns the data      counterweight: the data layer owns the model
   result:    SmolVLA = state-NN on SO-100; NN 2× better on ALOHA (≈0.11 epoch → undertrained?)
   first fix: one frozen test split for every intervention

Drill — answer from memory, files closed

  1. In one sentence, what does this system do, and what's its organizing principle?
  2. What's the difference between offline evaluation and closed-loop rollout, and which does this project do?
  3. Why didn't you just report the 2.6%-of-mean-action number as the headline?
  4. What breaks in production if someone treats the README's intervention comparisons as findings today?
  5. Interviewer framing: "Give me the two-minute version."

Appendix A — Model answers to the drill

A1. "It's an experiment, evaluation and feedback layer that fine-tunes SmolVLA on versioned robot datasets and reports results against baselines. Its principle is that the model layer never owns the data: every dataset, including every intervention, is an immutable, lineage-tracked version in a separate data engine."

Why that answer lands: it gives the what and the principle in under fifteen seconds, with the one word an interviewer will remember: lineage.

A2. "Offline evaluation compares predicted actions with recorded demonstrations on held-out episodes. The robot never moves, so errors can't compound. Closed-loop rollout lets the policy drive a robot or simulator and measures task success. This project only does offline evaluation, and it records rollout as unavailable rather than estimating it, because low offline error doesn't guarantee success once small mistakes push the robot off the demonstrated states."

Why that answer lands: it's precise about the mechanism (compounding error) and shows the project's honesty is a design choice.

A3. "Because the mean-action baseline is the weakest possible comparison. Any model that learned anything beats it. The informative comparison is the nearest-neighbour lookup over robot state, which SmolVLA ties on SO-100 and loses to on ALOHA. Leading with 2.6% would be technically true and misleading, and the whole point of scoring both baselines through the same evaluator is to report the one that matters."

Why that answer lands: it shows you understand baseline selection, and that you'd choose the less flattering framing deliberately.

A4. "They'd be comparing numbers measured on different test sets. Each intervention filters episodes and the engine then re-splits the remainder, so a 'reduced' run is scored on two or three test episodes that aren't even a subset of the baseline's five. Any difference could be which episodes landed in test. Combined with a single seed and a stochastic model, the comparison has no statistical footing. The fix is to freeze one test split and apply interventions to the train split only. Until then, they're pipeline validation, not findings."

Why that answer lands: it names the mechanism, quantifies it and gives the fix, which is the core of the project's methodological story in thirty seconds.

A5. Deliver §0.7.

Why that answer lands: it's bounded, it has a principle, a result, an honest interpretation and a next step, and it invites follow-up in exactly the areas you've prepared.

Module 0 Summary — the one page to revise

What this module is. The entry point: what the system does, its framing sentence and counterweight, twenty terms, the reading map, the stack, the numbers, and the two-minute pitch.

Five claims to make cold

  1. The model layer never owns the data; every intervention is a lineage-tracked engine version.
  2. The data layer owns the model; fit is in the engine, and this repo owns the procedure around it.
  3. Offline only: SmolVLA ties state-NN on SO-100 (0.0239) and loses on ALOHA (0.216 vs 0.106).
  4. ALOHA saw about 0.11 epoch, so it's undertrained before it's out-retrieved; continued training gained 18% on an identical split.
  5. First fix: one frozen test split for all interventions; then real seeds, frozen val subset, val-driven feedback, non-destructive re-scoring.

Decisions (the headline four)

ChoseOverBecauseCost
Interventions as engine versionslocal copies / ID liststraceable to raw episodeseach intervention gets its own test split
Two baselines through one evaluatormodel-only metricshonest comparisonthe result is a tie
"Unavailable" over estimatesproxy success numbersnever fabricateincomplete-looking reports
Laptop, files, one processinfrastructureone or two researchers, short runsconcurrency and durability gaps

Numbers and names: §0.6, the results table and the five derived numbers.

Sentences worth memorizing

  • "The model layer never owns the data, and the data layer owns the model."
  • "Offline, a state lookup tied my VLA; the harness was built to tell me that."
  • "My first fix is one frozen test split, because right now each intervention is graded on a different exam."

Module 1 — Foundations: the domain, the boundary, and the package

Files covered: pyproject.toml, .gitignore, src/model_training/config/__init__.py, src/model_training/data/__init__.py, src/model_training/deployment/__init__.py, src/model_training/evaluation/__init__.py, src/model_training/experiments/__init__.py, src/model_training/feedback/__init__.py, src/model_training/reports/__init__.py, src/model_training/tracking/__init__.py, src/model_training/training/__init__.py

Where we are: This is the first module, so there is nothing before it. Its job is to give you the vocabulary for the rest of the manual and to show exactly where this repository stops and its sibling, the Episodic Data Engine (rde), starts. That boundary is the most important structural fact about the project, and getting it wrong in an interview means claiming work this repository doesn't contain.

The one-line thesis: vla-finetune is a thin, import-light orchestration layer of about 2,700 lines. It owns experimental procedure (config, interventions, validation cadence, selection, reporting, provenance) and delegates every mechanism (data storage, transforms, the training step, scoring, failure detection) to a separately installed engine.

1.1 Prerequisites — the absolute basics

Robot learning vocabulary

  • Demonstration / episode. One recorded attempt at a task, usually a human teleoperating a robot. It's a time-ordered sequence of frames. "Episode" is the unit of data in this codebase. Splits, interventions, failure reviews and weights are all per-episode.
  • Frame. One timestep in an episode: the camera image(s), the robot's state (joint positions, sometimes efforts) and the action commanded at that moment. Code keys look like state.joint, action.joint and rgb.top (these appear in tests/smoke/test_smolvla_smoke.py).
  • Action. A vector of numbers sent to the robot. For SO-100 it has 6 dimensions (5 joints plus the gripper). For bimanual ALOHA it has 14 (two 7-dimensional arms). The configs record this: the aloha_coffee_baseline.yaml header says "14-DoF action across two arms, four cameras".
  • DoF (degrees of freedom). The number of independently controllable joints.
  • Embodiment. The physical robot body: its kinematics, cameras and action space. "Cross-embodiment" means the same pipeline or model works across different bodies. The config tag embodiment: so100 or aloha records it.
  • Behaviour cloning (imitation learning). Training a policy to reproduce the demonstrated action given the observation. It's supervised regression from observation to action. Everything here is behaviour cloning.
  • Policy. A function from observation to action. The trained model is a policy.
  • VLA (Vision-Language-Action model). A policy whose inputs are camera images plus a natural-language instruction ("Pick up the cube and place it in the box." is literally a task string in tests/conftest.py) plus robot state, and whose output is actions. It's usually built by attaching an action-generating head to a pretrained vision-language model.
  • SmolVLA. The specific VLA used here, loaded from the Hugging Face checkpoint lerobot/smolvla_base (configs/base.yaml). Per its public release, which this repository doesn't contain and I can't verify from here, it's a small VLA from the LeRobot project. Its action expert generates chunks of future actions with flow matching. The repository's own evidence that actions are stochastic is the README's §2.3 note that re-scoring the same frames moves MSE by up to 5%. The evidence for chunking is the smoke-test comment about an "action-queue bug" where a cached action chunk was reused across batches.
  • Flow matching. A generative-modelling technique: the network learns a vector field that transports random noise into a sample of the target distribution (here, an action chunk). What matters for this project: prediction is stochastic. Score the same input twice and you get different actions. That fact matters later for checkpoint selection (Module 6) and for how to read small metric differences (Module 7).
  • LeRobot. Hugging Face's robot-learning library and dataset format. The source datasets (lerobot/svla_so100_pickplace, lerobot/aloha_static_coffee) are LeRobot datasets, and the test fixture fakes one with rde.testing.make_lerobot_dataset.
  • Fine-tuning. Continuing to train a pretrained model on new task data. "Fine-tuning SmolVLA" means starting from smolvla_base weights and taking gradient steps on so100 or aloha episodes.

Evaluation vocabulary

  • Offline (open-loop) evaluation. Feed recorded observations, compare predicted actions with the recorded human actions, and compute error. The robot never acts, so errors can't compound. This is the only kind of evaluation the project runs.
  • Closed-loop rollout. Let the policy actually control a robot or simulator and measure task success. Errors compound because the policy's own actions change what it sees next. The code refuses to fake this: mtrain rollout writes "status": "unavailable".
  • Why the distinction matters: a low offline MSE doesn't imply a high success rate. A policy can match demonstrations on average and still fail at the one moment that matters, such as the grasp. Every claim the project makes is offline, and the code is built to keep the two from being confused.
  • MSE in normalized units. Actions are normalized per dimension (subtract the mean, divide by the standard deviation; the test contract fixtures carry normalization: {mean, std}). An MSE of 1.0 means about one standard deviation of error. That's why the mean-action baseline sits near 1.0 (README Table 3: 0.908 and 1.010). Predicting the mean leaves roughly the variance.
  • Baseline. A trivial model the real model must beat. Here there are two: mean_action (always predict the training mean) and nearest_neighbor (return the action of the most similar training frame, i.e. retrieval). Both are implemented in rde, not here.
  • Train/val/test split. The validation split is used during training to pick a checkpoint. The test split is touched once, at the end, to report. Splits are episode-level: every frame of an episode goes to the same split. If frames from one episode landed in both train and test, near-identical neighbouring frames would leak and inflate the scores.
  • Leakage. Any path by which test information influences training or selection.
  • Checkpoint. A saved snapshot of model weights at a given step.
  • Checkpoint selection. Choosing which snapshot to report, by a validation metric and never by test.

Data-engineering vocabulary

These terms are defined by the sibling engine. They're inferred from call sites here, not read from the engine's source.

  • Dataset version. An immutable, named snapshot such as raw/v1, clean/v1 or training/v6. The prefix is a stage (rde.schemas.lineage.VersionStage, with at least CLEAN and TRAINING, used in interventions.py).
    • raw/vN is ingested source data.
    • clean/vN is the output of transforms (normalization, episode filtering).
    • training/vN is a built training set with split assignments.
  • Catalog. The engine's metadata store: which versions exist and which episodes they contain (catalog.get_manifest, catalog.get_episodes). It's located by settings.paths.catalog_db, and the name suggests a database file. I haven't verified the engine.
  • Lineage. The recorded chain of operations that produced a version (ingest → transform → build_training_dataset). The README shows real output of rde version lineage.
  • Training contract. A dict describing a version's modalities: observation (each key with a plane, shape and optional normalization) and action (key, shape, normalization). The plane says where a modality physically lives. inline means numeric columns stored with the frame (state). external means stored outside the table (images or video). This repo branches on plane == "inline" and plane == "external" in profile.py, deployment/loader.py and reports/model_card.py.
  • DatasetView. The read handle on a version. It has episode_ids(split=...), episodes, contract, iter_samples, get_episode_frames, splits() and close(), and it's a context manager (with engine.get_dataset(...) as view in profile.py).
  • Adapter. Converts a list of samples into model input tensors (VisionLanguageAdapter, StateAdapter). An adapter is the "preprocessing" stage.

Python packaging vocabulary

  • pyproject.toml. The standard (PEP 621) file declaring a package's name, dependencies and build system.
  • Build backend. The tool that turns source into an installable wheel. Here that's hatchling.
  • Distribution name vs import name. You pip install vla-finetune but import model_training. The two names are independent.
  • Console script. [project.scripts] mtrain = "model_training.cli:main" makes installation create an mtrain executable that calls main().
  • Extras / optional dependencies. pip install pkg[dev] adds a named group.
  • Editable install. -e: the installed package points at your source tree, so edits take effect without reinstalling.
  • src/ layout. Code lives under src/ so tests can't accidentally import the uninstalled working copy.
  • Namespace package. A directory with no __init__.py that Python still imports (PEP 420), merging every same-named directory on sys.path. This matters below.
  • __all__. The list of names a package declares public, and what from pkg import * exports.
  • Import-light discipline. Not importing heavy libraries (torch) at module import time, so cheap commands and tests run without them. It's done with lazy imports, meaning imports inside functions.

1.2 What this repository is, measured

ThingMeasured value
Source lines (src/)about 2,700 across 28 files
Largest modulesrunner.py 314, cli.py 306, profile.py 303, trainer.py 256, interventions.py 212
Test functions49 in tests/unit, 3 in tests/integration, 4 in tests/smoke. The README's "52 unit + integration tests" (line 282) matches 49 + 3
CInone: no .github/, no other CI config
Lockfilenone: no uv.lock and no requirements*.txt
Engine present in this checkoutno: import rde fails, and ../episodic-data-engine doesn't exist here

That last row shapes the whole manual. I couldn't run any test or command while writing it. Every statement about what rde does is either inferred from a call site here (and marked as such) or quoted from this repo's docs (and treated as a claim).

1.3 The boundary: what rde owns and what this repo owns

Here is every rde symbol imported by src/, grouped by who imports it (from grep):

rde symbolImported by (this repo)What it does for us
catalog.Catalog, config.Settings/get_settingsrunner, interventions, profile, feedback, clilocate and read versions
engine.DataEngine, engine.DatasetView, engine.Samplerunner, profile, split_integrity, trainer, offline, loader, cliopen a version for reading
transformations.TransformPipeline, EpisodeSubsetFilterinterventionscreate a clean/vN by dropping episodes
dataset_builder.DatasetBuilder, build_training_contractinterventions, profilecreate a training/vN with splits and weights
versioning.next_version, schemas.lineage.VersionStageinterventionsallocate the next version name
exploration.queries.DatasetExplorerprofile, clistats, histograms, raw SQL over frames
model_adapters.models.SmolVLAPolicytrainer, runner, deployment artifact and loader, clithe model, including fit(): the training step itself
model_adapters.VisionLanguageAdapter, MODEL_REGISTRY, build_modeltrainer, runner, offline, loader, clipreprocessing and baselines
evaluation.Evaluator, EvaluationResulttrainer, offline, feedbackscoring predictions against demonstrations
feedback.FeedbackLoopfeedbackfailure detection and episode nomination

So the split is:

              this repo (model_training)                          rde (episodic-data-engine)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐      ┌─────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ WHAT to run: config schema, merge, identity  │      │ storage, catalog, versions, lineage │
  │ WHICH episodes: intervention resolution      │─────▶│ transforms, DatasetBuilder, splits  │
  │ WHEN to validate/checkpoint/prune/select     │─────▶│ SmolVLAPolicy.fit (one grad step)   │
  │ WHAT to score: baselines, slices, tails      │─────▶│ Evaluator (pred vs demo)            │
  │ HOW to package: run dir, index, reports      │─────▶│ FeedbackLoop (failure detection)    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘      └─────────────────────────────────────┘

Decision: put the model adapter, including SmolVLAPolicy.fit, inside the data engine.

  • What: the class that wraps SmolVLA, builds batches and runs forward/backward lives in rde.model_adapters.models, not here. docs/ARCHITECTURE.md line 35 says so outright: SmolVLAPolicy was "Extended in place: fit() now performs real gradient steps; save_checkpoint() and from_checkpoint() were added."
  • Why (inferred): the adapter has to read the engine's training contract to build feature shapes and normalization statistics. The smoke test calls rde's private _policy_features_from_contract and _dataset_stats_from_contract. Keeping the model next to the contract keeps those two in lockstep.
  • Alternative: the model lives in this repo, and the engine exposes only data, a contract and an iterator.
  • Cost: the dependency direction is inverted relative to the README's slogan. The README says "the model layer never owns the data". That's true, but the data layer owns the model. The most technically demanding code (batch construction, optimizer, loss, the action-queue fix) isn't in this repository. In an interview, say plainly: "the training step lives in the engine; this repo owns the run around it." Don't claim the optimizer loop as code in this repo.
  • When the answer flips: once a second model family arrives (a diffusion policy, ACT), its adapter would also have to go into the data engine. At that point a separate model_adapters package depended on by both repos is the right shape.

1.4 pyproject.toml, line by line

[project]
name = "vla-finetune"
version = "0.1.0"
description = "Experiment, training, evaluation, deployment and feedback layer on top of episodic-data-engine"
license = "MIT"
authors = [{ name = "Kushagra Srivastava" }]
requires-python = ">=3.10"
  • requires-python = ">=3.10": the code uses X | None union syntax in annotations at runtime-evaluated positions (for example, Pydantic field types like float | None in schema.py). Pydantic evaluates those, so Python 3.10 is a real floor and not an arbitrary one. The README installs with 3.11 (uv venv --python 3.11), so 3.10 itself is declared but never shown to have been tested.
  • authors and [project.urls] Repository = "https://github.com/kushagrathisside/VLAFinetuningExperiments" name a person and a repository other than the checkout this manual was written from. If you present this project as yours, have a one-sentence account of the relationship (fork, collaboration, renamed), because an interviewer who opens the repo will see it. (README line 298 credits the SO-100 runs to AdityaKishore0698 and the ALOHA run to chytra3. See gap 11.)
dependencies = [
    "episodic-data-engine",
    "pydantic>=2",
    "pyyaml>=6",
    "typer>=0.9",
    "rich>=13",
    "psutil>=5",
    "matplotlib>=3.8",
    "numpy>=1.24",
]
  • Floors only, no ceilings, no lockfile. pydantic>=2 admits any future major version. The code uses v2-only API (model_validator, model_validate, model_dump(mode="json")), so the floor is load-bearing, but nothing stops a breaking v3.
    • Alternative: a committed uv.lock.
    • Cost: two installs months apart can resolve different versions. The project compensates after the fact by recording versions into environment.json (Module 3). That records drift but doesn't prevent it.
  • episodic-data-engine with no version constraint at all. The repository is coupled to the engine's private functions (the smoke test imports _policy_features_from_contract), yet it declares no compatible engine version. The only real pin is the git commit recorded per run in git.json (Module 3).
  • psutil is a hard dependency, yet trainer.py and run_dir.py both wrap import psutil in try/except ImportError marked # pragma: no cover. A declared dependency can't be missing in a correct install, so those branches are dead defensive code. They're harmless, but don't call them "graceful degradation".
  • matplotlib is a hard runtime dependency of a library, used only by reports/figures.py. That's acceptable because every run renders figures. It does make the "light" environment heavier than it needs to be.
[project.optional-dependencies]
# The heavy VLA stack (torch, lerobot) is pulled in transitively through
# episodic-data-engine's own `vla` extra -- installed once, in .venv-vla, per
# the engine's own import-light discipline. Nothing here re-declares torch.
dev = ["pytest>=8", "pytest-cov>=5"]

Decision: torch and lerobot are not declared here at all.

  • What: there's no vla extra on this package. You get torch only by installing episodic-data-engine[vla].
  • Why: single ownership. The engine's SmolVLAPolicy is the only code that needs torch, so the engine declares it, and two packages can't pin conflicting torch versions.
  • Alternative: vla-finetune[vla] = ["episodic-data-engine[vla]"], a pass-through extra that would make pip install vla-finetune[vla] work in one step.
  • Cost: pyproject.toml alone gives no way to get a working training environment. You have to know to install the engine's extra, which only the README tells you (line 177). And trainer.py and run_dir.py do import torch directly, so this package does use torch without declaring it. For trainer.py the comment covers it ("safe: policy.load() above already required it to succeed"). It's still an undeclared direct dependency.

pytest-cov is declared, but addopts = "-q" doesn't enable coverage, and there's no coverage config. Nothing in the repo runs it, so it's an unused dependency.

[project.scripts]
mtrain = "model_training.cli:main"

The docstring in cli.py explains the name: "mtrain, not a bare model, to avoid colliding with the engine's own rde model ... sub-app". That's a real rationale, and it's verifiable because cli.py exists and is the target.

[build-system]
requires = ["hatchling"]
build-backend = "hatchling.build"

[tool.hatch.build.targets.wheel]
packages = ["src/model_training"]

[tool.hatch.metadata]
allow-direct-references = true
  • packages = ["src/model_training"] tells hatch where the import package is. That's needed because the import name differs from the distribution name.
  • allow-direct-references = true permits dependencies written as direct URLs (pkg @ file:///... or git+https://...). No dependency in this file is a direct reference. episodic-data-engine is a bare name. The setting is vestigial, probably left over from an earlier episodic-data-engine @ file:../... form. Harmless, but it's a loose thread.
[tool.uv.sources]
episodic-data-engine = { path = "../episodic-data-engine", editable = true }

Decision: resolve the engine from a sibling directory via uv.

  • What: when installed with uv, the bare dependency name resolves to the local checkout ../episodic-data-engine, editable.
  • Why: the two repos are developed together, so engine edits (like the in-place SmolVLAPolicy extensions) take effect immediately.
  • Alternative: publish the engine to PyPI or a private index and pin a version, or vendor it as a git submodule.
  • Cost: [tool.uv.sources] is a uv-only table. Plain pip install -e . ignores it and tries to fetch episodic-data-engine from PyPI. I couldn't verify whether a package of that name exists there. If it doesn't, the install fails; if a different package squats the name, it installs the wrong code. The install also needs a specific directory layout (../episodic-data-engine next to this repo), which is why runner.py hard-codes parents[4] / "episodic-data-engine" (Module 11). The same layout assumption appears in three places, with nothing enforcing it.
  • When the answer flips: once anyone other than the author installs it, or once CI exists. CI would need to check out both repos side by side.
[tool.pytest.ini_options]
testpaths = ["tests"]
addopts = "-q"

testpaths = ["tests"] matters because there's a file called scripts/test.py. Without testpaths, pytest's default discovery pattern (test_*.py or *_test.py) wouldn't match test.py anyway, so this is belt-and-braces, not a fix for a real problem.

1.5 .gitignore: what the repo refuses to version

runs
experiments/*.jsonl
paper_ready/
...
.venv/

# Internal code-review audit -- kept locally as a private record, not published.
docs/CODE_REVIEW.md
  • runs (no trailing slash, no leading slash) ignores any file or directory named runs at any depth. That's intended for <repo>/runs/, where ExperimentRunner writes by default.
  • Consequence: the README's results can't be reproduced from the repository alone. Every run directory, checkpoint and metric file is git-ignored. docs/figures/results.svg is committed, but the runs/... inputs to scripts/plot_results.py aren't. The numbers in README Table 2 are claims backed by files that aren't in the repo. That's normal for ML (checkpoints are gigabytes), but say it before you're asked: "the run artifacts are local; the figure script regenerates from them."
  • experiments/*.jsonl ignores the cross-run index (Module 3). The pattern is unanchored, so it would also match src/model_training/experiments/*.jsonl. No such files exist, so it's harmless.
  • .venv/ is listed, but the README creates both venvs at ../.venv and ../.venv-vla, outside the repo. The entry is defensive, so the missing .venv-vla/ entry isn't a real gap. (I raised it as a possible gap in my first survey; on reading the README's install table it isn't one.)
  • docs/CODE_REVIEW.md is deliberately excluded: there's an internal audit the public repository doesn't contain. Several code comments read like responses to it. For example, trainer.py's crash handler says "A crash here ... previously left the run directory in an undetectable half-finished state", and registry.py's get docstring says "returning the first match returned the oldest". Those are fixed bugs narrated in comments. In an interview this is useful: "I ran an adversarial review and fixed what it found; the comments record the before-state."

1.6 The package surface: the __init__.py files

There is no src/model_training/__init__.py

$ ls src/model_training/__init__.py
ls: cannot access 'src/model_training/__init__.py': No such file or directory

Every subpackage has an __init__.py, but the top-level model_training doesn't. So model_training is an implicit namespace package (PEP 420).

  • Probably why: it was never needed, and nothing reads model_training.__version__. collect_environment gets the version from installed metadata via importlib.metadata.version("vla-finetune") instead.
  • Alternative: an __init__.py, which costs one empty file.
  • Cost: a namespace package merges every directory named model_training on sys.path. Any other installed project with a top-level model_training directory, which is a generic name, would silently interleave with this one, and a model_training/cli.py from the wrong tree could be imported. It's also a less common setup, which some tooling (mypy without namespace_packages, older coverage configs) handles worse. This looks accidental rather than designed; nothing in the code or docs mentions it.

The nine subpackage __init__.py files are re-export façades

Each one re-exports a curated public API with an explicit __all__, for example:

# src/model_training/deployment/__init__.py
from .artifact import ModelArtifact, build_model_artifact
from .loader import DeployedModel, DeploymentHealthError, LatencyBreakdown
from .registry import ModelRegistry

__all__ = ["DeployedModel", "DeploymentHealthError", "LatencyBreakdown",
           "ModelArtifact", "ModelRegistry", "build_model_artifact"]
  • Pattern: Façade at the package level. Callers write from model_training.deployment import DeployedModel and don't depend on the file layout.
  • Why: module files can be reorganized without breaking importers.
  • Cost 1: being exported is not being used. The façades make names look first-class. Grep shows several exported names that nothing in src/ or scripts/ calls:
Exported nameUsed by src//scripts/?Used by tests?
deployment.ModelRegistryno (the runner writes models via ExperimentIndex.record_model, not the registry)yes, test_deployment.py
deployment.DeployedModelno (only named in a model-card string)yes
experiments.run_data_fraction_sweepno (no CLI command calls it)no (verified in Module 11: no test references it)
reports.build_model_dataset_matrixnono

These are covered properly in Modules 9, 10 and 11. For now the point is that the public API is wider than the wired system.

  • Cost 2: eager imports defeat laziness. Importing a subpackage executes every module it re-exports. from model_training.experiments import ExperimentRunner imports runner.py, which at module top imports ..deployment.artifact, which at module top does from rde.model_adapters.models import SmolVLAPolicy. So any import of the runner imports the engine's SmolVLA module. The runner's careful lazy from ..training.trainer import Trainer inside if config.training.model == "smolvla": saves nothing on the rde side, because deployment/artifact.py has already pulled in the same models module. It works in the light venv only if the engine's models.py itself imports torch lazily. The trainer comment ("import torch # safe: policy.load() above already required it") implies it does, and the README says test_experiment_runner.py passes without torch. I couldn't verify this without the engine. If the engine ever adds a top-level import torch to models.py, the whole light test suite breaks, and nothing in this repo would show why.

The CLI is the one place laziness is done right

cli.py imports only typer, yaml and rich at the top. Every rde and model_training import sits inside a command function, so mtrain --help and mtrain rollout don't load the engine at all. That's the import-light discipline applied where it pays off most: a CLI should start fast and fail only for the command you actually ran. Module 12 covers it.

1.7 The two-environment design (from the README, partly verified)

The README (lines 176–177) and docs/ARCHITECTURE.md (lines 143–144) describe:

VenvContentsWhat runs there
../.venv (light)engine core plus this package, no torchconfig, profiling, interventions, tracking, reports, CLI, unit and integration tests, baselines
../.venv-vla (full)episodic-data-engine[vla] (torch, lerobot)real fine-tuning, checkpoint evaluation, the smoke test

What the code confirms: the smoke test begins torch = pytest.importorskip("torch") and pytest.importorskip("lerobot"), so it skips cleanly in the light venv. The integration test docstring says it "Uses the baseline models (no torch needed)". The runner test uses "training": {"model": "mean_action"} precisely so that "the whole runner ... without requiring torch/lerobot" runs.

Decision: two environments rather than one.

  • Why: torch plus lerobot is gigabytes, and CUDA builds are platform-specific (docs/IMPLEMENTATION_PLAN.md line 254 records reinstalling torch with CUDA). Fifty-two tests that need no GPU shouldn't pay for that.
  • Alternative: one environment with torch always installed, plus test markers.
  • Cost: two environments drift independently, and nothing tests that the light environment stays light. No test asserts "torch" not in sys.modules after importing the runner. The design is a convention, not an enforced invariant.

1.8 Honest gaps to own before an interviewer finds them

  1. The training step isn't in this repository. SmolVLAPolicy.fit, batch construction, the optimizer and the loss all live in rde.model_adapters.models. Consequence: claiming "I wrote the fine-tuning loop" is false for this repo. What this repo owns is the run around it (Module 6).
  2. Nothing can be run from this checkout. The engine isn't present, [tool.uv.sources] needs a sibling directory, and plain pip ignores that table. Consequence: a reviewer can't reproduce anything without reconstructing your directory layout.
  3. No lockfile, floors-only versions, and an unpinned engine. Consequence: reproducibility depends on after-the-fact environment.json and git.json capture (Module 3), which records drift but doesn't prevent it.
  4. Coupling to the engine's private API. tests/smoke/test_smolvla_smoke.py imports _policy_features_from_contract and _dataset_stats_from_contract. Consequence: an engine refactor that's legitimate from the engine's side breaks this repo's tests, and the missing version constraint means nothing warns you.
  5. No CI. Consequence: the "52 tests pass" claim is a statement about the author's machine on some date. It isn't continuously enforced.
  6. The top-level package has no __init__.py (implicit namespace package under a generic name). Consequence: there's a silent import-merging risk with any other model_training on the path.
  7. Light-environment laziness is unenforced and partly illusory. The runner's lazy trainer import is undone by deployment/artifact.py's eager import. Consequence: a top-level torch import added in the engine would break the light suite with no local cause.
  8. Unused declarations. allow-direct-references = true (no direct references), pytest-cov (never invoked), psutil ImportError fallbacks (it's a hard dependency). Consequence: small, but each one is a "why is this here?" you should answer as "vestigial", not invent a reason for.
  9. The public API is wider than the wired system (ModelRegistry, DeployedModel, run_data_fraction_sweep, build_model_dataset_matrix). Consequence: the package surface overstates what the pipeline actually exercises.
  10. Undeclared direct torch dependency. trainer.py and run_dir.py import torch, but this package never declares it. It's deliberate, delegated to the engine's extra, and the README explains it, but the metadata alone is incomplete.
  11. The pyproject author and repository URL point elsewhere. Consequence: you need a one-line provenance story ready. Correction added during Module 3: README line 298 states the SO-100 fine-tuning runs were performed by AdityaKishore0698 and the ALOHA run by chytra3. The accurate framing is therefore a collaborative project: one author owns the codebase, you ran the SO-100 experiments and someone else ran ALOHA. Claim exactly that, and be ready for "which code did you write?".

The shape to carry into Module 2

                   ┌──────────────────────── vla-finetune (model_training) ────────────────────────┐
  YAML configs ──▶ │ config ──▶ experiments.runner ──▶ data.{interventions,split_integrity,profile} │
                   │               │                 ──▶ training.trainer                           │

                   │               └───────────────▶ tracking.{run_dir,index} ──▶ reports           │
                   └────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                                                    │ imports (no version pin)
                   ┌────────────────────────────────▼──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
                   │ rde: Catalog · DataEngine/DatasetView · TransformPipeline · DatasetBuilder     │
                   │      SmolVLAPolicy.fit/predict/save · Evaluator · FeedbackLoop · baselines     │
                   └────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

   * = exported but not called by src/

Drill — answer from memory, files closed

  1. What's the difference between offline evaluation and closed-loop rollout, and which one does this project do? Why can a model with low offline MSE still fail on the robot?
  2. Why didn't you just put torch in this package's dependencies, or make one environment for everything?
  3. Where does the gradient step actually happen, and what exactly does this repository own about training?
  4. What breaks in production if a teammate runs pip install -e . in a fresh clone?
  5. Interviewer framing: "Walk me through the dependency structure of this project. Is there anything about it you'd change?"

Appendix A — Model answers to the drill

A1. Offline vs closed-loop. "Offline evaluation feeds the model recorded observations from held-out demonstrations and compares its predicted action with what the human actually did, so it's regression error, in normalized action units here. Closed-loop rollout lets the policy drive a robot or simulator and measures whether the task succeeds. This project only does offline evaluation. There's no simulator or robot attached, and the rollout command writes 'unavailable' instead of inventing a number. Low offline MSE can still fail in closed loop for two reasons. First, compounding error: a small deviation puts the robot into states the demonstrations never visited, and the policy has never seen how to recover. Second, averaging: MSE is dominated by the many easy frames, and a policy can be accurate on average and wrong on the few frames that decide success, like the moment of grasp. That's why I report tails (p99 and max) and per-phase error rather than just the mean, and why I never call the tolerance metric a success rate."

Why that answer lands: it names both mechanisms (distribution shift and averaging) instead of saying "sim-to-real is hard", and it ties them to concrete design choices in the codebase. It also shows you know the limits of your own number before the interviewer has to point them out.

A2. Why not declare torch here or use one environment? "Torch and lerobot are only needed by the SmolVLA adapter, which lives in the engine, so the engine owns that dependency through its vla extra. One owner, and no chance of two packages pinning conflicting torch builds. The two environments exist because about 52 of the tests and most of the commands (config, profiling, interventions, reports, baselines) don't need a GPU stack, and a CUDA torch install is several gigabytes and platform-specific. The cost is that it's a convention, not an invariant. Nothing asserts the light environment stays light, and in fact the runner transitively imports the engine's model module, so this relies on the engine importing torch lazily. I'd add a test that imports the runner and asserts torch isn't in sys.modules. I'd flip to one environment the moment CI runs on a GPU image anyway."

Why that answer lands: it gives the reason, then names the cost without being asked, then gives the concrete enforcement fix and the condition under which you'd abandon the design. That's the "no, until X" shape.

A3. Where the gradient step happens. "The step itself (batch construction, forward, loss, backward, optimizer, warmup) is SmolVLAPolicy.fit in the data engine's model_adapters package. I extended that class there because it has to build feature shapes and normalization stats from the engine's training contract. This repository owns the run around the step. It passes an on_step callback that does periodic validation on the val split only, periodic checkpointing with verification, pruning that never deletes the best checkpoint, a forced final checkpoint, a crash marker, peak memory tracking, and freeing the GPU before the evaluation model loads. So the procedure is here and the mechanics are there."

Why that answer lands: it's precise about ownership and doesn't overclaim, which interviewers test for directly. And it turns "I didn't write the loop here" into a clean separation of mechanism and procedure, which is a real design idea.

A4. What breaks with pip install -e .. "The engine dependency is declared by bare name, and the only thing pointing it at the local checkout is [tool.uv.sources], which only uv reads. Plain pip would go to PyPI for episodic-data-engine. That either fails, or worse, installs whatever has that name. Even with uv it needs the engine checked out as a sibling directory, and the runner separately assumes that layout to find the engine's git commit. So a fresh clone isn't installable on its own. The fix is either publishing the engine with a version and pinning a compatible range, or a submodule plus a lockfile, and CI that does exactly the fresh-clone install so this is tested and not just documented."

Why that answer lands: it shows you know the difference between pip and uv semantics, spots the dependency-confusion risk, and ends on a fix that's verified by CI rather than by documentation.

A5. "Walk me through the dependency structure. Anything you'd change?" "Two layers. The engine owns data: versions, lineage, transforms, the builder that assigns splits, the evaluator, failure detection, and also the SmolVLA adapter including fit. This package owns experimental procedure: a validated config, resolving an intervention into a lineage-tracked dataset version, the training cadence and checkpoint selection, stratified evaluation against baselines, and packaging everything into a run directory and an index. All rde imports in the CLI are lazy, so commands start fast. Three things I'd change. First, the model adapter living in the data engine inverts the dependency. It's fine with one model, but with a second model family I'd pull adapters into their own package. Second, the engine is unpinned and the smoke test imports two of its private functions, so I'd pin a version range and promote those functions to public. Third, I'd add a lockfile and CI that checks out both repos, because right now 'the tests pass' isn't continuously true."

Why that answer lands: it describes the structure in terms of responsibility, not files, then volunteers three ranked, concrete changes, each tied to a trigger. Being critical of your own architecture is what separates a designer from someone who only implemented it.

Module 1 Summary — the one page to revise

What this module is. The vocabulary of VLA fine-tuning and offline evaluation, and the shape of the codebase: a thin procedural layer (model_training, about 2,700 lines) over a separately installed data engine (rde) that also owns the model and its training step.

Five claims to make cold

  1. All evaluation is offline (predicted vs demonstrated action, normalized units). Rollout is explicitly reported as unavailable.
  2. Splits are episode-level, to prevent near-duplicate-frame leakage.
  3. SmolVLAPolicy.fit lives in the engine. This repo owns cadence, selection, checkpointing and packaging.
  4. Torch is owned by the engine's vla extra. A light venv runs 52 tests without it, by convention and not by enforcement.
  5. The repo isn't installable alone: there's a sibling-directory uv source, no lockfile, no CI, and an unpinned engine.

Decisions

ChoseOverBecauseCost
Model adapter and fit inside the engineadapter in this repomust share the training contract's shapes and normalizationdata layer owns the model; hardest code isn't here
torch via episodic-data-engine[vla]a vla extra heresingle owner of the heavy stackpyproject alone can't build a training env; undeclared direct torch import
Two venvsone env52 tests don't need CUDAunenforced "lightness"
[tool.uv.sources] sibling pathpublished, pinned engineco-development with editable enginepip-incompatible; layout assumption repeated in code
Re-export façades with __all__deep importsstable public APIeager imports defeat laziness; API wider than what's wired
mtrain script namemodelavoids rde model collisionnone

Volunteer unprompted: no CI; no lockfile; private-API coupling; run artifacts are git-ignored so README numbers aren't reproducible from the repo; no top-level __init__.py; authorship and URL point elsewhere.

Numbers and names: about 2,700 lines in src; 49 unit, 3 integration and 4 smoke tests (README's 52 matches); Python ≥3.10 declared, 3.11 used; lerobot/smolvla_base; datasets so100_pickplace (6-DoF, 2 cameras, 30 fps) and aloha_coffee (14-DoF, 4 cameras, 50 fps); console script mtrain; build backend hatchling.

Sentences worth memorizing

  • "The engine owns the mechanics of one gradient step; this repo owns the procedure around it."
  • "Every number I report is offline. The code refuses to produce a rollout number it can't measure."
  • "Light-environment import discipline is a convention I'd turn into a test."
  • "The run artifacts are deliberately not versioned; the figure script regenerates the README figure from them."

Module 2 — Configuration as the unit of reproducibility

Files covered: src/model_training/config/schema.py, src/model_training/config/loader.py, configs/base.yaml, configs/smolvla/so100_pickplace.yaml, configs/experiments/baseline.yaml, configs/experiments/baseline_extended.yaml, configs/experiments/reduced.yaml, configs/experiments/imbalanced.yaml, configs/experiments/rebalanced.yaml, configs/experiments/aloha_coffee_baseline.yaml

Where we are: Module 1 established that this repository owns procedure and the engine owns mechanism. Procedure starts here. An ExperimentConfig is the object every later stage reads, and its fully resolved form is what gets written next to every run. If the config can't be trusted to mean what it says, nothing downstream can be either. That makes this module the right place to test the project's central claim: "every experimental parameter that matters lives here."

The one-line thesis: Configuration is a two-layer YAML deep-merge validated by Pydantic. The validators do enforce semantic rules: an imbalanced or rebalanced experiment can't run without a declared target. But the schema accepts unknown keys silently, validates several fields that nothing consumes, and has one imbalanced path that silently becomes a full-data run. So the "single object a run is reproducible from" is reproducible, but it isn't always truthful.

2.1 Prerequisites — the absolute basics

  • YAML. A human-readable data format. Mappings (key: value), lists ([a, b] or - a), scalars. Two features appear here:
    • > (a folded block scalar): the following indented lines are joined with spaces into one string. Used for hypothesis: and objective:.
    • null: Python None.
  • yaml.safe_load vs yaml.load. safe_load builds only plain types (dicts, lists, strings, numbers). load with the full loader can construct arbitrary Python objects from tags like !!python/object. That's a code-execution vector if a config file is untrusted. This code uses safe_load and safe_dump everywhere.
  • Deep merge. Combining two nested dicts so that nested dicts are merged key by key, and anything else (scalars, lists) in the override replaces the base value.
  • Pydantic (v2). A library that turns a class with type annotations into a validator. Model.model_validate(dict) checks types, applies defaults, coerces where allowed, and raises ValidationError on failure. Key terms:
    • Field(default=..., ge=0.0, le=1.0): a default plus numeric bounds (greater-or-equal, less-or-equal).
    • Field(default_factory=lambda: [...]): builds a fresh mutable default for each instance, avoiding Python's shared-mutable-default bug.
    • Literal["a", "b"]: the value must be exactly one of those.
    • @model_validator(mode="after"): a method that runs after field validation, on the constructed object. It can check cross-field rules and, as here, mutate fields.
    • Lax mode (the default): sensible coercions are allowed, such as "3" → 3 for an int field or dict key. An int is not coerced to str in v2.
    • extra behaviour: by default (extra="ignore") unknown keys are silently dropped. extra="forbid" makes them errors. Nothing in this schema sets it.
    • model_dump(mode="json"): export to plain JSON-compatible types (dict keys become strings, and so on).
  • Configuration hierarchy / layered config. Shared defaults in one file, per-experiment overrides in another. This is the pattern Hydra or OmegaConf implement in fuller form.
  • Resolved config. The final config after merging and after defaults are applied. Persisting the resolved form means a run doesn't depend on code defaults that might change later.
  • Content-addressed ID vs time-stamped ID. A content-addressed ID is a hash of the content, so the same content gets the same ID. A time-stamped ID mixes in a clock value, so it's unique per creation.

2.2 The loader: merge first, validate once

def _deep_merge(base: dict[str, Any], override: dict[str, Any]) -> dict[str, Any]:
    out = dict(base)
    for k, v in override.items():
        if k in out and isinstance(out[k], dict) and isinstance(v, dict):
            out[k] = _deep_merge(out[k], v)
        else:
            out[k] = v
    return out
  • What: a recursive merge. Dicts merge key by key; everything else is replaced.
  • Why dict(base): a shallow copy, so the caller's base dict isn't mutated at the top level. Nested dicts that the override never touches are shared between out and base, which is safe only because nothing mutates them afterwards.
  • Lists replace, not append. That's the right semantics for this project. aloha_coffee_baseline.yaml sets slices: [per_length_bucket] to replace base's [per_task, per_length_bucket], and the comment explains why (one task, so per_task would duplicate overall). Appending would make it impossible to remove a slice.
  • A key can't be deleted. An override can set null, but it can't remove a key. That's fine here: every schema field has a default, so null and absent differ only for fields whose default isn't None.
  • Alternative: Hydra or OmegaConf, which give interpolation, command-line overrides (training.lr=3e-5), and structured-config validation.
  • Cost of the hand-rolled version: no CLI overrides, so changing a learning rate means editing or copying a YAML file. That's arguably a feature for an experiment log, since every run has a file.
  • When the answer flips: once sweeps over hyperparameters (not just data fractions) are routine.
def load_yaml(path: str | Path) -> dict[str, Any]:
    path = Path(path)
    if not path.exists():
        raise FileNotFoundError(f"no config file at {path}")
    with path.open() as f:
        data = yaml.safe_load(f) or {}
    if not isinstance(data, dict):
        raise ValueError(f"{path} must contain a YAML mapping at the top level")
    return data
  • or {} handles an empty file: safe_load("") returns None. Without it, the isinstance check would raise a misleading "must contain a mapping".
  • The isinstance(data, dict) check catches a file whose top level is a list or a bare string. Without it, _deep_merge would fail later with an AttributeError that doesn't name the file. It's a good defensive branch with a specific error.
def load_experiment_config(path, base_path=None) -> ExperimentConfig:
    path = Path(path)
    data = load_yaml(path)

    if base_path is None:
        candidate = path.parent.parent / "base.yaml"
        base_path = candidate if candidate.exists() else None

    if base_path is not None:
        base_data = load_yaml(base_path)
        data = _deep_merge(base_data, data)

    data["source_config_path"] = str(path)
    return ExperimentConfig.model_validate(data)

Decision: find the base by directory convention (<grandparent>/base.yaml).

  • What: configs/experiments/x.yaml picks up configs/base.yaml automatically.
  • Why: zero ceremony for the layout the project ships.
  • Alternative: an explicit base: key inside each experiment file, or an explicit --base flag.
  • Cost: what a config file means depends on where it sits on disk. Copy baseline.yaml to /tmp/ and load it: the grandparent of /tmp/baseline.yaml is /, there's no /base.yaml, and the base is skipped. baseline.yaml alone has no dataset: block, so this fails loudly (dataset is required), which is the lucky case. A copied file that does declare dataset: would load and run silently with code defaults (steps: 200, checkpoint_every: 50, batch_size: 32 for eval) instead of base.yaml's values (1000, 100, 16). The resolved config records which values were used, so it's recoverable, but nothing warns you.

Merge-then-validate is the right order, and the module docstring states it. The merged dict is what Pydantic sees, so a base value plus an override value are validated together. A validator like "fractions sum to one" sees the final numbers, not each file alone.

Doc vs code: --base doesn't exist. configs/smolvla/so100_pickplace.yaml's header says:

include its contents in an experiment file's training: block, or point --base at it explicitly

The only CLI caller is cli.py:67, cfg = load_experiment_config(config), and mtrain train has options --config and --seed only. There's no --base flag. base_path is reachable only from Python. And load_experiment_config takes one base, so the "second model-specific layer" that file talks about can't be stacked on top of base.yaml at all. The file is effectively documentation. Grep shows nothing loads it. Its values also duplicate base.yaml's exactly, so even if it were wired up it would change nothing.

source_config_path overwrites the user. It's assigned after merging, so a YAML source_config_path: is overwritten. Good: provenance can't be spoofed through this one field. But see 2.6 for the two identity fields that can be.

2.3 DatasetRef and the "identity, not a path" decision

class DatasetRef(BaseModel):
    """The dataset version an experiment starts from -- an identity, not a path."""
    dataset_id: str
    version: str = Field(..., description="e.g. 'clean/v1' -- the input to the intervention/build step")

Decision: name data by (dataset_id, version), never by filesystem path.

  • Why: a path can be overwritten in place; a catalog version is immutable (the engine's claim, inherited). This is what makes "this model was trained on so100_pickplace@training/v6" a stable statement.
  • Alternative: data_dir: /data/so100/ (the common LeRobot style).
  • Cost: you can't train on anything that hasn't been ingested into the engine first. There's no "just point at a folder" path. That's deliberate.

The input version is clean/v1, not training/v1. base.yaml's comment explains it:

dataset:
  dataset_id: so100_pickplace
  version: clean/v1   # the intervention/build step consumes this; NOT training/v1,
                       # which is itself an *output* of that step

The config names the version the intervention starts from. Every run then produces its own training/vN (Module 4). A consequence to have ready: two runs with identical configs produce two different training/vN versions. The README's lineage example is training/v6, which tells you at least six had been built.

2.4 DatasetIntervention: semantics enforced, mostly

kind: Literal["full", "fraction", "reduced", "imbalanced", "rebalanced", "custom"] = "full"
fraction: float | None = Field(default=None, ge=0.0, le=1.0)
variable: str | None = None
variable_value: Any = None
episode_ids: list[int] | None = None
episode_weights: dict[int, float] | None = None
oversample_factor: float = 3.0
seed: int = 0
reason: str = ""

This is a tagged union flattened into one class: kind is the discriminator, and which other fields matter depends on it.

  • Alternative: Pydantic's discriminated unions, i.e. one class per kind (FullIntervention, FractionIntervention(fraction: float), and so on), selected by Field(discriminator="kind"). Each class would declare only its fields, so kind: full, fraction: 0.3 would be rejected as an unknown field (with extra="forbid").
  • Cost of the flat form: irrelevant fields are accepted and ignored. kind: full with episode_ids: [1, 2] is valid, and resolve_episode_subset returns all episodes for full before it ever looks at episode_ids (Module 4). It's a silent no-op on a field the user explicitly set.

The validator:

@model_validator(mode="after")
def _check_shape(self) -> "DatasetIntervention":
    if self.kind in ("fraction", "reduced") and self.episode_ids is None and self.fraction is None:
        if self.kind == "reduced":
            self.fraction = 0.5
        else:
            raise ValueError("intervention.kind='fraction' requires 'fraction' or an explicit 'episode_ids'")
    if self.kind == "imbalanced" and self.episode_ids is None and self.variable is None:
        raise ValueError("... requires 'variable' (from a dataset profile) or an explicit 'episode_ids' "
                         "-- the weak dimension must not be presumed")
    if self.kind == "rebalanced" and self.variable is None and not self.episode_weights:
        raise ValueError("... requires 'variable' ... or an explicit non-empty 'episode_weights'")
    if self.kind == "custom" and self.episode_ids is None:
        raise ValueError("intervention.kind='custom' requires an explicit 'episode_ids'")
    return self

Line by line:

  • reduced defaults fraction to 0.5. "Reduced" means "the project-standard ~50%", so reduced is just fraction with a default. A mutating after-validator is unusual but legal, and Pydantic v2 allows assignment inside mode="after". The cost: 0.5 appears as a magic number in two places. The other is resolve_episode_subset's intervention.fraction if intervention.fraction is not None else 0.5, which is unreachable for configs that went through this validator. Two sources of one default can drift.
  • imbalanced requires variable or episode_ids. This encodes the design rule "the weak dimension must not be presumed": you can't ask for an imbalance without naming along what. It's a methodological rule enforced as a type error. That's the strongest thing in this file, and it's tested (test_imbalanced_requires_variable_or_episode_ids).
  • rebalanced requires variable or non-empty episode_weights. not self.episode_weights treats both None and {} as missing. That's exactly why the shipped rebalanced.yaml template, with episode_weights: {}, fails to load, as its header promises and test_rebalanced_template_fails_loudly_until_filled_in asserts. Doc and code agree here.
  • custom requires episode_ids, but [] passes (it checks is None, not falsiness). That asymmetry is deliberate: imbalanced.yaml is a custom template with episode_ids: [], and its header says it is "Loadable as-is ... but resolving it ... will fail loudly with 'no episodes'". It does: build_intervention_dataset raises "resolved to zero episodes" (Module 4). So the two templates fail at different stages, one at load and one at resolution. The headers document this correctly, but it's an inconsistency you could be asked about. The honest answer is that they're placeholders with different shapes, and both fail before any training step.

What the validator does not check

  1. imbalanced with variable set but variable_value missing passes. Follow it through resolve_episode_subset (Module 4): with variable="task_id", val=None, it keeps e where task_of.get(e) != None, which is every episode that has a task. With variable="episode_length_bucket", val=None, no episode has bucket None, so all are kept. The subset equals the full set, so build_intervention_dataset sets clean_version = input_version and trains a full-data run labelled imbalanced. That's exactly the "silently run as a no-op" outcome the rebalanced.yaml header says the design forbids. Rebalanced has a guard for this (variable_value is not None before resolving targets, then "resolved to empty episode_weights" raises). Imbalanced doesn't.
  2. variable_value: Any isn't coerced. Hand-write variable: task_id, variable_value: "0" (a string) and the comparison task_of.get(e) != "0" is true for every integer task id, so again a silent full run. The CLI's build-intervention converts task_id values to int (value = int(variable_value)), so the generated path is safe. The hand-written path isn't.
  3. oversample_factor and episode_weights values are unbounded. oversample_factor: 0 or -1 passes validation. What a zero or negative sampling weight does inside the engine's sampler is unverified. A torch.multinomial-style sampler would reject negatives. Either way, it isn't caught here.
  4. fraction with kind: full (or rebalanced) is accepted and ignored.

episode_weights: dict[int, float] and YAML string keys. mtrain build-intervention writes weights as {str(e): oversample_factor}, so YAML holds '3': 3.0. Pydantic lax mode coerces a numeric string dict key to int, so this round-trips correctly. It's correct, but it works by coercion and not by design. A non-numeric key would be a validation error, which is the right outcome.

2.5 SplitConfig

class SplitConfig(BaseModel):
    train: float = 0.8
    val: float = 0.1
    test: float = 0.1
    seed: int = 42
    mode: Literal["quantile", "independent"] = "quantile"

    @model_validator(mode="after")
    def _sums_to_one(self) -> "SplitConfig":
        total = self.train + self.val + self.test
        if abs(total - 1.0) > 1e-6:
            raise ValueError(f"train+val+test must sum to 1.0, got {total}")
        return self
  • The 1e-6 tolerance is needed because 0.8 + 0.1 + 0.1 in binary floating point isn't exactly 1.0 (it's 0.9999999999999999 or 1.0000000000000002 depending on order). An exact == 1.0 would reject the default config. Anything looser would accept typos like 0.8/0.1/0.11.
  • Missing: non-negativity. train: 1.2, val: -0.1, test: -0.1 sums to 1.0 and passes. So does val: 0.0. What the engine's assign_splits does with a negative fraction is unverified. A zero val split means the trainer's validation pass scores an empty split, val_metric comes back missing or None, no checkpoint is ever "best", and selection silently falls back to the final checkpoint (Module 6). The fix is one line: Field(ge=0.0, le=1.0) on each, plus val > 0.
  • mode: quantile vs independent. These are the engine's split-assignment modes. base.yaml's comment, "exact split sizes at this episode count (48)", plus the README's 38/5/5, suggests quantile assigns exact counts (48 × 0.8 = 38.4 → 38, and 4.8 → 5 for each of val and test). independent would presumably draw each episode's split independently, giving sizes that vary run to run. Both are inferred from the name and the comment; the engine's code isn't here. Why it matters at n = 48: with independent draws, a 10% split of 48 episodes has a standard deviation of about √(48·0.1·0.9) ≈ 2 episodes, so a 5-episode test set could easily come out as 3 or 7. Exact sizes are the right choice at this scale.
  • seed: 42 (split seed) is separate from training.seed: 0. That separation is a real decision. You can vary the training seed while holding the test split fixed, which is what makes "both SO-100 runs are scored on the identical test split" possible. The cost: there are now three seeds (split, intervention, training), and the CLI's --seed touches none of them (Module 11).

The docstring says "identical semantics to rde.transformations.ops.assign_splits, because it is that call." That's honest only partly. The fractions, seed and mode are passed straight through to DatasetBuilder.build (Module 4), but SplitConfig's validation is this repo's, so the two layers could disagree about what's legal (for example, if the engine accepts fractions that don't sum to 1).

2.6 TrainingConfig, EvaluationConfig, ExperimentConfig

Fields validated but never consumed

I grepped every TrainingConfig and EvaluationConfig field against src/:

FieldConsumed?Evidence
training.num_workersnoonly in schema.py
training.mixed_precisionnoonly in schema.py
evaluation.success_tolerancenoonly in schema.py; never passed to Evaluator
training.deviceyesSmolVLAPolicy(..., device=tc.device)
everything else in TrainingConfigyespassed to policy.fit or used in Trainer

Doc vs code: docs/API_REFERENCE.md describes success_tolerance as "Tolerance band defining Evaluator's success_rate proxy." Setting it to 0.05 changes nothing. The evaluator uses its own default (the README's "below 0.1" matches the schema default by coincidence, or because both copy the engine's value; I can't verify which). These three fields make the config lie: a resolved config that says mixed_precision: true describes a run that used full precision.

The comment pointing at a module that doesn't exist

#: Metric used for checkpoint selection. Must be computed on validation,
#: never test -- enforced by model_training.experiments.selection, not here.
selection_metric: str = "mse"

model_training/experiments/ contains __init__.py, runner.py and data_fraction.py. There's no selection module. The val-only rule is enforced, but structurally, in Trainer.on_step, which hard-codes split="val" (Module 6). So the guarantee holds and the citation is stale. Say it exactly that way.

selection_metric: str is a free string. If you name a metric the evaluator doesn't produce ("msee"), result.metrics.get(ec.selection_metric) returns None, no checkpoint ever becomes best, and the trainer silently falls back to final (Module 6). A Literal[...] of known metric names would catch it at load.

training.model: str, the most dangerous free string

model: str = "smolvla". The runner branches on config.training.model == "smolvla", and anything else skips training, appends a limitation note, and still completes a "successful" run that scores baselines only. That path is intended for mean_action (the runner unit test uses it). But a typo, model: SmolVLA or smol_vla, produces a run directory, a report, a summary and an index row, with no fine-tuning and exit code 0. The only trace is one line under "Limitations" in the report. Literal["smolvla", "mean_action", "nearest_neighbor"] would turn this into a load-time error.

Two sources of defaults

Fieldschema.py defaultbase.yaml value
training.steps2001000
training.checkpoint_every / validate_every50 / 50100 / 100
training.warmup_steps050
evaluation.batch_size3216
evaluation.slices["per_task"]["per_task", "per_length_bucket"]

Which one applies depends on whether base.yaml was found (2.2). Persisting the resolved config makes it auditable, but a reader of schema.py gets the wrong mental model of a "default" run. Pick one home, ideally the YAML, with schema defaults kept only for fields that are genuinely optional.

Identity stamping

experiment_id: str | None = None
created_at: str | None = None
source_config_path: str | None = None

@model_validator(mode="after")
def _stamp_identity(self) -> "ExperimentConfig":
    if self.created_at is None:
        self.created_at = _dt.datetime.now(_dt.timezone.utc).isoformat()
    if self.experiment_id is None:
        basis = f"{self.experiment_name}:{self.dataset.dataset_id}:{self.dataset.version}:{self.created_at}"
        digest = hashlib.sha256(basis.encode()).hexdigest()[:10]
        self.experiment_id = f"{self.experiment_name}-{digest}"
    return self
  • What: an ID like baseline_full-3f9a1c0b2e. The readable prefix is for humans; the hash makes it unique.
  • Why created_at is in the hash: so two runs of the same experiment get different IDs. Without it, every run of baseline_full on clean/v1 would collide in the index.
  • The hash covers name, dataset and version only, not the hyperparameters. So the ID isn't content-addressed. Two configs differing only in learning rate get IDs that differ only because of the timestamp. It's a unique run identifier wearing a content hash's clothes. Calling it "deterministic" (as the test name test_experiment_id_is_deterministic_given_same_inputs does) is true only when created_at is pinned, which never happens from YAML.
  • Alternative: uuid4() for uniqueness, plus a separate config_hash = sha256(resolved_dict minus identity fields). The second would let you ask "have I already run exactly this config?", which nothing in the system can answer today.
  • Ten hex characters is 40 bits. Collisions are irrelevant at this scale, so that's fine.
  • Doc vs code: "not user-editable." The field comment says "Set once at load time; not user-editable via YAML," and API_REFERENCE repeats it. Nothing prevents it. experiment_id: anything in a YAML is accepted, the is None guard skips stamping, and the run is indexed under the user's string. Two runs could then share an ID. It's fixable with a mode="before" validator that pops these keys, or with Field(exclude=True) plus init=False semantics. Meanwhile, data_fraction.py relies on re-stamping by explicitly setting both to None before re-validating (Module 11). That pattern shows the author knew the guard exists.
def resolved_dict(self) -> dict[str, Any]:
    return self.model_dump(mode="json")

mode="json" ensures the dump contains only JSON-safe types, so yaml.safe_dump won't fail on, say, an int-keyed dict (its keys become strings). The persisted config.resolved.yaml therefore has string keys for episode_weights, and re-loading it coerces them back to int. It round-trips through coercion again.

extra is never forbidden, the root gap

None of the six models sets model_config = ConfigDict(extra="forbid"). So:

training:
  learning_rat: 3.0e-5     # typo

loads cleanly. learning_rate keeps its inherited 1.0e-4, and the run trains at the wrong learning rate. Worse, the resolved config drops the typo, so the run's own record shows learning_rate: 1e-4 with no trace that you ever asked for something else. For a project whose schema docstring opens with "Every experimental parameter that matters lives here", this is the single most important one-line fix in the codebase.

2.7 The shipped configs, read as experiments

FileKindRunnable as-is?Notes from the code
baseline.yamlfullyes18 lines; inherits everything else from base
baseline_extended.yamlfull + resume_fromonly on the author's machineresume_from: runs/2026-09-03/baseline_full/seed_0/checkpoints/step_1000, relative to the current working directory, a dated path, and git-ignored (Module 1)
reduced.yamlreduced, fraction 0.5, seed 0yestest_reduced_config_loads_with_fraction asserts the 0.5
imbalanced.yamlcustom, episode_ids: []loads; fails at resolutionintentional template
rebalanced.yamlrebalanced, empty weightsfails at loadintentional template; test asserts it
aloha_coffee_baseline.yamlfull on aloha_coffeeyes, if ingestedoverrides slices to drop per_task

Points to slow down on:

  • baseline_extended.yaml resumes weights, not optimizer state. Its header says the reduced LR is "freshly-warmed-up" (warmup_steps: 20, learning_rate: 3.0e-5). That's consistent with Trainer writing "optimizer_state_saved": False into each checkpoint (Module 6). The optimizer's moment estimates are lost on resume, which is why a fresh warmup is needed: Adam-style optimizers with zeroed second moments take large steps at first.
