The comment and the code agree, and it's a genuinely good decision. Learning-rate schedule semantics after warmup (API_REFERENCE says "cosine decay") live in the engine and are unverified.
  • The extended run builds a new training/vN (intervention: kind: full), not the same one baseline_full used. "The test set is identical" therefore depends on DatasetBuilder assigning the same splits given the same episodes, seed (42) and mode. That's plausible for a deterministic seeded builder, and the README asserts it, but it isn't checked by any code here. A test comparing split_integrity.json split membership across the two runs would prove it. The README shows split sizes, not membership.
  • aloha_coffee_baseline.yaml's hypothesis says "generalizes without code changes". That's a claim about the engine's adapter and fit(), which "read camera/action shape from the dataset contract". The smoke test test_policy_features_concatenate_every_inline_modality_like_state_adapter_does exists because ALOHA's two inline modalities (28 state dims) exposed a mismatch. So it generalized after a fix, which is a better story to tell than "without changes".
  • baseline_extended.yaml duplicates many base values (model, pretrained_checkpoint, batch_size, checkpoint_every, ...) that the deep merge would supply anyway. It's harmless, but it contradicts base.yaml's own guidance that "only the fields that differ need to appear there."

2.8 Honest gaps to own before an interviewer finds them

  1. Unknown keys are silently ignored, and the resolved config erases the evidence (no extra="forbid" on any model). Consequence: a typo trains with the wrong hyperparameter, and the run record looks correct.
  2. training.model is a free string, and any value except "smolvla" silently skips training. Consequence: a case typo produces a complete, exit-0 run with no fine-tuning, flagged only in the report's Limitations.
  3. Three dead fields: num_workers, mixed_precision, success_tolerance. Consequence: the persisted config misdescribes the run, and API_REFERENCE documents an effect success_tolerance doesn't have.
  4. imbalanced with variable but no (or a wrongly typed) variable_value silently becomes a full-data run labelled imbalanced. Consequence: the "never silently no-op" guarantee that rebalanced enforces doesn't hold for imbalanced.
  5. experiment_id and created_at are user-settable despite the "not user-editable" comment. Consequence: ID collisions in the index are possible from a copied resolved config.
  6. experiment_id isn't content-addressed (hyperparameters aren't in the hash). Consequence: there's no way to detect "already ran this exact config".
  7. The schema comment cites model_training.experiments.selection, which doesn't exist. Consequence: a stale reference. The guarantee itself is real but lives in Trainer.
  8. Split fractions aren't bounded to [0, 1] and val can be 0. Consequence: a zero val split silently disables checkpoint selection.
  9. The base-config location depends on directory convention, and there's no --base flag despite configs/smolvla/so100_pickplace.yaml saying there is. Consequence: a config's meaning changes when the file moves, and the model-specific layer file is dead.
  10. Two sources of defaults (schema vs base.yaml) disagree on steps, cadence, warmup, eval batch size and slices. Consequence: there's no single answer to "what's the default run?".
  11. baseline_extended.yaml hard-codes a dated, CWD-relative, git-ignored checkpoint path. Consequence: it isn't runnable by anyone else, or by you after cleaning runs/.
  12. The flat intervention class accepts fields irrelevant to its kind (kind: full plus episode_ids). Consequence: user intent is silently ignored.
  13. The 0.5 "reduced" default lives in two places. Consequence: drift risk. The second copy is unreachable for validated configs.

The shape to carry into Module 3

 configs/base.yaml ─┐  (found by path.parent.parent convention; skipped if absent)
                    ├─ _deep_merge (dicts merge, lists/scalars replace)
 configs/experiments/<name>.yaml ─┘
                    │  + source_config_path (forced)
                    ▼
        ExperimentConfig.model_validate        extra keys: IGNORED (!)
        ├── DatasetRef        (dataset_id, version)   identity, never a path
        ├── DatasetIntervention  kind ∈ {full,fraction,reduced,imbalanced,rebalanced,custom}
        │      after-validator: reduced→0.5; imbalanced/rebalanced need a declared target
        ├── SplitConfig       sums to 1 ± 1e-6 (no bounds), seed 42, quantile
        ├── TrainingConfig    model:str (!), 3 dead fields
        ├── EvaluationConfig  selection on val (enforced in Trainer, not "selection")
        └── identity: created_at=now(), experiment_id=name-sha256(name:ds:ver:ts)[:10]
                    │
                    ▼  resolved_dict() = model_dump(mode="json")
             config.resolved.yaml  (written into the run dir — Module 3)

Drill — answer from memory, files closed

  1. Walk through what happens, field by field, when configs/experiments/reduced.yaml is loaded. Where does fraction: 0.5 come from, and where else does 0.5 appear?
  2. Why didn't you just use Hydra or OmegaConf?
  3. What breaks in production: a teammate writes learning_rat: 3e-5 in a new experiment file. What happens, and what does the run directory say?
  4. Why are there three separate seeds, and which one does mtrain train --seed 1 change?
  5. Interviewer framing: "How do you make sure an experiment can't silently run as something other than what was intended?"

Appendix A — Model answers to the drill

A1. Loading reduced.yaml. "load_experiment_config reads the file, finds configs/base.yaml by going two directories up, and deep-merges the experiment file over the base. Nested dicts merge key by key; lists and scalars replace. So dataset, splits, training and evaluation all come from base, and intervention, experiment_name and tags come from the experiment. It forces source_config_path, then calls ExperimentConfig.model_validate once on the merged dict. In this file fraction: 0.5 is set explicitly. If it weren't, the DatasetIntervention after-validator would set it, because kind: reduced with no fraction and no episode IDs defaults to 0.5. There's a second 0.5 fallback in resolve_episode_subset that validated configs can never reach, which is a small duplication I'd remove. Finally the identity validator stamps created_at and an ID of the form reduced_data- plus ten hex characters of a SHA-256 over name, dataset, version and timestamp."

Why that answer lands: it follows the real order of operations (merge, then validate, then stamp) and volunteers the duplicated default. That shows you've read the code path, not the docstring.

A2. Why not Hydra? "The surface I needed was small: one base layer, one override layer, and strong semantic validation, like 'an imbalanced run must name the dimension it skews'. Pydantic gives me the semantic validators, and the merge is ten lines. Hydra would add command-line overrides and multirun sweeps, but here every run is meant to correspond to a file, because the config file is the experiment record and carries a hypothesis and an objective. The cost is no training.lr=... overrides and a directory-convention base lookup that changes meaning if you move a file. I'd move to Hydra, or at least OmegaConf with structured configs, once hyperparameter sweeps became routine rather than data-fraction sweeps, which the code already handles by generating configs programmatically."

Why that answer lands: it justifies the choice by what the configs are for (an experiment log), admits the concrete cost, and names the trigger for switching.

A3. The learning_rat typo. "It loads without error, because none of the Pydantic models set extra='forbid', so unknown keys are dropped. learning_rate keeps the base value of 1e-4, and the run trains at the wrong rate. The worse part is the record: config.resolved.yaml is a dump of the validated model, so the typo is gone. The run directory says learning_rate: 1e-4, which is true, and there's no trace that you asked for 3e-5. That's the most important one-line fix in the project: model_config = ConfigDict(extra='forbid') on every config model. Together with turning training.model into a Literal, it closes the two ways a config can silently mean something other than what was written."

Why that answer lands: it identifies both the failure and the evidence-erasure, which is the subtler point, and gives the exact fix. It sounds like someone who has debugged this kind of bug.

A4. Three seeds. "The split seed (42) decides which episodes are in train, val and test. Keeping it fixed is what lets two runs share an identical test set. The intervention seed decides which episodes a fraction draw keeps, so a reduced run is a reproducible random subset. The training seed decides sampling order and initialization noise during fine-tuning. Separating them lets me vary one source of randomness while holding the others fixed. Honestly, mtrain train --seed 1 changes none of them. It only names the run directory seed_1 and records 1 in the summary, while training.seed stays at 0. That's a real bug. The flag should set training.seed, and probably loop over the config's seeds list, which today ignores everything after the first entry."

Why that answer lands: it explains the design reason for separating seeds, then volunteers a real bug in your own code before being caught. That's the single most credibility-building move available.

A5. "How do you make sure an experiment can't silently run as something other than intended?" "At the config layer there are semantic validators. An imbalanced intervention can't load without naming the variable it skews, a rebalanced one can't load with empty weights, and split fractions must sum to one. The shipped templates are built to fail before any training step. Downstream, the resolved config, including every default, is persisted with the run. But I have to be straight about the holes I found reviewing it. Unknown keys are ignored, so typos vanish. The model name is a free string, and anything but smolvla skips training while still producing a complete run. Three fields, including mixed_precision, are validated but never used. And an imbalanced intervention with a variable but no value resolves to the full dataset. The fixes are extra='forbid', Literal types for model and metric names, deleting or wiring the dead fields, and the same 'resolved to the full set' guard that rebalanced already has."

Why that answer lands: it leads with what works and why, then lists specific, ranked holes with specific fixes. The interviewer learns you can audit your own system, which is what the question is really probing.

Module 2 Summary — the one page to revise

What this module is. A two-layer YAML config (base plus experiment) deep-merged and validated once by Pydantic into an ExperimentConfig whose resolved dump is persisted with every run. Semantic validators enforce the project's methodology (declare the weak dimension); the type surface is too permissive to guarantee the config is truthful.

Five claims to make cold

  1. Merge first, validate once. Lists replace, dicts merge, and the resolved config, defaults included, is persisted.
  2. Data is named by (dataset_id, version) and never by path. The input is clean/v1, and each run builds its own training/vN.
  3. Imbalanced and rebalanced interventions can't load without a declared target. "The weak dimension must not be presumed" is enforced as a validation error.
  4. There are three seeds: split 42, intervention 0, training 0. The split seed is what keeps test sets identical across runs.
  5. The biggest gap is no extra="forbid". Typos vanish, and the resolved record hides them.

Decisions

ChoseOverBecauseCost
Hand-rolled deep merge plus PydanticHydra/OmegaConfsmall surface; file = experiment recordno CLI overrides; location-dependent base
Version identityfilesystem pathimmutability, traceable lineagecan't train on un-ingested data
Flat intervention class with a kind tagdiscriminated unionone simple classirrelevant fields silently accepted
Mutating after-validator (reduced→0.5)explicit fraction requiredergonomic shorthanddefault duplicated in resolver
name-sha256(name:ds:ver:ts)[:10]uuid4 plus config hashreadable and uniquenot content-addressed; can't detect re-runs
Exact (quantile) splitsindependent drawsn = 48 makes independent sizes vary ±2none at this scale
Fresh warmup on resumerestoring optimizer stateoptimizer state isn't savedlost Adam moments; LR schedule restarts

Volunteer unprompted: no extra="forbid"; model: str silently skips training; num_workers, mixed_precision and success_tolerance are dead; imbalanced can silently no-op; experiment_id is user-settable; there's no --base flag despite a config comment; the selection module citation is stale; split fractions are unbounded.

Numbers and names: split 0.8/0.1/0.1, seed 42, quantile → 38/5/5 on 48 episodes and 32/4/4 on 40; base training 1000 steps, batch 4, lr 1e-4, wd 1e-5, warmup 50, ckpt/val every 100, val_batches 8, keep 3, clip 1.0; extended lr 3e-5, warmup 20; eval batch 16, tolerance 0.1; sum tolerance 1e-6; ID hash 10 hex characters (40 bits).

Sentences worth memorizing

  • "The config file is the experiment record. It carries the hypothesis as well as the hyperparameters."
  • "An imbalanced run that doesn't name its dimension is a validation error, not a judgement call."
  • "The resolved config is persisted so a run never depends on an unwritten default. The gap is that it also can't show a key you mistyped."
  • "The split seed is separate from the training seed so the test set stays fixed while training varies."

Module 3 — The run directory and the cross-run index

Files covered: src/model_training/tracking/run_dir.py, src/model_training/tracking/index.py

Where we are: Module 2 produced a validated ExperimentConfig. Before any data is touched, the runner gives that config a place to live: a run directory, plus snapshots of the environment and git state, plus rows in a project-wide index. Every later module writes into the structure defined here, so the guarantees and non-guarantees of this module are inherited by everything after it.

The one-line thesis: Run tracking is plain files: one directory per run that refuses to be overwritten, plus four append-only JSONL files queryable without this code. The design is honest and tool-agnostic, but none of the writes are atomic, the index isn't transactional with the run, and the provenance it captures (git commit of both repos) can silently come out as null for the repo that matters most.

3.1 Prerequisites — the absolute basics

  • Experiment tracking. Recording, for every training run, what went in (config, data, code version, environment) and what came out (metrics, checkpoints), so runs can be compared and reproduced. Off-the-shelf tools: MLflow, Weights & Biases, TensorBoard, DVC, Aim.
  • Run directory. A folder holding one run's artifacts. Here it's runs/<date>/<experiment_name>/seed_<seed>/.
  • JSON / JSONL. JSON is one structured document. JSONL (JSON Lines) is one JSON object per line. Its virtue is that you can append a record without rewriting the file, and tools like jq, pandas.read_json(lines=True) and DuckDB read_json_auto read it directly.
  • Append-only log. A file that's only ever added to, never edited. History is preserved, and "the current value" for a key is the latest row for that key (the event-sourcing idea in miniature).
  • Atomic write. A write that either fully happens or doesn't happen at all, so no reader ever sees half a file. The standard recipe is to write to a temporary file in the same directory, fsync, then os.replace(tmp, target). os.replace is atomic on POSIX filesystems.
  • Torn write. What you get without atomicity when a process dies mid-write: a truncated file or a half line.
  • TOCTOU (time-of-check to time-of-use). A race where you check a condition ("is this directory empty?") and then act on it, and another process changes the condition in between.
  • O_APPEND. The OS flag behind Python's open(..., "a"). Each write() syscall goes to the current end of file. On a local POSIX filesystem a single write() of a modest size won't interleave with another process's append. Python's buffered text I/O may split one logical line into several syscalls if it exceeds the buffer (8 KiB by default), and network filesystems (NFS) don't guarantee append atomicity at all.
  • Dirty working tree. A git checkout with uncommitted changes (git status --porcelain non-empty). A commit hash alone doesn't identify the code that ran from a dirty tree.
  • CUDA compute capability vs driver version. Compute capability (for example 8.9) is the GPU architecture's feature level. The driver version is the installed NVIDIA kernel driver. torch.version.cuda is a third thing: the CUDA toolkit version PyTorch was compiled against.

3.2 RunDir and make_run_dir: the no-overwrite rule

@dataclass
class RunDir:
    root: Path
    experiment_name: str
    seed: int
    date: str

    _SUBDIRS = ("checkpoints", "predictions", "evaluation", "failures", "reports", "logs", "metrics")

    @property
    def path(self) -> Path:
        return self.root / self.date / self.experiment_name / f"seed_{self.seed}"
  • _SUBDIRS has no type annotation, so @dataclass treats it as a class attribute, not a field. That's intentional: it's a constant of the layout, not per-instance state.
  • Layout date / name / seed. It groups by day first, which suits a researcher scanning what they ran today. Alternative: name / date / seed groups all runs of one experiment together, which suits comparison. Cost of the choice: every reference to a run embeds its date. baseline_extended.yaml hard-codes runs/2026-09-03/... (Module 2), and scripts/plot_results.py needs three separate dated paths.
  • seed in the path. Multi-seed runs of the same experiment sit side by side. As Module 2 showed, the seed here is the CLI/summary seed, which doesn't control training.seed. So seed_1/ and seed_0/ from mtrain train --seed 1 vs the default differ only in name (Module 11 covers this in detail).
def make_run_dir(base_runs_dir, experiment_name, seed, date=None) -> RunDir:
    date = date or _dt.datetime.now(_dt.timezone.utc).strftime("%Y-%m-%d")
    rd = RunDir(root=Path(base_runs_dir), experiment_name=experiment_name, seed=seed, date=date)
    if rd.path.exists() and any(rd.path.iterdir()):
        raise FileExistsError(
            f"run directory already exists and is non-empty: {rd.path} -- "
            "never silently overwrite an experiment run"
        )
    rd.ensure()
    return rd

Decision: refuse a non-empty run directory and never overwrite.

  • What: if runs/<today>/<name>/seed_<n>/ has anything in it, raise.
  • Why: overwriting would destroy checkpoints and metrics. The guarantee is tested (test_runner_refuses_to_overwrite_an_existing_run expects FileExistsError on the second run).
  • Alternative 1: auto-suffix (seed_0_2, or a timestamp or UUID directory). Alternative 2: MLflow-style opaque run IDs, with names as tags.
  • Cost:
    1. A failed run blocks its own retry for the rest of the (UTC) day. The runner creates the directory and writes environment.json, git.json and config.resolved.yaml before the intervention step (Module 11). So a typo in a dataset version, a missing GPU, anything at all that fails, leaves a non-empty directory. The next attempt raises FileExistsError. The message explains what to do, which is the right behaviour for a guard, but there's no --force and no crash marker outside the trainer (Module 6 has crash.json; earlier failures have nothing).
    2. The date is UTC. A run started at 23:50 and retried at 00:10 UTC gets a different directory and doesn't collide. Collision behaviour therefore depends on the wall clock, which is surprising but harmless.
    3. Check-then-create is a TOCTOU race. Two processes launching the same experiment and seed at once can both see "empty", both call ensure(), and both write into the same directory. It's unlikely for a single researcher on one GPU. It's guaranteed to bite with a job scheduler. Fix: path.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=False) on the leaf, which is atomic at the filesystem level, instead of check-then-exist_ok=True.
  • An empty existing directory is reused on purpose (any(rd.path.iterdir())), which is reasonable: an empty directory holds nothing to destroy.
def ensure(self) -> Path:
    p = self.path
    p.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    for sub in self._SUBDIRS:
        (p / sub).mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    return p

Seven subdirectories are pre-created. logs/ is created and never written. Grep for "logs" finds only the _SUBDIRS tuple, and the package has no logging at all (there's no import logging; output goes to the terminal through rich.Console). Training progress printed by the engine isn't captured into the run directory, so an empty logs/ folder in every run implies a log that doesn't exist. Either tee stdout/stderr into logs/run.log, or delete the directory.

def write_json(self, name: str, data: Any) -> Path:
    target = self.path / name
    target.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    target.write_text(json.dumps(data, indent=2, default=str))
    return target

def append_jsonl(self, relpath: str, record: dict[str, Any]) -> None:
    target = self.path / relpath
    target.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    with target.open("a") as f:
        f.write(json.dumps(record, default=str) + "\n")

def sub(self, name: str) -> Path:
    p = self.path / name
    p.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    return p
  • write_json isn't atomic. write_text truncates and then writes. A kill between the two leaves an empty or truncated summary.json or training_outcome.json. mtrain validate and mtrain compare then fail with JSONDecodeError and no hint of what happened. The code goes to some length to defend checkpoints against partial writes (the trainer verifies each one, Module 6) but not its own metadata.
  • default=str is a silent type-coercion escape hatch. Anything not JSON-serializable (a Path, a datetime, a NumPy scalar that isn't a float subclass, such as np.float32) becomes a string instead of raising. For Path, that's what you want. For a metric, it's a hazard: an np.float32 MSE would be written as the string "0.0239", and every downstream comparison or plot would then deal in strings. Whether the engine's Evaluator returns Python floats or NumPy scalars is unverified. np.float64 is a float subclass and serializes correctly, so the risk is limited to other dtypes. It's still a correctness question settled by a fallback instead of a type.
  • Not every writer uses RunDir. The runner writes config.resolved.yaml, evaluation/*.json, predictions/test_outcomes.jsonl, failures/feedback_artifact.json and reports/*.md by building paths and calling write_text or open directly (Module 11). So the run directory has no single writer through which atomicity or a manifest could be added later. The abstraction exists but is only partly used.
  • sub() creates on every call, so it's idempotent and cheap. Callers use it as "path to subdirectory, guaranteed to exist."

The artifacts that end up in a run directory

Collected by grepping every write_json/append_jsonl call plus the runner's direct writes:

FileWritten byWhen
environment.json, git.json, config.resolved.yamlrunnerfirst, before data
dataset.jsonrunnerafter intervention
split_integrity.json, dataset_profile.jsonrunnerafter the leakage check and profile
metrics/train.jsonl, metrics/validation.jsonl, metrics/checkpoint_errors.jsonltrainerduring training
resume_source.json, crash.jsontraineron resume, or on a training exception
training_outcome.json, model.jsonrunnerafter training
evaluation/{baselines,test_overall,stratified}.jsonrunnerafter evaluation
predictions/test_outcomes.jsonlrunnerper-frame test outcomes
model_artifact.json, failures/feedback_artifact.jsonrunnerafter a SmolVLA evaluation
metrics/test.jsonl, metrics/rollout.jsonlrunnerend
reports/experiment_report.md, reports/model_card.md, reports/figures/*.pngrunnerend
summary.jsonrunnerlast, which makes it the de facto "completed" marker
manifest.jsonnobodyread by reports/comparison.py and cli.py export, but never written (gap S12)

summary.json being written last is the closest thing to a completion marker, though no code uses it as one. compare and export read whatever exists. Making it explicit (for example, a status field written first as running and atomically replaced with completed or failed) would turn the directory into a reliable state machine.

3.3 Provenance capture: git and environment

def _git_info(repo_path: Path) -> dict[str, Any]:
    if not (repo_path / ".git").exists():
        return {"repo": str(repo_path), "commit": None, "dirty": None, "branch": None,
                 "note": "not a git repository"}
    try:
        def _run(*args: str) -> str:
            return subprocess.check_output(
                ["git", "-C", str(repo_path), *args], text=True, stderr=subprocess.DEVNULL
            ).strip()
        commit = _run("rev-parse", "HEAD")
        dirty = bool(_run("status", "--porcelain"))
        branch = _run("rev-parse", "--abbrev-ref", "HEAD")
        return {"repo": str(repo_path), "commit": commit, "dirty": dirty, "branch": branch}
    except Exception as exc:  # pragma: no cover - environment-dependent
        return {..., "error": str(exc)}

Decision: record git identity of both repositories.

  • Why: Module 1 established that the training step lives in the engine, so the engine's commit is more important to reproducibility than this repo's. Recording both is correct and unusual. Most projects record only their own repo.
  • .exists() is true for a .git file too (worktrees and submodules use one), so those cases work.
  • dirty is recorded, but the diff isn't. A dirty run is flagged as unreproducible-from-commit, but the uncommitted change isn't saved. Fix: write git diff HEAD to git_<repo>.patch when dirty.
  • status --porcelain counts untracked files. That's why the .gitignore entries runs and experiments/*.jsonl (Module 1) matter: without them, every run after the first would mark the repo dirty because of the previous run's outputs. That's a real, non-obvious reason those ignore rules exist.
  • stderr=DEVNULL plus a broad except: git failures (git not installed, "dubious ownership" safe-directory errors in containers) become an "error" string in git.json, and the run continues. That's the right failure mode, since provenance shouldn't block training, but it's silent at the terminal.

Where the paths come from (in runner.py, shown here because it decides what this captures):

RDE_REPO = Path(os.environ.get("RDE_REPO_PATH", str(_default_repo_root() / "episodic-data-engine")))
MT_REPO  = Path(os.environ.get("MODEL_TRAINING_REPO_PATH", str(Path(__file__).resolve().parents[3])))
  • The engine path is a guess from directory layout (parents[4], the parent of this repo, plus /episodic-data-engine), overridable by an environment variable.
  • The commit recorded isn't necessarily the code that ran. The git info is read from wherever RDE_REPO points, not from where the imported rde package actually lives (rde.__file__). If you installed the engine from a different checkout, or non-editable from a wheel, git.json reports the commit of an unrelated directory, or "not a git repository", and the run proceeds. For the repository that owns fit(), provenance can be null or wrong without any warning. Fix: resolve the repo from Path(rde.__file__).resolve() and walk up to the .git.
  • MT_REPO = parents[3] is correct only for an editable src/ install. For a regular wheel install it resolves to somewhere like .../lib/python3.11/, which isn't a git repo. The runner's default runs/ and experiments/ directories are derived from the same MT_REPO (Module 11), so they'd be created inside the Python installation. Because of the sibling-path dependency, the project is effectively editable-only (Module 1), so in practice this is latent.

Doc vs code: the report's git line is always n/a. collect_git_info returns keys "episodic_data_engine" and "vla_finetune", while reports/experiment_report.md's renderer reads env.get('git', {}).get('model_training', ...) (Module 10). The model_training commit therefore always prints n/a in every report, even though git.json has it. A one-word bug.

def collect_environment() -> dict[str, Any]:
    ...
    info = {
        "python_version": sys.version,
        "os": platform.platform(),
        "hostname": socket.gethostname(),
        "cpu_count": os.cpu_count(),
        "ram_total_gb": ram_total_gb,
        "packages": {
            "episodic-data-engine": _package_version("episodic-data-engine"),
            "vla-finetune": _package_version("vla-finetune"),
            "numpy": _package_version("numpy"),
            "pydantic": _package_version("pydantic"),
        },
        "torch": None, "cuda": None, "gpu": None, "lerobot": None, "notes": [],
    }
    try:
        import torch
        info["torch"] = torch.__version__
        info["cuda"] = {"available": torch.cuda.is_available(), "version": torch.version.cuda}
        if torch.cuda.is_available():
            props = torch.cuda.get_device_properties(0)
            info["gpu"] = {
                "name": torch.cuda.get_device_name(0),
                "total_memory_gb": round(props.total_memory / 1e9, 2),
                "driver_capability": f"{props.major}.{props.minor}",
            }
    except ImportError:
        info["notes"].append("torch not installed in this environment (light .venv, no `vla` extra)")
  • Pattern: "observe, don't fabricate." Missing dependencies produce None plus a note, never a guess. That matches the project's stated principle, and it's what the runner keys its limitations list on (environment.get("gpu") is None, Module 11).
  • driver_capability is mislabelled. props.major and props.minor are the GPU's compute capability (8.9 for an RTX 4060), not a driver version. The driver version isn't recorded anywhere. Neither is cuDNN (torch.backends.cudnn.version()). And cuda.version is torch.version.cuda, the compile-time toolkit, not what the driver supports.
  • The package list misses what actually determines model behaviour. The versions that matter for SmolVLA numerics are torch (recorded), lerobot (recorded via __version__), and transformers plus safetensors (SmolVLA's backbone loader; not recorded). numpy and pydantic are recorded instead. vla-finetune is always 0.1.0 and carries no information; the git commit is the real identity. A full pip freeze (or importlib.metadata.distributions()) dump would be cheap and complete.
  • import torch at run start costs a second or two and initializes CUDA. That's harmless here, since training follows immediately.
  • Only GPU 0 is described. That's fine for a single-GPU laptop (README: RTX 4060 Laptop, 8 GB). The record would be wrong under CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES remapping on a multi-GPU box, where device 0 might not be the one used.
  • hostname is recorded. That's fine for local use. Note that mtrain export doesn't copy environment.json into the paper-ready bundle (it copies configs and manifests only; Module 12), so hostnames don't leak into exported artifacts. It's unclear whether that was deliberate, but the effect is right.

3.4 JsonlIndex and ExperimentIndex: the cross-run index

class JsonlIndex:
    def __init__(self, path: Path):
        self.path = Path(path)
        self.path.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)

    def append(self, record: dict[str, Any]) -> None:
        with self.path.open("a") as f:
            f.write(json.dumps(record, default=str) + "\n")

    def read_all(self) -> list[dict[str, Any]]:
        if not self.path.exists():
            return []
        out = []
        with self.path.open() as f:
            for line in f:
                line = line.strip()
                if line:
                    out.append(json.loads(line))
        return out

    def query(self, **filters: Any) -> Iterator[dict[str, Any]]:
        for row in self.read_all():
            if all(row.get(k) == v for k, v in filters.items()):
                yield row

Decision: JSONL files, not a database or a tracking server.

  • What: four files under experiments/, namely experiments.jsonl, metrics.jsonl, datasets.jsonl and models.jsonl, one row per event.
  • Why: the module docstring says "queryable with jq, pandas.read_json(lines=True), or DuckDB's read_json_auto without this project running". That's the "scrapable output" principle. No server, no schema migration, readable decades later, diffable.
  • Alternative 1: MLflow (a tracking server plus artifact store, with a UI). Alternative 2: SQLite, which is transactional and indexed, and is plausibly what the engine's own catalog already uses (unverified). Alternative 3: W&B (hosted, rich UI, network dependency).
  • Cost:
    1. No atomicity or crash tolerance on read. read_all does json.loads on every line with no try. If a process dies mid-append, leaving a half line, every subsequent read_all, query, ModelRegistry.get and ModelRegistry.list raises JSONDecodeError forever, until someone hand-edits the file. One torn write poisons the whole index. Fix: skip and count undecodable lines (and warn), or write each record with a single os.write on an O_APPEND file descriptor.
    2. Concurrent appends from several processes are safe on local disk only while each line goes out in one write() syscall. models.jsonl rows carry the full observation and action schema plus both repos' git info (Module 9), so they can approach or exceed the 8 KiB buffer, at which point lines from two processes can interleave. It's unlikely on one laptop and plausible under a scheduler or NFS.
    3. Every query is a full scan. That's O(rows) per lookup, which is irrelevant at tens or hundreds of runs. It becomes painful only at tens of thousands, and by then DuckDB over the same files is the natural upgrade with no format change. That's a real strength of the format choice.
    4. No schema. record_experiment(**fields) accepts anything. Rows written by different code versions differ silently, and there's no schema_version field.
class ExperimentIndex:
    def __init__(self, root: Path):
        self.root = Path(root)
        self.experiments = JsonlIndex(self.root / "experiments.jsonl")
        self.metrics = JsonlIndex(self.root / "metrics.jsonl")
        self.datasets = JsonlIndex(self.root / "datasets.jsonl")
        self.models = JsonlIndex(self.root / "models.jsonl")

    def record_experiment(self, **fields): self.experiments.append(fields)
    def record_metric(self, **fields):     self.metrics.append(fields)
    def record_dataset(self, **fields):    self.datasets.append(fields)
    def record_model(self, **fields):      self.models.append(fields)
  • Pattern: a Façade over four append-only logs, close to the Repository pattern without the query side. Only ModelRegistry (Module 9) ever reads one of these files back. Nothing in src/ reads experiments.jsonl, metrics.jsonl or datasets.jsonl. mtrain compare reads run directories, not the index (Module 10). So the index is currently write-only from the system's point of view. It's a sound design, but it's a log for external tools rather than a component the system relies on.
  • The index isn't transactional with the run directory. The runner writes record_dataset right after the intervention, before training (Module 11). record_model, record_experiment and record_metric come at the end. So a run that crashes in training leaves a datasets.jsonl row pointing at a training/vN with no matching experiments.jsonl row, and there's no row anywhere saying the run failed. You can't list failed runs from the index. Even orphaned dataset versions are invisible unless you join datasets.jsonl against experiments.jsonl by experiment_id.
  • Doc check: the index docstring lists the four files and their row semantics ("one row per run", "one row per (run, split, metric snapshot)", ...). That matches the call sites, with one nuance: models.jsonl gets one row per SmolVLA run only, because baseline-only runs never build a model artifact.

3.5 Honest gaps to own before an interviewer finds them

  1. No write is atomic (write_json truncates then writes; append_jsonl and JsonlIndex.append are plain buffered appends). Consequence: a kill mid-write corrupts summary.json or a JSONL line, and readers fail with an unexplained JSONDecodeError.
  2. One torn line poisons the whole index. read_all has no per-line error handling. Consequence: ModelRegistry.get/list and every query fail until the file is repaired by hand.
  3. A failed run blocks its own same-day retry, because the directory is created and populated before any validation of data or environment, and there's no --force or crash marker. Consequence: the user has to rename the experiment or delete the directory, which defeats the no-overwrite guarantee's purpose.
  4. make_run_dir has a TOCTOU race (check non-empty, then mkdir(exist_ok=True)). Consequence: two concurrent identical launches share one directory.
  5. Engine provenance can be silently wrong or null. The git info is read from a layout-guessed path, not from the imported rde package's location. Consequence: for the code that owns fit(), git.json may record nothing, or an unrelated checkout.
  6. Dirty trees are flagged but the diff isn't saved. Consequence: a dirty run is unreproducible by construction.
  7. environment.json mislabels compute capability as driver_capability, omits the driver, cuDNN and transformers versions, and records a meaningless vla-finetune 0.1.0. Consequence: the record misses exactly the versions most likely to explain a numerics difference.
  8. The report's git line always prints n/a (key vla_finetune written, model_training read). Consequence: the human-readable report hides provenance that git.json holds.
  9. logs/ is created in every run and never written, and there's no logging at all. Consequence: engine training output isn't captured, and the empty folder implies otherwise.
  10. The index is write-only inside the system, not transactional with the run, and has no status field. Consequence: failed runs and orphaned dataset versions are undiscoverable from the index.
  11. default=str silently stringifies non-JSON types. Consequence: a non-float64 NumPy metric would be stored as a string. Unverified whether the engine produces one.
  12. Writers bypass RunDir for about half the artifacts. Consequence: there's no single choke point to add atomicity, a manifest or checksums later.
  13. Default runs/ and experiments/ locations derive from parents[3] of the installed file. Consequence: with a non-editable install they'd land inside the Python installation (latent, because the project is editable-only).

The shape to carry into Module 4

 runs/                                         experiments/            (both git-ignored)
 └── <UTC date>/                               ├── experiments.jsonl   one row per COMPLETED run
     └── <experiment_name>/                    ├── metrics.jsonl       one row per test eval
         └── seed_<n>/   ← make_run_dir:       ├── datasets.jsonl      one row per intervention
             │            raise if non-empty   └── models.jsonl        one row per SmolVLA artifact
             ├── environment.json  (torch/cuda/gpu or None+note)       append-only, full-scan,
             ├── git.json          (engine + this repo; dirty flag)    no locking, no schema,
             ├── config.resolved.yaml                                  write-only inside system
             ├── checkpoints/ predictions/ evaluation/
             ├── failures/ reports/ metrics/
             ├── logs/            ← always empty
             └── summary.json     ← written LAST (implicit "completed")
   writes: write_text / open("a")  — none atomic

Drill — answer from memory, files closed

  1. What happens if mtrain train fails during the intervention step and you immediately rerun the same command?
  2. Why didn't you just use MLflow or Weights & Biases?
  3. What breaks in production if the machine loses power while the runner is appending to experiments/models.jsonl?
  4. Why does .gitignore excluding runs matter for the correctness of git.json, beyond keeping the repo small?
  5. Interviewer framing: "How would I reproduce one of your runs six months from now? What would stop me?"

Appendix A — Model answers to the drill

A1. Fail-then-rerun. "The runner creates the run directory and writes environment.json, git.json and the resolved config before it touches the data. So if the intervention fails, say a wrong dataset version, the directory is already non-empty, and the rerun raises FileExistsError from make_run_dir, which never reuses a non-empty run directory. The guard is deliberate, because overwriting a real run would destroy checkpoints. But here it protects nothing, since the failed run has no results. I'd fix that by validating the dataset and environment before creating the directory, writing a status marker that a failed run can be identified by, and allowing a retry over a directory whose status is failed. Today you rename the experiment or delete the directory, and the error message tells you that."

Why that answer lands: it traces the exact order of writes, defends the guard's intent, and then shows that the guard misfires in one specific case, with a fix that keeps the guarantee.

A2. Why not MLflow or W&B? "The requirements were reproducibility and inspectability, not a dashboard. Run directories plus append-only JSONL mean every artifact is a plain file you can query with jq, pandas or DuckDB without this code, a server, a login or network access, and the format will still be readable in ten years. There's also no second source of truth: the run directory is the record. The costs are that I own the durability problems myself (none of my writes are atomic yet), there's no UI, and every query is a full scan. I'd switch, or add MLflow as a sink alongside the files, once more than one person was running experiments or once runs numbered in the thousands. At that point locking and a UI earn their keep, and the JSONL files map directly onto MLflow params and metrics."

Why that answer lands: it argues from requirements, admits the costs you inherited by rolling your own, and names a migration path that keeps the existing format, not a rewrite.

A3. Power loss during an index append. "The append is a buffered text write with no fsync, so after a power loss the last line can be truncated. The reader parses every line with json.loads and no error handling, so from then on every read of that file raises, including the model registry's get and list. One torn write takes out the whole registry until someone hand-edits the file. The fix has two halves: make the reader skip and report undecodable lines, and make the writer emit each record as a single os.write on an O_APPEND descriptor, with fsync if durability matters. The same class of bug exists for the run directory's JSON files, which are truncated and rewritten in place, so I'd move those to write-to-temp plus os.replace."

Why that answer lands: it gets the failure exactly right (a single bad line breaks all reads, not just the lost record), gives both halves of the fix, and generalizes to the sibling bug unprompted.

A4. Why ignoring runs matters for git.json. "git.json records a dirty flag from git status --porcelain, which counts untracked files. If run outputs lived unignored inside the repo, every run after the first would see the previous run's files as untracked and mark the tree dirty, so the flag would be permanently true and carry no information. Ignoring runs and the index files keeps 'dirty' meaning 'the code changed', which is the only meaning that's useful for reproducibility. The remaining gap is that when the tree really is dirty, I record the flag but not the diff, so I'd save git diff HEAD as a patch next to it."

Why that answer lands: it connects two files that look unrelated, showing systems-level reading, and ends with the next gap.

A5. "How would I reproduce one of your runs in six months?" "Start from the run directory. config.resolved.yaml has every parameter including defaults, dataset.json names the exact training version in the engine's catalog, and the engine's lineage resolves that back to raw episodes. git.json has the commit of both repositories, and environment.json has the Python, torch, lerobot and GPU details. Then check out both commits, install, and rerun the config against the same input version. What would stop you, honestly: there's no lockfile, so transitive versions like transformers may differ and aren't recorded. If either tree was dirty, the diff wasn't saved. The engine's commit is read from a path guessed from directory layout, not from the package that was actually imported, so it could be wrong. And the checkpoints and run directories aren't versioned, so you need my disk, not just the repo. Even with all of that fixed, SmolVLA's flow-matching sampling is stochastic, so you'd reproduce the metrics within noise, a few percent, not bit-for-bit."

Why that answer lands: it gives the full chain first, then lists the breaks precisely, and closes with the irreducible stochasticity. That's the sign of someone who has actually tried to reproduce ML results.

Module 3 Summary — the one page to revise

What this module is. One directory per run (runs/<UTC date>/<name>/seed_<n>/) that's never overwritten, populated with environment and git snapshots and every artifact, plus four append-only JSONL index files that external tools can query without this code. The format is good; durability, transactionality and provenance accuracy are the weak points.

Five claims to make cold

  1. A non-empty run directory is never reused: FileExistsError, tested.
  2. Both repos' git commits and dirty flags are recorded, because the engine owns fit().
  3. Missing dependencies become None plus a note, never a guess, and the runner turns these into report limitations.
  4. The index is four JSONL files, append-only, readable by jq, pandas or DuckDB with no server.
  5. No write is atomic, and one torn JSONL line breaks every subsequent read of that file.

Decisions

ChoseOverBecauseCost
Plain run directories plus JSONLMLflow/W&Binspectable, serverless, durable formatdurability, locking, UI are on me
Refuse non-empty directoryauto-suffix/UUIDnever destroy a runfailed runs block same-day retry; TOCTOU race
date/name/seed layoutname/date/seedscan by daydated paths leak into configs and scripts
Record git of both reposown repo onlyengine owns trainingengine path guessed, can be null or wrong
default=str in JSON dumpsstrict serializationnever crash on Path/datetimesilently stringifies odd types
Pre-create 7 subdirectoriescreate on demandpredictable layoutlogs/ always empty

Volunteer unprompted: the torn-write poisoning; the retry-blocking guard; the driver_capability mislabel; the report's n/a git line; no diff saved for dirty trees; the index is write-only and has no failed-run status; manifest.json is read but never written.

Numbers and names: 7 subdirectories (checkpoints predictions evaluation failures reports logs metrics); 4 index files; env vars RDE_REPO_PATH and MODEL_TRAINING_REPO_PATH; engine path parents[4]/episodic-data-engine; repo root parents[3]; summary.json written last; Python's default text buffer is 8 KiB.

Sentences worth memorizing

  • "Every artifact is a plain file you can query without my code; that's the design, and durability is the price I still owe."
  • "I record the engine's commit as well as mine, because the engine owns the training step."
  • "One torn line in a JSONL index breaks every read after it, so the reader has to be tolerant and the writer atomic."
  • "Ignoring runs/ isn't just tidiness; it keeps the dirty flag meaningful."

Module 4 — Data interventions as lineage

Files covered: src/model_training/data/interventions.py (the data/__init__.py façade was covered in Module 1)

Where we are: Module 2 validated a DatasetIntervention, and Module 3 gave the run a directory. This module is the first one that changes the world: it turns "halve the data" or "drop the short episodes" into new, immutable dataset versions inside the engine's catalog. This is the project's central idea, the one the README's organizing principle rests on ("Even a data intervention ... is executed as a lineage-tracked transform inside the engine, never as a local copy").

The one-line thesis: Every intervention becomes a real clean/vN (an episode filter) plus a real training/vN (a split assignment plus sampling weights), so the provenance is impeccable. But because the filter runs before the split, every excluding intervention is scored on a different, smaller test set than the baseline it's meant to be compared against. The lineage design quietly undermines the comparisons the experiments exist to make.

4.1 Prerequisites — the absolute basics

  • Data-centric ML / data intervention. Holding the model and training recipe fixed and changing the data, to measure what the data contributes. Here the four primary interventions are full, reduced (fewer episodes, chosen at random), imbalanced (deliberately skewed along one attribute) and rebalanced (oversampling under-represented episodes).
  • Quantity vs coverage. Two different hypotheses about why more data helps. Quantity: more examples of the same thing reduce variance. Coverage: examples of different situations extend what the model can handle. A random 50% subset changes quantity while preserving coverage in expectation. Removing every "short" episode changes coverage. The experiment set is designed to tell the two apart (the configs' hypothesis: quantity_vs_coverage tag).
  • Oversampling / importance weighting. Instead of duplicating data, give some examples a higher probability of being drawn into a training batch. A weight of 3.0 means "drawn about three times as often as a weight-1.0 episode", in expectation, under a weighted sampler. Nothing is copied on disk.
  • Stratum / slice. A subset of the evaluation data defined by an attribute (task, length bucket). Covered in depth in Module 7.
  • Terciles / percentiles. The 33.3rd and 66.7th percentiles split a sorted list into three roughly equal groups. np.percentile uses linear interpolation between data points by default.
  • Banker's rounding. Python's round() rounds halves to the nearest even integer: round(2.5) == 2, round(3.5) == 4. This matters whenever fraction × count lands on .5.
  • numpy.random.default_rng(seed). A modern NumPy random generator (PCG64). With the same seed and the same input array it produces the same draws on the same NumPy version. rng.choice(ids, size=n, replace=False) draws n distinct elements.
  • Nested subsets. In a data-efficiency curve, the 25% subset being contained in the 50% subset (and that in the 75%) reduces variance between curve points, because each point adds data to the last instead of swapping it out. The standard way to get them is to take prefixes of one random permutation.
  • Orphaned version. A dataset version created in the catalog by a run that then failed, so no experiment refers to it.

4.2 The version pipeline this module drives

 input version (config.dataset.version, e.g. clean/v1)
      │
      ├─ kind = full / rebalanced, or a subset equal to every episode
      │        → no transform; clean_version := input_version
      │
      └─ kind = fraction / reduced / imbalanced / custom with a real subset
               → next_version(CLEAN) = clean/vK
                 TransformPipeline([EpisodeSubsetFilter(subset_ids, reason)]).run(input → clean/vK)
      ▼
 next_version(TRAINING) = training/vM
 DatasetBuilder.build(clean_version → training/vM,
                      splits=train/val/test fractions, seed=42, split_mode=quantile,
                      episode_weights = {...} or None)

Decision: an intervention is two engine operations, never a local copy.

  • What: episode selection is an engine transform (EpisodeSubsetFilter), which records dropped_episode_ids in lineage. Split assignment and weights are an engine build.
  • Why: the README's lineage output shows exactly what this buys: transform ['raw/v1'] -> clean/v1 episodes 50->48 dropped=[9, 31]. Any training/vN can be traced back to raw episodes, with the reason for each drop. test_reduced_intervention_selects_half_and_records_lineage checks that the transform run's output_episode_ids and dropped_episode_ids equal what this module computed. That test verifies the core claim end to end, and it's the strongest test in the repo.
  • Alternative: filter episode IDs at training time (pass episode_ids= to fit) with no new version, or copy a subset to a new directory.
  • Cost: (1) every run writes to the shared catalog, even a full run, which still allocates a new training/vN; (2) the order "filter, then split" has a consequence big enough to get its own section (4.5).

4.3 compute_length_bucket

def compute_length_bucket(episode_rows: list[dict[str, Any]]) -> dict[int, str]:
    """short/medium/long by within-dataset episode-length terciles.
    ... Same bucketing method as ``model_training.data.profile``'s coverage section, so
    the two stay comparable.
    """
    if len(episode_rows) < 3:
        return {r["episode_id"]: "medium" for r in episode_rows}
    lengths = [r["num_frames"] for r in episode_rows]
    t1, t2 = np.percentile(lengths, [33.3, 66.7])
    return {
        r["episode_id"]: ("short" if r["num_frames"] <= t1 else "long" if r["num_frames"] > t2 else "medium")
        for r in episode_rows
    }

Why length is a variable at all. docs/EXPERIMENTS.md §3 says the primary dataset so100_pickplace has one task instruction across all 48 episodes, so task_id has nothing to select between. Trajectory length "326 to 475 frames per episode" is the only real per-episode attribute that varies. The schema has no "variation" field (profile.py's note says so too). This is an honest choice made from what the data actually contains, and it's the right story to tell: "I couldn't find a meaningful variation axis in the schema, so I used the one that exists, and I said so."

Line by line:

  • < 3 → all "medium". Terciles of one or two values are meaningless, so every episode gets the neutral bucket. Without this guard np.percentile would still return numbers and produce a meaningless split. Fine.
  • 33.3 and 66.7, not 100/3 and 200/3. These are rounded magic numbers. They're harmless, but they're approximations of a tercile, not a definition.
  • <= t1 → short, > t2 → long. The asymmetric boundaries put ties at the low edge into "short". With heavily tied lengths the buckets collapse. The test-file comment admits the synthetic fixture's equal-length episodes all bucket to "short". A real case: the README says all four ALOHA test episodes are 1,100 frames. If ALOHA's whole dataset is tied that way (I can't verify the full distribution), then t1 = t2 = 1100, every episode is "short", and:
    • imbalanced, variable_value: short drops everything, and the run fails loudly with "resolved to zero episodes". Good.
    • imbalanced, variable_value: long (or medium) drops nothing, the subset equals the full set, and a full-data run proceeds labelled "imbalanced" (the same silent-no-op class as Module 2 gap 4).
  • The docstring's comparability claim is false. It says the method is the "Same bucketing method as model_training.data.profile's coverage section". Grep for percentile or tercile in profile.py finds nothing. The profile reports the engine's duration histogram, not terciles. The only other tercile code is in evaluation/offline.py:171, and that one computes terciles within the evaluated split, not the whole dataset (docs/EXPERIMENTS.md Table 2 confirms: "within-split episode-length terciles"). So the "short" you dropped at intervention time (whole-dataset cut points) and the length_short slice you score at test time (test-split cut points, from 5 episodes) are different sets with different thresholds. Any claim that "dropping short episodes hurt the short slice" is comparing two different definitions of short.

4.4 resolve_episode_subset

kind = intervention.kind
if kind == "full":
    return sorted(all_episode_ids), "full dataset, no episode selection"
if intervention.episode_ids is not None:
    return sorted(intervention.episode_ids), intervention.reason or f"explicit episode_ids ({kind})"
  • full short-circuits before episode_ids. kind: full, episode_ids: [1, 2] returns every episode. That matches the docstring "explicit episode_ids always wins" only for non-full kinds. That's a silent override of user input (Module 2 gap 12).
  • Explicit IDs aren't validated against the dataset. episode_ids: [999] returns [999]. The caller computes excluded = set(all_ids) - {999} (everything) and asks the engine's filter for an episode that doesn't exist. What EpisodeSubsetFilter does then is unverified. It might raise, or it might produce an empty version that fails later in the build. Duplicates ([3, 3, 5]) survive sorted and make subset_ids == sorted(all_ids) false even when every episode is listed, so they'd create an unnecessary clean/vK.
  • reason is always recorded. It's either user-supplied or generated. It travels into the engine's transform (EpisodeSubsetFilter(subset_ids, reason=reason)), and so into lineage. That's the auditable "why" for every dropped episode, and it's a genuinely good detail.

Fraction and reduced

if kind in ("fraction", "reduced"):
    frac = intervention.fraction if intervention.fraction is not None else 0.5
    rng = np.random.default_rng(intervention.seed)
    ids = sorted(all_episode_ids)
    # frac=0.0 is a legitimate, explicit request (a zero-data anchor
    # point on a data-efficiency sweep) and must be honored as 0
    # episodes, not silently floored to 1 ...
    n = round(frac * len(ids))
    if frac > 0:
        n = max(1, n)
    chosen = sorted(int(i) for i in rng.choice(ids, size=n, replace=False))
    return chosen, intervention.reason or f"seeded random {frac:.0%} draw over all episodes (seed={intervention.seed})"
  • sorted(all_episode_ids) before drawing makes the draw independent of the order the catalog returns rows in. Without it, the same seed could select different episodes if the catalog's row order changed. This is the non-obvious line that makes test_reduced_intervention_is_deterministic_given_seed meaningful.
  • int(i) converts NumPy integers to Python int, so they serialize cleanly into JSON and catalog records (Module 3's default=str would otherwise be the only safety net).
  • The 0.5 fallback is unreachable for validated configs (Module 2 gap 13).
  • Banker's rounding: round(0.5 × 5) = 2, round(0.5 × 7) = 4. For the real dataset (48 episodes) every multiple of 1/48 is exact, so this only matters in sweeps over small sets. It's still a "why is my 50% run using 2 of 5?" question you should be able to answer.
  • The floor at 1 (max(1, n) for frac > 0) prevents "1% of 3 episodes" from silently meaning "no data". That's sensible.

Doc and test vs code: the zero-fraction anchor can't run. The comment insists frac=0.0 "must be honored as 0 episodes", and test_reduced_intervention_at_zero_fraction_is_honored asserts that resolve_episode_subset returns []. But the only caller, build_intervention_dataset, does this next:

subset_ids, reason = resolve_episode_subset(intervention, all_ids, task_of, length_bucket_of)
if not subset_ids:
    raise ValueError(f"intervention kind={intervention.kind!r} resolved to zero episodes")

So a data-fraction sweep including 0.0 (the "zero-data anchor" the comment names as the use case) raises before training. The test passes because it tests the helper in isolation, never the path a sweep actually takes. That's a textbook unit-tests-pass, system-fails gap. The honest resolution is to decide what a zero-data point means. Most likely it's "evaluate the pretrained smolvla_base with no fine-tuning", which doesn't need a dataset version at all, and should be implemented as its own path, not as an empty filter.

Draws at different fractions aren't nested. rng.choice(ids, size=n, replace=False) with the same seed at n = 12 and n = 24 isn't guaranteed to return a 12-subset of the 24-subset. NumPy's Generator.choice without replacement doesn't promise prefix consistency across sizes, and I haven't checked which algorithm it selects for these sizes. Fix: perm = rng.permutation(ids); chosen = sorted(perm[:n]) gives nested subsets by construction, which is the standard design for data-efficiency curves.

Imbalanced

if kind == "imbalanced":
    var, val = intervention.variable, intervention.variable_value
    if var == "task_id":
        keep = sorted(e for e in all_episode_ids if task_of_episode.get(e) != val)
        return keep, intervention.reason or f"drop every episode with task_id={val!r}"
    if var == "episode_length_bucket" and length_bucket_of_episode is not None:
        keep = sorted(e for e in all_episode_ids if length_bucket_of_episode.get(e) != val)
        return keep, intervention.reason or f"drop every episode in length bucket {val!r}"
    raise ValueError(f"... no automatic rule for variable={var!r}; supported: 'task_id', ...")
  • "Imbalanced" here means removed entirely, not under-represented. Every episode with the chosen value is dropped. The imbalanced.yaml hypothesis template says "Skewing the demonstration set to under-represent <variable>=<value>". Zero coverage isn't under-representation. It turns the experiment into an out-of-distribution test ("can it handle a regime it never saw?"), which is a different and arguably more interesting question, but not the one the config states. Under-representation would keep, say, 20% of those episodes. Name the semantics correctly in an interview.
  • The unsupported-variable path raises, and it's tested (test_resolve_episode_subset_unknown_variable_raises, plus the end-to-end test_unknown_dataset_variation_raises in test_failure_modes.py). The design rule "never guess" is enforced here.
  • The None/mistyped-value path doesn't raise (Module 2 gap 4): != None keeps everything.
  • The module docstring is stale. It says imbalance today uses "task_id, the one grouping key this dataset's schema actually carries". The code also supports episode_length_bucket, and the function's own docstring two definitions down explains that so100_pickplace is single-task, so task_id imbalance has nothing to select there. The two docstrings disagree, and the code and EXPERIMENTS.md side with the length bucket.

4.5 build_intervention_dataset, and the test set that moves

manifest = catalog.get_manifest(dataset_id, input_version)
if manifest is None:
    raise LookupError(f"unknown version: {dataset_id}@{input_version}")
episode_rows = catalog.get_episodes(dataset_id, input_version)
all_ids = [e["episode_id"] for e in episode_rows]
task_of = {e["episode_id"]: e["task_id"] for e in episode_rows}
length_bucket_of = compute_length_bucket(episode_rows)
if not all_ids:
    raise ValueError(f"{dataset_id}@{input_version} has no episodes to build from")
  • The LookupError for an unknown version is tested (test_intervention_on_unknown_input_version_raises). It's the right exception type, matching the engine's get_dataset behaviour asserted in test_invalid_dataset_version_raises.
  • Every episode in the input version is a candidate. There's no filter on QC status here. The conftest comment says DatasetBuilder.build() "admits only PASSED/WARNING by default", so failed-QC episodes are presumably dropped at build time, after this module has computed episode_ids and excluded_episode_ids. If that's right (unverified), then dataset.json's episode_ids can list episodes that never made it into the training version. The engine's build_summary would show the true count, but this module's own fields wouldn't. For so100_pickplace, whose clean/v1 already dropped QC failures (episodes 9 and 31), it probably doesn't bite.

Rebalanced: weights, never exclusion

if intervention.kind == "rebalanced":
    weights = dict(intervention.episode_weights or {})
    if not weights and intervention.variable is not None and intervention.variable_value is not None:
        target = _target_episodes(intervention.variable, intervention.variable_value)
        if not target:
            raise ValueError(f"no episodes with {intervention.variable}={intervention.variable_value!r} to upweight")
        weights = {e: intervention.oversample_factor for e in target}
    if not weights:
        raise ValueError("rebalanced intervention resolved to empty episode_weights -- "
                         "nothing would change relative to a full-data run")
    subset_ids = sorted(all_ids)
    excluded: list[int] = []
    clean_version = input_version

Decision: rebalance by sampling weight, not by duplication or synthesis.

  • Why: docs/EXPERIMENTS.md §4 states the constraint plainly: the project collects no new data, so a rebalanced run "can only redistribute training attention over data that already exists". Weights are honest about that; duplicating episodes on disk would just be a heavier, less traceable way to get the same effect.
  • Alternative: physically duplicate episodes in a new clean/vK, which would be visible in lineage as copies, or use loss re-weighting instead of sampling re-weighting.
  • Cost: the effect depends on the engine's weighted sampler in fit, which isn't verifiable here. Sampling with replacement at weight 3.0 also means those episodes' frames repeat within an epoch, which raises overfitting risk on exactly the regime you're trying to help.

Double guard against a no-op, the counterpart of the gap imbalanced has: both "variable set but no matching episodes" and "nothing resolved" raise. Note that _target_episodes returns [] for an unsupported variable too, so the error says "no episodes with camera=dim to upweight" when the truth is "camera isn't a supported variable". It's a misleading message, though it's at least loud.

Explicit weights aren't validated. Keys naming nonexistent episodes have no effect, silently. Weights ≤ 0 pass (Module 2 gap 15). Weights are also attached to every targeted episode in the pool, including those that end up in val and test. The runner only reads weights for train IDs (Module 11), so this is harmless, but it does mean dataset.json's episode_weights overstates which episodes were actually upweighted during training.

docs/EXPERIMENTS.md §4 overclaims: "Every experiment report generated by the system states this limitation explicitly in its 'Limitations' section." Grep for rebalanc in experiments/ and reports/ finds nothing. The runner's limitations list covers only GPU, torch and non-SmolVLA model notes (Module 11). A rebalanced report would not contain the caveat. The caveat exists only in the YAML comment and in the docs.

The excluding path

else:
    subset_ids, reason = resolve_episode_subset(intervention, all_ids, task_of, length_bucket_of)
    if not subset_ids:
        raise ValueError(f"intervention kind={intervention.kind!r} resolved to zero episodes")
    excluded = sorted(set(all_ids) - set(subset_ids))
    weights = intervention.episode_weights or {}
    if subset_ids == sorted(all_ids):
        clean_version = input_version
    else:
        clean_version = next_version(catalog, dataset_id, VersionStage.CLEAN)
        TransformPipeline([EpisodeSubsetFilter(subset_ids, reason=reason)],
                          settings=settings, catalog=catalog).run(dataset_id, input_version, clean_version)

training_version = next_version(catalog, dataset_id, VersionStage.TRAINING)
build = DatasetBuilder(settings=settings, catalog=catalog).build(
    dataset_id, clean_version, training_version,
    splits=splits.as_fractions(), seed=splits.seed, split_mode=splits.mode,
    episode_weights=weights or None,
)
  • subset_ids == sorted(all_ids) → reuse the input version. No pointless transform for a filter that drops nothing. It's sensible, and it's exactly why the silent-no-op imbalanced case (4.4) passes through invisibly: no new clean version, and excluded_episode_ids == []. The only trace is kind: imbalanced alongside zero exclusions in dataset.json.
  • weights or None turns an empty dict into None, presumably so the engine skips weighting entirely rather than recording empty weights. That's inferred from the call site.
  • next_version then run isn't atomic. Two concurrent runs can both be allocated training/v7. Whether the engine's catalog rejects the second write is unverified. If it doesn't, two experiments claim the same version.
  • Failures leave orphans. If DatasetBuilder.build fails after the transform has succeeded, clean/vK exists with no experiment pointing at it. And because the runner calls this function after make_run_dir (Module 3), the run directory exists too, blocking a retry. There's no cleanup, and none is possible, since versions are immutable by design. The right fix is to make orphans discoverable (a run status that links to its versions), not deletable.
  • Version proliferation. Every run allocates a fresh training/vN, even full runs with identical config and seed, which produce byte-for-byte equivalent splits if the builder is deterministic. The README's training/v6 shows this in practice. Content-addressing a build (hash of input version, subset, splits, seed and weights) would let identical requests reuse one version.

The finding that matters most: the test set depends on the intervention

DatasetBuilder.build assigns splits over clean_version, which for any excluding intervention is the filtered subset. Splits are therefore assigned after filtering:

RunEpisode pool splitTest episodes (0.8/0.1/0.1, quantile)
baseline_full485 (README: 38/5/5)
reduced_data (fraction 0.5)24about 2–3, and not a subset of the baseline's 5
imbalanced (drop "short", about 16 of 48)about 32about 3, with no short episodes at all
rebalanced (weights only)485; plausibly identical to the baseline's if the builder is deterministic in pool and seed (unverified)

Consequences:

  1. reduced vs baseline_full compares MSE on different test episodes, of different sizes. With 2–3 test episodes, the per-episode spread (README: SO-100 per-episode MAE 0.085–0.111 across five episodes) is of the same order as the effect you'd be trying to detect.
  2. The imbalanced run can't measure the regime it removed. Its test split contains no short episodes, so there's no length_short slice for it to fail on. The experiment designed to "give the rebalanced experiment a real weak regime to target" (its objective:) can't observe that weak regime.
  3. The feedback artifact's recommended action, "retrain, then compare against this run on the same fixed test slice" (Module 8), is impossible for any excluding intervention.

What the docs say. docs/EXPERIMENTS.md §6 does acknowledge this, and that's to its credit: "Comparing two different dataset interventions is, necessarily, a comparison across two different training/vN versions' test splits, since an intervention can change which episodes are assigned to test". Two corrections to that sentence:

  • It isn't necessary. It follows from filtering before splitting. Split the full pool once (a frozen training/v_base), then apply interventions only to its train split (filter or weight train episodes, inherit val and test unchanged). Every intervention is then scored on the identical test set by construction. The engine's lineage model supports this shape: a transform whose input is the split training version.
  • It says "each experiment's dataset.json records exactly which ... episode identifiers were evaluated". It doesn't. dataset.json's episode_ids is the whole selected pool (train + val + test). split_integrity.json stores split sizes and overlaps, not membership. The evaluated test episodes can only be recovered from predictions/test_outcomes.jsonl (one episode_id per frame, Module 11), or by reopening the training version.

This is the gap to raise yourself in an interview. Finding it in your own experimental design, and having the "split once, intervene on train only" fix ready, is worth more than any of the features.

4.6 Honest gaps to own before an interviewer finds them

  1. Filter-then-split moves the test set. Every excluding intervention is evaluated on a different, smaller test split than baseline_full, and the imbalanced run's test split doesn't contain the removed regime. Consequence: the core quantity-vs-coverage comparisons aren't like-for-like, and the imbalanced → rebalanced story can't be measured as designed.
  2. The zero-fraction anchor fails (the resolver returns [], the builder raises), despite the comment and a passing unit test. Consequence: a sweep including 0.0 crashes, and the test gives false confidence.
  3. Imbalanced means total removal, not under-representation, contrary to the config template's hypothesis. Consequence: the experiment measures out-of-distribution generalization, so it must be described that way.
  4. Length buckets differ between intervention (whole-dataset terciles) and evaluation (within-split terciles), and the "same method as profile.py" docstring is false (profile has no tercile code). Consequence: "short" means different things at train time and test time.
  5. Tied lengths collapse the buckets (<= t1 puts ties in "short"). Consequence: on a dataset with uniform episode lengths, imbalancing "long" or "medium" silently runs full data.
  6. Fraction draws aren't nested across fractions. Consequence: data-efficiency curves carry extra between-point variance from swapping episodes. Prefixes of a single permutation fix it.
  7. Explicit episode_ids and episode_weights aren't validated against the dataset (unknown IDs, duplicates, non-positive weights). Consequence: engine-side behaviour is undefined here; duplicates create needless versions.
  8. The rebalanced "limitation in every report" claim is false (EXPERIMENTS.md §4). Consequence: the report for a rebalanced run omits the key caveat about what the result can't show.
  9. dataset.json doesn't record test membership, contrary to EXPERIMENTS.md §6. Consequence: the evaluated episodes are recoverable only from the predictions file.
  10. Version allocation isn't atomic, failed runs orphan versions, and identical requests always mint new versions. Consequence: catalog clutter, plus a possible version collision under concurrency (unverified engine behaviour).
  11. The module docstring is stale (it says task_id is "the one grouping key"). Consequence: the docs disagree with themselves; the code and EXPERIMENTS.md use the length bucket.
  12. A misleading error for unsupported rebalance variables ("no episodes with X=Y"). Consequence: it slows down diagnosis.
  13. Weights are recorded for val and test episodes too. Consequence: dataset.json overstates which episodes were upweighted in training.

The shape to carry into Module 5

 DatasetIntervention ──▶ build_intervention_dataset(dataset_id, input_version, splits)
                          │ catalog.get_manifest  (LookupError if unknown)
                          │ catalog.get_episodes  → all_ids, task_of, length_bucket_of (whole-dataset terciles)
                          │
        ┌─────────────────┴─────────────────────────┐
        │ rebalanced                                 │ full / fraction / reduced / imbalanced / custom
        │  weights = explicit | {target: factor}     │  subset = resolve_episode_subset(...)
        │  raise if empty                            │  raise if empty   (← kills frac=0.0)
        │  clean = input                             │  subset == all ? clean = input
        │                                            │                : clean/vK = EpisodeSubsetFilter
        └─────────────────┬─────────────────────────┘
                          ▼
            training/vM = DatasetBuilder.build(clean, splits, seed=42, quantile, weights)
                          │  splits assigned over the FILTERED pool  ← test set moves per intervention
                          ▼
            InterventionResult(clean_version, training_version, episode_ids (pool, not test),
                               excluded_episode_ids, episode_weights, selection_reason, build_summary)

Drill — answer from memory, files closed

  1. Trace what happens to the catalog when you run reduced.yaml against so100_pickplace@clean/v1. Which versions are created, and what does the lineage record?
  2. Why didn't you just pass a list of episode IDs to fit() instead of creating new dataset versions?
  3. What breaks in production: someone runs run_data_fraction_sweep with [0.0, 0.25, 0.5, 1.0].
  4. Is reduced_data's test MSE comparable to baseline_full's? Explain precisely, and say what you'd change.
  5. Interviewer framing: "Tell me about a flaw in your own experimental design."

Appendix A — Model answers to the drill

A1. Running reduced.yaml. "The intervention is reduced at fraction 0.5 with seed 0. The module reads the 48 episodes of clean/v1 from the catalog, sorts the IDs so the draw doesn't depend on row order, and uses a seeded NumPy generator to draw 24 without replacement. Because that's a strict subset, it allocates the next clean version, say clean/v2, and runs the engine's EpisodeSubsetFilter transform from clean/v1 to clean/v2. The lineage for that transform records the 24 kept and 24 dropped IDs and the reason string, 'seeded random 50% draw over all episodes (seed=0)'. Then it allocates the next training version and calls the engine's builder, which assigns train/val/test over those 24 episodes with split seed 42 in quantile mode. So the catalog gains two versions, and rde version lineage on the training version walks back through the filter to raw. The unit test checks that the transform's recorded kept and dropped IDs equal exactly what my code computed."

Why that answer lands: it's concrete down to the version names and the recorded reason, and it ends with how the claim is verified, not just asserted.

A2. Why not pass episode IDs to fit()? "Because then the experiment's data would exist only as an argument in one process. The question 'what exactly did this model train on' would depend on my run directory surviving and being read correctly. As engine versions, every intervention is an immutable, named dataset with lineage back to raw episodes and a recorded reason for every exclusion, so the data has an identity independent of my code. The cost is real. Every run writes to the shared catalog, identical requests mint duplicate versions, failures leave orphaned versions, and, the big one, because I filter before splitting, the split is recomputed over each filtered pool. If I were redoing it, I'd keep versions but change the order: split the full pool once, then express interventions as transforms on the train split only."

Why that answer lands: it defends the decision on provenance grounds, then volunteers that the same decision causes the design flaw, and gives the fix without abandoning the principle.

A3. A sweep with 0.0. "The 0.0 point fails before training. The resolver honours zero, returning an empty list, and there's a unit test that checks exactly that. But the only caller, build_intervention_dataset, raises 'resolved to zero episodes' on an empty subset, so the first sweep point throws and the sweep stops.
