The unit test passes because it tests the helper in isolation, not the path the sweep uses. Even if it got through, a zero-episode version can't be split or trained on. What a zero-data anchor should really mean is 'evaluate the pretrained base checkpoint with no fine-tuning', and that needs its own code path, not an empty filter. Separately, the other fractions' subsets aren't nested, so I'd draw them as prefixes of a single permutation."

Why that answer lands: it identifies the contradiction between the helper and the caller, explains why the test missed it, and redefines the experiment correctly. That shows judgement, not just bug-spotting.

A4. Is reduced comparable to baseline_full? "Not on a like-for-like basis. Splits are assigned after the filter, so the baseline is scored on 5 test episodes out of 48, and the reduced run on about 2 or 3 out of 24, which aren't even guaranteed to be a subset of the baseline's 5. With per-episode error varying by roughly 25% across episodes, a difference on 2–3 episodes is mostly noise about which episodes landed in test. The docs acknowledge the test sets differ, but call it necessary, and it isn't. I'd split the full 48 once and freeze that, then apply every intervention only to the train split, so every run is scored on the same 5 test episodes. And because those 5 are few, I'd report per-episode results and run several training seeds before claiming a quantity effect."

Why that answer lands: a precise "no", with the mechanism, the magnitude of the problem and the fix. Correcting your own docs' "necessarily" is exactly the kind of rigour the question is fishing for.

A5. "Tell me about a flaw in your own experimental design." "The data interventions are implemented as engine transforms that filter episodes, and the training-set builder then assigns splits over whatever's left. That gives perfect lineage, but it means each intervention gets its own test split. So 'reduced vs full' compares different test episodes, and worse, the imbalanced run, which drops every short episode, has no short episodes in its test set, so it can't show the failure it was designed to create. I found it by tracing what the builder's split input actually was, not by looking at results. The fix keeps the provenance model and changes the order: split once on the full pool, then intervene only on the train split, so every experiment shares one frozen test set. I'd also define the length buckets once, on the full dataset, and use the same cut points for the intervention and the evaluation slice, because today they're computed differently."

Why that answer lands: it's a real, non-trivial flaw, explained mechanistically, discovered by reasoning, with a fix that preserves the system's core principle. That's the most valuable answer type in a research-engineering interview.

Module 4 Summary — the one page to revise

What this module is. The translator from a declared data intervention to engine operations: an optional EpisodeSubsetFilter transform into clean/vK, then a DatasetBuilder.build into training/vM with splits and optional sampling weights. Provenance is excellent; filtering before splitting makes cross-intervention test sets incomparable.

Five claims to make cold

  1. Interventions are engine transforms with recorded reasons and dropped IDs, never local copies, and a test verifies the lineage matches.
  2. Rebalancing uses sampling weights over existing episodes. Nothing is duplicated or synthesized, so it can't close a coverage gap.
  3. "Imbalanced" removes a regime entirely. It's an out-of-distribution test, not under-representation.
  4. Splits are assigned after filtering, so every excluding intervention has its own smaller test set. The fix is to split once and intervene on train only.
  5. The length variable exists because so100_pickplace has one task. The intervention and evaluation buckets use different cut points.

Decisions

ChoseOverBecauseCost
Engine versions per interventionIDs passed to fitimmutable identity, lineage, reasonscatalog churn, orphans, duplicate versions
Filter → build (split)split → intervene on trainuses existing transform/build shapetest set changes per intervention
Sampling weightsduplication / synthesishonest about no new datarepeated frames; sampler unverifiable here
Length-bucket tercilesa task_id variableonly varying attribute in so100tie collapse; mismatched with eval terciles
Seeded rng.choice over sorted IDsunseeded / unsorteddeterministic per seednot nested across fractions
Reuse input when subset = allalways transformno pointless versionshides silent no-op imbalances

Volunteer unprompted: the moving test set; the 0.0 anchor that can't run despite its test; total removal vs under-representation; the bucket definitions don't match; EXPERIMENTS.md overclaims the rebalanced limitation and dataset.json test membership.

Numbers and names: so100 has 48 episodes, 326–475 frames, one task; terciles at the 33.3/66.7 percentiles; < 3 episodes → all "medium"; default oversample 3.0; split seed 42, quantile; EpisodeSubsetFilter, TransformPipeline, DatasetBuilder.build, next_version, VersionStage.CLEAN/TRAINING; README lineage raw/v1 → clean/v1 (50→48, dropped [9, 31]) → training/v6.

Sentences worth memorizing

  • "An intervention is a dataset version with a reason, not an argument to a training call."
  • "I filter before I split, so each intervention gets its own test set. The fix is to split once and intervene on train only."
  • "Dropping every short episode isn't imbalance; it's an out-of-distribution test, and its test set can't contain the regime it removed."
  • "Rebalancing redistributes attention over data I already have; it can't close a coverage gap, and I don't claim it does."

Module 5 — Pre-training guards: split integrity and the dataset profile

Files covered: src/model_training/data/split_integrity.py, src/model_training/data/profile.py

Where we are: Module 4 produced a training/vN version. Before a single gradient step, the runner does two things to it: it re-checks that the splits don't leak, and it profiles the version (statistics, coverage, QC findings) for the report. Both are framed in the docs as guards, evidence that the data is fit to train on. This module asks how much each one actually guards.

The one-line thesis: The split check is honest code that checks the one property that can't fail, and passes the properties that can fail (unassigned episodes, empty splits). The profile is careful about saying what it can't measure, but its headline "coverage" number is normalized by the number of occupied bins, so it rewards a dimension that uses two bins exactly as much as one that uses all ten.

5.1 Prerequisites — the absolute basics

  • Data leakage (split leakage). Information from the evaluation data reaching training. At episode level, that's the same episode ID in two splits. At content level, it's different episodes that are near-duplicates (recorded twice, or the same session and scene with trivially different motion). ID-level checks can only catch the first kind.
  • Partition. A division of a set into disjoint subsets that together cover it. "Splits form a partition" means: no overlap, and nothing left over.
  • Defense in depth / independent verification. Re-checking an invariant with code that doesn't share a path with the code that established it. It's only valuable if the invariant can actually be violated in the representation you check.
  • Tautological check. A check whose failure condition can't be represented by the data it reads. It always passes, and it proves nothing.
  • Test double (fake). A minimal stand-in object with just the methods a function under test calls. _FakeView in the tests is one.
  • Shannon entropy. For a probability distribution p₁…p_k, H = −Σ pᵢ log₂ pᵢ bits. It's 0 when all mass is on one outcome, and log₂ k when spread evenly over k outcomes.
  • Normalized entropy (evenness). H / H_max. The value of a normalized entropy depends entirely on what you pick as H_max: log₂(number of possible bins) measures how much of the possible space is used evenly, while log₂(number of occupied bins) measures only how even the occupied bins are (ecologists call this Pielou's evenness). The two answer different questions.
  • Equal-width histogram. Split [min, max] into B equal intervals and count values per interval. Here B = 10 (_HIST_BINS).
  • Coefficient of variation (CV). std / mean, a scale-free measure of spread. Not (max − min) / mean. For a roughly normal sample of 48 values, the expected range is about 4.5 standard deviations, so range/mean comes out around 4.5× the CV.
  • DuckDB. An in-process analytical SQL engine that queries Parquet and Arrow directly. The engine's explorer.sql(...) runs SQL against a version's frames table (inferred from the query text here). DuckDB list indexing is 1-based.
  • SQL injection. Building SQL by string interpolation lets a crafted value change the query's meaning. Identifier quoting ("col" with embedded " doubled) is the defence for column names; parameters are the defence for values.

5.2 verify_split_integrity

class SplitLeakageError(Exception):
    """Raised when any two of train/val/test share at least one episode id."""

@dataclass
class SplitIntegrityReport:
    dataset_id: str
    version: str
    split_sizes: dict[str, int]
    train_val_overlap: list[int]
    train_test_overlap: list[int]
    val_test_overlap: list[int]
    unassigned_episodes: list[int]

    @property
    def ok(self) -> bool:
        return not (self.train_val_overlap or self.train_test_overlap or self.val_test_overlap)

    def to_dict(self) -> dict:
        return {**self.__dict__, "ok": self.ok}
def verify_split_integrity(view: DatasetView, *, raise_on_failure: bool = True) -> SplitIntegrityReport:
    train = set(view.episode_ids(split="train"))
    val = set(view.episode_ids(split="val"))
    test = set(view.episode_ids(split="test"))
    all_ids = {e.episode_id for e in view.episodes}

    report = SplitIntegrityReport(
        ...,
        split_sizes={"train": len(train), "val": len(val), "test": len(test)},
        train_val_overlap=sorted(train & val),
        train_test_overlap=sorted(train & test),
        val_test_overlap=sorted(val & test),
        unassigned_episodes=sorted(all_ids - train - val - test),
    )
    if raise_on_failure and not report.ok:
        raise SplitLeakageError(...)
    return report

What it does: it reads split membership back from the materialized view, intersects every pair, and raises on any overlap. The runner calls it with the default raise_on_failure=True, right after opening the training version (Module 11), and writes split_integrity.json. The README quotes that file as evidence: "Before any training step, an independent check confirms that no episode appears in more than one split, and the run aborts if one does."

Why it's framed as valuable (module docstring): the builder "already guarantees this by construction", and this check "exists to catch a real leak regardless of where it would come from -- including a bug this project's own intervention builder introduces -- by re-deriving the partition independently".

What it can actually catch. Look at how split membership is stored. evaluation/offline.py reads it as e.attributes.get("split") == base_split (Module 7). So each episode carries one split attribute with one value. An episode can't simultaneously have split = "train" and split = "test" in that representation. If view.episode_ids(split=s) filters on that attribute (very likely, though I can't verify the engine), then train ∩ test is empty by construction of the data structure, not just by the builder's correctness. The test file says as much in its fake's docstring:

"used to prove the checker actually detects an engineered overlap, since a real DatasetView built by rde's own (correct) builder cannot produce one."

So the overlap check is close to tautological. It verifies a property the storage format makes unrepresentable. It isn't harmful, and it's cheap (three set operations over at most 48 IDs), but "independent re-verification" overstates it. The only way it could fire is if the engine stored membership as three separate ID lists, which isn't how this repository reads it.

What can actually go wrong, and passes:

  1. Unassigned episodes don't fail the check. ok ignores unassigned_episodes. An episode with no split attribute is silently excluded from train, val and test, so it's lost data, and the report says "ok": true. The partition property ("covers the set") isn't enforced, only disjointness.
  2. Empty splits don't fail the check. A version with val empty (for example from splits.val: 0.0, which Module 2 showed validates) or a tiny pool after a reduced intervention passes. An empty val split disables checkpoint selection (Module 6), and an empty test split makes the headline metric meaningless. Neither is caught here.
  3. Split sizes aren't compared with the config. The report records split_sizes but never checks them against config.splits. A builder that put 46/1/1 for a requested 0.8/0.1/0.1 would pass.
  4. Content-level leakage is out of scope, and that's the leak that actually matters in single-setup robot datasets. Episodes from one session in one scene are highly correlated across splits. The README itself concedes nearest-neighbour retrieval "is a strong baseline when test episodes come from the same setup". Nothing here (or anywhere) detects near-duplicate episodes across splits.

The raise_on_failure=False escape hatch is used only by tests. The production path always raises, which is right. The test_split_leakage_report_can_be_inspected_without_raising test exists to show the report is introspectable.

The tests use a hand-written fake (_FakeView, which exposes only episodes and episode_ids). That's good test design: the only way to exercise the failure branch is to construct an impossible view. It also proves the point above. The failure branch is reachable only with a fake.

What I'd change. Keep the disjointness check (it's free) and make ok mean the property that can fail:

ok = no_overlaps and not unassigned and all(split_sizes[s] > 0 for s in ("train", "val", "test"))

Also compare sizes against the requested fractions within ±1 episode for quantile mode, and frame the README sentence as "verifies the version is a complete, non-empty, disjoint partition". Content-level leakage would need episode embeddings or state-trajectory hashing. That's out of scope; name it as a known limitation.

5.3 build_dataset_profile, part 1: reuse, don't recompute

explorer = DatasetExplorer(settings=settings, catalog=catalog)

overview = explorer.overview(dataset_id, version)
durations = explorer.duration_distribution(dataset_id, version)
tasks = explorer.task_summary(dataset_id, version)
modalities = explorer.modality_summary(dataset_id, version)
dims = explorer.dimensionality(dataset_id, version)
qc = explorer.qc_summary(dataset_id, version)
lineage = explorer.lineage(dataset_id, version)

Decision: package the engine's explorer outputs instead of recomputing.

  • Why: the module docstring says "this module's only real job is packaging those into the profile shape and adding the two things DatasetExplorer does not compute: coverage/diversity scores and the train/val/test split distribution". It's a single source of truth for statistics, so the profile and rde's own CLI can't disagree.
  • Cost: the profile's correctness is the explorer's correctness, and the profile's shape depends on undocumented dict keys (durations["duration_s"]["max"], dims[k]["per_dimension"][i]["min"], lineage["chain"][-1]["inputs"]). An engine change to any of those keys raises KeyError here, and nothing pins the engine version (Module 1).
  • explorer.overview on an unknown version raises LookupError (test_profile_of_unknown_version_raises), so that error surfaces before any of the fallbacks below are reached.
parent_version = lineage["chain"][-1]["inputs"][0] if lineage["chain"] and lineage["chain"][-1]["inputs"] else None

The parent is the first input of the last lineage step. That's correct for this project's single-input transforms and builds. test_profile_to_dict_is_json_shaped asserts parent_version == "clean/v1" for the fixture's training version. For a multi-input operation (a merge), it would silently report only one parent.

manifest = catalog.get_manifest(dataset_id, version)
contract = manifest.attributes.get("training_contract") if manifest else None
if not contract:
    from rde.dataset_builder.builder import build_training_contract
    contract = build_training_contract(manifest) if manifest else {"observation": {}, "action": {}}
  • Fallback contract: a clean/vN version has no stored training contract (only builds create one, by inference), so the profile derives one on the fly. That's why mtrain profile works on clean/v1, which is exactly what EXPERIMENTS.md's workflow needs before choosing an imbalance variable.
  • The if manifest else {...} empty-contract branch is effectively unreachable: a missing manifest means an unknown version, and explorer.overview already raised LookupError above.
act_key = contract.get("action", {}).get("key")
state_keys = [k for k, v in contract.get("observation", {}).items() if v.get("plane") == "inline"]
image_keys = [k for k, v in contract.get("observation", {}).items() if v.get("plane") == "external"]

Inline vs external is the modality test used everywhere (Module 1 vocabulary). Note that "state" means every inline modality. For ALOHA that's state.joint and force.effort (the smoke test's 14 + 14 = 28 dimensions). So "state-space coverage" for ALOHA includes effort sensors, not just joint positions.

Splits: "unassigned" isn't a split

if manifest is not None:
    engine = DataEngine(settings)
    try:
        with engine.get_dataset(dataset_id, version) as view:
            raw_splits = view.splits()
        if set(raw_splits) - {"unassigned"}:
            splits = raw_splits
    except Exception as exc:
        notes.append(f"could not resolve splits for {dataset_id}@{version}: {exc}")
if not splits:
    notes.append(f"{version!r} carries no split assignment (it is not a training/vN version) -- "
                 "splits reported empty, not fabricated")
  • The "unassigned" bucket is the subtle line. The engine's view.splits() reports episodes with no split attribute under the key "unassigned". A clean version is entirely unassigned, which means "no split exists yet", not "a split called unassigned". Without this line, the profile of clean/v1 would report {"unassigned": 48} as if it were a real partition. It's tested (test_profile_on_clean_version_has_no_splits).
  • But on a training version with some unassigned episodes, raw_splits keeps the "unassigned" key, so the profile reports it as a fourth split alongside train, val and test, while split_integrity.json reports it separately and still says ok. That's a second place unassigned episodes would show up and still not stop anything.
  • The broad except Exception plus a note means profiling never fails because of the split lookup. Then the "no split assignment (it is not a training/vN version)" note is also appended, which is false if the cause was an exception on a training version. Two notes, one of them misleading.
  • The with ... as view context manager is used here, while the runner and CLI use explicit try/finally: view.close(). Both are correct; the codebase just isn't consistent.

5.4 build_dataset_profile, part 2: coverage

_HIST_BINS = 10

def _shannon_entropy_bits(counts: list[int]) -> float | None:
    total = sum(counts)
    if total <= 0:
        return None
    probs = [c / total for c in counts if c > 0]
    return -sum(p * math.log2(p) for p in probs)

def _normalized_entropy(counts: list[int]) -> float | None:
    """0 (all mass in one bucket) .. 1 (uniform across every non-empty bucket)."""
    h = _shannon_entropy_bits(counts)
    if h is None:
        return None
    k = sum(1 for c in counts if c > 0)
    if k <= 1:
        return 0.0
    return round(h / math.log2(k), 4)

The normalization is by k, the number of occupied bins, not by _HIST_BINS. Work two examples through:

Dimension's 10-bin countsH (bits)kNormalized entropy reportedlog₂(10)-normalized
[50, 0, 0, 0, 0, 0, 0, 0, 0, 50] (bimodal, e.g. a gripper that's open or closed)1.021.00.30
[10]*10 (uniform across the whole range)3.32101.01.0
[90, 10, 0, …]0.4720.470.14

A dimension using two of ten bins scores the same "perfect coverage" as one spread across all ten. The metric is evenness among occupied bins (Pielou's J), not coverage. Range-filling, which is what "coverage" means in ordinary speech and in the report's label "action-space coverage", is ignored, because the empty bins are the gaps and they're excluded from the denominator.

What the docs say:

  • The function's docstring is accurate: "uniform across every non-empty bucket".
  • The methodology string written into every profile is half accurate: "0 = all mass in one bin, 1 = uniform across every non-empty bin. Measures how evenly the observed range is used". The first sentence is right. The second is wrong: the observed range is [min, max] split into ten bins, and empty interior bins are exactly unused range.
  • The module docstring says coverage is "a real, computable proxy for 'how much of the observed range gets used'". That's the same overclaim.

Fix (one token): return round(h / math.log2(_HIST_BINS), 4) for per-dimension coverage. Keep the evenness variant if you want it, under its real name. For task entropy, k-normalization is arguably fine, because there's no "possible task" denominator.

Edge behaviour, for completeness:

  • k <= 1 → 0.0: a single task (so100) gives task entropy and instruction diversity of 0.0. That's correct and honest, and it's how the profile shows that task_id can't be the imbalance variable.
  • total <= 0 → None: an empty version produces None, not a fake number.
  • round(..., 4): four decimals, display precision only.

The per-dimension histogram query

def _dim_coverage(explorer, dataset_id, version, column, index, lo, hi):
    if hi <= lo:
        return {"normalized_entropy": None, "reason": "zero-range (constant) dimension"}
    col = '"' + column.replace('"', '""') + '"'
    sql = (
        f"SELECT LEAST({_HIST_BINS - 1}, GREATEST(0, "
        f"CAST(FLOOR({_HIST_BINS} * ({col}[{index + 1}] - {lo}) / ({hi} - {lo})) AS INTEGER))) "
        f"AS bucket, COUNT(*) AS n FROM frames GROUP BY 1"
    )
    try:
        rows = explorer.sql(dataset_id, version, sql)
    except Exception as exc:  # pragma: no cover
        return {"normalized_entropy": None, "reason": f"histogram query failed: {exc}"}

Every non-obvious piece:

  • hi <= lo guard: without it, a constant dimension divides by zero inside DuckDB. It's reported as None with a reason, which is the project's "unavailable, not fabricated" pattern applied well.
  • Identifier quoting ('"' + column.replace('"', '""') + '"'): column names like action.joint contain a dot, which DuckDB would otherwise parse as table.column. Doubling embedded quotes is the correct SQL escape. This is necessary for correctness, not just security.
  • [index + 1]: DuckDB lists are 1-indexed; Python's enumerate is 0-indexed. Without the + 1, every dimension would be histogrammed as its neighbour, and the last would read out of bounds (NULL).
  • LEAST(9, GREATEST(0, …)) clamp: a value exactly equal to hi computes bucket 10, and the clamp folds it into bucket 9. It also silently folds anything outside [lo, hi] into the edge bins. If the explorer's min/max were ever computed on a different population than the frames table (for example, raw vs normalized values), every value would pile up in bins 0 and 9 with no error.
  • lo and hi are interpolated as Python float literals. That's safe from injection because they're numbers from the engine. A nan or inf would produce invalid SQL and fall into the except. Parameter binding would be cleaner.
  • NULL values produce bucket IS NULL, and if b is not None skips them. Missing values are silently excluded from coverage. That's defensible, but it isn't reported.
  • Cost: one full scan of frames per dimension. For ALOHA that's 14 action plus 28 state dimensions, so 42 scans per profile. That's seconds at this data size. The video plane isn't touched, which matters: only the inline columns are read.

Two smaller mislabels

"episode_length_cv": (
    round(length_frames.get("mean", 0) and
          (float(durations["duration_s"]["max"] - durations["duration_s"]["min"])
           / max(durations["duration_s"]["mean"], 1e-9)), 4)
    if durations else None
),
  • It isn't a coefficient of variation. It's (max − min) / mean of duration. For a roughly normal sample of 48 episodes, range ≈ 4.5σ, so this reports about 4.5× the true CV. The label invites exactly the wrong reading.
  • length_frames.get("mean", 0) and (...) uses Python's and as a guard: if the frames mean is 0 (or missing), the value is 0; otherwise it's the ratio. It guards on the frame mean but divides by the duration mean. That works only because both are zero together. The max(..., 1e-9) guard is then redundant. It's convoluted for what should be statistics.pstdev(lengths) / mean(lengths).
  • task_coverage.normalized_entropy and instruction_diversity.diversity_normalized_entropy are the same number, computed twice from task_episode_counts. The profile presents one fact as two metrics.

The parts that are genuinely good

"visual_diversity": {"status": "unavailable",
                     "reason": "requires an image embedding/similarity model; ..."},
"episode_diversity": {"status": "partial", ..., "reason_partial": "limited to task-id and length variability; ..."},
missing_data = {
    "source": "rde QC finding records (qc_records) for this version -- only failing/warning "
              "checks are persisted as rows (passing checks leave no row), so a check's "
              "absence below means either it found nothing or has not been run against "
              f"{dataset_id}@{version} specifically; cross-check with `rde obs quality`",
    ...
}
  • Refusing to compute visual diversity without an embedding model, and saying so in the data, is the right call. A pixel-variance "diversity score" would look rigorous and mean nothing.
  • The QC caveat is the best-written line in the module. It names a real ambiguity in absence-of-evidence ("no row" means "passed or never ran") that most tools hide. Quote it in an interview as an example of honest metadata.
  • variation_note states that the schema has no variation field and that task_id and length are proxies "rather than an invented 'variation' axis". It's consistent with Module 4.

What population is profiled

The runner profiles training_version, the whole post-intervention version, including val and test episodes (Module 11). So the report's "action-space coverage" and "Dataset statistics (from profile)" describe train ∪ val ∪ test, not what the model trained on. For a "quantity vs coverage" study, you'd want coverage of the train split, ideally next to coverage of the test split, so you can say whether test states fall inside the trained range.

to_dict lists all 20 fields by hand instead of dataclasses.asdict(self). Add a field and forget to add it to to_dict, and it silently vanishes from dataset_profile.json. asdict would deep-copy nested dicts, which is a cost here, but {f.name: getattr(self, f.name) for f in fields(self)} would avoid both problems.

5.5 Honest gaps to own before an interviewer finds them

  1. The overlap check is near-tautological. Split is a single attribute per episode, so an overlap can't be represented, and the failure branch is reachable only with a fake view. Consequence: "an independent check confirms no leakage" overstates what's verified.
  2. ok ignores unassigned episodes, empty splits, and sizes vs config. Consequence: the properties that can actually fail (lost episodes, an empty val split that disables selection) pass with "ok": true.
  3. No content-level leakage detection. Consequence: near-duplicate episodes from the same session can sit in train and test. This is the realistic leak in single-setup datasets, and it's consistent with nearest-neighbour retrieval being so strong.
  4. "Coverage" is normalized by occupied bins. Consequence: a two-bin bimodal dimension scores 1.0, the same as a uniform one. "Action-space coverage" in every report measures evenness, not range use, and the methodology string misdescribes it.
  5. episode_length_cv is range/mean, about 4.5× the true CV at n = 48. Consequence: a mislabelled number in every profile.
  6. The profile covers train ∪ val ∪ test, not the train split. Consequence: it can't answer "did training cover the test states?", which is what a coverage study needs.
  7. Dependence on undocumented explorer dict keys, with no engine pin. Consequence: an engine refactor breaks profiling with KeyError.
  8. The histogram clamp silently folds out-of-range values into the edge bins, and NULLs are dropped unreported. Consequence: a min/max mismatch produces confident but wrong coverage.
  9. Duplicate metric (task entropy = instruction diversity). Consequence: one fact is presented as two.
  10. A misleading note after a split-lookup exception ("it is not a training/vN version"). Consequence: it misdiagnoses.
  11. to_dict is hand-listed. Consequence: new fields can silently disappear from the persisted profile.
  12. parent_version takes the first input only. Consequence: wrong for future multi-input operations.

The shape to carry into Module 6

 training/vN (DatasetView)
   │
   ├─ verify_split_integrity(view)                 runner: raise_on_failure=True
   │     train/val/test = view.episode_ids(split=…)
   │     overlaps  → SplitLeakageError   (unrepresentable if split is one attribute/episode)
   │     unassigned / empty / size-vs-config → NOT checked → ok=true
   │     → split_integrity.json
   │
   └─ build_dataset_profile(dataset_id, training_version)     (all splits pooled)
         DatasetExplorer: overview · durations · tasks · modalities · dims · qc · lineage
         contract = stored | build_training_contract(manifest)
         splits   = view.splits() minus pure-"unassigned"
         coverage = per-dim DuckDB 10-bin histogram → H / log2(#occupied bins)   (evenness!)
         visual_diversity = "unavailable" (honest)   episode_length_cv = range/mean (mislabel)
         → dataset_profile.json → report + task/length figures

Drill — answer from memory, files closed

  1. What exactly does verify_split_integrity check, and what would have to be true about the storage format for it to ever fail on a real dataset?
  2. Why didn't you just trust the builder's guarantee and skip the check, or, alternatively, why not make the check stronger?
  3. Compute the profile's reported normalized entropy for a gripper dimension whose values fall half in the lowest bin and half in the highest. What's wrong with that number?
  4. What breaks in production: a builder bug leaves three episodes with no split attribute. What happens across the pipeline?
  5. Interviewer framing: "How do you know your test set isn't leaking into training?"

Appendix A — Model answers to the drill

A1. What the split check verifies. "It reads the train, val and test episode IDs back from the materialized training version, intersects each pair, and raises SplitLeakageError if any intersection is non-empty. It also records split sizes and any episode in none of the three. For the overlap branch to fire on real data, the engine would have to be able to store an episode in two splits at once. But split is a single attribute on each episode, and the evaluation code reads it as attributes['split'], so an overlap isn't representable. The test file says so itself: it uses a fake view because the real builder can't produce an overlap. So the overlap check is cheap insurance against a representation change, not a meaningful independent verification."

Why that answer lands: it describes the check precisely, then shows you've reasoned about whether it can fail. That's the move that separates real validation from ceremony.

A2. Skip it or strengthen it? "I'd keep it, since three set intersections over 48 IDs are free and it documents the invariant in the run directory, but I'd make it check what can actually go wrong. Today ok ignores unassigned episodes and empty splits, and those are the realistic failures. An empty val split silently disables checkpoint selection, and unassigned episodes silently vanish from training and evaluation. So ok should mean a complete, non-empty partition whose sizes match the configured fractions within one episode. The harder leak, near-duplicate episodes from the same recording session ending up on both sides, needs content-level checks like trajectory hashing or embedding similarity. I'd list that as a known limitation rather than imply the ID check covers it."

Why that answer lands: it rejects both extremes, specifies the stronger invariant exactly, and distinguishes ID-level from content-level leakage. That's a sign of real data-quality experience.

A3. The gripper example. "Two occupied bins with equal mass give 1 bit of entropy. The code normalizes by the log of the number of occupied bins, log₂ 2 = 1, so it reports 1.0, the same score as a dimension spread uniformly across all ten bins. Normalized by the ten possible bins it would be about 0.30. So the number measures evenness among occupied bins, not how much of the range is used. The empty bins are exactly the coverage gaps, and they're excluded from the denominator. The function's docstring says 'uniform across every non-empty bucket', which is accurate, but the methodology text and the report label call it coverage of the observed range, which it isn't. The fix is to normalize by log₂ of the bin count."

Why that answer lands: you do the arithmetic out loud, find the conceptual error, separate the accurate docstring from the inaccurate label, and give a one-token fix. Interviewers love a worked number.

A4. Three unassigned episodes. "The split check computes them as unassigned but still reports ok: true, so nothing stops. The training loop asks for train IDs and never sees them. Validation and test ask for their splits and never see them. So three episodes silently drop out of the experiment. The profile, which runs over the whole version, would include them in its statistics, and would also show an extra 'unassigned' split, so the profile and the actual training data disagree. dataset.json would still list them among the selected episodes. The only place you'd notice is comparing split sizes against the episode count. The fix is to make unassigned episodes fail the integrity check, because a partition that doesn't cover the set isn't a partition."

Why that answer lands: it traces the failure through every downstream consumer, including the inconsistency between artifacts, and ties the fix to a crisp definition.

A5. "How do you know your test set isn't leaking into training?" "At the episode level: splits are assigned per episode, never per frame, so neighbouring near-identical frames can't straddle the boundary, and each episode carries exactly one split label. A check before training reads the splits back and confirms they're disjoint. Selection uses validation only; the trainer hard-codes the val split for scoring. The test set is touched once, after a checkpoint is chosen. At the content level I can't claim it isn't leaking. Both datasets are single-setup recordings, so test episodes are close relatives of training episodes, and you can see it in the results: frame-level nearest-neighbour retrieval ties fine-tuned SmolVLA on SO-100. That's the honest framing. These are in-distribution held-out episodes, not a generalization test, and I'd add a held-out-session or held-out-scene split before claiming generalization."

Why that answer lands: it gives the real protections, then volunteers the limitation, backed by evidence from your own results, and names the fix that would justify a stronger claim.

Module 5 Summary — the one page to revise

What this module is. Two pre-training steps over the new training/vN: a split-disjointness check that raises on overlap, and a profile that packages the engine's explorer statistics and adds per-dimension histogram "coverage" plus honest "unavailable" markers. Both are useful records, and both are weaker guards than the docs imply.

Five claims to make cold

  1. The split check runs before training and raises on overlap. Overlap is essentially unrepresentable because split is one attribute per episode.
  2. ok doesn't cover unassigned episodes, empty splits, or sizes vs config. Those are the failures that can actually happen.
  3. ID-level checks can't detect content-level leakage. Single-setup data makes held-out episodes in-distribution, and nearest-neighbour's strength shows it.
  4. Profile coverage is entropy normalized by occupied bins, so it's evenness, not range use. The fix is to normalize by log₂(10).
  5. The profile refuses to fabricate visual diversity, and documents that missing QC rows mean "passed or never ran".

Decisions

ChoseOverBecauseCost
Re-check splits from the viewtrust the builderdefense in depth, recorded evidencechecks an unrepresentable failure; misses real ones
Explorer reuserecomputing statssingle source of truth with rde CLIcoupled to undocumented dict keys
10 equal-width bins plus entropyno coverage metric / k-NN coveragecheap, one DuckDB pass per dimensionoccupied-bin normalization; clamp hides range mismatch
"unavailable" visual diversitypixel-variance proxyno embedding model; refuse to fakeprofile is numeric-only
Fallback contract for clean versionsrequire a training versionprofile before choosing an interventionnone
Profile training_versionprofile train split onlyone call, the whole versioncoverage includes test episodes

Volunteer unprompted: the tautological overlap branch; ok ignores unassigned and empty splits; the occupied-bin normalization; episode_length_cv is range/mean; coverage pools all splits; there's no content-level leak check.

Numbers and names: _HIST_BINS = 10; DuckDB 1-based list index ([index + 1]); clamp LEAST(9, GREATEST(0, …)); k <= 1 → 0.0; ALOHA state = 28 dimensions (joint 14 + effort 14), so 42 histogram queries; range of n = 48 normal samples ≈ 4.5σ; README split 38/5/5, all overlap: [], ok: true.

Sentences worth memorizing

  • "A check you can only make fail with a fake object isn't verifying anything the data can do."
  • "A partition has to be disjoint and complete; I only checked the half that couldn't break."
  • "My 'coverage' metric was really evenness: dividing by occupied bins hides exactly the gaps."
  • "These are held-out episodes from the same setup, which is why retrieval is such a strong baseline. It's not a generalization test."

Module 6 — The training loop around fit

Files covered: src/model_training/training/trainer.py (training/__init__.py was covered in Module 1)

Where we are: Modules 4 and 5 produced a verified, profiled training/vN. This module spends the GPU budget. Module 1 established that the gradient step itself, SmolVLAPolicy.fit, lives in the engine. What's here is everything around the step: the callback that validates, checkpoints, selects and prunes, plus crash recording and memory release. This is the code you can honestly say you own in "the training loop."

The one-line thesis: Trainer is a careful Template-Method-by-callback wrapper. It runs validation only on the val split, verifies every checkpoint before it can become "best," never prunes the best one, and always saves a final checkpoint. But the checkpoint it selects is chosen by 32 validation frames of a stochastic policy. That's small enough that the README's validation MSE is about 13× lower than its test MSE. The crash handler also catches fewer failures than its comment claims.

6.1 Prerequisites — the absolute basics

  • Training step. One forward pass, loss, backward pass and optimizer update on one batch. "1,000 steps at batch size 4" means 4,000 sampled frames (or sample windows), not 1,000 epochs.
  • Gradient accumulation (grad_accum_steps). Sum the gradients of k micro-batches before one optimizer update. The effective batch is k × batch_size. Here k = 1.
  • Gradient clipping (max_grad_norm = 1.0). If the gradient vector's L2 norm exceeds the threshold, rescale it down. This stops one bad batch from blowing up the weights.
  • Weight decay (1e-5). A small pull toward zero on the weights each step (L2-style regularization).
  • Learning-rate warmup and schedule. Start the LR near zero and ramp it up linearly over warmup_steps. A sudden full LR on pretrained weights, with an optimizer whose statistics are still empty, can wreck them. docs/API_REFERENCE.md says the schedule is "warmup then cosine decay". That lives in the engine and is unverified here.
  • Optimizer state. Adam-family optimizers keep two running averages per parameter (first and second moments). Restarting without them behaves differently from continuing, which is why resuming needs a fresh warmup (Module 2).
  • Checkpoint cadence. Save every checkpoint_every steps. Validation cadence means scoring on held-out data every validate_every steps.
  • Model selection / early stopping. Pick the checkpoint with the best validation score instead of the last one. That's a guard against overfitting.
  • Winner's curse (selection bias). If you take the minimum of m noisy measurements, the selected value is biased low. The more candidates and the noisier the estimate, the bigger the bias. The selected checkpoint's validation score then overstates how good it is.
  • Effective sample size. Consecutive frames of one episode are highly correlated, so 32 adjacent frames carry roughly the information of a handful of independent samples, not 32.
  • Template Method pattern. A fixed algorithm skeleton (here, fit's loop) with hook points that subclasses or callers fill in (here, on_step). When the hook is a function argument rather than a subclass override, it's often called inversion of control or a callback hook.
  • Closure / nonlocal. A nested function that captures variables from its enclosing function. nonlocal x lets the nested function rebind x.
  • BaseException vs Exception. KeyboardInterrupt (Ctrl-C) and SystemExit inherit from BaseException, not Exception, so except Exception doesn't catch them. SIGTERM's default action terminates the process without raising anything. SIGKILL (the Linux OOM-killer) can't be caught at all.
  • PyTorch CUDA memory. torch.cuda.max_memory_allocated() reports the peak bytes handed out by the caching allocator to tensors. It excludes the allocator's cached-but-free blocks and the CUDA context (often several hundred MB), so it's lower than what nvidia-smi shows. empty_cache() returns unreferenced cached blocks to the driver. It can't free memory that something still references.

6.2 Shape of Trainer.train

train(train_view, val_view, episode_ids, episode_weights)
  ├─ guard: model must be "smolvla"
  ├─ build SmolVLAPolicy(resume_from or pretrained); load(contract or None)
  ├─ reset CUDA peak stats; resolve train ids (raise if empty)
  ├─ define on_step(record)            ← the hook: log · validate · checkpoint · verify · select · prune
  ├─ try:   fit_result = policy.fit(..., on_step=on_step)      ← engine owns the loop
  │  except Exception: write crash.json; del policy; empty_cache; raise
  ├─ save checkpoints/final (always)
  ├─ peak GPU; del policy; empty_cache
  └─ return TrainingOutcome(best_checkpoint = best or final, ...)

Decision: drive the engine's fit loop through a callback rather than writing the loop here.

  • What: fit owns the iteration. This module injects behaviour at each step through on_step(record).
  • Why: the step mechanics (batching against the dataset contract, weighted sampling, the optimizer) are engine-coupled (Module 1). The run policy (when to validate, what to keep) is experiment-coupled. The callback is the seam between them.
  • Alternative: fit(steps=100) called in a loop of chunks, returning control between chunks. That's simpler to reason about, but it needs optimizer and schedule state carried across calls (fit does accept optimizer_state= per docs/API_REFERENCE.md line 792, which is unverified). The other alternative is a framework trainer (PyTorch Lightning, HF Trainer) with its own callback system.
  • Cost: the callback runs inside the engine's loop, so validation, checkpoint I/O and pruning all happen on the training thread, between steps, and their time is counted in wall_clock_s (the README's "Training time 324 s" includes them). An exception inside on_step (a full disk during save_checkpoint) propagates out of fit and is recorded as a training crash.

6.3 Setup: model construction and resume

if tc.model != "smolvla":
    raise ValueError(f"Trainer implements real gradient-based fine-tuning for model='smolvla' only ...")

This duplicates the runner's own branch, which already only constructs a Trainer when model == "smolvla" (Module 11). It's defensive and correct, and it makes Trainer safe to call directly.

checkpoint_dir = self.run_dir.sub("checkpoints")
policy = SmolVLAPolicy(checkpoint=tc.resume_from or tc.pretrained_checkpoint, device=tc.device)
# Resuming one of our own checkpoints: it is already configured (shapes
# and normalization stats) for this exact dataset -- reload as-is. Only
# a *fresh* pretrained checkpoint (which may target a different
# embodiment than train_view's) needs its shapes/stats overridden from
# the dataset contract ...
policy.load(contract=None if tc.resume_from else train_view.contract)
if tc.resume_from:
    meta = SmolVLAPolicy.verify_checkpoint(tc.resume_from)
    self.run_dir.write_json("resume_source.json", meta)

Decision: pass the dataset contract only for a fresh start.

  • Why: smolvla_base was pretrained with its own input and output shapes and normalization. The SO-100 run needs 6-dimensional action, and ALOHA needs 14-dimensional action plus 28-dimensional state (the smoke test's cross-embodiment cases). Loading with the contract rewrites the features and normalization stats from this dataset. A resumed checkpoint already has them. Passing the contract again could overwrite the checkpoint's stats with the new version's (identical here, but not in general).
  • Cost: a resume isn't checked against the dataset. resume_source.json records the source checkpoint's dataset_id and dataset_version (written into every checkpoint's extra, see below) but never compares them with train_view. Resume an SO-100 checkpoint against ALOHA by mistake and the shapes disagree. The failure would surface somewhere inside the engine's batching, as a tensor-shape error far from the cause. if meta["dataset_id"] != train_view.dataset_id: raise is one line.
  • Order: load runs before verify_checkpoint, so a corrupt resume directory fails inside load with whatever error the engine raises, and the clear "incomplete checkpoint" message from verify_checkpoint (asserted by test_corrupted_checkpoint_directory_rejected) is never reached. Swap the two lines.

Optimizer state isn't saved, and it could be. Every periodic checkpoint is written with "optimizer_state_saved": False, and resume passes no optimizer state. Module 2 framed the fresh warmup on resume as the consequence. Per docs/API_REFERENCE.md, the engine's fit returns "optimizer_state" and accepts optimizer_state=. So this is a choice, not an engine limitation (unverified, since I can't read the engine). The likely reason is disk: Adam's two moments are twice the parameter count, roughly tripling checkpoint size. That's a reasonable choice on a laptop. It isn't recorded as a reason anywhere, and the API doc's §9.2 presents the LR restart as inherent.

import torch  # safe: policy.load() above already required it to succeed

This is the lazy import that keeps the module importable without torch (Module 1), but trainer.py's top-level from rde.model_adapters.models import SmolVLAPolicy means importing this file still loads the engine's model module. The comment is accurate about torch specifically.

if torch.cuda.is_available():
    torch.cuda.reset_peak_memory_stats()
peak_rss = proc.memory_info().rss if proc else None

Peak stats are reset after the model is loaded, so peak_gpu_mb measures training, not the load. The README's "Peak GPU memory 2.2 GB / 3.0 GB" is therefore allocator-reported peak during training. It's lower than nvidia-smi would show (context plus cache excluded), so it understates what you'd need to provision on a GPU.

ids = episode_ids if episode_ids is not None else train_view.episode_ids(split="train")
if not ids:
    raise ValueError("no training episodes resolved -- nothing to fine-tune on")

The fallback to split="train" is for direct callers. The runner always passes train IDs. The empty guard runs after the model has been loaded onto the GPU, so an empty-train failure costs a full model load first. Moving it to the top is free.

6.4 The hook: on_step

val_adapter = VisionLanguageAdapter(val_view.contract)
evaluator = Evaluator()
max_val_frames = tc.val_batches * tc.batch_size     # 8 × 4 = 32 with base.yaml

checkpoints: list[dict[str, Any]] = []
best: dict[str, Any] = {"metric": None, "checkpoint": None}
last_step = 0

def on_step(record: dict[str, Any]) -> None:
    nonlocal peak_rss, last_step
    last_step = record["step"] + 1
    self.run_dir.append_jsonl("metrics/train.jsonl", {"experiment_id": self.config.experiment_id, **record})
    if proc is not None:
        peak_rss = max(peak_rss or 0, proc.memory_info().rss)

    step = last_step  # steps completed so far, 1-indexed
    do_ckpt = bool(tc.checkpoint_every) and step % tc.checkpoint_every == 0
    do_val = bool(tc.validate_every) and step % tc.validate_every == 0
    if not (do_ckpt or do_val):
        return
  • best is a dict, not two variables, so the closure can mutate it without nonlocal. peak_rss and last_step are rebound, so they need nonlocal. Mixing both styles in one closure is legal but inconsistent.
  • record["step"] + 1 assumes the engine reports 0-indexed step numbers. With steps = 1000 and checkpoint_every = 100, saves land at 100, 200, …, 1000, which matches the README's "step_1000". With grad_accum_steps > 1 it's unknown whether record fires per micro-batch or per optimizer step. If per micro-batch, every cadence would be off by a factor of k. That's unverifiable here and unexercised (grad_accum_steps: 1 everywhere).
  • bool(tc.checkpoint_every) and … makes 0 mean "never" and avoids step % 0 (a ZeroDivisionError). That's the defensive branch, and it has a purpose.
  • One JSONL append per step means 1,000 file open/write/close cycles. That's negligible next to a SmolVLA step (around 300 ms per step at the README's 324 s per 1,000 steps), but it's an fsync-free append, so the Module 3 torn-write caveat applies.
  • Peak RSS is sampled once per step. It misses transient spikes between samples, which is fine as a rough number.

Validation: what "best" is chosen by

    val_metric = None
    if do_val:
        policy.eval_mode()
        result = evaluator.evaluate(
            val_view, policy, val_adapter, split="val", batch_size=tc.batch_size,
            max_frames=max_val_frames, persist=False,
        )
        policy.train_mode()
        val_metric = result.metrics.get(ec.selection_metric)
        self.run_dir.append_jsonl("metrics/validation.jsonl",
                                  {"experiment_id": self.config.experiment_id, "step": step, **result.metrics})
  • split="val" is hard-coded here, and this is where "selection never touches test" is actually enforced. It isn't in the nonexistent model_training.experiments.selection that the schema comment cites (Module 2). Say it that way: it's enforced structurally, by the only call site.
  • persist=False stops the evaluator from writing these interim results into the engine's catalog (inferred from the flag name). Contrast this with the feedback step, which uses persist=True (Module 8).
  • eval_mode() / train_mode() toggling switches dropout and normalization behaviour. If evaluate raises, train_mode() is never called, but the exception aborts training anyway, so it doesn't matter.

The validation sample is tiny. max_val_frames = val_batches × batch_size = 8 × 4 = 32 frames. The SO-100 val split is 5 episodes. The README's test split of 5 episodes has 1,908 frames, so val is about 1,900 frames. Validation scores roughly 1.7% of the validation set. Which 32 frames is decided by the engine's Evaluator with max_frames, and I can't verify that. If it takes the first N frames in iteration order (the most common implementation), the 32 frames are the first ~1 second (at 30 fps) of the first validation episode.

Circumstantial evidence that this matters, from the README itself:

  • README §2.2 says SO-100 error "is lowest at the start (0.051) ... of an episode and peaks in the middle ... at 0.144". That's mean absolute error by progress decile on test.
  • README result 2 says validation MSE "reached its minimum (0.0018) at step 1,800", while the selected checkpoint's test MSE is 0.0239. Test is about 13× worse than validation.
  • An MAE of about 0.05 corresponds to an MSE on the order of 0.003, the same order as the validation numbers. Validation on start-of-episode frames would produce exactly this pattern.

I can't prove the first-N behaviour without the engine. But the 13× val–test gap is a real number in the README, and nothing else in the code explains it as naturally. (Distribution shift between val and test episodes is possible with 5 episodes each, but 13× is large.) Even if the frames were drawn at random, the setup has three compounding problems:

  1. Correlated frames. 32 frames from one or two episodes have a tiny effective sample size.
  2. A stochastic policy. README §2.3 says flow-matching sampling moves MSE by up to 5% when re-scoring the same frames.
  3. Selection over 10–20 noisy estimates (every 100 steps across 1,000–2,000 steps) means a winner's-curse bias in best_val_metric. The reported "selection value" is optimistic by construction.

Decision: a fixed small validation budget instead of the full val split.

  • Why (inferred): a full validation pass over about 1,900 frames at the README's roughly 47–50 ms per frame is about 90 seconds per pass, about 15 minutes over ten passes. That would triple a 5-minute training run on a laptop GPU.
  • Cost: checkpoint selection is close to noise on SO-100, where validation MSE was still falling. It probably picked the late checkpoint correctly because the trend dominated the noise.
  • The fix that keeps the budget: choose the 32 (or 128) validation frames once, stratified across every val episode and across episode progress, with a fixed seed. Score every checkpoint on that same frozen subset, and average two stochastic samples per frame. The cost stays the same, the noise drops sharply, and every checkpoint is compared on identical inputs.

Checkpoint, verify, select, prune

    if do_ckpt:
        ckpt_path = checkpoint_dir / f"step_{step}"
        policy.save_checkpoint(ckpt_path, extra={
            "experiment_id": ..., "step": step,
            "dataset_id": train_view.dataset_id, "dataset_version": train_view.version,
            "optimizer_state_saved": False,
        })
        # A process killed mid-write (disk full, OOM-killer, Ctrl-C)
        # can leave a partial checkpoint directory; verifying before
        # trusting it as `best` (and therefore protected from
        # pruning) keeps a corrupt save from silently displacing a good one.
        try:
            SmolVLAPolicy.verify_checkpoint(ckpt_path)
            verified = True
        except (FileNotFoundError, ValueError) as exc:
            verified = False
            self.run_dir.append_jsonl("metrics/checkpoint_errors.jsonl", {...})
        checkpoints.append({"step": step, "path": str(ckpt_path), "val_metric": val_metric, "verified": verified})
        if verified and val_metric is not None:
            better = (best["metric"] is None
                      or (ec.selection_mode == "min" and val_metric < best["metric"])
                      or (ec.selection_mode == "max" and val_metric > best["metric"]))
            if better:
                best.update(metric=val_metric, checkpoint=str(ckpt_path))
        self._prune_checkpoints(checkpoint_dir, checkpoints, tc.keep_last_n_checkpoints, best)
  • extra metadata stamps every checkpoint with its experiment, step and dataset version. That's what lets build_model_artifact later trust the checkpoint's own claim about its training data (Module 9). It's a good provenance detail.
  • The verify-before-trust comment describes a scenario its mechanism can't handle. "A process killed mid-write (disk full, OOM-killer, Ctrl-C)": if the process is killed, the verification line never runs in that process, and there's no subsequent best to protect. A disk-full error during save_checkpoint would raise, not return, so it would crash training instead of producing an unverified checkpoint. Verification only catches a save_checkpoint that returns normally but wrote an incomplete directory. What verify_checkpoint checks is, per test_corrupted_checkpoint_directory_rejected, that all required files exist ("only one of five required files" gives "incomplete"). That's a presence check, not an integrity check: a truncated model.safetensors would pass. The mechanism is sound as cheap insurance, but the comment oversells the threat model. The case where partial checkpoints really bite, resuming from one after a kill, goes through the resume path (6.3), where load runs first.
  • Validation and checkpoint cadences must coincide for selection to work. A checkpoint saved at a step with no validation has val_metric = None and can never become best. A validation at a step with no checkpoint has no weights to select. With checkpoint_every: 50, validate_every: 30, no checkpoint is ever eligible, and selection silently falls back to final (below). Nothing validates that checkpoint_every % validate_every == 0. Both are 100 in base.yaml, so the shipped configs are fine.
  • A NaN validation metric can lock selection. val_metric is not None admits float("nan"). If the first validated checkpoint has a NaN metric, best["metric"] is None makes it better, so best = NaN. After that, x < NaN is always False, and no later checkpoint can ever displace it. Whether the engine's fit raises on a NaN loss (the crash-handler comment says "a NaN loss" as if it raises) or keeps going is unverified. If it keeps going, this is a real path. Guard with math.isfinite(val_metric).
  • selection_mode is checked with string equality. Literal["min", "max"] at the schema level (Module 2) makes the third case impossible. Good.

_prune_checkpoints

@staticmethod
def _prune_checkpoints(checkpoint_dir, checkpoints, keep_last_n, best) -> None:
    """Drop periodic checkpoints beyond ``keep_last_n``, always keeping the
    best-by-validation one regardless of recency."""
    if keep_last_n <= 0:
        return
    import shutil

    periodic = [c for c in checkpoints if not c.get("final")]
    periodic.sort(key=lambda c: c["step"])
    keep_paths = {best.get("checkpoint")} | {c["path"] for c in periodic[-keep_last_n:]}
    for c in periodic[:-keep_last_n] if len(periodic) > keep_last_n else []:
        if c["path"] in keep_paths:
            continue
        p = Path(c["path"])
        if p.exists():
            shutil.rmtree(p, ignore_errors=True)
        c["pruned"] = True

Walk it for keep_last_n = 3, with saves at 100…500 and the best at 200: periodic[-3:] = {300, 400, 500}, keep_paths = those plus 200, and the candidates periodic[:-3] = {100, 200}. So 100 is deleted and 200 is kept. That's correct.

  • keep_last_n <= 0 means "keep everything". That's a reasonable reading of "keep the last 0", read as "pruning disabled", but it's undocumented and opposite to the literal meaning.
  • Unverified (possibly corrupt) checkpoints count toward the last-N. A corrupt step_500 occupies a slot and pushes a good step_200 out, unless 200 is best.
  • ignore_errors=True plus c["pruned"] = True unconditionally means a deletion that fails (permissions, a file handle still open on network storage) is marked pruned anyway. The record and the disk disagree, silently.
  • Pruned entries stay in checkpoints with pruned: True, so training_outcome.json lists paths that no longer exist. They're marked, so a reader can tell. That's fine.
  • This is a pure function with no I/O except rmtree, and it has no unit test. Grep for _prune in tests/ finds nothing. The only coverage is the smoke test (torch required), which sets keep_last_n_checkpoints: 1 with checkpoints at steps 2 and 4 and doesn't assert what was pruned. It's the easiest function in the trainer to test with tmp_path, and it's untested.

6.5 Calling fit, the crash path, and the final checkpoint

started = time.perf_counter()
try:
    fit_result = policy.fit(
        train_view, episode_ids=ids, episode_weights=episode_weights,
        steps=tc.steps, batch_size=tc.batch_size, learning_rate=tc.learning_rate,
        weight_decay=tc.weight_decay, grad_accum_steps=tc.grad_accum_steps,
        max_grad_norm=tc.max_grad_norm, warmup_steps=tc.warmup_steps,
        seed=tc.seed, on_step=on_step,
    )

What's passed and what isn't: num_workers, mixed_precision and device (device went to the constructor) aren't passed to fit. This confirms Module 2's dead-field finding at the only call site. seed=tc.seed means the training seed comes from config only. The CLI --seed never reaches here (Module 11).

except Exception as exc:
    # A crash here (OOM, a NaN loss, SIGTERM) previously left the run
    # directory in an undetectable half-finished state ...
    self.run_dir.write_json("crash.json", {
        "experiment_id": ..., "error_type": type(exc).__name__, "error": str(exc),
        "steps_completed": last_step, "checkpoints": checkpoints,
        "wall_clock_s": time.perf_counter() - started,
    })
    del policy
    if torch.cuda.is_available():
        torch.cuda.empty_cache()
    raise

Decision: record a crash marker and re-raise; never swallow.

  • Why: the comment narrates a real bug that was fixed. A crashed run used to be indistinguishable from one that never started. crash.json with steps_completed and the surviving checkpoint list makes a crashed run diagnosable and partly salvageable, since a verified step_N can be resumed from.
  • What it actually catches, versus what the comment claims:
FailureCaught by except Exception?crash.json written?
CUDA out-of-memory (torch.OutOfMemoryError, a RuntimeError subclass)yesyes
NaN lossonly if the engine raises on NaN (unverified)conditional
Exception inside on_step (full disk during save, JSON error)yesyes
Ctrl-C (KeyboardInterrupt, a BaseException)nono
SIGTERM (scheduler or kill)no: default handler terminates without an exceptionno
Host OOM-killer (SIGKILL)no: uncatchableno

The comment's "SIGTERM" is wrong, and the run-directory status gap from Module 3 remains for the two most common ways a long job dies on a shared machine. Fix: except BaseException, for Ctrl-C, plus a signal.signal(SIGTERM, …) handler that raises, and a status: running marker written before fit, so that a SIGKILL'd run is at least identifiable as "running with no heartbeat".

  • del policy; empty_cache() frees less than it appears to. At this point the exception object exc holds a traceback whose frames include fit's frame, and fit's self is the policy. That's a live reference to every parameter and optimizer tensor until the exception is fully handled upstream. So empty_cache() here can't return that memory. The del is harmless, but the stated purpose, "release the GPU memory this policy was holding rather than leaving it for the caller's next model", isn't achieved while the exception propagates. The runner doesn't load another model after a training exception anyway (it re-raises out of run), so the practical impact is nil. The comment just misdescribes the effect.
wall_clock_s = time.perf_counter() - started

# Always checkpoint the final step, even off-cadence, so resume and
# deployment never require rerunning training to get a usable artifact.
final_path = checkpoint_dir / "final"
policy.save_checkpoint(final_path, extra={..., "step": fit_result["steps"], ..., "final": True})
checkpoints.append({"step": fit_result["steps"], "path": str(final_path), "val_metric": None, "final": True})
  • wall_clock_s excludes the final save but includes every periodic validation and save. It's "training plus in-loop overhead", not pure optimization time.
  • The final checkpoint is never verified (no verify_checkpoint call, unlike the periodic ones). It's verified later only if it becomes the selected checkpoint, inside build_model_artifact (Module 9).
  • The final checkpoint is never validated (val_metric: None), so it can never compete for best. When steps % checkpoint_every == 0 (the shipped configs: 1000 % 100), the last periodic checkpoint step_1000 and final are the same weights saved twice: one is validated and selectable, the other isn't. SmolVLA is reported publicly as about 450M parameters, so each save is plausibly around 1–2 GB (I didn't measure this). Under the shipped configs it's a duplicate on every run.
  • Placing the final save outside the try means a failure while saving it (disk full) raises without a crash.json, though training itself finished.
peak_gpu_mb = torch.cuda.max_memory_allocated() / 1e6 if torch.cuda.is_available() else None
del policy
if torch.cuda.is_available():
    torch.cuda.empty_cache()

return TrainingOutcome(
    steps_completed=fit_result["steps"],
    final_train_loss=fit_result["final_loss"],
    best_checkpoint=best["checkpoint"] or str(final_path),
    best_val_metric=best["metric"],
    ...
)
  • Releasing memory before return is correct here: the runner immediately loads a second SmolVLAPolicy from the selected checkpoint for evaluation (Module 11), and two copies resident at once is exactly what an 8 GB laptop GPU can't afford. On this path there's no traceback pinning the model. fit_result may still hold optimizer_state (if the engine returns it, per the API doc) until train returns, which is moments later.
  • The / 1e6 is megabytes in SI units, not MiB. The README's "2.2 GB" is consistent with SI.
  • best["checkpoint"] or str(final_path) is a silent fallback. If no checkpoint was ever eligible (cadence mismatch, a wrong selection_metric name, a zero-size val split from Module 5, every checkpoint unverified), the run reports best_checkpoint = final with best_val_metric = None. The runner writes that into model.json as selection_value: null, and no limitation is recorded. The run looks selected, but it was just last-step. Add a limitation string whenever the fallback fires.

6.6 Honest gaps to own before an interviewer finds them

  1. Checkpoint selection uses 32 validation frames (val_batches × batch_size), probably the opening second of one episode (unverified engine behaviour), on a stochastic policy, with a min over 10–20 candidates. Consequence: the README's selected val MSE (0.0018) is about 13× lower than test (0.0239). Selection is near-noise, and best_val_metric is optimistically biased.
  2. The crash handler misses Ctrl-C, SIGTERM and the OOM-killer, contrary to its comment. Consequence: the most common ways long jobs die leave no crash.json.
  3. A silent fallback to final when no checkpoint is eligible, with no limitation recorded. Consequence: "selected checkpoint" can mean "last step" with no indication.
  4. A NaN first validation locks best forever (NaN passes is not None, and every later comparison is false). Consequence: selection is pinned to a broken checkpoint (if fit doesn't raise on NaN).
  5. Validation and checkpoint cadences aren't checked for alignment. Consequence: misaligned settings silently disable selection.
  6. A resume isn't checked against the training dataset, and load precedes verify_checkpoint. Consequence: a wrong resume fails as an obscure tensor error, and the clear verification message is unreachable.
  7. Optimizer state is never saved or restored, though the engine's fit reportedly supports it. Consequence: resume restarts warmup and schedule. It's a defensible disk trade-off, but undocumented as a choice.
  8. final duplicates the last periodic checkpoint, is never verified or validated, and is saved outside the try. Consequence: about one wasted model-size copy per run, and a save failure without a crash marker.
  9. verify_checkpoint is a presence check, and the comment's threat model (process killed mid-write) can't be caught in-process. Consequence: the verification is narrower than described.
  10. _prune_checkpoints is untested; failed deletions are marked pruned; corrupt checkpoints occupy keep slots. Consequence: disk state and records can diverge silently.
  11. del policy; empty_cache() in the crash path can't free memory pinned by the traceback. Consequence: a comment that misdescribes the effect (no practical harm today).
  12. peak_gpu_mb is allocator-peak, measured after load. Consequence: the README's GPU figures understate what you'd need to provision.
  13. The empty-train-IDs guard runs after the model load. Consequence: a wasted GPU load on a trivially detectable error.
  14. Step semantics under grad_accum_steps > 1 are unknown. Consequence: the cadence could be off by a factor of k (unexercised).

The shape to carry into Module 7

 Trainer.train(view, view, train_ids, weights)
   SmolVLAPolicy(resume_from | smolvla_base).load(contract | None)
   │
   │  engine fit loop (steps, bs=4, lr, wd, clip, warmup, seed) ──calls──▶ on_step(record)
   │                                                                     ├─ train.jsonl  (every step)
   │                                                                     ├─ every validate_every:
   │                                                                     │    Evaluator(val, max_frames=32) ─▶ validation.jsonl
   │                                                                     ├─ every checkpoint_every:
   │                                                                     │    save step_N (no optimizer) → verify (presence)
   │                                                                     │    best ← min(val_metric)  [needs val at same step]
   │                                                                     └─ prune: keep last N + best
   ├─ except Exception → crash.json, re-raise     (not Ctrl-C / SIGTERM / SIGKILL)
   ├─ save final (unverified, unvalidated)
   └─ TrainingOutcome(best_checkpoint = best or final  ← silent fallback, best_val_metric, peaks, wall clock)
                     │
                     ▼  runner reloads best_checkpoint for test evaluation (Module 7)

Drill — answer from memory, files closed

  1. Walk through exactly what happens at step 300 of a run with checkpoint_every: 100, validate_every: 100, keep_last_n_checkpoints: 3.
  2. Why didn't you just validate on the whole validation split every time?
  3. What breaks in production: the job scheduler sends SIGTERM at a time limit, 70% through training. What's on disk, and what can you recover?
  4. The README reports validation MSE 0.0018 and test MSE 0.0239 for the same checkpoint. Explain the gap.
  5. Interviewer framing: "How did you choose which checkpoint to evaluate, and how confident are you that it was the right one?"

Appendix A — Model answers to the drill

A1. Step 300. "The engine's fit finishes optimizer step 300 and calls my on_step callback. It appends the step's loss, learning rate and grad norm to train.jsonl and samples resident memory. 300 is a multiple of both cadences, so it switches the policy to eval mode, runs the evaluator on the val split capped at 32 frames, switches back to train mode, and logs the full metrics to validation.jsonl. Then it saves checkpoints/step_300 with metadata (experiment, step, dataset version, and a flag that optimizer state wasn't saved), and verifies the directory has all required files. If it's verified and its val MSE beats the running best, it becomes best. Finally pruning keeps the last three periodic checkpoints plus the best. At step 300 there are only three, so nothing is deleted yet. At step 400, step 100 would be deleted unless it was the best."

Why that answer lands: it's the exact sequence, including the eval/train toggle and the conditions for becoming best, plus a correct worked pruning example. It shows you could reimplement it.

A2. Why not validate on the full split? "Cost. The val split is about 1,900 frames, and SmolVLA inference ran at roughly 50 ms per frame on the laptop GPU, so a full pass is about a minute and a half. Ten of them would roughly triple a five-minute run. So I capped it at val_batches × batch_size, 32 frames. That turned out to be the weak point of the design. Thirty-two correlated frames, probably all from the start of one episode, scored with a stochastic flow-matching sampler, and then a minimum taken over ten or twenty checkpoints. The selected validation number is noisy and biased low. The fix that keeps the cost is to choose a fixed, stratified subset once (frames spread across every val episode and across episode progress), score every checkpoint on that same subset, and average a couple of samples per frame."

Why that answer lands: it gives the real budget arithmetic, owns the consequence precisely, and offers a fix that respects the original constraint rather than just saying "use more data."

A3. SIGTERM at 70%. "Python's default SIGTERM handler terminates the process without raising, so my except Exception never runs. No crash.json, no final checkpoint, no training_outcome.json or summary.json. On disk you'd have train.jsonl and validation.jsonl up to about step 700, and the last three periodic checkpoints plus the best, each verified when saved, so you can resume from one of them in a new run with resume_from. You'd lose the optimizer state, so the resume restarts warmup. The run directory itself is now non-empty, so retrying the same experiment the same day is blocked. The fix is a SIGTERM handler that raises, catching BaseException so Ctrl-C is recorded too, and a 'running' status file written before training, so a hard kill is at least identifiable."

Why that answer lands: it knows that SIGTERM doesn't raise by default (a classic gap), inventories exactly what survives, and connects it to the retry-blocking behaviour from another module.

A4. 0.0018 val vs 0.0239 test. "Several things push validation down, and I think one dominates. Validation scores only 32 frames, and if the evaluator takes the first frames in order, that's the first second of one validation episode. My own per-phase analysis shows error is lowest at the start of episodes and peaks mid-episode. Second, the selected value is the minimum over about twenty noisy checkpoint evaluations, so it's biased low by selection alone. Third, the val and test splits are five episodes each, so episode-to-episode variation is large. I can't fully separate these without the engine code in front of me, but I wouldn't report that validation number as an estimate of anything. The test number is the honest one, and the fix is a frozen, stratified validation subset."

Why that answer lands: it gives a mechanistic hypothesis tied to your own data, then two other contributing causes, and states the uncertainty explicitly. That's the posture of a careful experimentalist.

A5. "How did you choose which checkpoint, and how confident are you?" "Checkpoints every 100 steps, each validated on the val split only (the trainer hard-codes val, so test never influences selection) and verified for completeness before it's eligible. The lowest validation MSE wins, and pruning never deletes it. If nothing is eligible, it falls back to the final checkpoint, which I'd now flag explicitly in the report, because today it's silent. Confidence: moderate on SO-100, low as a general method. On SO-100, validation was still decreasing at the end of training, so 'pick a late checkpoint' was almost certainly right regardless of noise. But the selection signal itself is 32 frames of a stochastic policy, and the validation minimum is about 13× below test, so it's not a precise estimator. If the curve had been flat, I'd have been picking among checkpoints at random."

Why that answer lands: it separates the mechanism's correctness (no test leakage, verified, protected) from the estimate's quality. It also explains why the result was probably right anyway, which is more convincing than either defending or disowning it.

Module 6 Summary — the one page to revise

What this module is. A wrapper around the engine's SmolVLAPolicy.fit that injects run policy through an on_step callback: val-only validation, checkpointing with presence verification, best-by-validation selection, pruning that protects the best, a forced final checkpoint, a crash marker, and GPU release before evaluation.

Five claims to make cold

  1. The gradient step is the engine's. The trainer owns cadence, selection, pruning, crash recording and memory release.
  2. Selection never sees test, because the only validation call hard-codes split="val".
  3. Only verified checkpoints with a validation score can be best, and the best is never pruned.
  4. Selection uses 32 frames of a stochastic policy, so the val minimum (0.0018) is about 13× below test (0.0239). The fix is a frozen, stratified subset.
  5. The crash marker covers in-process exceptions, not Ctrl-C, SIGTERM or SIGKILL.

Decisions

ChoseOverBecauseCost
Callback into engine fitown loop / Lightningstep mechanics are contract-coupledcallback time counted as training; on_step errors look like training crashes
Contract only on fresh startalways pass contractresumed checkpoint already has its statsresume not checked against dataset
No optimizer state in checkpointssave momentsabout 3× smaller checkpoints (inferred)resume restarts warmup and schedule
32-frame validationfull val splitabout 90 s per full pass vs a 5-minute runnear-noise selection, winner's curse
Verify before eligibletrust every savea corrupt save can't become a protected bestpresence check only
Keep last N plus bestkeep all / last N onlybounded disk, best preserveduntested; failed deletes marked pruned
Always save finalrely on periodica usable artifact even off-cadenceduplicate of step_N when aligned; unverified
Record crash.json and re-raiseswallow / no markerdiagnosable crashesmisses BaseException and signals

Volunteer unprompted: the 13× val–test gap and its likely cause; the silent fallback to final; NaN locking best; the signals not caught; _prune_checkpoints has no test; final duplicates step_1000.

Numbers and names: base config 1,000 steps, batch 4, lr 1e-4, warmup 50, wd 1e-5, clip 1.0, ckpt and val every 100, val_batches 8 (so 32 frames), keep 3; extended lr 3e-5, warmup 20; README val min 0.0018 at step 1,800 vs test 0.0239; SO-100 324 s and 385 s, ALOHA 576 s; peak GPU 2.2 / 3.0 GB (allocator peak, SI); about 50 ms per frame for SO-100 inference; files train.jsonl, validation.jsonl, checkpoint_errors.jsonl, crash.json, resume_source.json, checkpoints/step_N, checkpoints/final.

Sentences worth memorizing

  • "The engine owns the step; I own what happens between steps."
  • "Test can't influence selection because the only validation call in the trainer hard-codes the val split."
