  • "My validation signal was 32 frames of a stochastic policy, which is why val sits 13× below test. Selection was right on SO-100 because the curve was still falling, not because the estimate was good."
  • "except Exception doesn't see Ctrl-C, and SIGTERM doesn't raise at all; the crash marker covers less than its comment says."

Module 7 — Offline evaluation: baselines, tails, time and slices

Files covered: src/model_training/evaluation/offline.py (evaluation/__init__.py was covered in Module 1)

Where we are: Module 6 produced a selected checkpoint. This module turns it into the numbers the project is judged by. It scores two baselines through the same evaluator as SmolVLA, then breaks the test error down by tail, by episode, by phase of the episode, and by named slice. Everything in README §2, the headline results, comes out of these five functions plus the engine's Evaluator.

The one-line thesis: The decompositions are the right ones to report. Baselines are scored through the same call, tails come before means, error is broken down by phase, and metrics that can't be computed are refused. But each slice re-runs a stochastic model instead of slicing the predictions already made. The slices are tiny (1–2 episodes), and nothing carries an uncertainty estimate, so the finer-grained claims in the README are mostly noise.

7.1 Prerequisites — the absolute basics

  • Per-frame error, as defined here. For one frame, FrameOutcome.error is the mean absolute error across action dimensions (per docs/METRICS.md §4 and the error_distribution docstring). Each frame produces one scalar.
  • Split-level MSE vs "per-episode MSE". The split's mse squares each dimension's error and then averages. This module's per-episode "mse" squares the per-frame mean absolute error and averages. Those are different quantities: by Jensen's inequality (mean |e|)² ≤ mean e², so the per-episode number is systematically smaller. METRICS.md §4 states this honestly.
  • Percentiles / tail metrics. p90 is the error below which 90% of frames fall. Tails matter for control because one wildly wrong action can end a task even when the average is low.
  • Normalized episode progress. frame_index / (episode_length − 1), from 0 at the start to 1 at the end, so episodes of different lengths line up by phase.
  • Stratified evaluation. Reporting metrics separately on predefined subsets (strata or slices) instead of only in aggregate. Aggregates can hide a subgroup that fails.
  • Mean-action baseline. It always predicts the training-set mean action. In normalized units its MSE is about the variance, roughly 1.0 (README: 0.908 SO-100, 1.010 ALOHA). Any model must beat it.
  • Nearest-neighbour baseline (behaviour retrieval). Per docs/API_REFERENCE.md, NearestNeighborPolicy paired with StateAdapter: find the training frame whose robot state is closest to the query frame's state, and return that frame's action. It uses no images and no language. It's described there as "a genuinely strong baseline on single-task data". (This is from the docs, because the engine code isn't here, but the integration test's StateAdapter plus nearest_neighbor pairing corroborates it.)
  • Stochastic inference. SmolVLA samples from noise each call (flow matching, Module 1). Scoring the same frames twice gives different errors.
  • Sampling uncertainty. With 5 test episodes, a metric's spread over which episodes happened to be in test is large. A bootstrap over episodes (resampling episodes with replacement and recomputing the metric) is the standard way to put an interval on it.

7.2 evaluate_baselines: same call, different inputs

def evaluate_baselines(view, names, *, split="test", train_split="train",
                       batch_size=32, max_frames=None) -> dict[str, EvaluationResult]:
    """Fit and score each baseline through the exact call used for the model
    under test, so the comparison is apples-to-apples."""
    ev = Evaluator()
    out: dict[str, EvaluationResult] = {}
    for name in names:
        if name not in MODEL_REGISTRY:
            raise KeyError(f"unknown baseline model {name!r}; known: {sorted(MODEL_REGISTRY)}")
        model, adapter = build_model(name, view.contract)
        model.fit(view, split=train_split)
        out[name] = ev.evaluate(view, model, adapter, split=split, batch_size=batch_size,
                                max_frames=max_frames, persist=False)
    return out

Decision: score baselines through the identical Evaluator.evaluate call, in every run.

  • Why: the metric code, frame selection, normalization and max_frames cap are shared, so a ratio to the baseline is meaningful. METRICS.md says it directly: "a model that cannot outperform the training-set mean has, by definition, learned nothing that this metric can detect."
  • What "apples-to-apples" does and doesn't mean: the evaluation is identical; the inputs aren't. build_model returns each model's own adapter: StateAdapter for both baselines, VisionLanguageAdapter for SmolVLA. That's correct, since a baseline can't use images, but it changes what the comparison says (next point).
  • Fitted on the same train split of the same training/vN, so for an intervention run, the baselines see the same reduced or filtered training pool as SmolVLA. They don't receive episode_weights, so in a rebalanced run the baselines are unweighted. That's a small asymmetry, worth knowing.
  • The KeyError for an unknown name is tested (test_evaluate_baselines_rejects_unknown_model). It's raised before any work, with the list of known names.
  • Refit every run (k-NN index built over all training frames each time). That's cheap on CPU and avoids stale caches.

What the headline result actually means. README Table 2: SO-100 test MSE is SmolVLA 0.0239 and nearest-neighbour 0.0239. ALOHA: SmolVLA 0.2157, nearest-neighbour 0.1062. Since the nearest-neighbour baseline looks up the closest robot state and replays its action, the tie says:

On these single-setup datasets, robot state alone nearly determines the next action, and a 450M-parameter vision-language-action model, fine-tuned for 2,000 steps, adds nothing measurable offline over a state-space lookup table, and does worse on the harder bimanual task.

That isn't a failure of the evaluation; it's what the evaluation is for. Offline, though, it can't tell you what the image and language inputs buy in closed loop, where retrieval typically breaks down once the robot leaves the demonstrated states. That's the strongest argument for the rollout evaluation the project can't run. Have this paragraph ready; it's the most interesting sentence the project can say.

Doc vs code: METRICS.md §2 says baseline-relative metrics (mse_ratio, mae_ratio) "are computed by rde.evaluation.Evaluator.compare and by model_training.evaluation.offline.evaluate_baselines". evaluate_baselines computes no ratio; it returns raw results. The only ratio in this repo is calculated in the report renderer (m["mse"] / mean_mse, Module 10). Whether Evaluator.compare exists in the engine is unverifiable here, and nothing in src/ calls it.

7.3 error_distribution and episode_metrics

def error_distribution(result: EvaluationResult) -> dict[str, Any]:
    """Median / p90 / p95 / p99 of per-frame mean-absolute error."""
    if not result.outcomes:
        return {"status": "no outcomes"}
    errs = np.array([o.error for o in result.outcomes])
    return {"median": float(np.median(errs)), "p90": float(np.percentile(errs, 90)),
            "p95": float(np.percentile(errs, 95)), "p99": float(np.percentile(errs, 99)),
            "min": float(errs.min()), "max": float(errs.max())}
  • Tails, not just means. README §2.1 uses these: continued training cut p99 by 7% and the worst frame by 32% (0.553 → 0.375), while the median moved less. That's a genuinely useful observation, because tail frames are where control fails.
  • float(...) casts turn NumPy scalars into Python floats, so the JSON is exact. This line is what keeps Module 3's default=str stringification hazard from applying here.
  • The empty guard returns a status dict instead of crashing np.percentile on an empty array. plot_error_distribution then skips plotting because the keys are missing (Module 10). That's consistent.
  • The p99 of 1,908 correlated frames is set by about 19 frames, which may all come from one difficult moment in one episode. METRICS.md §3 says so ("statistically noisy; the frame count should always be reported"). The returned dict doesn't include the frame count, though, so the caveat relies on the reader.
def episode_metrics(result: EvaluationResult) -> list[dict[str, Any]]:
    by_ep: dict[int, list[float]] = {}
    for o in result.outcomes:
        by_ep.setdefault(o.episode_id, []).append(o.error)
    out = []
    for ep, errs in sorted(by_ep.items()):
        a = np.asarray(errs)
        out.append({"episode_id": ep, "frames": len(a), "mae": float(a.mean()),
                    "mse": float((a ** 2).mean()), "rmse": float(np.sqrt((a ** 2).mean())),
                    "max_error": float(a.max())})
    return out
  • The "mse" and "rmse" keys are misnamed. They're computed from per-frame mean absolute error, so they're "mean squared MAE", not MSE in the sense every other mse in the system uses. METRICS.md §4 discloses it, which is to its credit, but a JSON consumer or a compare --metric mse reader won't see the doc. Rename to mean_sq_frame_mae or compute true per-episode MSE from per_dim_error (which FrameOutcome carries). README §2.2 quotes per-episode MAE only, which is the correctly named one.
  • sorted(by_ep.items()) makes the output order deterministic by episode ID, so diffs between runs are stable.
  • Per-episode is the right grain for a 5-episode test set. README: SO-100 per-episode MAE spans 0.085–0.111. That spread (about 25%) is the honest measure of how much a single test-split number depends on which episodes are in test. Use it as your uncertainty argument (7.6).

7.4 temporal_error: error by phase of the episode

def temporal_error(result, view, buckets: int = 10) -> dict[str, Any]:
    if not result.outcomes:
        return {"status": "no outcomes"}
    episodes_touched = {o.episode_id for o in result.outcomes}
    frame_index: dict[int, int] = {}
    ep_len: dict[int, int] = {}
    for ep_id in episodes_touched:
        table = view.get_episode_frames(ep_id, columns=["frame_id", "frame_index"])
        d = table.to_pydict()
        ep_len[ep_id] = max(1, len(d["frame_id"]))
        for fid, fidx in zip(d["frame_id"], d["frame_index"]):
            frame_index[int(fid)] = int(fidx)

    bucket_errors: list[list[float]] = [[] for _ in range(buckets)]
    unresolved = 0
    for o in result.outcomes:
        fidx = frame_index.get(o.frame_id)
        if fidx is None:
            unresolved += 1
            continue
        length = ep_len[o.episode_id]
        progress = fidx / (length - 1) if length > 1 else 0.0
        b = min(buckets - 1, max(0, int(progress * buckets)))
        bucket_errors[b].append(o.error)
    return {"buckets": buckets, "progress_bucket_edges": [...],
            "mean_error_by_progress_bucket": [round(float(np.mean(b)), 6) if b else None for b in bucket_errors],
            "n_by_bucket": [len(b) for b in bucket_errors], "unresolved_frames": unresolved}
  • Why the re-read. The docstring explains it: FrameOutcome has frame_id but not frame_index, so the function fetches the small frame_id → frame_index table once per touched episode. It reads two narrow columns, not images. That's the right cost profile.
  • columns=[...] projects only the needed columns. Without it, the engine might materialize full frames (state, action and image references) for every episode.
  • length = len(d["frame_id"]) assumes frame_index runs from 0 to length−1 with no gaps. If an episode has dropped frames (the profile reports episodes_with_missing_or_discontinuous_frames, Module 5), frame_index can exceed length − 1, so progress > 1, and the clamp min(buckets − 1, …) silently piles those frames into the last bucket. Using max(frame_index) instead of the row count would be robust to gaps.
  • max(1, …) and if length > 1 else 0.0 guard single-frame episodes against division by zero.
  • The min/max clamp handles progress == 1.0 exactly (the last frame), which would otherwise compute bucket 10.
  • unresolved_frames is reported rather than hidden. That's the honest-metadata pattern.
  • Weighting: every bucket pools frames across episodes, so longer episodes contribute more frames per bucket. With SO-100's 326–475-frame spread, that's mild.
  • With a max_frames cap and first-N frame selection (Module 6's open question), later buckets would be empty. The function correctly returns None and n = 0 for them instead of a fabricated mean.

What it found: README §2.2 says SO-100 error is lowest at the start (0.051) and end (0.049), and peaks mid-episode (0.144 at 50–60% progress). That's plausible physically, since the middle is the grasp and transport phase. It's also the evidence behind Module 6's hypothesis that start-of-episode validation frames are unrepresentatively easy.

7.5 stratified_evaluate: slices that re-run the model

ev = Evaluator()
out: dict[str, EvaluationResult] = {
    "overall": ev.evaluate(view, model, adapter, split=base_split, batch_size=batch_size,
                            max_frames=max_frames, persist=False)
}
for name in slices or []:
    if name == "per_task":
        for task_id in sorted({e.task_id for e in view.episodes if e.task_id is not None}):
            ep_ids = view.episode_ids(split=base_split, task_id=task_id)
            if not ep_ids:
                continue
            out[f"task_{task_id}"] = ev.evaluate(
                view, model, adapter, split=f"{base_split}:task_{task_id}",
                episode_ids=ep_ids, batch_size=batch_size, max_frames=max_frames, persist=False)
    elif name == "per_length_bucket":
        lengths = {e.episode_id: e.num_frames for e in view.episodes
                   if e.attributes.get("split") == base_split}
        if len(lengths) >= 3:
            t1, t2 = np.percentile(list(lengths.values()), [33.3, 66.7])
            groups = {"short": [], "medium": [], "long": []}
            for eid, n in lengths.items():
                groups["short" if n <= t1 else "long" if n > t2 else "medium"].append(eid)
            for bucket_name, ids in groups.items():
                if ids:
                    out[f"length_{bucket_name}"] = ev.evaluate(..., episode_ids=ids, ...)
    else:
        raise ValueError(f"unknown evaluation slice {name!r}; supported: 'per_task', 'per_length_bucket' ...")
return out

Decision: define slices from real per-episode metadata only, and reject anything else.

  • Why: the docstring says "Slice names are resolved from real per-episode metadata, never invented". Asking for "variation" raises (test_stratified_evaluate_rejects_unknown_slice). This is the same "refuse to fabricate" discipline as the profile.
  • The split label f"{base_split}:task_{id}" is passed to evaluate with explicit episode_ids. Per docs/API_REFERENCE.md, episode_ids "takes precedence over split for frame selection" and split becomes a label. That's subtle and correct: the slice is restricted to test episodes by construction, because ep_ids came from episode_ids(split=base_split, ...). test_stratified_evaluate_per_task_and_length_bucket asserts that every slice's episodes are a subset of overall's.

Problem 1: every slice is a fresh stochastic inference pass. Each ev.evaluate(...) calls model.predict again on frames that "overall" already predicted. For SmolVLA, whose outputs are sampled, a slice's frames get different predictions from the same frames inside "overall". Consequences:

  • Slices don't reconcile with overall. A frame-weighted average of the three length slices won't equal the overall MSE, beyond rounding.
  • The README's noise estimate is this effect. README §2.3: "The stratified pass re-scores all 1,908 SO-100 test frames, and its MSE differs from the main pass by up to 5% (0.0305 against 0.0290 ...)". SO-100 has a single task, so task_0 is every test episode, which makes it a full re-scoring of overall with fresh noise. That's actually a lucky natural experiment: it's the only measurement of inference variance in the project. But it was produced by redundancy, not design.
  • Cost. With base.yaml's slices: [per_task, per_length_bucket] on single-task SO-100, test inference runs over overall (1×) + task_0 (1×) + three length slices (about 1× together), so about 3× the necessary inference. At about 50 ms per frame over 1,908 frames, that's about 95 s per pass, or about 3 minutes extra per run.
  • Config inconsistency. aloha_coffee_baseline.yaml drops per_task because "a 'per_task' slice would be an exact duplicate of 'overall' -- pure redundant video-decode cost". SO-100 is also single-task (EXPERIMENTS.md §3), but base.yaml keeps per_task for it. Same reasoning, opposite configuration.
  • The fix: predict once, then slice the outcomes of "overall" by episode_id in Python. Every slice then becomes a partition of one set of predictions, reconciles exactly with overall, and costs zero extra inference. If you want an inference-noise estimate, measure it deliberately (score overall twice with different seeds), not by accident.

Problem 2: the length slices hold 1–2 episodes each. The test split has 5 episodes (SO-100) or 4 (ALOHA). The 33.3/66.7 percentiles of 5 lengths, with <= t1 short and > t2 long, give groups of about 2/1/2. README §2.2 claims "long SO-100 episodes are easier (MSE 0.0206) than short (0.0290) and medium (0.0311) ones". That's a comparison of one or two episodes per group, each scored once by a stochastic model whose re-scoring moves MSE by about 5%, among episodes whose per-episode MAE already varies by about 25% (0.085–0.111). That claim isn't supported by the data's resolution. It might be true, but you can't tell from this. In an interview, don't repeat it as a finding. Say that the slices exist and that the test set is too small for them to be informative.

  • For ALOHA, the README correctly notes the length slice is uninformative because "all four of its test episodes are 1,100 frames long and fall into one bucket". With ties, <= t1 puts everything into "short" (the Module 4 tie collapse). That's a correct caveat in the README.
  • len(lengths) >= 3 silently skips the length slices for test splits under 3 episodes. A reduced run with 2–3 test episodes (Module 4's moving test set) would silently get no length slices, with no note.

Problem 3: within-split cut points. Here terciles are computed over the test split's own 5 lengths; in Module 4's intervention they're computed over the whole dataset. So length_short at evaluation time and "short" at intervention time are different sets (Module 4 gap 4). They also differ between runs whenever the test split changes, which is every excluding intervention. A "short slice" isn't a fixed slice.

docs/EXPERIMENTS.md §6 claims "A slice is a frozen episode-identifier list, produced identically on every invocation". That's true for one training/vN (same test episodes, same lengths, same cut points), and false across interventions, and the cut points themselves depend on the 5 test lengths. "Fixed evaluation slices" in the docs means "deterministic given the version", not "the same slice across experiments".

7.6 What isn't here: uncertainty

Nothing in this module (or anywhere in the repo) produces a confidence interval, a standard error, or a multi-seed aggregate. With 5 test episodes, one training seed and a stochastic policy, a single test MSE has three variance sources: which episodes are in test, the training seed, and inference sampling. The project measured the third once (about 5%), by accident. The README's "18% improvement from continued training" sits outside that 5% inference band, but it isn't tested against episode variance or seed variance.

The cheapest honest addition is an episode-level bootstrap. Resample the 5 test episodes with replacement 1,000 times, and recompute MSE from the stored per-frame per_dim_error (already written to predictions/test_outcomes.jsonl in Module 11). That gives an interval at zero inference cost. A paired comparison between two runs on the same test episodes (only possible for full and rebalanced runs, Module 4) is stronger still: per-episode differences cancel the episode-difficulty variance.

7.7 Honest gaps to own before an interviewer finds them

  1. Slices re-run stochastic inference instead of slicing one prediction set. Consequence: slices don't reconcile with overall, cost about 3× the necessary inference on SO-100, and the project's only inference-noise estimate is an accident of a redundant slice.
  2. Length slices contain 1–2 test episodes, so the README's "long episodes are easier" is below the data's resolution. Consequence: a claim that can't survive an interviewer's "how many episodes?"
  3. There's no uncertainty estimate anywhere (no bootstrap, no seeds, no intervals). Consequence: no difference in the README is statistically qualified except against the accidental 5% noise band.
  4. The nearest-neighbour baseline is state-only retrieval, and it ties or beats SmolVLA. Consequence: this isn't a bug. It's the key interpretive fact, and you must frame it as "offline, state nearly determines action on single-setup data".
  5. Per-episode "mse"/"rmse" are mean squared per-frame MAE, not MSE. Consequence: a misnamed key that's disclosed only in METRICS.md.
  6. base.yaml keeps per_task for single-task SO-100, which the ALOHA config explicitly removes as redundant. Consequence: about 1 wasted full test pass per SO-100 run.
  7. METRICS.md §2 says evaluate_baselines computes ratios. It doesn't. Consequence: a doc overclaim. The ratio lives only in the report renderer.
  8. Length slices are silently skipped below 3 test episodes, with no note. Consequence: small-pool intervention runs lose slices invisibly.
  9. temporal_error assumes contiguous frame_index. Consequence: episodes with gaps pile frames into the last bucket.
  10. Baselines don't receive episode_weights in rebalanced runs. Consequence: a minor asymmetry in "apples-to-apples".
  11. Tail percentiles don't carry their frame count, despite METRICS.md saying it should always be reported. Consequence: the noise caveat depends on the reader.
  12. success_tolerance is accepted by the engine's Evaluator.evaluate (per the API doc) but never passed. Consequence: this confirms that Module 2's dead config field could be wired in one line.

The shape to carry into Module 8

 selected checkpoint ──▶ SmolVLAPolicy + VisionLanguageAdapter
 training/vN view ─────┐
                       ├─ evaluate_baselines: build_model(name) → (policy, StateAdapter); fit(train); Evaluator(test)
                       │       mean_action (≈1.0 MSE)   nearest_neighbor (state-space retrieval; ties SmolVLA on SO-100)
                       │
                       └─ stratified_evaluate(test, slices)
                              overall  ───────── Evaluator(test)                    ← pass 1
                              task_k   ───────── Evaluator(episode_ids=…)           ← re-inference (dup on 1-task data)
                              length_* ───────── Evaluator(episode_ids=…)           ← re-inference, 1–2 episodes each
                              (within-split terciles; skipped if < 3 episodes)
                                 │
                 overall.outcomes ├─ error_distribution  (median/p90/p95/p99/min/max of per-frame MAE)
                                  ├─ episode_metrics     (mae ✓, "mse"/"rmse" = squared MAE ✗)
                                  └─ temporal_error      (10 progress buckets; re-reads frame_index)
                                 ▼
                 FeedbackLoop.detect(overall)  → Module 8

Drill — answer from memory, files closed

  1. What exactly does the nearest-neighbour baseline do, and what does it mean that it ties SmolVLA on SO-100?
  2. Why didn't you just compute slice metrics by filtering the overall predictions?
  3. The README says long SO-100 episodes are easier than short ones. Would you defend that claim?
  4. What breaks in production: you add a third slice type and run on a dataset with 40 tasks. What happens to evaluation time, and why?
  5. Interviewer framing: "Your model ties a nearest-neighbour lookup. Why should I believe it learned anything useful?"

Appendix A — Model answers to the drill

A1. The nearest-neighbour baseline. "It's behaviour retrieval over robot state. During fit it indexes every training frame's state vector and action. At test time it finds the training frame whose state is closest to the query's state and replays that action. No images, no language. It's scored through exactly the same evaluator call as SmolVLA. On SO-100 both reach 0.0239 test MSE, so offline, on a single-setup, single-task dataset, the robot's current joint configuration almost determines the demonstrated next action, and the vision-language model adds nothing measurable on top of a lookup table. On ALOHA, retrieval is twice as good. That's the honest result. What offline evaluation can't show is closed-loop behaviour, where retrieval usually fails as soon as the robot drifts into states the demos never visited. That's where vision should earn its keep, and it's exactly the experiment I couldn't run without a robot or simulator."

Why that answer lands: it explains the mechanism precisely, states the uncomfortable result without spin, and identifies exactly what experiment would distinguish the models.

A2. Why not slice the overall predictions? "I should have. Each slice calls the evaluator again, which reruns SmolVLA, and since SmolVLA samples actions from noise, the same frame gets a different prediction inside a slice than inside overall. So slices don't reconcile with the overall number, and on SO-100, which has a single task, the per-task slice is a complete second pass over the test set. Together with the length slices, that's about three times the inference I needed. Funnily enough, that redundant pass is the only place I measured inference noise (about 5%). The right design predicts once, slices the per-frame outcomes by episode ID, and measures inference noise deliberately by scoring twice with different seeds."

Why that answer lands: it admits the flaw directly, explains the stochastic mechanism that makes it more than a performance issue, and turns the accidental noise estimate into a point about deliberate measurement.

A3. "Long episodes are easier"? "No, not as a finding. The test split has five episodes, and the length terciles of five values put about two episodes in short, one in medium and two in long. Each is scored once by a stochastic model whose re-scoring moves MSE by about five percent, and per-episode error already varies by about twenty-five percent across the five episodes. A difference between one and two episodes at that noise level isn't evidence. I'd say the slicing infrastructure works, and that this test set is too small for slice-level conclusions. To make a real claim, I'd want a bootstrap interval over episodes at minimum, and ideally a larger held-out set or several seeds."

Why that answer lands: it retracts the overreach yourself, with arithmetic, before the interviewer can. Volunteering a downgrade buys credibility for every claim you keep.

A4. A third slice type on a 40-task dataset. "Evaluation time scales with the number of slices, because every slice is a separate inference pass. per_task alone would add 40 passes. They're disjoint subsets, so together about one more full pass, plus 40 rounds of evaluator overhead and model batching inefficiency on small slices. Add a third slice type and every new axis costs another pass. And because the passes are stochastic, the slices wouldn't sum to overall. If slices ever overlapped, like task crossed with length, the cost would grow with the number of combinations. The fix is the same: one inference pass, with slicing as a cheap group-by over stored per-frame outcomes. Then any number of slice definitions is essentially free."

Why that answer lands: it reasons about scaling from the code's structure, catches the stochastic-reconciliation issue again, and gives a fix that changes the complexity class.

A5. "Your model ties a nearest-neighbour lookup. Why believe it learned anything?" "Offline, I'd say it learned the demonstrated state-to-action mapping about as well as memorizing it does. It's at 2.6% of the mean-action error, so it clearly learned the task distribution, but it isn't better than retrieval here, and on ALOHA it's worse. I wouldn't claim more than that. What would justify more is a test where retrieval should fail: held-out scenes or object positions, or closed-loop rollouts where the policy's own errors push it off the demonstrated states. That's where a model using vision and language should beat a state-space lookup. Without a robot or simulator, I made the system report rollout as unavailable rather than imply it. The value of the project at this stage is that the evaluation harness told me the truth, including that the baseline is this strong."

Why that answer lands: it concedes exactly what the evidence supports, names the experiment that would change the conclusion, and reframes the project's value around rigorous measurement, which is defensible, rather than model performance, which isn't yet.

Module 7 Summary — the one page to revise

What this module is. Five functions over the engine's Evaluator: score baselines through the identical call, then decompose test error into tails, per-episode, per-phase and named slices, each resolved only from real metadata. The decompositions are right; the slicing re-runs inference, and nothing carries uncertainty.

Five claims to make cold

  1. Baselines go through the same Evaluator.evaluate call. Mean-action is about 1.0 MSE in normalized units; nearest-neighbour is state-only retrieval.
  2. SmolVLA ties nearest-neighbour on SO-100 (0.0239 vs 0.0239) and loses on ALOHA (0.216 vs 0.106). Offline, state nearly determines action on single-setup data.
  3. Tails and phases are reported, not just means. Continued training cut the worst frame by 32%, and mid-episode error is about 3× start and end error.
  4. Each slice is a fresh stochastic pass, so slices don't reconcile with overall. The fix is to predict once and slice outcomes.
  5. With 5 test episodes and 1–2 per length slice, slice-level claims are below resolution, and there's no bootstrap or multi-seed interval.

Decisions

ChoseOverBecauseCost
Same evaluator for baselinesseparate baseline scoringcomparable ratiosinputs still differ (state vs vision and language)
Slices from real metadata only; reject othersproxy "variation" slicesrefuse to fabricateonly task and length available
Slice by re-calling evaluateslice stored outcomesreuses the evaluator's metric codeabout 3× inference; non-reconciling stochastic slices
Within-split length tercilesdataset-level cut pointsadapts to the splitslices move across runs; differ from intervention buckets
Per-frame MAE as the error scalarper-dimension MSEone scalar per frameper-episode "mse" is misnamed
Re-read frame_index per episodeadd frame_index to outcomesno evaluator changeassumes contiguous indices

Volunteer unprompted: the redundant per_task on SO-100 and the accidental 5% noise estimate; the "long episodes are easier" overreach; no intervals; the misnamed per-episode MSE; METRICS.md's ratio claim.

Numbers and names: SO-100 test 1,908 frames from 5 episodes; ALOHA 4,400 frames from 4 episodes (all 1,100 frames); SmolVLA/mean-action 3.2% → 2.6% (SO-100), 21.4% (ALOHA); NN 0.0239 / 0.1062; mean-action 0.908 / 1.010; per-episode MAE 0.085–0.111 (SO-100), 0.296–0.405 (ALOHA); temporal 0.051 start / 0.144 mid / 0.049 end; re-scoring noise up to 5%; 10 progress buckets; terciles at the 33.3/66.7 percentiles, >= 3 episodes required; NN confidence–error correlation ≈ −0.79 (METRICS.md).

Sentences worth memorizing

  • "Offline, on single-setup data, the robot's state almost determines the next action; that's why a state-space lookup ties a VLA."
  • "The harness's job was to tell me the truth, and it did: the baseline was that strong."
  • "Every slice re-ran a stochastic model, so my slices didn't add up to my overall number. Predict once, slice the outcomes."
  • "Five test episodes can't support slice-level claims; I'd want a bootstrap over episodes before saying long episodes are easier."

Module 8 — The feedback artifact: closing the loop

Files covered: src/model_training/feedback/artifact.py (feedback/__init__.py was covered in Module 1)

Where we are: Module 7 produced test_overall, a per-frame record of how the selected checkpoint did on the test split. This module turns that into the project's "flywheel" output: a structured artifact that ranks failing episodes, offers candidate explanations, and recommends the next data intervention. README Figure 1's loop arrow ("the feedback artifact's recommended action informs the next data intervention") begins here.

The one-line thesis: The artifact is a thin, honest-sounding wrapper over the engine's FeedbackLoop. Its percentile-relative failure threshold guarantees roughly 10% of frames always "fail", so with this repo's test sizes it can't recommend "no action", and it essentially can't produce its "data defect" explanation. Worse, it runs on the test split, so following its advice and re-scoring on the same test set turns test into a tuning set.

8.1 Prerequisites — the absolute basics

  • Data flywheel. The loop deploy (or evaluate) → find failures → fix the data → retrain → re-evaluate. Each turn is supposed to improve the model through better data.
  • Failure detection by threshold. A frame "fails" if its error exceeds a threshold. Absolute threshold: error > 0.1. Relative (percentile) threshold: error above the 90th percentile of this run's errors. A relative threshold always flags about the same fraction of frames, by definition, however good or bad the model is.
  • Episode nomination. Aggregating frame-level failures into a per-episode review: failure rate (flagged frames ÷ evaluated frames in that episode), mean error, and a priority score.
  • Data defect vs model limitation. Two different causes of failure. A defect is a corrupted or mislabelled demonstration, so fix the data. A limitation is a regime the model hasn't learned, so get more coverage or a better model. The project's rule, stated in the code, is "a model failure does not automatically imply bad data."
  • Adaptive overfitting to the test set (the "garden of forking paths"). Every decision you make by looking at test results, such as which intervention to run next, makes subsequent test results optimistically biased. The test set has become part of your tuning loop. The textbook rule: decisions use validation, and test is touched once, at the end.
  • Quarantine. Marking an episode for human review without deleting it.

8.2 The code

def build_feedback_artifact(experiment_id, model_id, dataset_id, dataset_version, result, *,
                            settings=None, catalog=None,
                            error_percentile: float = 90.0, min_failures_per_episode: int = 3) -> dict:
    """Detect failures via the real flywheel, then package the structured artifact."""
    loop = FeedbackLoop(settings=settings, catalog=catalog)
    report = loop.detect(result, error_percentile=error_percentile,
                         min_failures_per_episode=min_failures_per_episode, persist=True)
    selection = loop.build_retraining_selection(report)

Decision: wrap the engine's FeedbackLoop instead of reimplementing detection.

  • Why: the module docstring says the engine's detection and nomination logic is reused, and this module adds "what that module deliberately leaves to a human: candidate explanations ... and a concrete recommended next action". It's a single implementation of detection.
  • Cost: every important number (the threshold, failure_rate, priority) is computed in code I can't read here. The behaviour below is derived from docs/FAILURE_ANALYSIS.md §2 and the explanation strings, so it's partly unverified.

persist=True: evaluation writes to the shared catalog. Unlike every Evaluator call in Modules 6 and 7 (persist=False), this one persists the failure report into the engine. So every SmolVLA run mutates the engine's state as a side effect of evaluation. That's intended (it's how a curator later finds flagged episodes with rde tools), but it means:

  • re-running an experiment writes a second set of failure records for the same episodes;
  • a crashed run after this point leaves engine records with no completed run directory pointing at them (Module 3's non-transactional index, again);
  • mtrain evaluate re-scores a run without re-running feedback (Module 12), so after a re-evaluation, feedback_artifact.json and the engine's records describe the old predictions.

error_percentile=90.0 and min_failures_per_episode=3 are hard-coded defaults. No config field reaches them. The runner calls this with defaults only (Module 11).

Why a relative threshold, and what it costs

FAILURE_ANALYSIS.md §2 defends the choice: "against percentiles computed from the current evaluation run, not a fixed absolute threshold; a fixed threshold would flag either everything or nothing the moment the model or the normalization scheme changes." That's a real argument: an absolute 0.1 would flag about half the frames for SmolVLA on SO-100 (README: 49.8–56.9% within tolerance) and nearly all of them on ALOHA (0.05%).

The cost is arithmetic. At the 90th percentile, by definition about 10% of evaluated frames are flagged, in every run, for every model.

  • SO-100 test: 1,908 frames, so about 190 flagged frames spread over 5 episodes of about 380 frames each.
  • An episode is reviewed if it has at least 3 flagged frames. With about 190 flagged frames across 5 episodes, every episode will almost certainly have 3 or more. So report.reviews is non-empty in essentially every real run.
  • Therefore the else branch ("no episode crossed the failure-concentration threshold ... no evidence") and the recommendation "no action indicated by this evaluation." are effectively unreachable on this project's datasets. The artifact will recommend an intervention for a perfect model too, because a perfect model's top 10% of errors are still "failures".

The two explanations, and why one almost never fires

if report.reviews:
    concentrated = [r for r in report.reviews if r.failure_rate > 0.5]
    if concentrated:
        candidate_explanations.append(
            f"{len(concentrated)} episode(s) fail on more than half their evaluated frames -- "
            "concentrated, episode-local failure is evidence worth checking for a data/collection "
            "defect ... (a model failure does not automatically imply bad data).")
    scattered = [r for r in report.reviews if r.failure_rate <= 0.5]
    if scattered:
        candidate_explanations.append(
            f"{len(scattered)} episode(s) fail on a minority of their evaluated frames -- more "
            "consistent with a model/coverage limitation ... than an episode-specific data defect.")

The epistemic framing is good: concentrated failure suggests a defect, scattered failure suggests a limitation, and neither is asserted. FAILURE_ANALYSIS.md §5 says "Neither pattern is presented as a conclusion". That's the right posture, and it's worth quoting.

But combine failure_rate > 0.5 with a 90th-percentile threshold. If failure_rate is flagged ÷ evaluated frames for that episode (which the explanation text, "fail on more than half their evaluated frames", implies), then the maximum failure rate any episode can reach is about

min(1, 0.10 × total_frames / episode_frames) ≈ 0.10 × 1,908 / 380 ≈ 0.50 on SO-100,

and that only happens if every flagged frame in the whole test set lands in that one episode. On ALOHA (4 episodes of 1,100 frames), it's 0.10 × 4,400 / 1,100 = 0.40. So "concentrated" (> 0.5) is mathematically unreachable on ALOHA and needs a perfect worst case on SO-100. The "data defect" explanation can, in practice, never be produced here. The artifact will say "scattered → model/coverage limitation" every time. That isn't evidence. It's a consequence of choosing p90 and 0.5 together. (It's reachable only when an episode is much shorter than average, or when there are many episodes and one is truly anomalous.)

Confirmed by the README's own numbers (added during the Data Provenance module). README §2.4 reports that on the 2,000-step SO-100 run the p90 rule flags 191 of 1,908 test frames (that's 10.0%, as the arithmetic predicts), "every test episode contains some of them", and no episode exceeds 13.8% (range 6.5–13.8%), so the artifact says "scattered". On ALOHA, 440 of 4,400 are flagged, and the worst episodes reach 22.6% and 12.1%. Both datasets sit far below the 0.5 cut, exactly as the bound says they must. The README presents "scattered → model or coverage limitation" as a finding. It's the only output these thresholds can produce at these sizes.

The two thresholds should be expressed relative to each other. For example, call an episode concentrated if its failure rate exceeds k× the expected base rate of (100 − p)/100, or test it with a binomial test against that base rate. That's the one-line fix that makes both branches reachable.

The recommendation is a template

recommended = (
    "no action indicated by this evaluation."
    if not report.reviews else
    "inspect the top-ranked episode(s) below for corruption/ambiguity before assuming a data fix "
    "will help; if the pattern holds and traces to a real shared attribute (task, length bucket), "
    "build a targeted correction with "
    "`mtrain build-intervention --kind rebalanced --variable <attribute> --variable-value <value>` "
    "and retrain, then compare against this run on the same fixed test slice."
)
  • It's one fixed string with placeholders. Nothing analyses the reviews to see whether failures share a task or length bucket, or names the attribute and value. FAILURE_ANALYSIS.md §6 says the field "names a concrete next step whenever the evidence supports one". It names the same step, with <attribute> and <value> left blank, whenever any episode is reviewed, which (above) is always. The recommendation is good advice; it isn't output.
  • "Compare against this run on the same fixed test slice" is impossible for any excluding intervention (Module 4: the test set moves). For rebalanced, the recommended kind, the test set plausibly is identical, because rebalancing excludes nothing. So the specific advice is internally consistent. That's worth noticing: rebalancing is the only intervention the loop can evaluate fairly.
  • "Inspect ... before assuming a data fix will help" puts a human gate on the loop, matching README Figure 1's dashed "decision taken by a person" arrow. Good.

The returned artifact

return {
    "experiment_id": ..., "model_id": ..., "dataset_id": ..., "dataset_version": ...,
    "affected_slice": {"split": result.split, "frames_evaluated": result.frames_evaluated},
    "failure_summary": report.summary(),
    "ranked_episode_reviews": [r.to_dict() for r in report.reviews],
    "candidate_explanations": candidate_explanations,
    "recommended_next_action": recommended,
    "retraining_selection": selection,
    "rde_artifacts": report.artifacts,
}
  • affected_slice is always the whole evaluated split ("test"), never a slice. The name promises a localization the code doesn't do.
  • retraining_selection comes from the engine's build_retraining_selection(report). By FAILURE_ANALYSIS.md §5, flagged episodes are "upweighted -- never dropped -- via the episode_weights mechanism", so this is presumably a weights proposal. Nothing in this repo consumes it. mtrain build-intervention computes weights from --variable and --variable-value, not from this field (Module 12). The engine's own nominated selection is computed, stored and ignored.
  • The integration test (test_prediction_to_evaluator_to_metrics_to_feedback_artifact) checks shape only: every ranked episode ID is real, and recommended_next_action exists. It doesn't check that either explanation branch can fire, which is exactly the gap above.

8.3 The methodological problem: the loop runs on test

The runner calls build_feedback_artifact(..., test_overall, ...) (Module 11), on the test split's outcomes. The loop the docs describe (FAILURE_ANALYSIS.md §6) is:

train → evaluate on TEST → feedback picks a weak regime FROM TEST → rebalance → retrain → compare on TEST

Each turn of that loop chooses the next dataset by looking at test errors, then judges the choice on the same test errors. With 5 test episodes, an intervention tuned to the specific episodes that happened to fail in test will tend to look like an improvement on those same episodes, whether or not it generalizes. This is adaptive overfitting, and it's the single most important methodological correction in the project. Training-time selection is strict about val (Module 6), but data-curation selection isn't.

The fix keeps everything else: run build_feedback_artifact on a validation evaluation (a full val pass of the selected checkpoint, scored once). Drive interventions from val failures, and keep test untouched until a final comparison. If the val split is too small to localize failures (5 episodes), that's itself the finding: the dataset is too small to run a data flywheel on and also keep an honest test set.

8.4 Docs vs code in FAILURE_ANALYSIS.md

ClaimReality in the code
§4: "predictions are deterministic given the checkpoint and the frame, they are reproducible on demand"False for SmolVLA. README §2.3 says flow-matching sampling makes re-scoring differ by up to 5%. The doc's justification for not storing predicted and ground-truth action vectors rests on a false premise. Re-running mtrain evaluate gives different predictions.
§3: per-action-dimension error is "surfaced per episode via episode_metrics"episode_metrics returns mae/mse/rmse/max_error per episode, with no per-dimension output (Module 7). Per-dimension error is in each frame's per_dim_error in test_outcomes.jsonl, but nothing aggregates it per episode.
§3: highest-error episodes come from "episode_metrics, sorted by mean squared error"episode_metrics sorts by episode ID (sorted(by_ep.items())).
§3 Table 1 lists stratification dimensions "this project adds"None of them appear in the feedback artifact. They're separate fields of test_overall.json.
§6: the recommendation "names a concrete next step whenever the evidence supports one"A fixed template with blank placeholders, emitted whenever any episode is reviewed (effectively always).
§2: SmolVLA's constant confidence gives confidence_used: falseEngine-side; unverifiable here. It's consistent with model_card.py's "confidence is an uncalibrated constant".

8.5 Honest gaps to own before an interviewer finds them

  1. The loop runs on test. Failures that drive the next intervention come from the test split, and the intervention is then judged on the same test split. Consequence: adaptive overfitting. Data curation tunes on test.
  2. "No action" is effectively unreachable. A 90th-percentile threshold always flags about 10% of frames, which spread across 4–5 episodes always exceed 3 per episode. Consequence: the artifact recommends an intervention for any model, including a perfect one.
  3. The "concentrated / data defect" branch is mathematically near-unreachable (maximum per-episode failure rate ≈ 0.50 on SO-100 and 0.40 on ALOHA, versus a > 0.5 rule). Consequence: the artifact always says "scattered → model limitation", by construction, not by evidence.
  4. The recommendation is a static template with unfilled <attribute>/<value> placeholders. Consequence: "names a concrete next step" overclaims; no analysis links reviews to attributes.
  5. FAILURE_ANALYSIS.md claims predictions are deterministic. They're stochastic. Consequence: the stated reason for not persisting predicted and target vectors is false, and "re-run to reproduce" doesn't reproduce.
  6. Several FAILURE_ANALYSIS.md §3 claims don't match episode_metrics (no per-dimension output, sorted by ID). Consequence: doc overclaims.
  7. persist=True writes failure records into the engine on every run, with no dedupe and no refresh on mtrain evaluate. Consequence: engine records go stale or duplicate relative to the run directory.
  8. retraining_selection is computed and never consumed. Consequence: the engine's nominated weights are dead output, and build-intervention ignores them.
  9. affected_slice is always the whole split. Consequence: the field name overclaims localization.
  10. Thresholds (error_percentile, min_failures_per_episode) aren't configurable. Consequence: you can't tune them per dataset without a code change.
  11. The integration test checks shape, not that either branch is reachable. Consequence: gaps 2–3 are invisible to the suite.

The shape to carry into Module 9

 test_overall (EvaluationResult on TEST)  ← should be VAL for curation decisions
        │
        ▼
 rde FeedbackLoop.detect(p90, min 3/episode, persist=True)  ──▶ engine failure records (side effect)
        │   ~10% of frames flagged by definition
        ▼
 report.reviews (failure_rate, mean error, priority)    loop.build_retraining_selection → (unused)
        │
        ├─ failure_rate > 0.5 → "data defect?"     ← max ≈ 0.50 (SO-100) / 0.40 (ALOHA): ~never
        ├─ failure_rate ≤ 0.5 → "model/coverage limitation"   ← always
        └─ recommended = fixed template "build-intervention --kind rebalanced --variable <…>"
        ▼
 failures/feedback_artifact.json ──▶ model card "Known failure modes", report, human decision
                                        │ (dashed arrow: a person picks the next intervention)
                                        ▼
                               Module 4: next training/vN

Drill — answer from memory, files closed

  1. Why does the feedback loop use a percentile threshold instead of an absolute one, and what does that choice make impossible?
  2. Show, with numbers, why the "concentrated failure" explanation essentially never appears on SO-100 or ALOHA.
  3. Why didn't you just run failure analysis on the test set? That's where the final model is evaluated.
  4. What breaks in production: you run the loop five times (feedback, rebalance, retrain, compare) and report the fifth model's test MSE. What's wrong with that number?
  5. Interviewer framing: "Walk me through your data flywheel. Does it work?"

Appendix A — Model answers to the drill

A1. Percentile vs absolute. "An absolute threshold depends on the model and the normalization. At 0.1 mean absolute error, SmolVLA would flag about half the SO-100 frames and nearly all of ALOHA's, so the flag would carry no information about which frames are unusually bad. A 90th-percentile threshold adapts to the run and always isolates the worst tenth. The cost is that it can never say 'nothing is wrong'. Ten percent of frames fail by definition, for a terrible model and a perfect one alike. With 1,900 test frames over five episodes, every episode gets well over the three-failure minimum, so the 'no action' branch is effectively dead and the artifact always recommends an intervention. I'd keep the relative threshold for ranking, and add an absolute or baseline-relative gate for whether to act, for example only recommending action if the flagged frames' error is worse than the nearest-neighbour baseline's on the same frames."

Why that answer lands: it defends the original choice with real numbers, then names the exact capability the choice destroys, and proposes a two-threshold fix that keeps the benefit.

A2. Concentrated failure is unreachable. "At p90, about 10% of all test frames are flagged: about 190 of SO-100's 1,908. An episode is about 380 frames. Even if every one of the 190 flagged frames landed in a single episode, its failure rate would be 190 / 380, which is 0.5, and the rule needs strictly more than 0.5. On ALOHA it's 440 flagged frames over 1,100-frame episodes, so at most 0.4. So the explanation that points at a data defect can't fire, and every run reports 'scattered failure, consistent with a model limitation'. That sounds like evidence, but it's a consequence of picking p90 and 0.5 independently. The fix is to define 'concentrated' relative to the 10% base rate, for example with a binomial test per episode."

Why that answer lands: it's a clean back-of-envelope proof that a code path is dead. Few things are more convincing in an interview than showing a branch can't execute.

A3. Why not run it on test? "Because the feedback artifact decides what to do next, and anything that makes decisions has to use data you're not going to report on. If test failures pick which regime to upweight and test then judges whether the upweighting helped, the test set has become a tuning set. With five test episodes, an intervention fit to the particular episodes that failed will look like an improvement on exactly those episodes. My training loop is strict about this, since checkpoint selection only ever sees val, but the data-curation loop isn't, and that's the most important methodological fix in the project. Run the feedback on a full val evaluation and keep test for the final comparison. If val is too small to localize failures, that tells me the dataset is too small for a flywheel and an honest test set."

Why that answer lands: it identifies adaptive overfitting precisely, contrasts it with the place the project gets it right, and turns the small-data constraint into an insight instead of an excuse.

A4. Five turns of the loop. "The fifth model's test MSE is optimistically biased, because each of the five interventions was chosen by looking at test failures and kept or rejected by test MSE. That's five rounds of selection on a five-episode test set, and the reported number is the winner of a search. It isn't an estimate of performance on new episodes. On top of that, the test set may not even be the same across those runs: rebalancing keeps it fixed, but any intervention that excludes episodes reshuffles the split. To report honestly, I'd either hold out a fresh test set that the loop never saw, or report the fifth model alongside the first on a split none of the decisions touched, with a bootstrap interval over episodes."

Why that answer lands: it names selection bias over repeated rounds, connects it to the moving-test-set issue from another module, and gives an actionable reporting protocol.

A5. "Walk me through your data flywheel. Does it work?" "Mechanically, yes: every stage is a real persisted artifact. A run's test predictions go through the engine's failure detector, which flags the top 10% of frames, rolls them up into ranked episode reviews, and writes them to the catalog for curators. My layer adds candidate explanations, careful not to equate model failure with bad data, plus a recommended next step: rebalance toward a shared attribute and retrain. That becomes a new, lineage-tracked dataset version. As a decision procedure, it doesn't work yet, for three reasons. It always recommends action, because the percentile threshold always flags 10%. The 'data defect' explanation can't fire at these test sizes. And it runs on test, so following it overfits the test set. I'd move it onto validation, add an absolute or baseline-relative gate for whether to act, and make the concentration test relative to the base rate. The plumbing is the hard part, and that's done; the decision logic needs those three changes."

Why that answer lands: it separates "the plumbing works" from "the decisions are sound", gives specific reasons for the second, and ends with a clear, bounded fix list. That's credible, not defensive.

Module 8 Summary — the one page to revise

What this module is. A wrapper that sends test-set outcomes through the engine's FeedbackLoop (p90 threshold, at least 3 failures per episode, persisted to the catalog), then attaches templated explanations ("concentrated → check data" or "scattered → model limitation") and a fixed recommendation to rebalance and retrain. The plumbing is sound; the decision logic is degenerate at these data sizes and runs on the wrong split.

Five claims to make cold

  1. Detection is the engine's. This layer adds explanations framed so that "model failure does not imply bad data."
  2. A p90 threshold flags about 10% of frames by definition, so "no action" is effectively unreachable.
  3. Concentrated (> 0.5) can't occur: the maximum is about 0.50 (SO-100) and 0.40 (ALOHA).
  4. Feedback runs on test, so the curation loop tunes on test. Move it to val.
  5. Rebalancing is the one intervention that keeps the test set fixed, so it's the only one the loop can evaluate fairly.

Decisions

ChoseOverBecauseCost
Wrap engine FeedbackLoopreimplementsingle detection implementationthresholds and metrics unverifiable here
Percentile threshold (p90)absolute thresholdadapts to model and normalizationcan never say "nothing is wrong"
Fixed 0.5 concentration cutbase-rate-relative testsimpleunreachable at p90 with 4–5 episodes
Templated explanation and recommendationattribute analysis of reviewshonest hedging, a human in the loopno concrete attribute or value produced
persist=Truein-memory onlycurators find flags via rdeside effects, duplicates, stale after re-evaluation
Run on test outcomesrun on valtest is what's evaluated at the endadaptive overfitting of test

Volunteer unprompted: the test-set loop; both dead branches, with the arithmetic; FAILURE_ANALYSIS.md's "deterministic predictions" claim contradicts README §2.3; retraining_selection is unused.

Numbers and names: error_percentile = 90.0, min_failures_per_episode = 3, concentration > 0.5; SO-100 1,908 frames, 5 episodes, about 190 flagged, max rate ≈ 0.50; ALOHA 4,400 frames, 4 × 1,100, max rate 0.40; priority = failure_rate × (1 + mean_error) per FAILURE_ANALYSIS.md; output at failures/feedback_artifact.json.

Sentences worth memorizing

  • "A percentile threshold is good for ranking failures and useless for deciding whether there are any."
  • "My training loop never selects on test; my data-curation loop did, and that's the fix I'd make first."
  • "At p90 with five test episodes, 'concentrated failure' can't happen. The branch is dead by arithmetic."
  • "The plumbing of the flywheel is real and persisted; the decision logic on top of it needs to move to validation and get an absolute gate."

Module 9 — Model artifact, registry and deployment loader

Files covered: src/model_training/deployment/artifact.py, src/model_training/deployment/registry.py, src/model_training/deployment/loader.py (deployment/__init__.py was covered in Module 1)

Where we are: Modules 6–8 produced a selected checkpoint, its test metrics and a feedback artifact. This module answers "what exactly is this checkpoint, and how would I run it?" build_model_artifact packages its identity, ModelRegistry is meant to let you look it up, and DeployedModel is meant to load and serve it with health checks and latency timing. The README and docs/DEPLOYMENT.md present this as the project's deployment layer.

The one-line thesis: The artifact is a good identity record, and it prefers the checkpoint's own claims about its training data. But the "deployment" layer is never executed by the pipeline, its health check never compares the checkpoint to the contract despite its docstring, it checks normalization on the object inference doesn't use, and it serves dataset Sample objects, not robot observations. It's a replay harness, not a deployment.

9.1 Prerequisites — the absolute basics

  • Model artifact. A trained model packaged with the metadata needed to use and trust it: weights, configuration, input/output schema, training data identity, code version, dependencies and metrics.
  • Model registry. A catalogue of artifacts that answers "which model is this?" and, in mature systems (MLflow Model Registry, SageMaker), "which version is promoted to staging or production?", through stages, aliases and transitions. Lookup alone is a catalogue. Promotion is what makes it a registry.
  • Pre- and postprocessor. The input normalization and tokenization before the network, and the action de-normalization after it. In LeRobot-style checkpoints these are saved as separate files (policy_preprocessor.json and policy_postprocessor.json, per docs/DEPLOYMENT.md Table 1), because the normalization statistics live there, not in the weights.
  • Health check / readiness probe. Verifying, before serving, that a model can actually serve the inputs it will get. The valuable checks are compatibility checks (does this model's expected input match what I'll feed it?), not just presence checks.
  • Training/serving skew. Any difference between how inputs were processed at training time and at serving time (different normalization stats, camera order, image size). It's the most common silent production ML failure.
  • Action chunking. SmolVLA predicts a sequence of future actions per call. A robot controller executes some of the chunk, then re-queries.
  • Latency measurement on a GPU. CUDA calls are asynchronous: Python returns before the GPU finishes. Timing with time.perf_counter() measures real GPU work only if something forces a synchronization (torch.cuda.synchronize(), or copying results to the CPU). The first call also includes one-off warm-up costs (kernel selection, memory allocation).

9.2 build_model_artifact

@dataclass
class ModelArtifact:
    model_id: str; model_version: str; artifact_path: str; architecture: str
    pretrained_model: str; training_run_id: str; experiment_id: str
    dataset_id: str; dataset_version: str
    observation_schema: dict; action_schema: dict; embodiment: str | None
    dependencies: dict; git_commit: dict; created_at: str; metrics: dict

def build_model_artifact(*, checkpoint_path, contract, run_dir_path, experiment_id, tags,
                         dataset_id, dataset_version, environment, git_info, metrics) -> ModelArtifact:
    """Assemble a ModelArtifact from a checkpoint already saved by
    SmolVLAPolicy.save_checkpoint -- verified, not assumed complete."""
    meta = SmolVLAPolicy.verify_checkpoint(checkpoint_path)
    model_id = f"{tags.get('model', 'smolvla')}-{experiment_id}"
    return ModelArtifact(
        model_id=model_id,
        model_version=str(meta.get("step", "final")),
        artifact_path=str(checkpoint_path),
        architecture="smolvla",
        pretrained_model=meta.get("checkpoint_source", ""),
        training_run_id=str(run_dir_path),
        experiment_id=experiment_id,
        dataset_id=meta.get("dataset_id", dataset_id),
        dataset_version=meta.get("dataset_version", dataset_version),
        observation_schema=contract.get("observation", {}),
        action_schema=contract.get("action", {}),
        embodiment=tags.get("embodiment"),
        dependencies={"torch": environment.get("torch"), "lerobot": environment.get("lerobot")},
        git_commit=git_info,
        created_at=_dt.datetime.now(_dt.timezone.utc).isoformat(),
        metrics=metrics,
    )

What's good:

  • It verifies the selected checkpoint. This is the only place the final checkpoint is ever verified, when it's the fallback selection (Module 6). So an incomplete selected checkpoint fails here, before the artifact is registered.
  • The dataset identity comes from the checkpoint's own metadata first. meta.get("dataset_id", dataset_id) prefers what Trainer stamped into the checkpoint at save time (extra={"dataset_id": ..., "dataset_version": ...}, Module 6) over what the caller says. The checkpoint is the authority on what it was trained on. That's the right provenance direction, and it would catch a caller passing the wrong version, though only by silently preferring the checkpoint's answer, not by flagging the disagreement.
  • Keyword-only arguments (*) make an eleven-field constructor call impossible to get wrong by position.

What's weaker:

  • metrics are the test metrics (test_overall.metrics in the runner, Module 11). docs/DEPLOYMENT.md Table 2 and the registry docstring both say row["metrics"] answers "What metrics justified selection?". Selection was justified by the validation metric (Module 6), which isn't in the artifact at all. Putting test metrics under that label invites exactly the confusion the project otherwise works hard to avoid.
  • model_id = f"{tags['model']}-{experiment_id}" takes its prefix from a free-form tag. experiment_id is unique per load (Module 2), so IDs don't collide, but the prefix is cosmetic and can disagree with architecture (hard-coded "smolvla").
  • model_version = str(meta.get("step", "final")). The final checkpoint is saved with "step": fit_result["steps"] (Module 6), so this is always a step number and the "final" default is unreachable for this codebase's checkpoints. "Version" here means "training step", not a registry version.
  • pretrained_model = meta["checkpoint_source"]. For a resumed run (baseline_extended), this is plausibly the resume path (the source the policy was constructed from), not lerobot/smolvla_base. That's engine-side and unverified. If so, the lineage of weights is one hop deep: you'd follow resume_source.json (Module 6) by hand to get to the base model.
  • observation_schema and action_schema come from the view's contract, not the checkpoint, so the artifact can't show a mismatch between them either.
  • dependencies records only torch and lerobot. It misses transformers and safetensors, as Module 3 found for environment.json.

9.3 ModelRegistry

class ModelRegistry:
    def __init__(self, path: Path):
        self._index = JsonlIndex(Path(path))

    def register(self, artifact: ModelArtifact) -> None:
        self._index.append(artifact.to_dict())

    def get(self, model_id: str) -> dict[str, Any] | None:
        """The most recent record for ``model_id``. ... returning the first match
        returned the oldest, stalest one. Later rows supersede earlier ones for the same id."""
        row = None
        for row in self._index.query(model_id=model_id):
            pass
        return row

    def list(self) -> list[dict[str, Any]]:
        return self._index.read_all()

    def for_experiment(self, experiment_id: str) -> list[dict[str, Any]]:
        return list(self._index.query(experiment_id=experiment_id))
  • "Last write wins" over an append-only log is the correct read semantics, and the docstring records the bug it fixed (the earlier version returned the first match, which was the stalest). The for row in ...: pass idiom is terse: it leaves the loop variable bound to the last yielded row, or None if there were none. Some reviewers will find it too clever. rows = list(...); return rows[-1] if rows else None reads more plainly.
  • It isn't wired in. docs/DEPLOYMENT.md §7 says so honestly: "ExperimentRunner.run writes each model record directly through ExperimentIndex.record_model ... and never calls ModelRegistry.register." Grep confirms that ModelRegistry appears only in deployment/__init__.py, registry.py and test_deployment.py. It's compatible with the runner's file, because record_model(**model_artifact.to_dict()) writes the same row shape, so ModelRegistry("experiments/models.jsonl").get(...) would work. It's just never constructed outside the test.
  • It's a catalogue, not a registry. There's no stage, alias, promotion, or "which model is deployed" field. The question a registry exists to answer, "what's in production?", has no representation. For a project with no production, that's honest scope. Just don't call it a registry in the MLflow sense.
  • It inherits every JsonlIndex weakness (Module 3): a full scan per lookup, no locking, and one torn line breaks get and list for good.
  • The one test (test_model_registry_round_trips) covers register, get, for_experiment and the missing-ID case. It doesn't cover the "most recent wins" fix that the docstring narrates. A two-row test would pin it.

9.4 DeployedModel: what it checks, and what it doesn't

class DeploymentHealthError(Exception):
    """Raised by DeployedModel.health_check on any checkpoint/contract
    mismatch, detected before inference rather than surfacing as a confusing
    tensor-shape error mid-prediction."""
def health_check(self) -> dict[str, Any]:
    """Everything that must be verified before inference. ..."""
    meta = SmolVLAPolicy.verify_checkpoint(self.checkpoint_path)  # raises on incomplete/foreign dir

    action = self.contract.get("action", {})
    if not action.get("shape"):
        raise DeploymentHealthError("dataset contract has no action shape -- cannot verify compatibility")

    obs = self.contract.get("observation", {})
    images = [k for k, v in obs.items() if v.get("plane") == "external"]
    if not images:
        raise DeploymentHealthError("dataset contract has no image modality -- SmolVLA requires at least one camera")
    for k in images:
        shape = obs[k].get("shape")
        if not shape or len(shape) != 3:
            raise DeploymentHealthError(f"camera {k!r} has no valid (H, W, C) shape in the contract: {shape}")

    state_keys = [k for k, v in obs.items() if v.get("plane") == "inline"]
    for k in state_keys:
        if not obs[k].get("normalization"):
            raise DeploymentHealthError(
                f"state modality {k!r} carries no normalization statistics in the dataset contract -- "
                "the checkpoint's preprocessor expects normalized state and per-run normalization is missing")

    return {"status": "ok", "checkpoint_meta": meta, "cameras": images, "state_keys": state_keys}

Read it adversarially. meta is loaded, and then never used again except to be returned. Every other check looks at self.contract alone. So:

Promised (docstrings, DEPLOYMENT.md §4)Actually checked
"any checkpoint/contract mismatch"No comparison is made. It checks the checkpoint for completeness and the contract for well-formedness, independently.
"cannot verify compatibility" (the action-shape error text)Only that a shape exists, not that it equals the checkpoint's action dimension, which rde_checkpoint_meta.json carries per DEPLOYMENT.md Table 1 ("action dimensionality").
SmolVLA needs camerasThat at least one camera exists with a 3-dimensional shape. Not that the camera count or names match what the checkpoint was trained with.
state normalization is presentPresent in the contract. But see below: inference doesn't use it.

A concrete passing mismatch. Load an ALOHA checkpoint (14-dimensional action, 4 cameras, 28-dimensional state) with the SO-100 contract (6-dimensional action, 2 cameras, 6-dimensional state). Every check passes: the checkpoint is complete, and the contract has an action shape, 3-dimensional cameras and normalized state. health_check returns {"status": "ok"}. Then predict fails mid-inference with exactly the "confusing tensor-shape error mid-prediction" the exception class says it exists to prevent. Or, worse, if the shapes happen to line up, it runs and produces nonsense. The test suite proves the gap: _make_checkpoint writes metadata with "dataset_id": "d" and an empty model.safetensors (write_bytes(b"")), and test_health_check_passes_on_well_formed_checkpoint_and_contract passes it against an SO-100-shaped contract.

The normalization check is on the wrong object. load() constructs the policy with SmolVLAPolicy.from_checkpoint(...), which per docs/API_REFERENCE.md "loads with contract=None". The checkpoint uses its own saved preprocessor statistics. DEPLOYMENT.md §5 confirms that predict "applies the checkpoint's own preprocessor (normalization and tokenization)". So the contract's normalization statistics, the only thing the health check inspects, aren't used at inference at all. The real training/serving-skew risk is the opposite one: the checkpoint's stats differing from the live data's distribution. That would need a comparison of the checkpoint preprocessor's mean and std against the contract's, and that comparison isn't made.

The fix is cheap, because the metadata already exists:

if meta.get("action_dim") not in (None, action["shape"][0]):
    raise DeploymentHealthError(f"checkpoint action_dim={meta['action_dim']} != contract {action['shape']}")
if meta.get("dataset_id") not in (None, expected_dataset_id): ...
# and compare camera keys/count and the preprocessor's state mean/std against the contract's

(The exact metadata key names are engine-side and unverified. DEPLOYMENT.md Table 1 says the metadata includes "action dimensionality, chunk size".)

def load(self) -> "DeployedModel":
    self.health_check()
    t0 = time.perf_counter()
    self._policy = SmolVLAPolicy.from_checkpoint(self.checkpoint_path, device=self.device)
    self.load_time_ms = (time.perf_counter() - t0) * 1000.0
    return self
  • Verification runs twice, once in health_check and again inside from_checkpoint. It's cheap (file-presence checks) and harmless.
  • load_time_ms excludes the health check, and it includes weight transfer to the device. It's measured once.
  • Returning self enables DeployedModel(...).load() chaining, as in the DEPLOYMENT.md example.
  • No fallback model on failure. DEPLOYMENT.md §4 makes "no silent substitution" a stated rule. That's good.

predict and the latency breakdown

def predict(self, samples: Sequence[Sample]) -> tuple[Prediction, LatencyBreakdown]:
    if self._policy is None:
        raise RuntimeError("call load() before predict()")
    t0 = time.perf_counter()
    model_input = self._adapter.adapt(samples)
    t1 = time.perf_counter()
    prediction = self._policy.timed_predict(model_input)
    t2 = time.perf_counter()

    preprocessing_ms = (t1 - t0) * 1000.0
    inference_ms = prediction.latency_ms
    postprocessing_ms = max(0.0, (t2 - t1) * 1000.0 - inference_ms)
    latency = LatencyBreakdown(model_load_ms=self.load_time_ms, preprocessing_ms=preprocessing_ms,
                               inference_ms=inference_ms, postprocessing_ms=postprocessing_ms,
                               total_ms=preprocessing_ms + inference_ms + postprocessing_ms)
    return prediction, latency
  • The input is Sequence[Sample], an engine dataset object. Samples come from DatasetView.iter_samples, as in the integration test. A robot controller has camera frames, a joint-state vector and an instruction string, not engine Samples. So DeployedModel can only replay recorded data. It can't be dropped into a control loop without an adapter from raw observations to Sample. That's the most important framing point in this module: this is an offline inference harness with timing, not deployment.
  • "postprocessing_ms" isn't postprocessing. Per DEPLOYMENT.md §5, SmolVLAPolicy.predict applies the postprocessor (action de-normalization) inside the predict call, so it's already included in prediction.latency_ms. What's left in (t2 − t1) − inference_ms is the overhead of the timed_predict wrapper itself, which is near zero. The label promises a stage that this subtraction doesn't isolate.
  • max(0.0, …) hides clock disagreement. If inference_ms (measured inside the engine) exceeds the outer interval, the negative value is clamped to 0, and total_ms (a sum of parts, not a measured interval) can then exceed the real wall time. Measuring total_ms = (t2 − t0) × 1000 directly would make the parts auditable against the whole.
  • GPU synchronization is unverified. If timed_predict returns tensors still on the GPU without synchronizing, inference_ms measures kernel launch time. The integration test's prediction.actions.shape suggests NumPy arrays, which would force a device-to-host copy and therefore a sync, but I can't confirm that from here.
  • No warm-up, no repetition, no percentiles. DEPLOYMENT.md §6 explicitly leaves p50, p95 and p99 to the caller, as "a benchmark-design decision". That's a defensible scope line, but nothing in the repo is that caller.
  • The LatencyBreakdown.note (preprocessing isn't split into decode, resize and tensor construction) is the project's honest-metadata habit applied well. But it names VisionStateAdapter, while this class uses VisionLanguageAdapter, which is related but not the same class.
  • Action chunks: whether Prediction.actions is the first action of each chunk or the whole chunk isn't visible here. A control loop needs to know.

Nothing calls it

Grep shows DeployedModel used only in deployment/__init__.py, tests/unit/test_failure_modes.py and tests/unit/test_deployment.py (health checks only, never load or predict), and as a string inside reports/model_card.py. DeployedModel.predict has never run in any test, any script or the pipeline. The README's latency figures ("47–50 ms per frame on SO-100 and 86 ms per frame on ALOHA") come from Evaluator's latency_ms_per_frame during test evaluation (Module 7). The README correctly says they "are not a deployment latency measurement". No deployment latency has ever been measured by this code.

Doc vs doc: DEPLOYMENT.md §5 says SmolVLAPolicy.predict "invokes select_action". docs/API_REFERENCE.md §7.4 says predict "Uses predict_action_chunk ... not select_action (which maintains a stateful action queue ... reusing it across independent evaluation batches silently replays stale actions)". That's the bug the smoke test's comment describes as fixed. DEPLOYMENT.md was written before the fix and never updated. Since you can't read the engine here, cite API_REFERENCE and the smoke test as the current behaviour.

9.5 Honest gaps to own before an interviewer finds them

  1. health_check never compares the checkpoint to the contract, despite "raised on any checkpoint/contract mismatch". Consequence: an ALOHA checkpoint against an SO-100 contract passes and then fails, or silently misbehaves, mid-inference. That's the exact failure the class exists to prevent.
  2. It checks normalization on the contract, but inference uses the checkpoint's own preprocessor stats. Consequence: the check guards an unused object, and real training/serving skew between checkpoint stats and live data is unchecked.
  3. DeployedModel consumes engine Samples, not robot observations. Consequence: it's a replay harness. Deploying to a robot needs an observation adapter that doesn't exist.
  4. DeployedModel.load and predict have never been executed by any test, script or pipeline. Consequence: the deployment path is unverified code, and no deployment latency exists.
  5. postprocessing_ms measures wrapper overhead, not postprocessing; total_ms is a sum, not an interval; negatives are clamped. Consequence: the breakdown can misattribute time and overstate the total.
  6. The registry is a catalogue (no stages, promotion or "deployed" concept) and isn't wired in (honestly documented). Consequence: "model registry" overclaims relative to the industry meaning.
  7. The artifact's metrics are test metrics, labelled as "what metrics justified selection". Consequence: the validation metric that actually justified selection is absent from the artifact.
  8. model_version is always a step number, pretrained_model may be a resume path (unverified), and model_id's prefix comes from a free tag. Consequence: identity fields are less meaningful than their names suggest.
  9. DEPLOYMENT.md §5 says predict uses select_action. API_REFERENCE and the smoke test say predict_action_chunk. Consequence: a stale doc describing the pre-fix bug as current behaviour.
