  10. Health-check tests use an empty model.safetensors and mismatched metadata, and pass. Consequence: the suite encodes the presence-only behaviour as correct.
  11. The registry's "most recent wins" fix isn't tested. Consequence: a regression to first-match wouldn't be caught.
  12. Dependencies recorded: torch and lerobot only. Consequence: the artifact can't explain a transformers-driven behaviour change.

The shape to carry into Module 10

 selected checkpoint ──verify_checkpoint──▶ meta {step, checkpoint_source, dataset_id, dataset_version, ...}
        │
        ▼
 build_model_artifact  (dataset identity: checkpoint's own claim wins)
   ModelArtifact{model_id=tag-expid, version=step, schema=VIEW contract, metrics=TEST (labelled "justified selection")}
        │                               │
        ├─▶ model_artifact.json         └─▶ ExperimentIndex.record_model → experiments/models.jsonl
        │                                         ▲ same row shape
        │                                 ModelRegistry(get = last row wins)   ← never constructed by src/
        ▼
 DeployedModel(checkpoint, contract)                                             ← never constructed by src/
   health_check: ckpt files present? contract has action shape / 3-D cams / state norm?   (no cross-check!)
   load: from_checkpoint(contract=None) → uses CHECKPOINT preprocessor stats
   predict(Sequence[Sample])  ← engine samples only, not robot observations
     → Prediction + LatencyBreakdown(pre | inference | "post" = wrapper overhead | total = sum)

Drill — answer from memory, files closed

  1. What does DeployedModel.health_check actually verify, and name one incompatible checkpoint/contract pair it would pass.
  2. Why didn't you just build a FastAPI server around the model?
  3. What breaks in production: you hand DeployedModel to a robotics engineer and say "here's the deployment layer." What do they hit first?
  4. Your model card says the registry answers "what metrics justified selection." What's in that field, and is the label right?
  5. Interviewer framing: "How did you deploy this model, and what was its inference latency?"

Appendix A — Model answers to the drill

A1. What health_check verifies. "Four things: the checkpoint directory has all its required files; the contract declares an action shape; it has at least one camera with a three-dimensional shape; and every state modality in the contract has normalization stats. What it doesn't do is compare the two. It reads the checkpoint's metadata and then never uses it. So an ALOHA checkpoint with 14-dimensional actions and four cameras, checked against the SO-100 contract with 6-dimensional actions and two cameras, passes, then fails inside inference with a shape error, which is exactly what the exception class says it prevents. And the normalization check looks at the contract's stats, while inference loads the checkpoint's own preprocessor stats, so it guards something that isn't used. The fix is to compare the checkpoint's recorded action dimension, camera set and normalization stats against the contract's. All of that metadata is already saved."

Why that answer lands: it states what's checked precisely, constructs a concrete passing counterexample, and spots the wrong-object normalization check. That's an adversarial reading of your own safety code.

A2. Why not a FastAPI server? "The brief was to avoid infrastructure that doesn't earn its keep, and there's no consumer for a network endpoint. No robot or simulator is connected, so a server would only add serialization, a process boundary and failure modes. The deployment layer is deliberately a function call. If a network interface were needed, it would be a thin wrapper around predict. The honest limitation isn't the missing server, though. It's that predict takes the engine's dataset Sample objects, so even in-process it can only replay recorded data. Before any server, I'd add an adapter from raw observations (camera frames, a joint vector, an instruction) to model input, and a closed-loop harness that executes action chunks. I'd add a server only once a separate process, like a robot controller, needed to call it."

Why that answer lands: it defends the minimalism with a reason, then volunteers the real gap, which is input type, not transport, and orders the next steps correctly.

A3. A robotics engineer gets DeployedModel. "First, it doesn't accept their inputs. predict wants a sequence of engine Sample objects from a dataset view, and they have camera images and joint states from a live robot. Second, if they wrap that, they'll find it returns predictions per call with no handling of action chunks or control frequency, and SmolVLA plans in chunks. Third, the health check would pass a checkpoint trained on a different embodiment, so a wrong model reaches inference. And fourth, none of it has ever been executed: load and predict have no test, and no latency was ever measured through this class. I'd tell them it's a replay and timing harness, and that a deployment needs an observation adapter, a chunk-execution loop, a real compatibility check and a warm-up-plus-percentile benchmark."

Why that answer lands: it ranks the failures in the order they'd actually be hit, and closes with a precise, honest reclassification of what the code is.

A4. "What metrics justified selection." "That field holds the test-split metrics, the MSE and so on of the selected checkpoint on the test set. But selection was justified by the validation metric, because the trainer only ever compares checkpoints on val, and that number isn't in the artifact at all. So the label is wrong, and it's wrong in the most sensitive direction, because it invites someone to think test was used for selection. I'd store both, under 'selection: val MSE at step N' and 'reported: test metrics', and rename the registry's question."

Why that answer lands: it catches a labelling error that touches the project's core validity principle, and fixes it in a way that reinforces the val/test separation.

A5. "How did you deploy this model, and what was its inference latency?" "I didn't deploy it to a robot. There was no robot or simulator connected, and the system says so rather than reporting a rollout number. What exists is a local loader class with a health check and a per-stage latency breakdown, but that path has never been run end to end, and its health check doesn't cross-check checkpoint and contract, which I'd fix first. The latency numbers I can give are evaluation-harness numbers: about 47–50 milliseconds per frame on SO-100 and 86 on ALOHA, which has four cameras. That's prediction time divided by batch size during batched offline evaluation on a laptop RTX 4060, excluding data loading. That isn't control-loop latency. A real number would be single-sample, warmed up, synchronized, reported as p50 and p99, and measured inside a control loop that executes action chunks."

Why that answer lands: it refuses the overclaim the question invites, gives the real numbers with their exact provenance, and describes what a correct measurement would look like. Precision here is what interviewers test.

Module 9 Summary — the one page to revise

What this module is. An identity record for the selected checkpoint (provenance taken from the checkpoint's own metadata), a JSONL "registry" that's a correct but unwired catalogue, and a DeployedModel loader/predictor that's never executed, doesn't cross-check checkpoint and contract, and only accepts dataset samples.

Five claims to make cold

  1. The artifact verifies the checkpoint and prefers the checkpoint's own dataset identity over the caller's.
  2. The registry reads last-write-wins over an append-only log, isn't wired into the runner (and the docs admit it), and has no promotion concept.
  3. health_check checks presence and well-formedness, not compatibility, and its normalization check targets the contract, which inference doesn't use.
  4. DeployedModel takes engine Samples, so it's a replay harness, and it has never run.
  5. README latency (about 50 ms/frame SO-100, 86 ms ALOHA) is evaluator-batched, not deployment latency.

Decisions

ChoseOverBecauseCost
Function-call deploymentnetwork serviceno consumer; avoid infrastructurenone yet, but the input type is the real gap
Checkpoint metadata as identity authoritycaller-supplied identitythe checkpoint knows its own datadisagreement silently resolved, not flagged
JSONL registryMLflow registryconsistent with the index; no serverno stages or promotion; inherits JSONL fragility
Load with checkpoint preprocessorre-derive stats from contractavoids training/serving skew from new statsthe contract check is then irrelevant
Single preprocessing latency bucketfabricated sub-stage splitcan't measure inside the adapter"post" bucket mislabelled instead
No fallback modelload a defaultnever silently substitutenone

Volunteer unprompted: no cross-check in health_check; the wrong-object normalization check; Sample-only input; never executed; the test-metrics-as-selection label; DEPLOYMENT.md §5's stale select_action claim.

Numbers and names: 5 checkpoint files (config.json, model.safetensors, policy_preprocessor.json, policy_postprocessor.json, rde_checkpoint_meta.json); model_id = <tag model>-<experiment_id>; latency fields model_load_ms, preprocessing_ms, inference_ms, postprocessing_ms, total_ms; README evaluator latency 47–50 ms/frame (SO-100), 86 ms/frame (ALOHA), batch 16.

Sentences worth memorizing

  • "My health check verified that the checkpoint and the contract each existed, not that they agreed."
  • "It takes dataset samples, not robot observations, so it's a replay harness with timing, not a deployment."
  • "The latency I can quote is evaluator throughput per frame, not control-loop latency, and I say that every time."
  • "The registry labelled test metrics as the selection justification; selection was on validation, and the artifact should say so."

Module 10 — Reports, figures and cross-run comparison

Files covered: src/model_training/reports/experiment_report.py, src/model_training/reports/model_card.py, src/model_training/reports/comparison.py, src/model_training/reports/figures.py, scripts/plot_results.py (reports/__init__.py was covered in Module 1)

Where we are: Modules 3–9 wrote every artifact a run produces. This module turns them into what people read: an experiment report, a model card, per-run figures, a cross-run comparison table, and the README's results figure. If an earlier module is honest and this one misreports it, the honesty is lost at the last step. So this module checks presentation against source.

The one-line thesis: The report and model card follow a strong discipline: every section always renders, and anything missing says "not run" or "unavailable" instead of disappearing. But the report is built from in-memory state despite claiming otherwise, it drops a real number (this repo's git commit, SmolVLA's MAE), the comparison table has no seed aggregation despite the docs, and the README figure script hard-codes the very selection points it claims to read from disk.

10.1 Prerequisites — the absolute basics

  • Experiment report. A human-readable record of one run: hypothesis, data, configuration, results, limitations, conclusion. Here it's reports/experiment_report.md.
  • Model card. A short document attached to a trained model describing intended use, training data, evaluation results and limitations. The convention comes from Mitchell et al. (2019). Here it's reports/model_card.md.
  • "Absent vs not run". In a report, an omitted section is ambiguous: was the thing not done, or did it fail silently? Always rendering the section, with an explicit "not run", removes that ambiguity. It's the reporting version of the project's "never fabricate" rule.
  • Matplotlib backend. The rendering engine. Agg is a non-interactive raster backend that works without a display (servers, CI). matplotlib.use("Agg") sets it process-wide.
  • Figure lifecycle. Each plt.subplots() allocates a figure that stays in memory until plt.close(fig). Long loops that never close figures leak memory, and matplotlib warns after 20 open figures.
  • Reproducible figure. A figure generated by a script from stored data, so it can be regenerated identically, as opposed to a hand-edited plot. The test is whether the script contains any hard-coded number that belongs to the data.
  • Seed aggregation. Reporting mean ± standard deviation (or a confidence interval) over runs that differ only in random seed.

10.2 render_experiment_report

def render_experiment_report(ctx: dict[str, Any]) -> str:
    """``ctx`` carries everything the report cites; see ExperimentRunner for exactly what it
    assembles. Fields that were not run/available render as stated text
    ("not run", "unavailable") rather than being omitted silently."""

Decision: fixed sections, with explicit "not run" text. Every section heading (Objective, Dataset, Model, Training configuration, Compute environment, Test metrics, Baselines, Stratified, Failure analysis, Rollout, Limitations, Conclusion, Data feedback recommendation) renders whether or not it has data. A baseline-only run still shows "Test metrics ... not run" and "Closed-loop / rollout evaluation: unavailable". The runner test asserts "unavailable" in report_text (rollout must be stated, not omitted). Why: a reader can tell "absent" from "failed". Alternative: a template engine (Jinja2) with conditionals that hide empty sections. Cost of the choice: a longer report with some empty sections, which is the right trade.

Doc vs code: "assembled entirely from saved run artifacts". The module docstring says the report is "assembled entirely from saved run artifacts, never from mutable live state, so re-running this function against a finished run's directory always reproduces the same report". In fact:

  • the function takes a ctx dict built in memory by the runner from live objects (intervention_result, training_outcome, test_overall, profile, feedback_artifact, Module 11), not from files;
  • no function exists that rebuilds ctx from a run directory, and no CLI command regenerates a report;
  • ctx["generated_at"] is now(), so even an identical ctx produces a different report.

The values in ctx match what was written to disk, because they come from the same objects at the same moment. So the report is consistent with the artifacts, but it isn't derived from them. Say "built from the same in-memory results the artifacts were written from", and say that a regenerate-from-disk command is missing.

Line-level findings

lines.append(f"- git (episodic-data-engine): {env.get('git', {}).get('episodic_data_engine', {}).get('commit', 'n/a')}")
lines.append(f"- git (model_training): {env.get('git', {}).get('model_training', {}).get('commit', 'n/a')}\n")

This is the confirmed bug from Modules 3 and S2. collect_git_info writes "vla_finetune", so the second line always prints n/a. The engine line works. Every report ever generated says this repo's commit is unknown, while git.json next to it has the commit.

lines.append(f"- selected checkpoint: `{model.get('selected_checkpoint', 'n/a')}`")

The runner always sets selected_checkpoint (to None for baseline-only runs), so .get(..., 'n/a') returns None and the report prints `None` instead of n/a. The default only applies to a missing key, not to a None value. It's the same pattern as _fmt's own None → "n/a", which isn't used here. Cosmetic, but it's the "absent vs not run" discipline slipping.

if ctx.get("model_test_mse") is not None and mean_mse:
    lines.append(f"| **{cfg['training']['model']} (this run)** | {_fmt(ctx['model_test_mse'])} | "
                 f"n/a | {_fmt(ctx['model_test_mse'] / mean_mse)} |")

SmolVLA's MAE is printed as n/a in the baseline table, though test_metrics["overall"]["mae"] is right there in ctx. Each baseline row shows its MAE, and the model under test doesn't. That's a hard-coded n/a for a number that exists.

def _fmt(v, digits=4):
    if v is None: return "n/a"
    if isinstance(v, float): return f"{v:.{digits}g}"
    return str(v)
  • 4 significant figures (g format), so 0.023942 becomes 0.02394 and 1.0e-4 becomes 0.0001. That's sensible for MSEs that range over orders of magnitude.
  • A NumPy float32 isn't a Python float, so it falls through to str(v) with full repr. Module 3's default=str concern again: harmless here, inconsistent display.
def _metrics_table(metrics):
    rows = [(k, v) for k, v in metrics.items() if not isinstance(v, (list, dict))]

Per-dimension MAE is a list, so it's dropped from the report. README Table 4 (per-joint MAE, "error grows from the base of the arm toward the end effector") is one of the project's most interpretable findings. It isn't in any generated report. It must have been read out of test_overall.json by hand. That's a reporting gap that hides a real result.

  • Limitations come only from the runner's list (no GPU, no torch, non-SmolVLA model). The rebalanced caveat EXPERIMENTS.md §4 promises is never added (Module 4 gap 8), and neither is the silent best → final fallback (Module 6 gap 3).
  • Conclusion is always None from the runner, so every report says "not yet written -- fill in after reviewing the metrics above.". That's honest (the code doesn't pretend to conclude), but it means every report is a draft by construction.
  • "Data feedback recommendation" repeats recommended_next_action, which was already printed under Failure analysis. It's a duplicated section, and Module 8 showed the text is the same template every run.
  • No escaping. hypothesis and objective text go into Markdown raw. A | in a hypothesis would break the following table only if it were inside one, and they aren't. Harmless in practice.

10.3 render_model_card

"""``model_card.md`` generation. Claims only what evaluation
actually measured -- no capability beyond what ``test_metrics``/``rollout`` carry is stated."""
  • The "claims only what was measured" promise is mostly kept. The card lists architecture, base model, dataset version, embodiment, input modalities, four training hyperparameters, raw test metrics, and two hard-coded limitations: confidence is an uncalibrated constant, and metrics are offline, not task success. There's no capability language. That's to its credit.
  • "Known failure modes" is the feedback artifact's candidate_explanations. Module 8 showed those are templates, and at these data sizes it's always the "scattered → model/coverage limitation" sentence. So every model card's "Known failure modes" section is the same canned sentence with a count, presented under a heading that implies analysis of this model.
  • "Deployment requirements: loader: model_training.deployment.DeployedModel (local, no server)" names a loader that has never run (Module 9 gap 4).
  • Metrics are printed raw (f"- {k}: {v}", no _fmt), so you get 17-digit floats next to the report's 4-significant-figure ones.
  • The confidence limitation is hard-coded. It's correct for SmolVLA, and only SmolVLA runs produce a model card (the runner builds one only when there's a selected checkpoint). It would be wrong if a second model family with real confidence got a card.
  • Embodiment comes from a free-form tag. "unspecified" if the tag is absent.

10.4 comparison.py

def load_run_summary(run_dir):
    run_dir = Path(run_dir)
    if not run_dir.exists():
        raise FileNotFoundError(f"no run directory at {run_dir}")
    summary = {"run_dir": str(run_dir)}
    for name in ("dataset.json", "model.json", "manifest.json", "summary.json"):
        p = run_dir / name
        if p.exists():
            summary[name[:-5]] = json.loads(p.read_text())
    ...  # test_overall.json, baselines.json, stratified.json likewise
  • This one genuinely is "reproducible from disk", as its docstring says. It reads only saved files. Good.
  • manifest.json is never written by anything (Module 3, gap S12), so summary["manifest"] is always absent. It's dead code that implies a file exists.
  • Missing files are silently skipped, so a crashed run (no summary.json, no test_overall.json) yields a row of Nones with no indication of why. There's no status to read (Module 3 gap 10).
  • A partially written JSON file (Module 3 gap 1) raises JSONDecodeError and aborts the whole comparison.
def build_comparison_table(run_dirs, metric="mse"):
    for rd in run_dirs:
        s = load_run_summary(rd)
        ...
        row = {"run_dir": ..., "model": model.get("architecture"), "dataset_id": ...,
               "training_version": ..., "intervention": dataset.get("kind"),
               metric: overall.get(metric)}
        for name, m in (s.get("baselines") or {}).items():
            row[f"{name}_{metric}"] = m.get(metric)
        rows.append(row)
  • There's no aggregation across seeds. docs/EXPERIMENTS.md §7: "mtrain compare can then report the mean and standard deviation across the resulting run directories." No mean, standard deviation or grouping code exists (I grepped for std, mean( and stdev in reports/ and experiments/ and found nothing). Each run is one row. That's a documented feature that isn't implemented (gap logged in Module 4).
  • There's no check that the runs share a test set. Rows from baseline_full and reduced_data sit side by side as if comparable, while Module 4 showed they're scored on different test episodes. The table should at least show each run's test-episode IDs or a hash of them.
  • Baseline columns are added per row, so runs with different baseline sets produce heterogeneous rows. write_comparison_csv handles that correctly (union of keys, in first-seen order, via csv.DictWriter). The terminal table in cli.py doesn't. It builds headers from rows[0].keys() and adds cells by position. Checking rich's source shows Table.add_row appends headerless extra columns instead of raising. So with heterogeneous rows, values land under the wrong headers, silently. That's a Module 12 issue, flagged here because it's this function's output shape that triggers it.
  • metric names a key in test_overall.metrics. --metric mse gets the split-level MSE (correct). There's no confusion with the per-episode misnamed "mse" (Module 7), because that lives in a different structure.
def build_model_dataset_matrix(rows, metric="mse"):
    """The model x dataset table: one row per (model, dataset/intervention) pair."""
    return [{"model": r.get("model"), "dataset": r.get("training_version"),
             "intervention": r.get("intervention"), metric: r.get(metric)} for r in rows]
  • It's not a matrix and it's not called. It's a projection of rows, one per run, and nothing in src/, scripts/ or tests/ calls it. It also keys "dataset" on training_version, which is unique per run (Module 4: every run mints a new training/vN), so even a real pivot on it would never group two runs together. Pivoting on dataset_id plus intervention is what "model × dataset" means.

10.5 figures.py

import matplotlib
matplotlib.use("Agg")
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt  # noqa: E402

def _save(fig, path):
    path.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    fig.savefig(path, dpi=150, bbox_inches="tight")
    plt.close(fig)
    return path
  • matplotlib.use("Agg") at import time makes figure rendering work headless, so training boxes and CI need no display. Cost: it's a process-wide side effect of importing the package. reports/__init__.py imports figures, and the runner imports reports, so importing model_training.experiments in a Jupyter notebook switches the notebook's backend to Agg. Inline plots then stop appearing until you reset it. The fix is to create figures with the object API (matplotlib.figure.Figure() plus FigureCanvasAgg), which needs no global backend.
  • plt.close(fig) in _save prevents the figure leak. Without it, a sweep over many runs would accumulate open figures. It's the non-obvious line that matters.
  • dpi=150, bbox_inches="tight" are sensible defaults for PNGs in Markdown.

The "never a fabricated placeholder chart" contract (module docstring) is kept. Every function returns None or [] when there's nothing to plot (no records, missing keys, fewer than 2 fraction points). That's consistent with Module 7's {"status": "no outcomes"} returns.

Per-function notes:

  • plot_training_curves: loss always; LR and grad-norm only if present. r["loss"] is a hard key access, so an engine change to the record shape is a KeyError that aborts the run (the call isn't guarded in the runner). Plotting can crash the whole run after training succeeded.
  • plot_validation_curve: filters rows that have the metric. That's robust.
  • plot_error_distribution: a bar chart of four quantiles (median, p90, p95, p99). It shows four numbers as bars, which is a weak encoding. A histogram or empirical CDF of the per-frame errors (all available in test_overall.outcomes) would show the actual tail shape.
  • plot_episode_length_distribution: expects the engine's histogram entries with lo, hi and count (another undocumented-shape coupling, as in Module 5). The x-label says duration in seconds.
  • plot_task_distribution: for single-task SO-100, a one-bar chart in every run.
  • plot_model_comparison and plot_data_fraction_curve are never called (grep finds only their definitions). The data-efficiency curve, the deliverable run_data_fraction_sweep exists to produce, has no caller and no CLI path.

10.6 scripts/plot_results.py: the README figure

"""Render the README results figure from completed run directories.

Reads only artifacts every run writes (``metrics/validation.jsonl``,
``evaluation/test_overall.json``, ``evaluation/baselines.json``), so the figure
is reproducible from disk: ..."""

It reads only those artifacts, but it also hard-codes facts that belong to the data:

first = _validation(a.so100)
cont = [(1000 + s, m) for s, m in _validation(a.so100_continued)]   # resume offset hard-coded
_curve(ax, first + cont, selected_step=1800)                         # selection hard-coded
ax.text(1030, 0.12, "resumed,\nlr 1e-4 → 3e-5", ...)                  # LRs hard-coded as text
ax.set_ylim(6e-4, 0.3)
...
_curve(ax, _validation(a.aloha), selected_step=800)                  # selection hard-coded
ax.set_ylim(0.09, 1.6); ax.set_yticks([0.1, 0.2, 0.5, 1.0])
  • The "selected checkpoint" ring is drawn at a hard-coded step (1800 and 800), not read from model.json / training_outcome.json's best_checkpoint, which the script has access to. If you regenerate it from different runs, it either raises KeyError (dict(points)[selected_step] with no such step) or rings the wrong point under the label "selected checkpoint". For these runs it agrees with the README ("validation MSE reached its minimum (0.0018) at step 1,800"), so the published figure is right. The script just isn't the general, from-disk generator its docstring describes.
  • The resume offset 1000 + s should be the base run's final step (training_outcome.steps_completed). The LR annotation should come from the two runs' config.resolved.yaml.
  • The axis limits are tuned to these numbers, so other runs may clip.
  • Panel (c) uses summary.mse from test_overall.json for SmolVLA and baselines.json for the baselines, both from the same run directory. That's the right source, the same test split for all three bars.
  • "svg.fonttype": "path" renders text as outlines, so the SVG looks identical without the font installed, at the cost of non-selectable text. That's a deliberate portability choice for a README image.
  • It reads r["mse"] from validation rows, so it's tied to MSE regardless of selection_metric.

Honest phrasing: "the figure is generated by a script from the runs' metric files; the selection markers and annotations are set by hand for these runs."

10.7 Honest gaps to own before an interviewer finds them

  1. The report's model_training git commit always prints n/a (key mismatch). Consequence: every report hides this repo's provenance.
  2. The report claims to be "assembled entirely from saved run artifacts", but it's built from in-memory ctx, with no regenerate-from-disk path, and it's timestamped. Consequence: an overclaim, and reports can't be rebuilt after a template fix.
  3. Per-dimension MAE is dropped from the report (lists are filtered out). Consequence: the per-joint finding (README Table 4) is invisible in generated reports.
  4. SmolVLA's MAE is a hard-coded n/a in the baseline table. Consequence: the model under test shows less than the baselines.
  5. mtrain compare has no seed aggregation, despite EXPERIMENTS.md §7. Consequence: a documented feature is missing.
  6. The comparison doesn't check or show whether runs share a test set. Consequence: cross-intervention rows look comparable when they aren't (Module 4).
  7. The CLI comparison table misaligns heterogeneous rows (positional cells under first-row headers; rich silently adds columns). Consequence: wrong numbers under wrong headers.
  8. plot_results.py hard-codes the selected steps, resume offset, LR text and axis limits, contrary to "reproducible from disk". Consequence: regenerating it from other runs mislabels or crashes.
  9. build_model_dataset_matrix, plot_model_comparison and plot_data_fraction_curve are uncalled. The "matrix" pivots on per-run-unique versions. Consequence: the data-efficiency curve has no path to exist.
  10. matplotlib.use("Agg") at import is a process-wide side effect. Consequence: importing the runner breaks inline plotting in notebooks.
  11. The model card's "Known failure modes" is a templated sentence (Module 8), and it names an unexecuted loader. Consequence: the card implies model-specific analysis it doesn't contain.
  12. The rebalanced and selection-fallback limitations are never added to reports. Consequence: the two caveats that matter most are missing.
  13. A plot KeyError (r["loss"]) can abort a run after successful training. Consequence: a presentation bug destroys an expensive result's completion (no summary.json).
  14. manifest.json is read but never written; missing files are silently skipped. Consequence: dead code, and crashed runs appear as rows of None.
  15. No unit tests for any reports module (the only coverage is the runner test's existence and substring checks). Consequence: every bug above shipped unnoticed.

The shape to carry into Module 11

 runner (in-memory ctx: config, dataset, profile, model, outcome, env+git, metrics, baselines,
         stratified, feedback, limitations, conclusion=None)
   │
   ├─ render_experiment_report(ctx) ──▶ reports/experiment_report.md   (all sections always; "not run")
   │        bugs: git(model_training)=n/a · SmolVLA MAE n/a · per-dim MAE dropped · `None` checkpoint
   ├─ render_model_card(artifact, {...}) ──▶ reports/model_card.md      (failure modes = template)
   └─ figures.* ──▶ reports/figures/*.png  (Agg at import; close every fig; None when nothing to plot)

 later, from disk only:
   mtrain compare --runs A B ──▶ load_run_summary (reads manifest.json: never exists)
                                └ build_comparison_table → one row per run, no seed aggregation,
                                  no shared-test-set check → rich table (positional!) / CSV (by key)
   scripts/plot_results.py ──▶ docs/figures/results.svg   (selected steps 1800/800 hard-coded)

Drill — answer from memory, files closed

  1. What does "absent vs not run" mean in this report generator, and why does it matter?
  2. Why didn't you just use Jinja2 templates, or log everything to W&B and screenshot it?
  3. What breaks in production: you fix a typo in the report template and want every past run's report regenerated. What happens?
  4. Is the README's results figure reproducible? Be precise.
  5. Interviewer framing: "How do you compare experiments, and how do you know a difference between two runs is real?"

Appendix A — Model answers to the drill

A1. Absent vs not run. "Every section of the report always renders. If something didn't happen (no GPU, no fine-tuning, no rollout), the section says 'not run' or 'unavailable' with a reason instead of disappearing. That matters because an omitted section is ambiguous: it could mean 'skipped on purpose' or 'crashed silently', and in a results document that ambiguity is where overclaiming hides. Rollout is the clearest case. Every report states there's no simulator or robot, and the runner test asserts the word 'unavailable' is in the report. I'll admit the discipline slips in a couple of places: a baseline-only run prints a literal None for the selected checkpoint, and SmolVLA's MAE is hard-coded to 'n/a' even though it exists."

Why that answer lands: it explains the principle in terms of what it prevents, gives the tested example, and volunteers where your own code doesn't live up to it.

A2. Why not Jinja2 or W&B? "The report is linear and small, so f-strings in one function keep it readable and dependency-free. Jinja would mostly add conditionals I deliberately don't want, because sections never hide. W&B gives you a dashboard, not a durable document. The report is meant to sit in the run directory next to the artifacts it cites, readable in ten years without a service. The real weakness isn't the templating choice, though. It's that the report is rendered from in-memory state at the end of the run, and there's no command to rebuild it from a run directory. I'd fix that by writing a ctx builder that reads only the saved artifacts, which compare already does for its own table."

Why that answer lands: it justifies the choice from the document's purpose, then redirects to the actual architectural flaw, with a fix grounded in code that already exists.

A3. Regenerating past reports. "You can't, with what exists. The report is built from a context dict the runner assembles in memory at the end of each run, and there's no function or CLI command that reconstructs that dict from a run directory. So a template fix only affects future runs. The artifacts needed are all on disk (resolved config, dataset, profile, training outcome, evaluation, feedback), so the fix is a build_report_ctx(run_dir) function and an mtrain report --run command, with a test that regenerating a fresh run's report reproduces it apart from the timestamp. That would also make the docstring's 'assembled from saved artifacts' claim true."

Why that answer lands: a precise "no", the reason, and a fix with its own acceptance test. It also turns a doc overclaim into a concrete deliverable.

A4. Is the README figure reproducible? "Partly. The data in it is: the curves and bars are read from each run's validation log and test and baseline JSON, so regenerating from the same run directories redraws the same data. But the script hard-codes things that belong to the data: the selected-checkpoint markers at steps 1,800 and 800, the 1,000-step resume offset, the learning-rate annotation and the axis limits. For these runs, those values are correct (the validation minimum really was at 1,800), but point the script at different runs and it either crashes looking up step 1,800 or rings the wrong point as 'selected'. The fix is to read the selection from each run's model.json, the offset from the base run's steps completed, and the LRs from the resolved configs."

Why that answer lands: it separates data reproducibility from annotation reproducibility, confirms that the published figure is correct, and gives the exact source each hard-coded value should come from.

A5. "How do you compare experiments, and how do you know a difference is real?" "Comparison reads only saved artifacts, so mtrain compare works on runs from any session and gives one row per run: model, dataset version, intervention, test MSE, and each baseline's MSE on the same split. Knowing a difference is real is where the project is weakest, and I'd say so. Each experiment is a single seed. The test split is five episodes. The policy is stochastic: re-scoring moves MSE by up to five percent. The docs say compare reports a mean and standard deviation over seeds, but that isn't implemented. And runs with different data interventions are scored on different test episodes. So the one difference I'd defend is the 18% improvement from continued training, which is on the identical test split and well outside the measured inference noise. For anything else, I'd want a paired comparison on a shared test set, a bootstrap over episodes and at least three seeds."

Why that answer lands: it describes the mechanism, then gives an honest, specific accounting of uncertainty, picks the one claim that survives and explains why, and names the minimum evidence needed for the rest.

Module 10 Summary — the one page to revise

What this module is. A Markdown experiment report and model card rendered at the end of each run, per-run PNG figures, a disk-only cross-run comparison table, and a script for the README figure. The "never omit, say not run" discipline is good; several values are lost or mislabelled on the way to the page, and two documented capabilities (seed aggregation, rebuild from disk) don't exist.

Five claims to make cold

  1. Every report section always renders, and missing work reads "not run" or "unavailable". Rollout is always stated as unavailable, and that's tested.
  2. Comparison reads only saved artifacts, so it's reproducible across sessions, with one row per run.
  3. The report is rendered from in-memory state. There's no regenerate-from-disk path, despite the docstring.
  4. There's no seed aggregation and no shared-test-set check in compare. Only continued training (same test split, 18%, outside about 5% noise) is defensible.
  5. The README figure's data comes from disk, but its selection markers (1,800 and 800) are hard-coded.

Decisions

ChoseOverBecauseCost
f-string Markdown with fixed sectionsJinja / dashboardsreadable, durable, no hidden sectionsno regenerate path; manual escaping
Render from runner ctxbuild from run directorydata already in memorycan't rebuild; overclaimed docstring
Agg at importobject-oriented figuresheadless renderingprocess-wide backend side effect
Return None when nothing to plotplaceholder chartsnever fabricatenone
Quantile bar charthistogram / CDFsimpleweak view of the tail
Hand-tuned README figurefully data-drivenpublication polishhard-coded selection and annotations

Volunteer unprompted: the n/a git line; per-dimension MAE dropped; seed aggregation missing; the positional rich-table misalignment; plot_results.py's hard-coded markers; no tests for the reports package.

Numbers and names: _fmt uses 4 significant figures; figures at dpi 150; README figure size 13.2×4.1 in, three panels, selected steps 1,800 (SO-100) and 800 (ALOHA), resume offset 1,000; test MSE bars SO-100 0.908 / 0.0239 / 0.0239, ALOHA 1.010 / 0.106 / 0.216; files reports/experiment_report.md, reports/model_card.md, reports/figures/{training_loss,learning_rate,gradient_norm,validation_mse,error_distribution,episode_length_distribution,task_distribution}.png.

Sentences worth memorizing

  • "A section that says 'not run' is honest; a section that's missing is ambiguous."
  • "My comparison table reads only what's on disk, but it can't tell you two runs used different test sets, and that's the column I'd add first."
  • "The only difference I'd defend is continued training: same test split, 18%, well outside the 5% inference noise."
  • "The README figure's data is regenerated from disk; its annotations were placed by hand."

Module 11 — Orchestration: ExperimentRunner and the data-fraction sweep

Files covered: src/model_training/experiments/runner.py, src/model_training/experiments/data_fraction.py (experiments/__init__.py was covered in Module 1)

Where we are: Modules 2–10 each described one stage. This module is the conductor that calls them in order and threads results between them: ExperimentRunner.run, plus a sweep driver that calls run once per data fraction. Most of the cross-module bugs found so far happen here. This is where a bad config meets an expensive GPU run, where seeds are or aren't applied, and where partial failure leaves its traces. So this module is mostly about ordering and identity.

The one-line thesis: The runner is a clear, linear Transaction Script that makes every stage leave a named artifact. But it validates late (config errors surface after training), it applies seed only to directory names (so a "multi-seed" study trains identical seeds under one shared experiment_id), and it isn't transactional. The sweep driver inherits all of that, and adds a curve whose points are scored on different test sets.

11.1 Prerequisites — the absolute basics

  • Orchestrator / pipeline driver. Code whose job is ordering calls to other components and passing results along, with little logic of its own.
  • Transaction Script (Fowler). One procedure that runs a whole business operation step by step. It's simple and readable, and it grows long. Alternatives: a pipeline of stage objects (Chain of Responsibility), or a DAG framework (Airflow, Prefect, Kedro) that adds caching, retries and resume.
  • Fail fast. Validate everything you can before doing anything expensive, so errors surface in seconds, not after a GPU hour.
  • Transactional vs best-effort side effects. A transaction commits all of its effects or none. This runner writes to four places (the run directory, the engine catalog, the JSONL index and engine failure records) with no coordination, so a failure midway leaves some writes done and others not.
  • Seed vs replicate. A replicate is a run that differs only in its random seed. A study of seed variance needs the seed to reach every source of randomness (initialization, data order, dropout, sampling). A run whose "seed" changes only its folder name isn't a replicate.
  • Data-efficiency (learning) curve. Test performance plotted against training-set size. To be valid, every point must be scored on the same test set, and ideally trained on nested subsets.

11.2 ExperimentRunner.__init__ and module constants

RDE_REPO = Path(os.environ.get("RDE_REPO_PATH", str(_default_repo_root() / "episodic-data-engine")))
MT_REPO = Path(os.environ.get("MODEL_TRAINING_REPO_PATH", str(Path(__file__).resolve().parents[3])))

class ExperimentRunner:
    def __init__(self, settings=None, catalog=None, runs_dir=None, index_dir=None):
        self.settings = settings or get_settings()
        self.catalog = catalog or Catalog(self.settings.paths.catalog_db)
        self.runs_dir = Path(runs_dir) if runs_dir else MT_REPO / "runs"
        self.index = ExperimentIndex(Path(index_dir) if index_dir else MT_REPO / "experiments")
  • Constructor injection with defaults. Every collaborator can be passed in, and the tests do exactly that (ExperimentRunner(settings=..., catalog=..., runs_dir=tmp_path/..., index_dir=...)). That's Dependency Injection done simply, without a container, and it's the reason the runner is testable against a temporary catalog.
  • Construction has side effects. ExperimentIndex(...) creates the experiments/ directory (each JsonlIndex.__init__ calls mkdir), so instantiating a runner touches disk.
  • The path constants are read at import time. Setting RDE_REPO_PATH after model_training.experiments has been imported has no effect. Module 3 covered the provenance consequences of guessing these paths.

11.3 run, in order, with what can fail where

def run(self, config: ExperimentConfig, seed: int | None = None) -> ExperimentRunResult:
    seed = seed if seed is not None else config.seeds[0]
    run_dir = make_run_dir(self.runs_dir, config.experiment_name, seed)

Seed: the identity bug

seed is used in exactly two places: make_run_dir(..., seed), which names the directory, and summary["seed"]. It never reaches config.training.seed, the intervention seed or the split seed. Trainer reads tc.seed (Module 6). So:

  • mtrain train --config X --seed 1 trains with training.seed = 0, like the default run, and writes to seed_1/.
  • docs/EXPERIMENTS.md §7 says: "Running the same configuration across several seeds is a matter of calling run once per seed; mtrain compare can then report the mean and standard deviation". Following that literally yields replicates that share a training seed. They'd differ only through GPU nondeterminism and flow-matching sampling, so the "seed variance" you measured would underestimate the real variance. That's arguably worse than having no replicates, because it looks like evidence.
  • The replicates also share one experiment_id. The config is loaded once, and experiment_id is stamped at load (Module 2). Calling run(cfg, seed=s) for several s reuses it. Downstream, model_id = f"{tag}-{experiment_id}" (Module 9) is identical across seeds, so ModelRegistry.get(model_id) returns only the last seed's model, and the index's experiments.jsonl, datasets.jsonl and metrics.jsonl rows can't be told apart by experiment_id.
  • config.seeds[1:] is never read by anything (Module 2).

The fix: cfg = config.model_copy(update={"training": config.training.model_copy(update={"seed": seed})}), re-stamp the identity, and loop over config.seeds in the CLI. Whether the split seed should vary across replicates is a design choice. Usually it shouldn't, so the test set stays fixed.

Stage 0: identity and environment, before anything can fail usefully

environment = collect_environment()
git_info = collect_git_info(RDE_REPO, MT_REPO)
run_dir.write_json("environment.json", environment)
run_dir.write_json("git.json", git_info)
(run_dir.path / "config.resolved.yaml").write_text(yaml.safe_dump(config.resolved_dict(), sort_keys=False))

limitations: list[str] = []
if environment.get("gpu") is None:
    limitations.append("no GPU detected in this environment at run time -- training ran on CPU if it ran at all")
if environment.get("torch") is None:
    limitations.append("torch not installed in this environment -- this run could not perform real fine-tuning")
  • Writing provenance first is a defensible choice. Even a crashed run records what it ran on. The cost (Module 3) is that the directory is now non-empty, so a same-day retry is blocked.
  • sort_keys=False keeps the resolved YAML in schema field order, so it reads like the source config. That's a small, deliberate readability touch.
  • The limitations list starts here and is threaded through to the report and model card. It's the right mechanism, but underused: it never receives the rebalanced caveat (Module 4), the best → final fallback (Module 6) or the shared-seed issue above.
  • CPU training isn't blocked. With no GPU, a SmolVLA run proceeds on CPU (the limitation text admits this). docs/IMPLEMENTATION_PLAN.md records that CPU-only fine-tuning was "impractical". A --require-gpu guard would turn hours of wasted CPU time into an instant error.

Stage 1: intervention, the first irreversible write

intervention_result = build_intervention_dataset(
    config.dataset.dataset_id, config.dataset.version, config.intervention,
    config.splits, settings=self.settings, catalog=self.catalog,
)
run_dir.write_json("dataset.json", {...})
self.index.record_dataset(experiment_id=..., training_version=..., kind=..., episode_count=len(...), recorded_at=...)
  • This creates engine versions (Module 4): clean/vK and training/vM. From here on, a failure orphans catalog versions.
  • record_dataset is written to the index before training, so a later crash leaves an index row for a run that never finishes (Module 3 gap 10).
  • episode_count = len(episode_ids) is the whole selected pool (train + val + test), not the training count. The column name invites misreading.

Stage 2: open the view, check, profile

training_version = intervention_result.training_version
engine = DataEngine(self.settings)
view = engine.get_dataset(config.dataset.dataset_id, training_version)
try:
    split_report = verify_split_integrity(view)  # raises on leakage
    run_dir.write_json("split_integrity.json", split_report.to_dict())
    profile = build_dataset_profile(config.dataset.dataset_id, training_version, settings=..., catalog=...)
    run_dir.write_json("dataset_profile.json", profile.to_dict())
    figures.plot_task_distribution(...); figures.plot_episode_length_distribution(...)
  • try/finally: view.close() guarantees the view is released on every exit path, including exceptions. Without it, a crashed run would leak file handles (and presumably a DuckDB connection) for the life of the process. That matters in a sweep (11.5), which runs many experiments in one process. It's correct, and it's the most important resource-management line in the file.
  • The profile opens its own second view internally (Module 5). Two views of one version are open at once, which is harmless and slightly wasteful.

Stage 3: training

train_ids = view.episode_ids(split="train")
episode_weights = {
    eid: float((view.episode(eid).attributes or {}).get("sampling_weight", 1.0))
    for eid in train_ids
}
episode_weights = episode_weights if any(w != 1.0 for w in episode_weights.values()) else None
  • Weights are read back from the built version, not from intervention_result.episode_weights. That's a good choice: the trainer uses what the engine actually stored (the sampling_weight attribute), so a disagreement between what was requested and what was built can't silently change training. It's the same "trust the materialized artifact" stance as Module 5's split check. Here it's actually load-bearing.
  • Only train episodes' weights are passed, which resolves Module 4's "weights recorded for val and test too" at the point that matters.
  • All-ones collapses to None, so unweighted runs take the engine's unweighted sampling path (inferred).
training_outcome = None
selected_checkpoint = None
if config.training.model == "smolvla":
    from ..training.trainer import Trainer
    trainer = Trainer(run_dir, config)
    training_outcome = trainer.train(view, view, episode_ids=train_ids, episode_weights=episode_weights)
    run_dir.write_json("training_outcome.json", training_outcome.to_dict())
    figures.plot_training_curves([json.loads(l) for l in (run_dir.path / "metrics" / "train.jsonl").read_text().splitlines()]
                                 if (run_dir.path / "metrics" / "train.jsonl").exists() else [], ...)
    ...
    selected_checkpoint = training_outcome.best_checkpoint
else:
    limitations.append(f"training.model={config.training.model!r} has no gradient-based fit; "
                       "this run only scored baselines, no fine-tuning occurred")
  • trainer.train(view, view, ...). The same view is passed as train_view and val_view. The trainer's two-view signature anticipates separate datasets for validation (for example, a held-out scene), and the runner never uses that.
  • The lazy Trainer import is the import-light idea. Module 1 showed it saves nothing on the engine side, because deployment.artifact already imports SmolVLAPolicy at module top.
  • The else branch is Module 2's typo trap. Any model string other than exactly "smolvla" lands here, and the run completes with exit 0.
  • Re-reading train.jsonl from disk for plotting (instead of using fit_result["history"], which the API doc says exists) is consistent with "figures from saved artifacts" (Module 10), and it costs one file read.

Stage 4: baselines, then the model under test

baseline_results = evaluate_baselines(view, config.evaluation.baseline_models, split=config.evaluation.split,
                                      batch_size=config.evaluation.batch_size, max_frames=config.evaluation.max_frames)
...
if selected_checkpoint:
    from rde.model_adapters.models import SmolVLAPolicy
    policy = SmolVLAPolicy.from_checkpoint(selected_checkpoint)
    adapter = VisionLanguageAdapter(view.contract)
    strat_results = stratified_evaluate(view, policy, adapter, base_split=config.evaluation.split, ...,
                                        slices=config.evaluation.slices)
    test_overall = strat_results.pop("overall")

Validation happens after the expensive part. Two config errors are detected only here, after fine-tuning has finished:

  • an unknown baseline name raises KeyError inside evaluate_baselines (Module 7);
  • an unknown slice name raises ValueError inside stratified_evaluate (Module 7).

A typo like baseline_models: [nearest_neighbour] (British spelling) costs a full training run (5–10 minutes on the README's hardware, or hours at scale) and then fails, leaving a run directory with checkpoints and no evaluation, and no summary.json. Both checks are pure name lookups that could run in stage 0 (or in the Pydantic schema with Literal). That's the clearest fail-fast violation in the codebase.

  • config.evaluation.split is used for the "test" evaluation. It defaults to "test". Setting it to "val" would make the headline "test metrics", the feedback artifact and the model card all describe validation, with every label still saying "test". It's a sharp edge, and arguably the hook for Module 8's fix: feedback should run on val in addition to test.
  • from_checkpoint(selected_checkpoint) is called without device=. training.device is honoured for training but not for evaluation, which auto-selects. Minor.
  • The evaluation policy is never released. After stratified evaluation, policy stays referenced until run returns. In a sweep (11.5), memory is reclaimed only when the next run frame replaces it. That's fine on one GPU, because the next run's trainer allocates after this frame is gone. It's worth an explicit del for symmetry with the trainer's careful release.

Stage 5: artifacts, feedback, model card

predictions_dir = run_dir.sub("predictions")
with (predictions_dir / "test_outcomes.jsonl").open("w") as f:
    for o in test_overall.outcomes:
        f.write(json.dumps({"run_id": str(run_dir.path), "experiment_id": ..., "dataset_id": ...,
                            "dataset_version": training_version, "frame_id": o.frame_id,
                            "episode_id": o.episode_id, "error": o.error, "confidence": o.confidence,
                            "per_dim_error": o.per_dim_error}, default=str) + "\n")
  • Per-frame outcomes with full provenance. This file is quietly the most valuable artifact in the run. It's the only place the evaluated test episodes are recorded (Module 4), the input for the bootstrap Module 7 recommends, and the base for per-joint analysis. FAILURE_ANALYSIS.md §4 is right to call it the traceability anchor.
  • run_id is the absolute path of the run directory, so it breaks if the runs directory is moved or copied to another machine. A relative path, or the experiment_id plus seed, would be portable.
  • Predicted and target action vectors aren't stored, only the errors. Module 8 showed the stated justification ("deterministic, reproducible on demand") is false for SmolVLA.
model_artifact = build_model_artifact(checkpoint_path=selected_checkpoint, contract=view.contract, ...,
                                      metrics=test_overall.metrics)
self.index.record_model(**model_artifact.to_dict())
feedback_artifact = build_feedback_artifact(config.experiment_id, model_artifact.model_id, ..., test_overall, ...)
(run_dir.sub("failures") / "feedback_artifact.json").write_text(json.dumps(feedback_artifact, ...))
run_dir.write_json("model_artifact.json", model_artifact.to_dict())
(run_dir.sub("reports") / "model_card.md").write_text(render_model_card(...))
  • record_model goes into the index before feedback runs. A failure in feedback (an engine error inside FeedbackLoop.detect) leaves a registered model in models.jsonl with no model_artifact.json in its run directory, because that's written after feedback. The index then points at a run that has no artifact file. Writing the local artifact first and indexing last would keep the run directory authoritative.
  • Feedback runs on test_overall. That's Module 8's central methodological issue, and this is the line to change.

Stage 6: closing writes

if test_overall is not None:
    run_dir.append_jsonl("metrics/test.jsonl", {"experiment_id": ..., **test_overall.metrics})
run_dir.append_jsonl("metrics/rollout.jsonl", {"experiment_id": ..., "status": "unavailable",
                                               "reason": "no simulator or physical robot connected in this environment"})
report_md = render_experiment_report(report_ctx)
(run_dir.sub("reports") / "experiment_report.md").write_text(report_md)
summary = {..., "test_mse": ..., "baseline_mean_action_mse": baseline_json.get("mean_action", {}).get("mse"),
           "steps_completed": ..., "wall_clock_s": ...}
run_dir.write_json("summary.json", summary)
self.index.record_experiment(run_dir=..., created_at=..., **summary)
if test_overall is not None:
    self.index.record_metric(experiment_id=..., run_dir=..., split=config.evaluation.split, **test_overall.metrics)
  • The rollout "unavailable" record is written in every run. That's the refusal-to-fabricate principle made into data, so a downstream tool reading rollout.jsonl can't mistake absence for zero.
  • summary.json records the mean-action baseline but not nearest-neighbour, which is the baseline that actually matters (Module 7's tie). So the index's experiments.jsonl, built from summary, can't show the project's key result without opening each run's baselines.json.
  • wall_clock_s is training only. Total run time (including about 3× test inference from the slices, Module 7) isn't recorded anywhere.
  • summary.json is written last and is the implicit completion marker (Module 3).
  • There's no top-level try/except. Any exception after stage 1 propagates out of run with partial state in four stores. The trainer's crash.json covers only fit (Module 6).

What run writes, and where, in order

#WriteStoreIrreversible?
1run directory, env, git, configrun directoryblocks same-day retry
2clean/vK, training/vMengine catalogyes (immutable versions)
3datasets.jsonl rowindexappend-only
4checkpoints, metrics, outcomerun directory—
5models.jsonl rowindexappend-only
6failure records (persist=True)engineyes
7artifacts, report, summary.jsonrun directory—
8experiments.jsonl, metrics.jsonl rowsindexappend-only

There's no coordinator across the three stores, and no status. Module 3's state-machine fix (status: running → completed/failed, written first and atomically replaced) is the minimal remedy.

11.4 Why a Transaction Script, and when it stops being right

Decision: one linear run method of about 250 lines.

  • Why: every stage's inputs are visible in one place, the order is obvious, and the README's Figure 1 ("seven ExperimentRunner stages") maps directly onto the code. For a research harness with one user, that readability is worth a lot.
  • Alternative: stage objects with a common interface (Stage.run(ctx) -> ctx), or a DAG tool (Kedro, Prefect, Airflow) with per-stage caching.
  • Cost: no stage can be re-run alone. If evaluation fails, you can't resume from the trained checkpoint through run; you'd use mtrain evaluate (Module 12), which re-implements a subset of stage 4 with different settings. There's no caching (a re-run rebuilds versions and retrains), and the method mixes orchestration with artifact formatting (the inline json.dumps for predictions and evaluation files).
  • When it flips: once you need resume-from-stage (multi-hour runs, preemptible GPUs) or sweeps of more than a handful of runs. Both want stage-level checkpointing and caching, which a DAG framework or a simple "skip stage if its output artifact exists and is complete" rule would give.

11.5 run_data_fraction_sweep

"""... Fractions are not executed unless explicitly requested -- calling this function *does* run them
(the framework makes them possible; they do not run by default); the caller decides which fractions and how many."""

def run_data_fraction_sweep(base_config, fractions, runner=None) -> list[dict[str, Any]]:
    """Run ``base_config`` once per fraction, varying only the data quantity.

    Every other knob (model, hyperparameters, splits, seed) is held fixed, so
    the resulting curve isolates the quantity effect -- ..."""
    runner = runner or ExperimentRunner()
    results = []
    for frac in fractions:
        data = base_config.model_dump()
        data["experiment_name"] = f"{base_config.experiment_name}_frac{int(round(frac * 100))}"
        data["intervention"] = {"kind": "fraction", "fraction": frac, "seed": base_config.intervention.seed}
        data["experiment_id"] = None
        data["created_at"] = None
        cfg = ExperimentConfig.model_validate(data)
        outcome = runner.run(cfg)
        results.append({"fraction": frac, **outcome.summary})
    return results
  • The module docstring contradicts itself ("not executed unless explicitly requested -- calling this function does run them ... they do not run by default"). What it means is that nothing calls this automatically. The prose reads like two edits merged. docs/API_REFERENCE.md Table 8 states the same idea cleanly.
  • Re-stamping identity (experiment_id = None, created_at = None) is deliberate and correct: each fraction gets a fresh ID. This is the pattern Module 2 noted as relying on the "not user-editable" guard.
  • "Isolates the quantity effect" is false, because of Module 4. Each fraction builds its own training/vN from the filtered pool, so every point on the curve is scored on a different test set, of a different size (at 25% of 48 episodes the test split is about 1 episode). The curve conflates "less training data" with "different, smaller test set". The fix is the same: split once, and subsample only the train split.
  • Non-nested subsets (Module 4 gap 6): every fraction draws with the same seed independently, so the 25% subset isn't inside the 50% subset.
  • fraction = 0.0 raises (Module 4 gap 2), and there's no try per fraction. One failing fraction aborts the sweep, and results for the completed fractions is lost from the return value. Only the run directories survive. A sweep that took hours returns nothing. Fix: catch per fraction, record the failure, continue, and persist results incrementally (for example, to experiments/sweeps/<name>.jsonl).
  • Name collisions: int(round(frac * 100)) maps 0.125 and 0.12 both to _frac12, and Python rounds 0.125 × 100 = 12.5 to 12. The second fraction's make_run_dir raises FileExistsError the same day. Use f"{frac:g}" or permille.
  • The base intervention is discarded. A sweep over a rebalanced or imbalanced base config silently becomes a sweep over plain random fractions. That's only acceptable if documented.
  • A single seed per fraction, run sequentially. A curve from one seed per point at these test sizes is mostly noise (Module 7).
  • The output isn't wired to its plot. plot_data_fraction_curve(rows, metric, ...) exists (Module 10). The rows here carry test_mse (from summary), so metric="test_mse" would work, but nothing calls it.
  • Untested and uncalled. No test or CLI command references run_data_fraction_sweep (grep). Module 1's table has been updated accordingly. Its first real use would hit the 0.0 crash and the moving test set.

11.6 Honest gaps to own before an interviewer finds them

  1. seed only names the directory. Replicates share training.seed and experiment_id. Consequence: a multi-seed study following EXPERIMENTS.md §7 produces fake replicates that underestimate variance, plus colliding model_ids (the registry returns only the last seed).
  2. Config errors are detected after training (unknown baseline names and slice names raise in stage 4). Consequence: a typo costs a full GPU run and leaves an incomplete run directory.
  3. Not transactional across four stores (run directory, engine catalog, index, engine failure records), with no status marker. Consequence: partial failures leave orphan versions, index rows without runs, and runs without artifacts.
  4. record_model is indexed before feedback and before model_artifact.json is written. Consequence: a feedback failure leaves an indexed model whose run lacks the artifact file.
  5. evaluation.split silently relabels. Setting it to "val" makes "test" reports describe validation. Consequence: a sharp edge on the project's central val/test discipline.
  6. No GPU guard. SmolVLA training proceeds on CPU with only a limitation note. Consequence: hours lost to an impractical configuration.
  7. summary.json and experiments.jsonl omit the nearest-neighbour baseline, and total run time isn't recorded. Consequence: the key comparison isn't queryable from the index.
  8. run_id in predictions is an absolute path. Consequence: outcomes don't survive moving the runs directory.
  9. No stage-level resume or caching (Transaction Script). Consequence: any failure after training means retraining, or using mtrain evaluate's divergent re-implementation.
  10. The sweep scores each fraction on a different test set and uses non-nested subsets. Consequence: the data-efficiency curve doesn't isolate quantity, contrary to its docstring.
  11. The sweep aborts on the first failure (0.0 always fails) and loses completed results. Consequence: hours of sweep produce an exception and no rows.
  12. Sweep name collisions (_frac12 for 0.12 and 0.125) and a discarded base intervention. Consequence: a FileExistsError mid-sweep, and silently changed semantics.
  13. The sweep is untested and has no CLI entry point. Consequence: a headline capability that has never run.
  14. training.device isn't used for evaluation, and the evaluation policy isn't released explicitly. Consequence: minor inconsistency.
  15. The same view serves as train and val; the trainer's two-view seam is unused. Consequence: none today. It's the natural place for a held-out-scene val set later.

The shape to carry into Module 12

 ExperimentRunner.run(config, seed)                     seed → dir name + summary ONLY
  0  make_run_dir · environment.json · git.json · config.resolved.yaml · limitations[]
  1  build_intervention_dataset ──▶ ENGINE: clean/vK, training/vM      (irreversible)
     dataset.json · index.datasets
  2  view = get_dataset(training/vM)  ┐ try
     verify_split_integrity · profile │
  3  weights ← view attrs (train only)│
     Trainer.train(view, view) ───────│──▶ checkpoints, metrics, training_outcome.json
  4  evaluate_baselines (KeyError on bad name ← AFTER training)
     SmolVLA.from_checkpoint(best) → stratified_evaluate (ValueError on bad slice ← AFTER training)
  5  predictions/test_outcomes.jsonl · index.models (before feedback!)
     FeedbackLoop on TEST ──▶ ENGINE failure records · feedback_artifact.json · model_artifact.json · model_card.md
  6  metrics/test.jsonl · rollout.jsonl("unavailable") · experiment_report.md · summary.json (last)
     index.experiments · index.metrics          ┘ finally view.close()

 run_data_fraction_sweep(base, fractions): for f: cfg(kind=fraction, new id) → run()
     different test set per f · not nested · aborts on first failure · results lost · untested

Drill — answer from memory, files closed

  1. List, in order, every place ExperimentRunner.run writes, and say which writes are irreversible.
  2. Why didn't you just use Airflow, Prefect or Kedro for the pipeline?
  3. What breaks in production: you run mtrain train --config baseline.yaml --seed 0, then --seed 1, then --seed 2, and report mean ± std. What's wrong?
  4. A teammate sets baseline_models: [nearest_neighbour]. Walk through what happens.
  5. Interviewer framing: "How would you produce a trustworthy data-efficiency curve with this system?"

Appendix A — Model answers to the drill

A1. The write order. "First the run directory itself, with environment, git info and the fully resolved config. Then the intervention, which creates new dataset versions in the engine's catalog. That's irreversible, since versions are immutable, and a dataset row goes into the index. Then the training artifacts in the run directory: checkpoints, train and validation metrics, the training outcome. Then evaluation files and the per-frame test outcomes, and a model row in the index. Then the feedback step, which persists failure records into the engine, so that's a second irreversible write. Then the feedback artifact, model artifact, model card, report and summary.json, and finally the experiment and metric rows in the index. Three stores, with no coordination between them, so a failure halfway leaves some stores updated and others not. The minimal fix is a status file written first and flipped atomically at the end."

Why that answer lands: it's complete, it's in the right order, and it identifies the two engine writes as the irreversible ones. That's a sign you've thought about failure, not just the happy path.

A2. Why not a DAG framework? "For one researcher running a handful of experiments, a single linear method is the most readable form. The stages, their order and their artifacts are all on one screen, and they map one-to-one onto the architecture diagram. A DAG tool would add a scheduler, a metadata store and a deployment surface, mostly to buy caching and retries I didn't need at five-minute runs. Where that stops being true is resume-from-stage and large sweeps. Today, if evaluation fails after training, I can't resume through the runner, I'd retrain. So the first step wouldn't be Airflow. It'd be making each stage skip itself if its output artifact already exists and is complete, which gives resume with no new infrastructure. I'd adopt Prefect or Kedro once runs got long or numerous enough that caching mattered."

Why that answer lands: it argues from scale, admits the concrete gap (no resume), and proposes an intermediate step before reaching for a framework. That's proportionate engineering.

A3. Three seeds, mean ± std. "Those aren't replicates. The --seed value only names the run directory and goes into the summary; it never reaches training.seed, which stays at the config's zero. All three runs train with the same seed, so any differences come from GPU nondeterminism and the stochastic action sampler, and the standard deviation would understate real seed variance, which is worse than reporting nothing. They also share one experiment ID, because the config was loaded once, so their model IDs collide, and the registry would return only the last one. The fix is for the runner to copy the config with training.seed set, re-stamp the identity, and for the CLI to loop over the config's seeds list, keeping the split seed fixed so all replicates share a test set."

Why that answer lands: it explains why the numbers are misleadingly small, not just wrong, catches the identity collision, and specifies the fix including the split-seed subtlety.

A4. nearest_neighbour. "Nothing checks baseline names until the evaluation stage. So the runner builds the dataset versions, runs the integrity check and profile, fine-tunes SmolVLA for the full budget, writes the checkpoints and training outcome, and only then calls evaluate_baselines, which raises KeyError because the registry name is nearest_neighbor. The run ends with checkpoints, no evaluation, no summary.json, orphaned dataset versions in the catalog, and a run directory that blocks a same-day retry. The same applies to an unknown slice name. Both are pure lookups that belong in config validation, as Literal types or a stage-zero check, so the error appears in under a second instead of after training."

Why that answer lands: it traces the exact sequence and its debris, then classifies it correctly as a fail-fast violation with a one-line fix.

A5. "How would you produce a trustworthy data-efficiency curve with this system?" "Not with the sweep function as it stands. It filters episodes and then re-splits, so each fraction is scored on a different and shrinking test set: at 25% of 48 episodes, the test split is about one episode. The subsets aren't nested, it fails outright at 0%, and it loses completed results if any fraction fails. I'd change four things. Split the full dataset once and freeze val and test. Subsample only the train split, as prefixes of one seeded permutation, so the subsets are nested. Make the zero point 'evaluate the pretrained base with no fine-tuning'. And run at least three training seeds per point, with real seed propagation, reporting a bootstrap interval over test episodes. Then each point differs only in training data quantity, which is what the curve claims to show."

Why that answer lands: it starts with a frank "not as it stands" and the precise reasons, then gives a protocol that addresses every confound. That's what an interviewer probing experimental design wants to hear.

Module 11 Summary — the one page to revise

What this module is. A linear Transaction Script that calls every stage in order (intervention, integrity, profile, train, baselines, stratified evaluation, artifact, feedback, reports, summary, index) with injectable collaborators and a finally-closed view. Plus a sweep driver that loops it over data fractions. The ordering and identity handling are where the project's cross-cutting bugs concentrate.

Five claims to make cold

  1. One method, seven-plus stages, every stage leaves a named artifact. Collaborators are constructor-injected for tests.
  2. seed names the directory only. training.seed is unchanged and replicates share experiment_id, so multi-seed studies are fake.
  3. Baseline and slice names are validated after training. That's the clearest fail-fast violation.
  4. Writes span the run directory, the engine catalog (irreversible), the index and engine failure records, with no transaction and no status.
  5. The sweep's points use different test sets and non-nested subsets, abort on the first failure, and have never been run.

Decisions

ChoseOverBecauseCost
Transaction Script runstage objects / DAG toolreadable, maps onto Figure 1no resume or caching; mixed concerns
Constructor DI with defaultsglobals / containertestable against a temporary catalogconstruction touches disk
Provenance written firstwrite at endcrashed runs keep provenanceblocks same-day retry
Weights read back from the built versionfrom the intervention requesttrain on what the engine storednone
try/finally view closerely on GCno handle leaks across a sweepnone
Sweep = loop over rundedicated sweep pipelinereuse everythinginherits moving test set; no fault isolation

Volunteer unprompted: fake seeds and the ID collision; late name validation; the four-store non-transactional writes; record_model before the artifact file; the sweep's test-set confound and lost results.

Numbers and names: stage order 0–6 above; summary.json keys experiment_id, experiment_name, dataset_id, training_version, intervention, tags, seed, model, test_mse, baseline_mean_action_mse, steps_completed, wall_clock_s; sweep names {name}_frac{round(f×100)}; env vars RDE_REPO_PATH, MODEL_TRAINING_REPO_PATH (read at import).

Sentences worth memorizing

  • "My --seed flag renamed a folder; it didn't change a seed. Replicates that share a seed understate variance, which is worse than no replicates."
  • "Every config error my runner can detect, it should detect before it touches the GPU. Two of them it detects after."
  • "The runner writes to three stores without a transaction; the minimum fix is a status file written first and flipped atomically last."
  • "A learning curve is only a learning curve if every point is scored on the same test set."

Module 12 — The CLI surface: mtrain and the script wrappers

Files covered: src/model_training/cli.py, scripts/train.py, scripts/evaluate.py, scripts/test.py, scripts/rollout.py, scripts/compare.py, scripts/analyze_failures.py, scripts/export.py (scripts/plot_results.py was covered in Module 10)

Where we are: Module 11 was the programmatic entry point (ExperimentRunner.run). This module is the human one, the eleven mtrain commands a researcher actually types. Most commands are thin views over run-directory artifacts. Three of them change state: train runs the pipeline, evaluate/test re-score and overwrite, and build-intervention rewrites a config file. Those three are where the CLI can undo the guarantees the rest of the system builds.

The one-line thesis: The CLI's structure is right: lazy imports, a dry run by default for config rewriting, and honest "unavailable" commands. But mtrain evaluate is a divergent reimplementation of the runner's evaluation stage. It overwrites the published test numbers in place, with different slices and batch settings, and leaves the rest of the run directory describing the old numbers. Its own reference doc recommends using it with --split val, which writes validation results into test_overall.json.

12.1 Prerequisites — the absolute basics

  • Typer. A library that builds a CLI from Python function signatures. @app.command() registers a function as a sub-command, and each parameter becomes an option via typer.Option(default, "--flag"). ... (Ellipsis) as the default means required. list[Path] options are given by repeating the flag (--runs a --runs b), not as space-separated values. "--dry-run/--apply" declares a boolean with paired on and off flags.
  • typer.Exit(code). Ends the command with an exit code, and no traceback.
  • rich. A terminal-formatting library: Console.print (with [yellow]…[/yellow] markup), print_json and Table.
  • Command–query separation (CQS). Distinguishing commands that change state from queries that only read it. CLIs that mix the two in one verb (e.g. "evaluate" that also overwrites) surprise users.
  • Single source of truth. Each behaviour implemented in exactly one place. Two implementations of "evaluate a run" will drift.
  • Idempotence. Running a command twice has the same effect as running it once. For a stochastic model, re-scoring is not idempotent: each run produces new numbers.
  • YAML round-trip loss. yaml.safe_load followed by yaml.safe_dump discards comments, and reflows quoting and folded strings. Preserving them needs a round-trip parser such as ruamel.yaml.

12.2 Structure: lazy imports and thin views

app = typer.Typer(no_args_is_help=True, add_completion=False)
console = Console()

def _print_json(data) -> None:
    console.print_json(json.dumps(data, indent=2, default=str))
  • Only typer, yaml and rich load at import. Every rde and model_training import is inside a command function. So mtrain --help, mtrain rollout and mtrain analyze-failures start without loading the engine or torch. This is the one place where the import-light discipline (Module 1) is applied fully, and it's where it matters most, because CLI startup latency is user-facing.
  • no_args_is_help=True prints help instead of an error for a bare mtrain. add_completion=False hides Typer's shell-completion install commands, so there's less surface.
  • The name mtrain avoids colliding with the engine's rde model (module docstring, Module 1).

Read-only views

CommandReadsWritesNotes
inspect --dataset --versionengine explorer overview—--version defaults to "training/v1", an arbitrary version that may not exist for a given dataset. That's the only CLI default that names data.
profile --dataset --version [--out]Module 5's build_dataset_profileoptional JSON filethe read-only profile the imbalance choice must come from
validate --runtraining_outcome.json, metrics/validation.jsonl—see below
analyze-failures --runfailures/feedback_artifact.json—prints the Module 8 artifact verbatim
compare --runs … [--metric] [--out]Module 10 build_comparison_tableoptional CSVterminal-table misalignment (Module 10 gap 7)

validate misdiagnoses.

outcome_path = run / "training_outcome.json"
if not outcome_path.exists():
    console.print(f"[yellow]no training_outcome.json in {run} -- this run had no gradient-based training[/yellow]")
    raise typer.Exit(1)
