Project-owned engine protocol (typing.Protocol for Catalog/View/Evaluator)one engine, real-engine testsa second backend, or the need for engine-free unit tests
Stage pipeline / DAG with caching5–10 minute runsmulti-hour or preemptible runs, sweeps over 10 runs
Unit of Work / transaction across storesone user, rare failuresconcurrent users, or any automation that retries
Schema-versioned persisted recordsone code version produced every runthe first breaking change to an artifact shape
Observer / event bus for run progressa terminal and files sufficea dashboard or remote monitoring
Retry with backofflocal disk and GPU errors aren't transientnetwork-backed storage or Hub downloads in CI
Locking (fcntl / sqlite) on the indexsingle processtwo concurrent mtrain train
Dependency-injection containerone composition root sufficesmultiple entry points wiring differently
Factory for modelsone trainable modela second trainable model family
Server (FastAPI / gRPC)no consumer processa robot controller in another process

14.12 Honest gaps to own before an interviewer finds them

  1. Interior contracts are untyped dicts, and persisted artifacts have no schema or version. Consequence: key-mismatch bugs (vla_finetune / model_training) and engine shape changes surface as KeyError or silent n/a, never at write time.
  2. There's no explicit run state machine. State is inferred from file existence, and killed, crashed and in-progress runs are indistinguishable. Consequence: retries are blocked, and compare and export can't tell complete from partial.
  3. The runner violates SRP (orchestration plus formatting plus naming), and the CLI duplicates runner internals. Consequence: the evaluate drift (Module 12), and no reusable stage functions.
  4. Concrete dependence on rde everywhere, with no project-owned protocol. Consequence: no seam for engine-free unit tests or a second backend (DIP strained).
  5. Open/closed is strained for interventions (3–4 edit sites per new kind) and slices (an elif chain). Consequence: extension means editing core functions.
  6. Only two custom exceptions, no top-level handler, and late validation. Consequence: expensive failures, and raw tracebacks for user errors.
  7. Write operations aren't idempotent, and nothing dedupes. Consequence: re-execution multiplies versions, index rows and engine failure records.
  8. Process-level concurrency is unsafe (TOCTOU, unlocked appends, non-atomic version allocation). Consequence: two concurrent runs can corrupt shared state.
  9. Import-time global side effects (matplotlib backend, path constants). Consequence: importing the package changes process behaviour.

The shape to carry into the High-Level Design module

  cli ─▶ experiments(runner = composition root) ─▶ {training, data, evaluation, feedback, deployment, reports}
                                                         │            ─▶ {config, tracking}
                                                         ▼
                                                  rde (concrete, unabstracted)
  Contracts:  YAML/CLI typed ──▶ value objects ──▶ dict[str, Any] ──▶ schemaless JSON on disk
  State:      CREATED → DATA_READY → PROFILED → TRAINING → (CRASHED | KILLED | TRAINED)
              → EVALUATED → COMPLETED → (RESCORED)        ← all implicit, inferred from files
  Errors:     raise built-ins · 2 custom types · convert-to-data in fit/observation only · no top handler
  Idempotent: reads yes · writes no · dedupe nowhere      Concurrency: single process assumed

Drill — answer from memory, files closed

  1. Draw the package dependency graph. Where's the composition root, and which edge breaks the layering?
  2. Name four design patterns in this codebase, with exact locations and what each buys.
  3. Why didn't you just define a typed Protocol for the data engine?
  4. What breaks in production: two researchers launch mtrain train at the same moment on a shared machine. Walk through every shared resource.
  5. Interviewer framing: "What's the weakest part of your low-level design, and how would you fix it?"

Appendix A — Model answers to the drill

A1. Dependency graph. "Four layers. At the bottom, config (Pydantic and YAML only) and tracking (standard library only). Above them, the stage packages: data and training depend on config, training and deployment also use tracking, and evaluation and feedback depend only on the engine. reports depends on nothing internal, because it takes plain dicts. Above that, experiments, whose runner is the composition root: it builds the catalog, engine, index, trainer and models, and calls every stage. The CLI sits on top. There are no cycles. The edge that breaks the layering is the CLI reaching past the façade into data.interventions internals and reimplementing the evaluation stage instead of calling a service function. And the engine sits underneath every layer as a concrete dependency, with no abstraction."

Why that answer lands: it's precise about direction and leaves, names the composition root, and identifies the real violation instead of claiming purity.

A2. Four patterns. "Template Method via callback in Trainer.train: the engine's fit owns the loop, and I inject an on_step hook that validates, checkpoints, verifies and prunes. It lets run policy live in this repo while the step mechanics stay in the engine. Transaction Script in ExperimentRunner.run: one linear method for the whole lifecycle, readable and mapping directly onto the architecture figure, at the cost of no resume. Specification via Pydantic model validators on DatasetIntervention: the methodology rule 'don't presume the weak dimension' becomes a load-time error. And a Null-Object-like explicit 'unavailable' record, in the profile's visual diversity, the rollout log and the environment's missing torch: absence is data, so nothing downstream mistakes it for a zero."

Why that answer lands: each pattern has a file, a mechanism and a payoff specific to this system, which shows the patterns are real and not name-dropped.

A3. Why not a Protocol for the engine? "Because there's one engine, and my tests deliberately use the real one, since the risky bugs live at that boundary, so a protocol would have had a single implementation and no test doubles. The cost showed up anyway. With no seam, pure logic like pruning, threshold arithmetic or report rendering can't easily be tested without spinning up a catalog, so a lot of it isn't tested. I'd introduce small protocols where a stage needs only a narrow slice of the engine: a SplitSource with episode_ids(split) for the integrity check, an EpisodeCatalog for intervention resolution. Then unit tests use fakes and the integration tests keep using the real engine. The trigger for a full abstraction would be a second backend."

Why that answer lands: it explains the original reasoning, admits the cost that materialized, and proposes a targeted, incremental fix with a clear trigger for going further.

A4. Two concurrent launches. "Four shared resources. First, the run directory: if it's the same experiment and seed, make_run_dir checks for emptiness and then creates, so both can pass the check and write into one directory. Second, the engine catalog: both call next_version and then write, which isn't atomic, so both may be allocated the same training/vN. Whether the engine rejects the second write I couldn't verify. Third, the JSONL index: appends are unlocked, and model rows can exceed the write buffer, so lines can interleave, and one torn line breaks every later read. Fourth, the GPU: two SmolVLA trainings on an 8 GB card will likely OOM one of them, and that one gets a crash.json. No threads are involved; it's all multi-process. Fixes are an atomic mkdir(exist_ok=False), a lock around version allocation and index appends, and a per-machine GPU lock or a queue."

Why that answer lands: it enumerates every shared resource, gives the precise race for each, separates verified from unverified, and proposes minimal fixes.

A5. "Weakest part of your low-level design?" "The contracts between stages. The edges are typed (YAML goes through Pydantic, the CLI through Typer), but inside the pipeline every handoff degrades to dict[str, Any], and everything on disk is schemaless JSON. That's why a one-word key mismatch made every report print 'n/a' for my own repo's commit, and why an engine change to a stats dict would show up as a KeyError after training. The fix is a Pydantic model per persisted artifact (dataset record, model record, summary, report context) with a schema version, used on both write and read. Second on the list is the implicit state machine: I'd add an atomic status file so crashed, killed and completed runs are distinguishable and retries are possible."

Why that answer lands: it picks a genuinely structural weakness, proves it with a real bug, and gives a fix that addresses the class of bugs, not the instance.

Module 14 Summary — the one page to revise

What this module is. The design view of the package: an acyclic four-layer graph with one composition root (the runner), about a dozen real patterns at known locations, SRP and DIP strained at the runner and the engine boundary, typed edges with untyped interiors, an implicit file-existence state machine, a built-in-exception error model, single-process assumptions and non-idempotent writes.

Five claims to make cold

  1. No cycles. config and tracking are leaves, reports imports nothing internal, and the runner is the composition root. The CLI is the one layering violation.
  2. Patterns: Template Method via callback (Trainer), Transaction Script (runner), Specification (validators), Façade (index and packages), explicit "unavailable" Null Objects.
  3. Contracts are typed at the edges and dicts inside; persisted JSON has no schema or version.
  4. Run state is implicit in which files exist. Killed equals in-progress, and every state blocks retry.
  5. Writes aren't idempotent and nothing dedupes. Concurrency is multi-process only, and unsafe.

Decisions

ChoseOverBecauseCost
Strict downward layering, reports dict-onlyshared typed modelsdecouplinguntyped interiors
Runner as the only composition rootDI containerone wiring pointSRP-heavy runner
Concrete rde dependenceproject protocolsone engine; real-engine testsno seam for unit tests or a second backend
Built-in exceptions, no top handlercustom hierarchysimplicitytracebacks for user errors; late validation
Errors as data in fit and observationraise everywherepartial results surviveinconsistent outside fit
File-existence stateexplicit statusnothing to maintainambiguous states; blocked retries

Volunteer unprompted: the untyped contracts and the n/a key bug as proof; the implicit state machine; the CLI layering violation; non-idempotent writes; the multi-process races.

Numbers and names: 4 layers; 2 custom exceptions; 6 raise typer.Exit sites; 36 raise/except sites outside the CLI; 7 broad or ImportError excepts (all in observation or trainer code); 0 threads; 4 shared resources under concurrency (run directory, catalog, index, GPU).

Sentences worth memorizing

  • "Typed at the edges, dicts in the middle, schemaless on disk. That's the weakest part of my design."
  • "The runner is the composition root and the only seam; that's why it does too much."
  • "My run state machine is implicit in which files exist, so a killed run looks like a running one."
  • "Reads are idempotent, writes aren't, and nothing dedupes."

Module 15 — High-Level Design & Scaling

Files covered: cross-cutting. Numbers come from README.md §1–2, docs/IMPLEMENTATION_PLAN.md §3 and §8, and configs/base.yaml; every derivation from them is shown. No new source files.

Where we are: Module 14 was the inside view (classes, contracts, states). This module is the outside view: what runs where, how long a run takes and where that time goes, what one machine can sustain, what breaks first as the work grows, and which parts would survive a 1000× scale-up unchanged.

The one-line thesis: This is an evidence-limited system, not a compute-limited one. On the README's own numbers, evaluation takes about as long as training, a fine-tuning run sees well under one epoch of its data, and the first thing that breaks as you scale is the statistical validity of comparisons (shared test sets, real seeds), long before GPU, disk or the index.

15.1 Prerequisites — the absolute basics

  • Deployment topology. Which processes run on which machines, and what they share (files, databases, GPUs).
  • Latency budget. A breakdown of where wall-clock time goes in one request, here one experiment run, so you know what to optimize.
  • Throughput / capacity. Work completed per unit time (steps per second, runs per day), and the ceiling set by the scarcest resource.
  • Epoch. One full pass over the training data. Fraction of an epoch = steps × batch_size / training_frames, assuming per-frame sampling. For SmolVLA each sampled frame supervises an action chunk of future actions, so per-frame "coverage" understates supervision somewhat. The engine's exact sampling unit is unverified.
  • Bottleneck analysis. Ordering resources by which one saturates first as load grows. The order tells you what kind of system you have.
  • Scaling ladder. For each order-of-magnitude increase, the single change without which the system fails.
  • Statistical power. The probability that an experiment detects a real effect of a given size. It falls with small test sets, few seeds and noisy metrics.
  • Content-addressed cache. Results stored under a hash of their inputs, so identical requests are served from cache.
  • Multi-tenancy. Several users or teams sharing one system with isolation (namespacing, quotas, access control).
  • Quality vs infrastructure metrics. Quality metrics say whether the results are right (test-set overlap, confidence intervals, baseline ratios). Infrastructure metrics say whether the system is healthy (GPU utilization, run failures, disk).

15.2 Deployment topology, as it actually is

 ┌──────────────────────────── one laptop: RTX 4060 Laptop GPU (8 GB) ──────────────────────────────┐
 │  Linux VM, ~10 GB RAM  (IMPLEMENTATION_PLAN §3: host-visible GPU via nvidia-smi, driver 592.82,  │
 │                          CUDA 13.1; "host virtual machine" — likely WSL2, inferred)             │
 │                                                                                                  │
 │  <parent>/                                                                                       │
 │   ├── episodic-data-engine/   (rde; editable)        ┌── ../.venv      light: no torch           │
 │   ├── vla-finetune/           (this repo; editable)  └── ../.venv-vla  torch(CUDA) + lerobot 0.4.4│
 │   │     ├── runs/<date>/<name>/seed_<n>/   (checkpoints, metrics, reports)    git-ignored        │
 │   │     └── experiments/*.jsonl            (cross-run index)                  git-ignored        │
 │   └── rde data dir (settings.paths): catalog DB + Parquet frames + video files  (engine-owned)    │
 │                                                                                                  │
 │  ~/.cache/huggingface  ← lerobot/smolvla_base weights (downloaded once; network on first run)    │
 │                                                                                                  │
 │  process model: ONE `mtrain` process at a time, single GPU, no daemon, no server, no queue      │
 └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          external: Hugging Face Hub (base checkpoint, source datasets) — first run only

Decision: single-machine, single-process, file-based. It's forced by C1 (one laptop GPU) and C9 (one or two researchers, short runs) from the Module 13 capstone. There's nothing to coordinate, so there's nothing to deploy. Cost: everything in Module 14's concurrency table becomes a problem the moment a second process appears.

15.3 The lifecycle of one run, with a latency budget

The README gives training time and per-frame inference time. I derived evaluation time from those. Lines marked est. are estimates I can't measure here.

SO-100 baseline_full (1,000 steps, batch 4, 1,908 test frames, about 1,900 val frames, inference about 48 ms/frame at batch 16):

StageTimeDerivation
env + git + config (torch import, CUDA init)~2–5 sest.
intervention: engine build of training/vNseconds–tens of sest., engine-side, unverified
split check + profile (12 histogram scans: 6 action + 6 state)secondsest. (DuckDB on inline columns only)
training (incl. 10 × 32-frame validations, 10 checkpoint saves)324 sREADME Table 2
of which validation~15 s10 × 32 frames × ~48 ms
baselines (NN index on ~15k train frames, score 1,908)secondsest. (CPU, state-only)
SmolVLA test overall~92 s1,908 × 48 ms
task_0 slice (single task ⇒ full duplicate, Module 7)~92 ssame frames re-inferred
three length slices (partition the test set)~92 ssame frames re-inferred
temporal re-read, feedback, reports, figuressecondsest.
total~10–11 min
evaluation share~45% (~275 s of ~610 s)

ALOHA aloha_coffee_baseline (1,000 steps, 4,400 test frames, about 86 ms/frame, slices: [per_length_bucket]):

StageTimeDerivation
training576 sREADME Table 2
test overall~378 s4,400 × 86 ms
length slice: all 4 test episodes are 1,100 frames, so one bucket = a full duplicate~378 sModule 7 tie collapse
total~22 min
evaluation share~57%, more than training

What the budget says:

  1. Evaluation, not training, dominates wall-clock. About 45% on SO-100 and about 57% on ALOHA. Half of that evaluation time is pure redundancy: the duplicate task_0 on single-task SO-100, and the all-in-one-bucket length slice on ALOHA. Predict-once slicing (Module 7) would cut total run time by about 25–30% with zero loss of information.
  2. These are lower bounds. The README's per-frame figure "excludes data loading", and ALOHA's frames come from four AV1 video streams (the config comment says "AV1, 4 cameras"). Decode time is extra and unmeasured.
  3. Validation is cheap (about 5% of training time) precisely because it's capped at 32 frames. That's the Module 6 trade: cheap, and too noisy to select well.

15.4 Capacity arithmetic: one laptop

Training throughput (SO-100): 1,000 steps / 324 s ≈ 3.1 steps/s, or about 12 samples/s at batch 4 (an upper bound on step time, since 324 s includes validation and saves).

How much of the data does one run see?

DatasetTrain episodes≈ Train framesSamples seen (1,000 steps × 4)≈ Fraction of an epoch
SO-1003838 × ~400 ≈ 15,200 (episodes 326–475 frames)4,000≈ 0.26
SO-100, extended (+1,000)38≈ 15,2008,000 total≈ 0.53
ALOHA3232 × 1,100 ≈ 35,200 (test episodes are 1,100; assumed similar for train)4,000≈ 0.11

This is the most important number in this module. The headline runs train on a quarter of an epoch (SO-100) and a ninth of an epoch (ALOHA), assuming per-frame sampling and before counting action-chunk supervision. Read that next to the Module 7 result:

  • On SO-100, doubling steps (0.26 → 0.53 epoch) cut test MSE by 18%. The model was still learning.
  • On ALOHA, at about 0.11 epoch, SmolVLA is 2× worse than nearest-neighbour retrieval, which by construction has "seen" 100% of the training frames.

So "SmolVLA loses to retrieval on ALOHA" is, plausibly, "SmolVLA was undertrained relative to a baseline that memorizes everything." That's a better-supported and more useful interpretation than "retrieval is just strong". It also suggests the cheapest next experiment: ALOHA at 5–10k steps, about 50–100 min on the same laptop by linear extrapolation. The caveat: if the engine samples action-chunk windows rather than frames, the effective coverage is higher, and I can't verify which.

Memory headroom: peak GPU 2.2–3.0 GB of 8 GB (allocator-reported, Module 6), and peak host 3.0–3.3 GB of about 10 GB. The 8 GB GPU isn't the binding constraint at batch 4. Batch 8 or 16 probably fits (unverified; activations scale roughly linearly with batch, and the allocator figure excludes the CUDA context). Batch size 4 is conservative, not forced, by the measured numbers. That revises the C1 row in the Module 13 capstone. The constraint motivated it, but the measurements show slack.

Runs per day, serially: about 10–11 min (SO-100) or about 22 min (ALOHA) per run → about 130 SO-100 runs/day or about 65 ALOHA runs/day at 100% utilization, so realistically a few dozen. Concurrency: 2–3 GB peak per training process means two would fit in 8 GB, but Module 14 showed two concurrent runs race on the run directory, catalog and index. Capacity is single-process by correctness, not by hardware.

Disk per run (est.): SmolVLA is publicly about 450M parameters, so a checkpoint is roughly 0.9 GB in 16-bit or 1.8 GB in 32-bit (unverified which). Kept per run: last 3 periodic, plus best if older, plus final (a duplicate of step_1000, Module 6), so 4–5 checkpoints ≈ 4–9 GB per run. At a few dozen runs, that's hundreds of GB. Disk fills long before the GPU saturates.

15.5 Bottleneck analysis, in the order things break

Scale the number of experiments (the axis a research harness actually grows on):

OrderBottleneckBreaks atSymptom
1Statistical validity of comparisonsthe first comparison between two interventionsdifferent test sets per intervention (Module 4), fake seeds (Module 11), no intervals (Module 7), feedback tuned on test (Module 8): conclusions are wrong now, at 3 runs
2Evaluation time~10 runsabout half of each run is inference, half of that redundant
3Disk~20–50 runs4–9 GB per run in checkpoints, with no retention policy beyond keep-last-3
4Single-GPU serial execution~50–100 runs per weeka queue of runs; no scheduler
5Process-level correctnessthe first time anyone parallelizesrun-directory TOCTOU, catalog version race, torn JSONL lines (Module 14)
6Index and catalog as filesthousands of runsfull-scan queries, no locking, no status
7Data path (video decode, loader)10–100× dataset sizedecode-bound evaluation; the profile's per-dimension DuckDB scans grow

What the ordering says about the system: the first failure isn't a resource at all. It's that the harness can't yet produce a trustworthy comparison between two experiments. The second is an algorithmic waste (redundant evaluation), and only then come the physical resources. That ordering identifies a research-methodology system: its scaling problem is evidence quality per GPU-hour, not GPU-hours. The engineering priority list follows directly: fix the comparison protocol before buying hardware.

15.6 The scaling ladder

Each step names the one change without which that step fails, plus what comes along with it.

Today (1×): about 3 published runs, 1 researcher, 1 laptop GPU, 40–48 episodes.

10× (tens of runs, a real intervention study, about 500 episodes).

  • The one thing: a frozen, shared test split plus predict-once evaluation. Split the full pool once, apply interventions only to its train split (Module 4), score every run on the same episodes, and slice stored predictions instead of re-inferring (Module 7).
  • With it: real seed propagation (Module 11), episode-bootstrap intervals from test_outcomes.jsonl (Module 7), feedback on validation (Module 8), and a checkpoint retention policy (keep best plus final, drop the step_N/final duplicate).
  • Without it, ten runs produce ten numbers that can't be compared.

100× (hundreds of runs, 2–5 researchers, a few GPUs).

  • The one thing: an explicit run state machine plus a scheduler. Write status.json atomically at every transition (Module 14 §14.7), and have one job queue that owns the GPUs and serializes catalog version allocation.
  • With it: the index moves to SQLite or DuckDB (or MLflow as a sink, Module 3), with per-user namespaces for experiment_name; checkpoints move to object storage with retention; config gets extra="forbid" and fail-fast name validation (Module 11), because a typo now wastes someone else's queue slot; and there's CI with both repos and a pinned engine version (Module 1).
  • Without it, concurrent runs corrupt the shared catalog and index.

1000× (thousands of runs, tens of thousands of episodes, multi-GPU training).

  • The one thing: the data path. Pre-decoded or streaming frame storage and a parallel loader owned by the engine (the num_workers and mixed_precision fields that exist in the config today and are wired to nothing, Module 2), plus distributed training (DDP or FSDP) inside the engine's fit.
  • With it: the engine catalog becomes a server database with transactional version allocation; content-addressed training/vN (identical builds reused, Module 4); sweeps become DAG jobs with stage caching (Module 11); profiling goes from per-dimension full scans to one pass with all dimensions; and real closed-loop evaluation (simulator farm), because at this scale offline MSE stops being an acceptable proxy for a decision.
  • Without it, every run is decode-bound and a single GPU per run can't finish an epoch in useful time.

15.7 Caching strategy

WhatCached today?OpportunityKey
Base checkpoint downloadyes, by the Hugging Face cache (external)—Hub revision
Training dataset versionno: every run mints a new training/vN (Module 4)content-addressed build reusehash(input version, subset, splits, seed, weights)
Baseline fits (NN index)no: refit every runcache per training/vNtraining version plus baseline name
Test predictionsno: re-inferred per slice and per re-scorepredict once, slice in memory; store vectorscheckpoint hash plus version plus split plus sampling seed
Decoded framesengine-side, unknownpre-decoded tensor cacheversion plus camera plus resolution
Profileno: recomputed per run on an immutable versioncache per version (immutable, so never stale)dataset version

Principle: because versions are immutable, everything keyed by a version is cacheable forever. That's an unusually strong position, and it's the payoff of Module 4's design. The system doesn't exploit it yet.

15.8 Multi-tenancy

Today: single tenant, none of the mechanisms.

  • experiment_name is a global namespace in runs/, so two users' baseline_full collide (FileExistsError) or, worse, interleave under TOCTOU.
  • The engine catalog is shared, with no access control (engine-side, unverified).
  • The index has no user field. environment.json records hostname but not the user.
  • make_run_dir's no-overwrite guard is the only isolation.

Minimum for multi-tenancy: a user (or team) path segment in runs/ and an index column; catalog writes through a service that serializes allocation; and per-user GPU quotas in the scheduler. None of this is warranted before the 100× step, and the C9 constraint (team of one or two) justifies its absence.

15.9 Observability: what to measure, quality first

Today: files only. metrics/train.jsonl (loss, LR, grad norm per step), validation.jsonl, checkpoint_errors.jsonl, crash.json, environment.json, peak memory and wall clock. There's no logging (Module 3), no live view, and no cross-run dashboard.

What I'd add, in priority order. Quality before infrastructure:

#MetricTypeWhy it's first
1test-set identity per run (hash of test episode IDs) and "comparable?" flag in comparequalitythe #1 bottleneck; makes the moving test set visible
2SmolVLA / nearest-neighbour MSE ratio, not just vs mean-actionqualitythe honest baseline; mean-action ratios (2.6%) flatter
3bootstrap 95% CI over test episodes for MSEquality5 test episodes; every difference needs an interval
4inference-noise band (score overall twice, report the spread)qualitymeasured once by accident (up to 5%); should be deliberate
5val–test gap for the selected checkpointquality13× today (Module 6); flags a broken selection signal
6epochs seen = steps × batch / train framesqualityexplains under-training (0.11 epoch on ALOHA)
7per-slice frame and episode countsqualitystops "long episodes are easier" from 1–2 episodes
8run status distribution (completed / crashed / killed)infrastructureneeds the state machine
9steps/s, eval frames/s, eval share of run timeinfrastructureevaluation dominates
10GPU utilization (not just allocator peak), disk per runinfrastructureheadroom and retention

Why quality metrics come first: this system's characteristic failure isn't a crash, which is loud and already mostly handled. It's a plausible, wrong conclusion produced silently: a comparison across different test sets, a variance from fake replicates, a recommendation that can never say "no action". Infrastructure dashboards would show a perfectly healthy system producing those. The metrics that would have caught every serious finding in this manual are quality metrics.

15.10 Cost model

Formula per run: cost ≈ GPU_hours × GPU_price + disk_GB × storage_price × retention + researcher_minutes × hourly_rate.

On the laptop (today): the marginal GPU cost is electricity, which is negligible. The real costs are researcher time and disk.

Cloud-equivalent order of magnitude (assumption: a single entry-level datacenter GPU at roughly $0.5–1 per hour; real prices vary by provider and region, and I haven't looked them up):

  • SO-100 run, about 0.18 h → about $0.10–0.20 per run
  • ALOHA run, about 0.37 h → about $0.20–0.40 per run
  • A 10-point data-fraction sweep with 3 seeds = 30 runs → about $5–10
  • Checkpoint storage at 4–9 GB per run quickly exceeds compute cost if kept indefinitely

Where money is actually wasted, in order:

  1. Wrong conclusions (re-running studies whose comparisons were invalid). That's unbounded, and it's the real cost.
  2. Redundant evaluation, about 25–30% of every run's GPU time (15.3).
  3. Duplicate and retained checkpoints (final = step_1000; keep-3 regardless of value).
  4. Config typos found after training (Module 11): one full run each.

What survives cost-wise: at these prices, compute is cheap enough that adding seeds and longer training costs dollars. The barrier to trustworthy results is protocol, not budget.

15.11 What survives unchanged: the good bones

These would carry through every rung of the ladder without redesign:

  1. Data identity by immutable version, with lineage (Modules 2 and 4). It scales from a laptop to a catalog server unchanged, and it's what makes caching safe forever.
  2. Interventions expressed as engine transforms with recorded reasons (Module 4). Only the order (split first) needs changing, not the mechanism.
  3. The fully resolved config persisted with every run (Module 2). It becomes the job specification for a scheduler as-is.
  4. The refusal to fabricate as data ("unavailable", verified: false, crash.json, rollout records). That's an honesty contract that only gets more valuable as more people read the results.
  5. Validation-only checkpoint selection, structurally enforced (Module 6). It's the right discipline; only the sample needs fixing.
  6. Per-frame outcomes with full provenance (test_outcomes.jsonl, Module 11). That's the raw material for bootstraps, per-joint analysis and predict-once slicing.
  7. Plain-file artifacts readable without this code (Module 3). DuckDB over the same JSONL is the 100× index with no format migration.
  8. Git identity of both repositories (Module 3). Only the path resolution needs fixing.
  9. Dry-run-by-default config resolution from the real catalog (Module 12). It's the human gate a multi-user system needs.
  10. Lazy, import-light CLI (Module 12). It stays fast as the dependency stack grows.

The pattern: the mechanisms (identity, lineage, honesty, provenance) are the bones, and they scale. The statistical protocol (one test set, real seeds, intervals, val-driven curation) is what's missing, and it's cheap to add.

15.12 Honest gaps to own before an interviewer finds them

  1. Evaluation is about 45–57% of run time, half of it redundant (derived from README numbers). Consequence: about 25–30% of GPU time per run is wasted.
  2. Headline runs see about 0.26 (SO-100) and about 0.11 (ALOHA) of an epoch (per-frame assumption). Consequence: the ALOHA loss to retrieval is plausibly under-training, and the README's "retrieval is a strong baseline" framing under-states this.
  3. Batch 4 uses 2.2–3.0 of 8 GB. Consequence: "constrained by the GPU" is softer than it sounds. Larger batches were plausibly available (unverified).
  4. Disk, not GPU, is the first physical limit (4–9 GB per run, estimated). Consequence: there's no retention policy beyond keep-last-3, plus a duplicate final.
  5. No caching despite immutable versions. Consequence: every run recomputes builds, baselines and profiles that could be reused forever.
  6. No observability beyond files, and no quality metrics (test-set identity, NN ratio, intervals, val–test gap, epochs seen). Consequence: the system looks healthy while producing incomparable results.
  7. Single tenant by design (global experiment names, shared catalog, no user field). Consequence: fine until a second researcher.
  8. All timing beyond training and per-frame inference is estimated, since data loading and video decode aren't measured anywhere. Consequence: the latency budget's lower bounds are real, and its totals are approximations.

The shape to carry into the Fixes & Roadmap module

 1 laptop · 8 GB GPU (2–3 GB used) · ~10 GB RAM · 1 process · files + engine catalog
 run (SO-100):  train 324 s  ▸  eval ~275 s (½ redundant)  ▸  total ~10–11 min  ▸  ~0.26 epoch
 run (ALOHA):   train 576 s  ▸  eval ~756 s (½ redundant)  ▸  total ~22 min     ▸  ~0.11 epoch
 breaks in order:  ① comparison validity ② eval time ③ disk ④ serial GPU ⑤ multi-process races
                   ⑥ file index/catalog ⑦ data path
 ladder:  10× frozen test split + predict-once   100× run state machine + scheduler
          1000× data path (decode/loader) + distributed fit
 bones that survive: version identity & lineage · resolved config · "unavailable" honesty ·
                     val-only selection · per-frame provenance · plain-file artifacts

Drill — answer from memory, files closed

  1. Give the latency budget of one SO-100 run and say where you'd cut first.
  2. How much of an epoch does a 1,000-step run see on each dataset, and what does that change about how you read the ALOHA result?
  3. Why didn't you just move this onto a cluster with a job scheduler and MLflow from the start?
  4. What breaks in production: the team grows from one researcher to five sharing two GPUs. List what fails, in order.
  5. Interviewer framing: "How would this system scale to a thousand times more data and experiments?"

Appendix A — Model answers to the drill

A1. SO-100 latency budget. "Training is 324 seconds for 1,000 steps, including ten 32-frame validations and ten checkpoint saves. Evaluation is the surprise. Test inference is about 48 milliseconds a frame over 1,908 frames, roughly 90 seconds per pass, and the default slices run three passes: overall, a per-task slice that's a full duplicate because SO-100 has one task, and three length slices that together cover the test set again. So about 275 seconds of evaluation, against 324 of training, around 45% of a ten-to-eleven-minute run. I'd cut there first. Predict once and slice the stored outcomes, and evaluation drops to about 90 seconds with no information lost. That's about a quarter of total run time, recovered with no hardware change. Those evaluation figures are lower bounds, since the per-frame number excludes data loading."

Why that answer lands: it derives the budget from real numbers, finds that evaluation, not training, is the big lever, and quantifies the fix. That's capacity reasoning, not guesswork.

A2. Epochs seen. "At batch 4, 1,000 steps is 4,000 samples. SO-100's 38 training episodes are around 15,000 frames, so that's about a quarter of an epoch, and the extended run about half. ALOHA's 32 training episodes at around 1,100 frames are about 35,000 frames, so 1,000 steps is about a ninth of an epoch. That assumes per-frame sampling. If the engine samples action-chunk windows, coverage is higher, which I couldn't verify. It changes how I read ALOHA: SmolVLA losing two-to-one to nearest-neighbour retrieval is a model that's seen about 11% of the data, against a baseline that has memorized all of it. Plus, doubling training on SO-100 gave 18%. So the defensible reading is 'undertrained', not 'retrieval is just better'. And the cheapest next experiment is ALOHA at five to ten thousand steps, about an hour on the same laptop."

Why that answer lands: a simple calculation reframes the project's most uncomfortable result into a testable hypothesis with a costed next step, and it states the assumption.

A3. Why not a cluster, scheduler and MLflow from day one? "Because none of the first bottlenecks are infrastructure. With one or two researchers and ten-minute runs, a scheduler and a tracking server would have added operational surface while the real problems were methodological: interventions scored on different test sets, seeds that didn't propagate, no intervals. Infrastructure doesn't fix any of those, and it would have produced the same incomparable numbers faster. The file-based design also kept the artifacts readable by DuckDB and pandas, so moving to MLflow or SQLite later is an export, not a migration. The trigger for a scheduler is the second concurrent user, because that's when the run-directory, catalog and index races I know about start firing."

Why that answer lands: it argues from the measured bottleneck order, shows the infrastructure wouldn't have helped the real problem, and names the precise trigger.

A4. Five researchers, two GPUs. "First, results stay incomparable, the same as with one researcher but with more of them. Then name collisions: experiment names are a global namespace in runs/, so two people's baseline_full either collide on FileExistsError or, if launched at the same moment, race into one directory. Then the shared engine catalog: version allocation isn't atomic, so concurrent runs can claim the same training version. Then the JSONL index: unlocked appends of large model rows can interleave, and one torn line breaks every later read of the registry. Then the GPUs: with no scheduler, two runs can land on one card. Each needs 2–3 GB, so they'd fit, but they'd contend. Then disk fills, at four to nine gigabytes of checkpoints per run. The fixes, in that order: frozen test split and real seeds, per-user namespaces, a status file plus a job queue that also serializes catalog writes, a SQLite or MLflow index, and a checkpoint retention policy."

Why that answer lands: it's ordered by when each failure actually occurs, distinguishes correctness races from resource contention, and maps each to a fix.

A5. "How would this scale to 1000×?" "In three steps, each with one non-negotiable change. At 10×, tens of runs and hundreds of episodes, the blocker is comparability, so: freeze one shared test split, apply interventions only to train, propagate real seeds, and predict once for evaluation. At 100×, multiple users and GPUs, it's correctness under concurrency, so: an explicit run state machine and a scheduler that owns GPUs and serializes catalog writes, with the index in SQLite or MLflow. At 1000×, the data path, so: pre-decoded or streaming frames, a parallel loader and distributed training in the engine, plus a transactional catalog service and real closed-loop evaluation, because at that scale offline MSE is too weak to make decisions on. What doesn't change at any step is the core: immutable versioned data with lineage, the resolved config as the job spec, per-frame outcomes with provenance, and the refusal to fabricate. Those are the bones."

Why that answer lands: each rung has exactly one named blocker and its fix, the ordering matches the bottleneck analysis, and it ends by naming what's already right. That shows you can tell the parts that need to change from the parts that don't.

Module 15 Summary — the one page to revise

What this module is. The system seen from outside: one laptop, one process, files plus an engine catalog; about 10–11 minutes per SO-100 run and about 22 minutes per ALOHA run, with evaluation the larger or near-equal share; under one epoch of training per run; and a bottleneck order that begins with statistical validity, not hardware.

Five claims to make cold

  1. Evaluation is about 45% (SO-100) and about 57% (ALOHA) of run time, and half of it is redundant slicing. Predict-once saves about 25–30% of each run.
  2. 1,000 steps at batch 4 is about 0.26 epoch on SO-100 and about 0.11 on ALOHA. The ALOHA loss to retrieval is plausibly under-training.
  3. Peak GPU is 2.2–3.0 GB of 8 GB, so batch 4 is conservative, not forced.
  4. Bottleneck order: comparison validity → eval time → disk → serial GPU → process races → file index → data path. It's an evidence-limited system.
  5. The ladder: 10× frozen test split plus predict-once; 100× state machine plus scheduler; 1000× data path plus distributed fit.

Decisions

ChoseOverBecauseCost
Single machine, single process, filescluster + scheduler + tracking serverC1, C9; nothing to coordinateevery concurrency race latent
Batch 4larger batch8 GB cautionunder 3 GB used; fewer samples per second
1,000-step budgetepoch-based budgetfast iteration0.11–0.26 epoch; under-training
No cachingcontent-addressed cachessimplicityrecompute despite immutable versions
File-only observabilitylogging plus dashboardsno service to runno quality metrics; no live view
No multi-tenancynamespaces and quotasteam of one or twocollisions at user two

Volunteer unprompted: evaluation dominates run time; the under-one-epoch arithmetic and the ALOHA reinterpretation; disk before GPU; immutable versions cached nowhere; quality metrics before infrastructure metrics.

Numbers and names: 324 / 385 / 576 s training; 47–50 ms/frame (SO-100) and 86 ms/frame (ALOHA) at eval batch 16; 1,908 and 4,400 test frames; about 3.1 steps/s; peak GPU 2.2–3.0 GB / 8 GB; host 3.0–3.3 GB / ~10 GB; SO-100 about 15,200 train frames (0.26 epoch), ALOHA about 35,200 (0.11 epoch); checkpoints about 0.9–1.8 GB each (estimated), 4–9 GB per run; cloud-equivalent about $0.10–0.40 per run (assumed $0.5–1/GPU-hour); lerobot 0.4.4, CUDA 13.1, driver 592.82.

Sentences worth memorizing

  • "Evaluation, not training, dominates my run time, and half of it was redundant."
  • "My ALOHA run saw about a ninth of an epoch; losing to a baseline that memorized everything says 'undertrained' before it says 'retrieval is better'."
  • "The first thing that breaks when this scales isn't the GPU. It's whether two of my numbers are comparable."
  • "Immutable versions mean everything keyed by a version is cacheable forever; I don't cache anything yet."

Module 16 — Fixes & Roadmap

Files covered: the running gaps register (docs/study/_gaps.md, about 200 entries across Modules 1–15), consolidated. Each fix names the file or files to change.

Where we are: Fifteen modules each ended with "honest gaps". This module turns them into a plan. They're deduplicated, so a gap found in three modules appears once, and they're prioritized by what they do to the project's conclusions, not by how easy they are. The module ends with a one-day shortlist, a feature roadmap, and an anti-roadmap of tempting changes that would make the system worse.

The one-line thesis: About a dozen fixes matter more than everything else, and almost all of them are about making two results comparable (shared test split, real seeds, validation-driven decisions, strict config, non-destructive re-scoring), not about features or infrastructure. Most of them are hours of work, not weeks.

16.1 Prerequisites — the absolute basics

  • Priority levels used here.
    • P0: the gap invalidates a result or a claim the project makes, now, at current scale. Fix it before reporting anything new.
    • P1: the gap causes wrong output, lost work or misleading artifacts under realistic use, or a doc states something false.
    • P2: hygiene, robustness, cost, or something that only bites at larger scale.
  • Effort estimates assume someone who knows this codebase, including writing a test for the fix: XS < 30 min, S about 1–2 h, M about half a day, L 1–3 days. They're estimates, and engine-side work could change them.
  • Regression test. A test written to fail before the fix and pass after it. Every P0/P1 below names one.
  • Anti-roadmap. Changes that look like progress but would make the system worse at its current stage, each with the condition under which the answer flips.

16.2 P0: fix before reporting anything new

Why these seven: each one, unfixed, produces a number that's wrong or a comparison that's invalid today. P0-1 is the only one larger than half a day, and it's the one the experiment design depends on.
