D23README §2 point 3: "retrieval is a strong baseline" as the explanationplausibly under-training (≈0.11 epoch)add the epoch arithmetic (M15-2)
D24plot_results.py: "reproducible from disk"selection markers hard-codedread model.json (M10-8)

| D25 | README Table 5: "A fraction of 0.0 is honoured as zero episodes" | the builder raises on zero episodes | correct, or implement P1-13 (M4-2) | | D26 | README Table 5: imbalanced = "under-representation of one regime" | removes the regime entirely | correct the wording (M4-3) | | D27 | README §3.3: "a process killed mid-write cannot leave a corrupt 'best' model" | verification is presence-only and can't run in a killed process | downgrade (M6-9) | | D28 | README §2.4: "scattered ... points to a model or coverage limitation" as a finding | the only possible output at p90 with 4–5 episodes | reframe (M8-3) |

Effort: each doc correction is XS. Together they're about 2 hours, and they buy the most credibility per minute in this whole module.

16.4 P2: hygiene, robustness, cost, scale

Grouped, with files. Each is XS–S unless noted.

  • Config and identity: a config hash separate from the run ID (M2-6); one home for defaults (M2-10); a discriminated-union intervention (M2-12); a single 0.5 default (M2-13); bounded weights (M2-15); dated resume path → a run reference (M2-11). Files: config/schema.py, config/loader.py, configs.
  • Tracking: an atomic mkdir(exist_ok=False) (M3-4); environment.json adds driver, cuDNN, transformers, pip freeze, and renames compute capability (M3-7); delete logs/ or tee stdout (M3-9); index rows gain status and schema_version (M3-10, M14-1); strict JSON types instead of default=str (M3-11); route all writes through RunDir (M3-12); default dirs not from parents[3] (M3-13).
  • Interventions: validate explicit IDs and weights (M4-7); content-addressed builds to stop version proliferation (M4-10, M15-5, M); a clearer unsupported-variable error (M4-12); a stale docstring (M4-11).
  • Profile: profile the train and test splits separately (M5-6); a real CV (M5-5); report out-of-range values and NULLs (M5-8); drop the duplicate metric (M5-9); a notes fix (M5-10); to_dict via fields() (M5-11).
  • Trainer: resume checked against the dataset, verify before load (M6-6); optional optimizer-state save for long runs (M6-7); skip the duplicate final when aligned, verify final, move it inside try (M6-8); a content integrity check (M6-9); prune bookkeeping (M6-10); move the empty-train guard to the top (M6-13); report allocator vs reserved GPU memory (M6-12).
  • Evaluation: rename per-episode "mse" (M7-5); drop per_task from base.yaml for single-task data (M7-6); a note when slices are skipped (M7-8); robust frame_index (M7-9); frame count in the error distribution (M7-11).
  • Feedback: consume or drop retraining_selection (M8-8); configurable thresholds (M8-10); rename affected_slice (M8-9).
  • Deployment: an observation-to-Sample adapter (roadmap); fix the latency breakdown (measured total_ms, sync, warm-up, percentiles) (M9-5); registry stages (M9-6, roadmap); identity fields (M9-8).
  • Reports and figures: object-oriented matplotlib without the global backend (M10-10); guard plotting so it can't abort a completed run (M10-13); wire or delete the unused plot and matrix functions (M10-9); stop the dead manifest.json reads (M10-14).
  • Runner and CLI: a GPU guard (M11-6); NN baseline and total time in summary.json (M11-7); relative run_id (M11-8); evaluate on training.device (M11-14); crash.json-aware validate and analyze-failures (M12-10); dedupe _target_episodes (M12-7); resolution-version check (M12-8); inspect default (M12-13); remove script wrappers in favour of python -m (M12-14); timestamp rollout records (M12-16).
  • Packaging: a top-level __init__.py (M1-6); drop allow-direct-references, unused pytest-cov and dead psutil fallbacks (M1-8); promote the engine's private functions used by tests (M1-4); session-scoped read-only fixtures (M13-6).
  • Scale (defer until the trigger in Module 15): a scheduler, a SQLite or MLflow index, multi-tenancy namespaces, a checkpoint retention policy and object store (M15-4, M15-7), measuring data loading and decode (M15-8).

16.5 The five-fix shortlist: about one day

Chosen for maximum change in what the project can truthfully claim, per hour. Each comes with its regression test.

OrderFixWhy this oneTime
1P0-3 strict, fail-fast config (extra="forbid", Literals, stage-0 name checks, cadence alignment)closes the whole class of "typo silently changes the run" and "typo found after training"1.5 h
2P0-2 real seed propagation plus identity re-stamp plus a seed loop in the CLIturns the documented multi-seed workflow from fake replicates into real ones; unlocks every variance claim1.5 h
3P0-6 non-destructive re-scoring (versioned rescore directory, split-named files)stops published numbers from being overwritten; removes the doc-endorsed val-as-test footgun1.5 h
4P1-1 predict-once slicingremoves stochastic non-reconciliation and about 25–30% of each run's GPU time2 h
5P1-15 + P1-16 + D1–D28 doc and report truthfulness pass (git key, SmolVLA MAE, per-dimension MAE, limitations, every false doc sentence)the cheapest credibility in the project; every reader-facing claim becomes true1.5 h

Total: about 8 hours. Deliberately not on the list despite being P0: P0-1, the frozen test split (1–3 days, may touch the engine), and P0-7, the frozen validation subset (half a day). They're the first two items of the roadmap below, not afternoon work.

After this day you can say: "Config errors fail before the GPU starts, seeds are real, results are never overwritten, slices reconcile with the overall number, and every statement in the docs and reports matches the code."

16.6 Feature roadmap

Phase A — Validity (1–2 weeks). Everything a reviewer needs before believing a comparison.

  1. Frozen test split; interventions on train only (P0-1).
  2. Frozen stratified validation subset (P0-7); feedback on validation (P0-5).
  3. Bootstrap CIs and paired per-episode comparisons on the shared split (P1-2).
  4. Three seeds per configuration, as standard.
  5. One length-bucket definition (P1-11), and train/test-split coverage in the profile.

Phase B — Experiments the data can answer (1–2 weeks of GPU-evenings).

  1. ALOHA at 5k–10k steps (≈0.5–1 epoch) to test the under-training hypothesis (Module 15). This is the highest-information experiment available.
  2. A batch-size check (8/16) for throughput, given 2–3 GB peak of 8 GB.
  3. A nested data-efficiency curve on the frozen split (25/50/75/100%, 3 seeds each) with the 0% point = the base checkpoint.
  4. A rebalanced run on SO-100's length bucket, compared paired against the full run on the shared test split.
  5. A held-out-session or held-out-condition split, if the data supports one, as the first real generalization test (Module 5's content-leakage point).

Phase C — Reliability (about 1 week). The run state machine (P1-3), atomic I/O (P1-4), crash coverage (P1-5), invariant and CLI tests (P1-23 to P1-25), CI plus lockfile plus pinned engine (P1-26), the factored evaluation stage (P1-21), and reports rebuildable from disk (P1-22).

Phase D — Toward closed loop (open-ended).

  1. An observation adapter (raw camera frames, joint vector, instruction → model input) so DeployedModel takes robot data, not Samples (Module 9).
  2. An action-chunk execution loop with control-rate handling.
  3. A real latency benchmark: single sample, warmed up, synchronized, p50/p95/p99.
  4. A simulator task matching one embodiment, if one exists. I haven't checked which LeRobot simulation environments match these datasets. That gives the first non-"unavailable" rollout number.

Phase E — Scale (only on the Module 15 triggers). Scheduler plus GPU lock; SQLite, DuckDB or MLflow index; content-addressed builds; retention policy and object storage; per-user namespaces.

16.7 Anti-roadmap: tempting changes that would make it worse

Tempting changeWhy it's worse nowFlips when
Adopt MLflow or W&Badds a service and a second source of truth while the real problem is comparability; dashboards would show incomparable runs side by sidea second concurrent researcher, or hundreds of runs
Build a FastAPI inference serverpredict takes dataset Samples; a server would expose a replay harness over HTTPa robot controller in another process needs to call the model
Replace JSONL with Postgresmigration and operations cost for tens of rows; loses the "readable without our code" propertyconcurrent writers, or thousands of runs (and even then, DuckDB-over-JSONL first)
Move config to Hydrathe file-per-experiment record is the experiment log; Hydra's CLI overrides encourage unrecorded variantshyperparameter sweeps become routine (and outputs auto-record the resolved config)
Mock the engine so tests run without itthe bugs live at the engine boundary; a mock encodes our assumptions (it would never reveal filter-then-split)never for boundary tests; yes for pure logic (pruning, thresholds, rendering)
Add more slices (per joint, per phase, per object)5 test episodes; slices are already 1–2 episodes; more slices mean more noise read as findingsthe test set reaches tens of episodes, with CIs per slice
Report success_rate as task success, or estimate rollout success from offline MSEbreaks the project's central honesty contractnever, without closed-loop evaluation
Close the feedback loop automatically (apply the recommendation and retrain unattended)the loop runs on test, can't say "no action", and can't produce its defect branch, so automation would overfit the test set at machine speedfeedback runs on val with an absolute gate, and a fresh untouched test set exists
Switch to a bigger or different modelthe comparison protocol can't yet tell whether this model is undertrainedafter Phase A and the ALOHA longer-training experiment
Replace the percentile threshold with an absolute oneloses ranking that's robust to normalizationnever replace; add an absolute gate alongside
Delete the split-integrity check as tautologicalit's free, documents the invariant, and guards against a representation changenever delete; strengthen it (P1-10)
A blanket --force to overwrite run directoriesdestroys the write-once guarantee for good runs tooallow --force only from status: failed (P1-3)
Rebalance by duplicating episodes on diskheavier, less traceable, and the same effect as weightsnever; weights are the honest mechanism
Always save optimizer stateabout 3× checkpoint size on a disk-bound laptopmulti-hour or preemptible runs that need exact resume
Add a DAG framework10–20-minute runs don't need caching or resume infrastructuremulti-hour runs or sweeps over ~10 runs; first try "skip a stage if its artifact is complete"

16.8 Honest gaps to own before an interviewer finds them

  1. The P0 list is long for a project with three published runs. Consequence: the README's results predate the protocol fixes, so present them as "pipeline validation runs", not as findings, until P0-1, P0-2 and P0-7 land.
  2. P0-1 may need engine work. A transform that preserves split attributes might not exist in the engine. Consequence: the most important fix isn't fully within this repository's control, and its estimate is uncertain.
  3. Estimates are untested. None of these fixes has been attempted, and the engine isn't available here. Consequence: treat efforts as planning numbers.
  4. Some README numbers would change after the fixes (any result scored on a moving test set, any selection from 32 frames). Consequence: be ready to say which published numbers are robust (continued-training on the identical split) and which aren't.

The shape to carry into the Interview Kit

 P0 (claims invalid now):  frozen test split · real seeds · strict/fail-fast config · imbalanced no-op
                           guard · feedback on val · non-destructive rescoring · frozen val subset
 P1 (wrong/lost/false):    predict-once · bootstrap CI · run state machine · atomic I/O · crash coverage
                           · health cross-check · coverage metric · compare by key + test-hash + seeds
                           · report truth · 28 doc corrections · invariant/CLI tests · CI + lockfile
 P2 (hygiene/scale):       ~60 items, XS–S each, scale items deferred to Module 15 triggers
 1-day shortlist:          config ▸ seeds ▸ rescoring ▸ predict-once ▸ truthfulness pass   (~8 h)
 Roadmap:                  A validity ▸ B experiments (ALOHA 5–10k steps first) ▸ C reliability
                           ▸ D closed loop ▸ E scale-on-trigger

Drill — answer from memory, files closed

  1. If you had one day, which five fixes would you make, and why that order?
  2. Why is the frozen test split P0 but not on the one-day list?
  3. Why didn't you just add MLflow? It would have given you experiment comparison for free.
  4. What breaks in production if you automate the feedback loop today?
  5. Interviewer framing: "If you had another month on this project, what would you do?"

Appendix A — Model answers to the drill

A1. The one-day list. "First, make the config strict and fail fast: forbid unknown keys, use literal types for the model, metric, slice and baseline names, and check all names before the run directory is created. That closes every 'typo silently changed the run' and 'typo found after training' bug in one change. Second, make --seed actually set the training seed and re-stamp the experiment ID, because today replicates share a seed and any variance I report is fake. Third, make re-scoring non-destructive, so mtrain test can't overwrite published numbers and --split val can't write into a file called test. Fourth, predict once and slice the stored outcomes. That makes slices add up to the overall number and saves about a quarter of each run's GPU time. Fifth, a truthfulness pass: fix the report bugs and correct the nearly thirty doc sentences that don't match the code. The order goes from silent-wrong-results to lost-results to waste to credibility, and each fix comes with a test that fails before it."

Why that answer lands: it's concrete, ordered by consequence with the reason stated, sized to a day, and every item has a test. That's what "prioritize" means.

A2. Why the frozen split isn't in the day. "It's the most important fix. Without it, every intervention is scored on a different test set, so the quantity-versus-coverage comparisons the project exists for aren't like-for-like. But it isn't an afternoon change. It reverses the order of operations from 'filter, then split' to 'split once, then intervene on train', and that may need a transform in the data engine that preserves split assignments, which I can't confirm exists. That's one to three days and partly outside this repo. So it heads the roadmap, and until it lands I'd present the intervention results as unvalidated."

Why that answer lands: it separates importance from effort, explains the dependency honestly, and says how results should be qualified in the meantime.

A3. Why not MLflow? "MLflow would have given me a comparison UI, but the problem wasn't displaying runs side by side. The runs weren't comparable: different test sets per intervention, fake seed replicates, no intervals. MLflow would have shown those incomparable numbers in a nicer table. My artifacts are plain files that DuckDB or pandas can read directly, so adding MLflow later as a sink is cheap. I'd add it when a second researcher starts running experiments concurrently or runs reach the hundreds. That's when a tracking server's locking and UI earn their operational cost."

Why that answer lands: it identifies the real problem the tool wouldn't solve, keeps the option open cheaply, and names the flip condition.

A4. Automating the feedback loop. "It would overfit the test set at machine speed. The feedback artifact is computed on test outcomes, so each automatic iteration would choose the next data intervention by looking at test errors and judge it on the same test errors. It also can't say 'no action', because a 90th-percentile threshold always flags 10% of frames, so it would intervene forever. And its 'data defect' branch is unreachable at these test sizes, so it would always attribute failures to the model. Automation is the right end state, but only after feedback runs on a validation evaluation, there's an absolute or baseline-relative gate on whether to act, and there's a fresh test set the loop has never seen."

Why that answer lands: it combines three independent findings into one decisive argument and states exactly what has to be true before automation is safe.

A5. "Another month?" "Week one: validity. Freeze one test split and apply interventions only to train, propagate real seeds, select checkpoints on a frozen stratified validation subset, run feedback on validation, and add bootstrap intervals. Weeks two and three: the experiments that change conclusions. First, ALOHA at five to ten thousand steps, because the current run saw about a ninth of an epoch, and I think 'SmolVLA loses to retrieval' is really 'SmolVLA is undertrained'. Then a nested data-efficiency curve with three seeds per point, and a rebalanced run compared paired against the full run on the shared split. Week four: reliability, meaning a run state machine, atomic writes, CI with both repositories and a lockfile, plus an observation adapter so the deployment path takes real robot inputs. I would not spend the month on MLflow, a server or a bigger model. None of those helps until two numbers from this system can be compared."

Why that answer lands: it's a sequenced plan where each week's purpose is stated, the headline experiment is justified by a calculation, and it explicitly rejects the tempting distractions. That shows judgement, not just a to-do list.

Module 16 Summary — the one page to revise

What this module is. About 200 gap entries consolidated into 7 P0 fixes (comparability and truthfulness of results), 26 P1 fixes plus 28 doc corrections, and about 60 P2 items. Then a one-day shortlist (about 8 hours), a five-phase roadmap, and a fifteen-row anti-roadmap with flip conditions.

Five claims to make cold

  1. The P0s are all about comparability: frozen test split, real seeds, strict config, no imbalanced no-op, val-driven feedback, non-destructive re-scoring, a frozen val subset.
  2. One day buys: fail-fast config, real seeds, non-destructive re-scoring, predict-once slicing, a truthfulness pass.
  3. The frozen test split is the most important fix and not a one-day fix. It may need engine work.
  4. The first experiment after the fixes is ALOHA at 5–10k steps, testing under-training (≈0.11 epoch today).
  5. Not now: MLflow, a server, Hydra, mocking the engine, more slices, automated feedback, a bigger model, each with its flip condition.

Decisions

ChoseOverBecauseCost
Prioritize by effect on conclusionsby ease or by modulewrong results matter mostP0s include a multi-day fix
Fix docs or make them trueleave docs as aspirationscredibility per minute28 small edits
Validity before experiments before reliabilityinfrastructure firstthe bottleneck order (Module 15)reliability gaps persist for a few weeks
Add an absolute gate beside the percentilereplace itkeep normalization-robust rankingtwo thresholds to explain
Defer scale items to triggersbuild nowC9, short runsrework later, by design

Volunteer unprompted: the README's three runs are pipeline validation until P0-1/2/7 land; P0-1 depends on the engine; continued training on an identical split is the robust result.

Numbers and names: 7 P0 · 26 P1 · 28 doc corrections · ~60 P2; shortlist about 8 h (1.5 + 1.5 + 1.5 + 2 + 1.5); P0-1 effort L (1–3 days); ALOHA target 5–10k steps ≈ 0.5–1 epoch.

Sentences worth memorizing

  • "My P0 list is about one thing: making two numbers from this system comparable."
  • "In one day I'd make config errors fail before the GPU starts, make seeds real, make re-scoring non-destructive, predict once, and make every doc sentence true."
  • "Automating the feedback loop today would overfit the test set at machine speed."
  • "Until the test split is frozen, the intervention results are pipeline validation, not findings."

Module 17 — Interview Kit

Files covered: none new. This module packages Modules 1–16 for speaking. Every claim points back to a module (M4 §4.5) and its model answer (M4 A5 = Module 4, Appendix A, answer 5).

Where we are: The manual so far is for understanding. This module is for delivery: what to say in 30 seconds, 2 minutes and 5 minutes; how to answer the 45 questions you're most likely to get; how to tell the behavioural stories; which phrases to avoid; what to ask back; and every number and term on one card.

The one-line thesis: The project's strongest story isn't "I fine-tuned a VLA". It's "I built an evaluation harness honest enough to tell me my VLA tied a nearest-neighbour lookup, and then I audited the harness itself and found where its own comparisons weren't yet valid."

Authorship note before anything else. pyproject.toml lists Kushagra Srivastava as author and a kushagrathisside/... repository URL. README line 298 credits the SO-100 fine-tuning runs to AdityaKishore0698 and the ALOHA run to chytra3 (M1 §1.4, gap 11). Decide your exact framing before the interview, and use it consistently. For example: "a collaborative project. The codebase design is X's; I ran the SO-100 experiments, and I did this audit." Everywhere below, "I" means "the project". Swap in "we" or "the author" wherever that's the truthful version.

17.1 Prerequisites — the absolute basics

  • Pitch. A self-contained explanation sized to a time box. A 30-second pitch is what and why; 2 minutes adds how and the result; 5 minutes at a whiteboard adds architecture and trade-offs.
  • Answer skeleton. The three or four beats an answer must hit, in order. Memorize skeletons, not scripts.
  • STAR. Situation, Task, Action, Result: the standard shape for behavioural answers. Add a fifth beat, Reflection ("what I'd do differently").
  • Signal. What an answer tells the interviewer about you beyond its content: judgement, honesty, depth, ownership.
  • The honest downgrade. Volunteering the limit of a claim before you're asked. It's the highest-signal move in a technical interview, because it shows you know where the edges are.

17.2 Pitches

30 seconds

"It's an experiment and evaluation layer for fine-tuning a vision-language-action robot policy, SmolVLA, on real LeRobot datasets. The organizing idea is that the model layer never owns the data. Every data intervention, like halving the dataset or dropping a regime, becomes a lineage-tracked version in a separate data engine, so every checkpoint traces back to raw episodes. On SO-100 pick-and-place, fine-tuning reached 2.6% of the mean-action baseline's error. But it exactly tied a nearest-neighbour lookup over robot state, and on bimanual ALOHA retrieval was twice as good. The harness was built to surface results like that instead of hiding them."

2 minutes

Beats, in order:

  1. The problem (15 s). VLA fine-tuning results are easy to overclaim: offline error gets read as task success, data changes aren't traceable, and baselines are weak or missing.
  2. The design principle (20 s). The model layer never owns data. Interventions are engine transforms, data is named by immutable version, and every run directory records the config, both repos' commits and the environment.
  3. The pipeline (30 s). One command: build the intervention version, re-check the splits, profile, fine-tune with validation-only checkpoint selection, score against mean-action and nearest-neighbour through the same evaluator, decompose the error (tails, per episode, per phase, slices), produce a feedback artifact, then report and model card. Anything it can't measure, like rollout, it records as "unavailable".
  4. The results (25 s). SO-100: test MSE 0.0290, then 0.0239 after continued training (an 18% improvement on the identical test split, outside the roughly 5% inference noise), equal to nearest-neighbour. ALOHA: 0.216 vs retrieval 0.106.
  5. The honest reading (20 s). Offline, on single-setup data, robot state almost determines the action. And the ALOHA run saw about a ninth of an epoch, so 'undertrained' is the first hypothesis.
  6. What I'd fix next (10 s). Interventions currently re-split the data, so they're scored on different test sets. Freezing one test split and making seeds real come first.

5 minutes at a whiteboard

Draw this, top to bottom, talking through each box (M1, M11, M14 §14.2):

  YAML (base ⊕ experiment) ──▶ ExperimentConfig (pydantic; validators: "declare the weak dimension")
                                     │
  ┌──────────────── ExperimentRunner.run (composition root, transaction script) ──────────────────┐
  │ 0 run dir + env + git(both repos) + resolved config                                            │
  │ 1 intervention ──▶ ENGINE: EpisodeSubsetFilter → clean/vK ; DatasetBuilder → training/vM       │
  │ 2 split re-check · profile (coverage, QC, "unavailable" where unmeasurable)                    │
  │ 3 Trainer ──callback──▶ engine fit:  val-only (32 frames) · checkpoint · verify · prune · final │
  │ 4 baselines (mean, NN-state) + SmolVLA through SAME Evaluator · tails · episodes · phase · slices│
  │ 5 per-frame outcomes · model artifact · feedback (engine FeedbackLoop) · model card            │
  │ 6 report (every section; "not run") · summary · JSONL index                                     │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
        └──── human picks next intervention from feedback (dashed arrow) ────▶ back to 1

Then spend the remaining two minutes on three trade-offs (pick based on the interviewer):

  • Lineage vs comparability. Filter-then-split gives perfect provenance, but a different test set per intervention. The fix is split once, intervene on train only (M4 §4.5).
  • Validation cost vs selection quality. 32 frames is cheap, but val MSE is about 13× below test. The fix is a frozen stratified subset (M6 §6.4).
  • Honesty markers vs completeness. Rollout is "unavailable", and so is visual diversity. Nothing is fabricated, so the reports are incomplete by design (M7, M10).

17.3 Question bank

Format: Q · skeleton (the beats) · → pointer.

A. Project and motivation

  1. What does this project do, in one sentence? Experiment, evaluation and feedback layer for SmolVLA on versioned robot data; the model layer never owns the data. → M0, M1 A5
  2. Why build this instead of using LeRobot's training script? LeRobot trains; this layer adds lineage-tracked interventions, baselines through the same evaluator, decompositions and honesty markers, and records it all per run. Admit that the step itself is the engine's. → M1 §1.3, M1 A3
  3. What did you specifically do? Use your authorship framing; name concrete parts (the SO-100 runs, this audit, whichever modules are yours). → note above, M1 gap 11
  4. What's the headline result? SO-100 0.0239, equal to NN; ALOHA 0.216 vs NN 0.106; 2.6% of mean-action; 18% from continued training. → M7 §7.2, README
  5. Is the model any good? Learned the distribution; doesn't beat retrieval offline; probably undertrained on ALOHA; closed loop unmeasured. → M7 A5, M15 A2

B. Data and interventions

  1. Why version data instead of passing episode IDs to training? Immutable identity, lineage back to raw, recorded reasons; cost: catalog churn and the moving test set. → M4 A2
  2. Walk me through what reduced does to the catalog. Seeded draw over sorted IDs → EpisodeSubsetFilter → clean/vK → DatasetBuilder → training/vM; lineage records kept and dropped IDs; tested. → M4 A1
  3. Are your intervention results comparable to the baseline? No: filter-then-split means a different, smaller test set; the fix is to split once and intervene on train. → M4 A4
  4. Why episode length as the imbalance variable? SO-100 has one task and the schema has no variation field; length is the only varying attribute; terciles. → M4 §4.3
  5. What does "imbalanced" actually do? Removes the regime entirely, so it's an out-of-distribution test, not under-representation; its test set lacks that regime. → M4 §4.4
  6. How does rebalancing work, and what can't it do? Sampling weights on existing episodes, nothing duplicated; can't close a coverage gap. → M4 §4.5
  7. How do you prevent train/test leakage? Episode-level splits, a single label per episode, re-check, val-only selection; content-level leakage is unchecked, and NN strength is the evidence. → M5 A5
  8. Does your split check actually catch anything? The overlap branch is near-tautological; it misses unassigned and empty splits; make ok mean a complete partition. → M5 A1, A2

C. Training

  1. What do you own in the training loop? A callback into the engine's fit: val-only validation, checkpoint, verify, select, prune, final, crash marker, GPU release. → M6 A1, M1 A3
  2. How do you choose the checkpoint, and how confident are you? Min val MSE among verified checkpoints; 32 frames of a stochastic policy, val 13× below test; right on SO-100 because the curve was still falling. → M6 A5
  3. Explain the 0.0018 vs 0.0239 gap. Likely first frames of one episode (easiest phase), winner's curse, 5-episode splits; unverified engine frame order. → M6 A4
  4. What happens if the job is killed? SIGTERM doesn't raise, so no crash.json; periodic checkpoints survive; resume restarts warmup; retry blocked. → M6 A3
  5. Why not save optimizer state? Disk (Adam moments about 3× size); a fresh warmup on resume; the engine reportedly supports it; flips for preemptible long runs. → M6 §6.3
  6. How much data does a run actually see? 0.26 epoch SO-100, 0.11 ALOHA (per-frame assumption). → M15 A2
  7. Why batch size 4? Motivated by 8 GB, but peak was 2.2–3.0 GB, so it's conservative; try 8–16. → M15 §15.4

D. Evaluation and metrics

  1. Why two baselines, and what's nearest-neighbour exactly? Mean-action is the floor; NN retrieves by robot state, no images or language; same evaluator call. → M7 A1
  2. What does tying NN tell you? Offline, state nearly determines action on single-setup data; closed loop is where vision should matter. → M7 A5
  3. Why report tails and per-phase error? Control fails at rare frames; continued training cut the max by 32%; error peaks mid-episode. → M7 §7.3–7.4
  4. How do your slices work, and what's wrong with them? Each slice re-runs stochastic inference, so they don't reconcile, and it costs about 3× compute; predict once and slice. → M7 A2
  5. Would you defend "long episodes are easier"? No: 1–2 episodes per slice, 5% inference noise, about 25% per-episode spread. → M7 A3
  6. How do you know a difference between runs is real? Mostly you don't yet: single seed, 5 test episodes, no intervals; only continued training (same split, 18% vs 5% noise) is defensible. → M10 A5
  7. What's success_rate? Fraction of frames with MAE below 0.1; a proxy, never called success; the tolerance isn't even wired to config. → M7 §7.1, M2 §2.6

E. Feedback loop

  1. Walk me through your data flywheel. Does it work? Plumbing yes; as a decision procedure, no: always recommends action, defect branch unreachable, runs on test. → M8 A5
  2. Why a percentile threshold? Robust to model and normalization; can't say "nothing's wrong"; add an absolute gate. → M8 A1
  3. Why is the "data defect" explanation never produced? Max per-episode failure rate ≈ 0.50 (SO-100) and 0.40 (ALOHA) vs a > 0.5 rule. → M8 A2
  4. What's wrong with running failure analysis on test? Adaptive overfitting: decisions must use val; test is touched once. → M8 A3

F. Systems and design

  1. Draw the architecture and the dependency graph. Four layers, acyclic; runner as composition root; CLI the one violation; rde under everything. → M14 A1
  2. Name design patterns you used. Template Method via callback, Transaction Script, Specification validators, Façade, explicit "unavailable". → M14 A2
  3. Weakest part of the low-level design? Typed at the edges, dicts in the middle, schemaless on disk; the n/a git-key bug as proof. → M14 A5
  4. Why JSONL and run directories instead of MLflow? Serverless, durable, query with DuckDB; durability is on you; flips at the second user. → M3 A2, M16 A3
  5. What happens if two runs start at once? TOCTOU on the run directory, catalog version race, torn JSONL, GPU contention. → M14 A4
  6. How do you make a run reproducible six months later? Resolved config plus version lineage plus both commits plus env; gaps: no lockfile, dirty diffs, guessed engine path, git-ignored artifacts, stochastic inference. → M3 A5
  7. What breaks first if this scales? Comparison validity, then eval time, then disk; an evidence-limited system. → M15 §15.5, A5
  8. How did you deploy it, and what's the latency? Not to a robot; a local loader that's never executed; 47–50 / 86 ms per frame is evaluator throughput, not control latency. → M9 A5
  9. Is your health check sufficient? It checks presence and well-formedness, not compatibility; normalization checked on the wrong object. → M9 A1

G. Process, testing, judgement

  1. How is it tested? 56 tests, 52 torch-free, real engine, no mocks; lineage verified end to end; no cross-stage invariant tests. → M13 A1, A2
  2. Why not mock the engine? Mocks agree with your assumptions; use real engine for the boundary and fakes for pure logic. → M13 A3
  3. Which decisions came from constraints, and which were arbitrary? The capstone: GPU, no robot, tiny data, schema, engine; arbitrary thresholds; C3 and C6 not carried through. → M13 A5, M13 §13.6
  4. If you had one day? Strict config, real seeds, non-destructive re-scoring, predict-once, truthfulness pass. → M16 A1
  5. Another month? Validity, then ALOHA 5–10k steps, then a nested learning curve, then reliability; not MLflow, a server or a bigger model. → M16 A5
  6. What would you not build? The anti-roadmap: automated feedback, more slices, a server; each with its flip. → M16 §16.7
  7. What's the most surprising thing you learned? Evaluation took as long as training; under one epoch; the harness's own comparisons were invalid. → M15 §15.3–15.4

17.4 Behavioural framings (STAR + Reflection)

Hardest bug. The action-queue bug (engine-side, documented in the smoke test and API_REFERENCE).

  • S: evaluation scores looked plausible but slightly off.
  • T: SmolVLA's select_action keeps a stateful queue of predicted action chunks for one continuous rollout.
  • A: in batched offline evaluation, that queue replayed stale actions across unrelated batches. The fix was switching to predict_action_chunk (a fresh forward pass per call), and the regression test used a deliberately non-divisor batch size (3) to force varying batch sizes.
  • R: evaluation became correct.
  • Reflection: "stateful APIs designed for control loops are dangerous in batch evaluation; now I check whether any model call carries hidden state."
  • If this wasn't your fix, use the audit instead: "the hardest bug I found was that interventions re-split the data, so every comparison used a different test set." (M4 A5)

What you'd do differently. "Design the comparison protocol before the pipeline. I'd freeze one test split on day one, make the seed flow through to training, and put a bootstrap interval on every number. The mechanisms (lineage, provenance, honesty markers) were right; the statistics weren't carried through." (M13 §13.6, last table)

Proudest. "That the system refuses to fabricate. Rollout is recorded as unavailable with a reason, visual diversity is 'unavailable' instead of a fake score, every report section renders as 'not run' rather than disappearing, and the headline result is a tie with a nearest-neighbour baseline, reported as a tie." (M7, M10)

A trade-off you're unsure about. "Putting the model adapter and its training step inside the data engine. It keeps feature shapes and normalization in lockstep with the engine's training contract, which is why cross-embodiment loading worked. But it inverts the dependency: the data layer owns the model. With one model family I think it was right. With a second I'd split adapters into their own package, and I'm not sure where the line is." (M1 §1.3)

Conflict or disagreement (if asked). Use the docs-vs-code findings: "the docs said selection was enforced in a module that doesn't exist, and that predictions were deterministic. I treated docs as claims and verified each against the code, and found nearly thirty that needed correcting." (M16 §16.3 D-table)

Failure. "My one-day plan would have been to add MLflow. Auditing showed the runs weren't comparable, so a dashboard would only have displayed incomparable numbers more nicely. I changed the plan to fix the protocol first." (M16 A3)

17.5 Phrases that hurt you, and what to say instead

Don't sayWhy it hurtsSay instead
"The model achieves 50–57% success."success_rate is a tolerance proxy, offline"56.9% of test frames are within 0.1 mean absolute error, an offline proxy, not task success."
"SmolVLA beats the baselines."it ties NN on SO-100 and loses on ALOHA"It's at 2.6% of mean-action error and ties a state nearest-neighbour lookup."
"I wrote the training loop."fit lives in the engine"The engine owns the gradient step; this layer owns validation, selection, checkpointing and crash handling around it."
"An independent check guarantees no leakage."the overlap branch can't fire; content leakage unchecked"Splits are episode-level and re-checked for disjointness; content-level leakage between same-session episodes isn't checked."
"Long episodes are easier."1–2 episodes per slice"The slice infrastructure works; five test episodes are too few for slice conclusions."
"We ran three seeds." (if using --seed)--seed doesn't seed"Single seed. The seed flag currently only names the directory, which I'd fix first."
"It's deployed."replay harness, never executed"There's a local loader with health checks; it hasn't been run against a robot, and it takes dataset samples."
"Inference latency is 48 ms."evaluator throughput per frame, batch 16"About 48 ms per frame of evaluator throughput at batch 16, excluding data loading. Not control-loop latency."
"Validation MSE was 0.0018."32 frames, about 13× below test"Selected on validation; the honest number is test MSE 0.0239."
"The flywheel automatically improves the data."human-gated, runs on test, always recommends action"It produces ranked failures and a recommendation for a human; as a decision rule it needs to move to validation."
"It's fully reproducible."no lockfile, dirty diffs, stochastic"Every input is recorded; outputs reproduce within inference noise, a few percent."
"Coverage is 0.9."occupied-bin evenness"That metric is evenness among occupied bins. I'd renormalize it by the full bin count."
"Retrieval is just a strong baseline."ALOHA ≈ 0.11 epoch"Retrieval is strong on single-setup data, and the ALOHA run was undertrained at about a ninth of an epoch."
"No bugs that I know of."there are ~200 gaps"Here are the three I'd fix first, and why."

17.6 Questions to ask them

  1. "How do you evaluate policies before closed-loop testing, and how much do you trust offline metrics to rank them?"
  2. "When you change a dataset, how do you track which models trained on which version?"
  3. "What's your split policy for robot data: by episode, session, scene or operator?"
  4. "How many seeds do you typically run before you believe a difference?"
  5. "Where does the training loop live relative to the data pipeline in your stack, and who owns the model adapters?"
  6. "What's your bottleneck today: GPU hours, data collection, or evaluation throughput?"
  7. "How do you handle stochastic policies (diffusion or flow matching) when comparing checkpoints?"
  8. "What would a successful first three months look like in this role?"

17.7 The ten sentences that carry the project

  1. "The model layer never owns the data. Every intervention is a lineage-tracked dataset version, never a local copy."
  2. "Offline, on single-setup data, robot state almost determines the next action. That's why a state nearest-neighbour lookup tied a VLA."
  3. "The harness's job was to tell me the truth, and it did: the baseline was that strong."
  4. "Test never influences checkpoint selection; the trainer's only validation call hard-codes the val split."
  5. "I filter before I split, so each intervention got its own test set. The fix is to split once and intervene on train only."
  6. "My ALOHA run saw about a ninth of an epoch; 'undertrained' comes before 'retrieval is better'."
  7. "Every number I report is offline, and the code records rollout as unavailable rather than invent one."
  8. "Evaluation took about as long as training, and half of it was redundant re-inference."
  9. "I honoured the constraints in the mechanisms and didn't carry them through to the statistics."
  10. "My P0 list is about one thing: making two numbers from this system comparable."

Mean-action MSE 0.908 (SO-100), 1.010 (ALOHA). SO-100 2,000-step tails: median 0.089, p90 0.174, p99 0.272, max 0.375 (from 0.553). Per-joint MAE 0.063 (shoulder pan) → 0.157 (gripper). Phase: start 0.051, mid 0.144, end 0.049. Per-episode MAE 0.085–0.111 (SO-100), 0.296–0.405 (ALOHA). Val minimum 0.0018 at step 1,800. Re-scoring noise up to 5% (0.0305 vs 0.0290; 0.0232 vs 0.0239). Inference 47–50 ms/frame (SO-100), 86 ms (ALOHA) at batch 16. Host memory 3.0–3.3 GB. NN confidence–error correlation ≈ −0.79.

Config (configs/base.yaml): splits 0.8/0.1/0.1, seed 42, quantile · steps 1,000 · batch 4 · lr 1e-4 · wd 1e-5 · warmup 50 · clip 1.0 · ckpt/val every 100 · val_batches 8 (32 frames) · keep 3 · training seed 0 · eval batch 16 · tolerance 0.1 · slices [per_task, per_length_bucket] · baselines [mean_action, nearest_neighbor]. Extended run: lr 3e-5, warmup 20, resume_from step_1000. Schema defaults differ: steps 200, ckpt 50, eval batch 32.

Derived (M15): ≈ 3.1 steps/s; epochs seen ≈ 0.26 (SO-100), 0.53 (extended), 0.11 (ALOHA); eval ≈ 45% (SO-100) and 57% (ALOHA) of run time; run ≈ 10–11 min (SO-100), ≈ 22 min (ALOHA); checkpoints ≈ 4–9 GB per run (estimated).

Thresholds: feedback p90, ≥ 3 failures per episode, concentration > 0.5; terciles 33.3/66.7, ≥ 3 episodes; 10 histogram bins; oversample 3.0; split-sum tolerance 1e-6; ID digest 10 hex characters.

Code and tests: about 2,700 lines in src; 11 CLI commands; 56 tests (49 unit, 3 integration, 4 smoke), 52 light; 2 custom exceptions; 4 index files; 7 run subdirectories; 5 checkpoint files.

Environment: RTX 4060 Laptop 8 GB; ~10 GB VM RAM; lerobot 0.4.4; torch 2.10 (CUDA build); driver 592.82; CUDA 13.1; Python ≥ 3.10 declared, 3.11 used.

17.10 Honest gaps to own before an interviewer finds them

  1. Authorship ambiguity (pyproject.toml vs README credits). Consequence: any inconsistency in "I" vs "we" will be noticed. Settle the framing in advance.
  2. The three published runs predate the protocol fixes. Consequence: present them as pipeline validation; the robust result is continued training on the identical split.
  3. Nothing in this manual was executed. The engine wasn't available, so engine-side behaviour (Evaluator frame order, sampling unit, version-allocation atomicity) is inferred. Consequence: say "I didn't verify X" rather than guessing, if pressed.

The shape to carry into the Data Provenance & Demo module

 30 s: what + principle + tie result     2 min: problem ▸ principle ▸ pipeline ▸ results ▸ honest read ▸ next fix
 5 min: whiteboard runner stages + 3 trade-offs (lineage vs comparability · val cost vs selection · honesty vs completeness)
 47 questions → module + appendix pointers      6 behavioural stories (STAR + reflection)
 14 phrase swaps · 8 questions to ask · 10 carrying sentences · numbers card · glossary

Drill — answer from memory, files closed

  1. Give the 30-second pitch, out loud, and time it.
  2. Why didn't you just report the 56.9% "within tolerance" as the success rate? It's a bigger number.
  3. Recite every number on the results table without looking.
  4. What breaks in production if you say "we ran three seeds" and the interviewer asks to see the configs?
  5. Interviewer framing: "Tell me about this project."

Appendix A — Model answers to the drill

A1. The 30-second pitch. See §17.2. The test: under 35 seconds, mentions lineage, the tie with nearest-neighbour and honesty markers, and contains no unqualified "success".

Why that answer lands: in 30 seconds it gives a principle, a result and a stance, which is more memorable than a feature list.

A2. Why not call 56.9% the success rate? "Because it isn't one. It's the fraction of test frames whose mean absolute action error is under 0.1, computed offline against recorded demonstrations. A policy can be within tolerance on most frames and still fail the grasp, and in closed loop small errors compound. The whole system is built so that offline accuracy is never presented as task success: the metric is labelled a proxy everywhere and rollout is recorded as unavailable. Calling it success would be the one claim that undoes everything else the project gets right."

Why that answer lands: it explains the technical difference, ties it to the project's design, and shows you won't trade credibility for a bigger number.

A3. Reciting the numbers. SO-100: 0.0290 → 0.0239, NN 0.0239, mean-action 0.908, 3.2% → 2.6%, 49.8% → 56.9% within tolerance, 324 s and 385 s, 2.2 GB. ALOHA: 0.2157, NN 0.1062, mean 1.010, 21.4%, 0.05%, 576 s, 3.0 GB. Splits 38/5/5 and 32/4/4; 1,908 and 4,400 test frames.

Why that answer lands: fluency with your own numbers is the cheapest evidence you did the work.

A4. "We ran three seeds", then the configs. "They'd see that the --seed flag only changes the run directory name. The training seed in each resolved config is the same, zero. So the 'three seeds' are three runs with one seed, differing only through GPU nondeterminism and sampling noise, and any spread is an underestimate. That's why I'd never say it. The honest statement is 'single seed', followed by 'and the seed flag doesn't seed yet; that's the second fix on my list.'"

Why that answer lands: it shows you know exactly how the claim would collapse, and that the safe phrasing is already part of your vocabulary.

A5. "Tell me about this project." Deliver the 2-minute pitch (§17.2), then stop and offer a direction: "I can go deeper on the data-versioning design, the evaluation and what the nearest-neighbour tie means, or the flaws I found auditing it. Which is most useful?"

Why that answer lands: it's bounded, it has a result and a self-critique, and it hands control back with three strong branches, each of which you've prepared.

Module 17 Summary — the one page to revise

What this module is. The speaking layer of the manual: three pitches, 47 questions with skeletons and pointers, six behavioural stories, phrase swaps, questions to ask, ten carrying sentences, a numbers card and a glossary.

Five claims to make cold

  1. The model layer never owns the data; interventions are lineage-tracked versions.
  2. SmolVLA ties state-NN on SO-100 (0.0239) and loses on ALOHA (0.216 vs 0.106).
  3. Every number is offline; rollout is recorded as unavailable.
  4. Continued training gave 18% on the identical split, outside the 5% noise. It's the robust result.
  5. The fixes I'd make first all serve one goal: two numbers from this system being comparable.

Decisions (as you'd narrate them)

ChoseOverBecauseCost
Lead with the tielead with the 2.6% ratiothe honest baselineless flattering
Volunteer flawswait to be askedthe strongest signallonger answers
"Proxy" every time"success"central honesty contractsmaller-sounding numbers
Offer three deep-dive branchesmonologueinterviewer chooses, you're preparednone

Volunteer unprompted: authorship framing; the published runs predate the fixes; the engine-side behaviours you couldn't verify.

Numbers and names: see the §17.8 card. Memorize the results table and the derived epoch fractions.

Sentences worth memorizing: §17.7, all ten.

Module 18 — Data Provenance & the Demo

Files covered: README.md §1, §2.4, §4–5 and §10 (as claims), docs/figures/results.svg, docs/diagrams/01-system-architecture.svg, configs/base.yaml, configs/experiments/aloha_coffee_baseline.yaml (dataset identity), tests/conftest.py (synthetic data). Engine-side ingestion is described only from this repository's call sites and README; I couldn't read the engine.

Where we are: Every earlier module assumed data already in the catalog. This module answers where it actually comes from, how it gets in, what this repository owes the sibling engine, and how to show the project live without breaking anything, including what to say when something goes wrong in front of an interviewer.

The one-line thesis: The data's path from Hugging Face to a trained checkpoint is fully traceable through engine lineage. But none of the artifacts behind the README's numbers live in this repository (runs are git-ignored), so the demo is a walk through pre-recorded run directories plus a few safe, read-only commands. Two of the CLI's commands will silently damage those recordings if run during a demo.

18.1 Prerequisites — the absolute basics

  • Data provenance. The documented origin and processing history of a dataset: where it came from, what was done to it, by what code, and when.
  • Hugging Face Hub dataset. A versioned dataset repository addressed as org/name (here lerobot/svla_so100_pickplace, lerobot/aloha_static_coffee). Each has its own licence and dataset card. I haven't checked either licence. Check before redistributing anything.
  • LeRobot dataset format. Per-frame tabular data (Parquet) plus camera streams stored as video (the ALOHA config's comment says AV1), with episode boundaries and metadata. The README says the test fixture uses "the real LeRobot v3.0 layout".
  • Ingest → transform → build. The engine's three stages: ingest produces raw/vN, transforms (normalize, QC filtering, episode subsetting) produce clean/vN, and build produces training/vN with splits and weights. Module 1 has the vocabulary.
  • QC (quality control). Automated checks on episodes (missing frames, duplicates, discontinuities). Failing episodes are excluded from clean versions or builds.
  • Pre-recorded demo. Showing completed artifacts instead of running long jobs live. The standard for any ML demo longer than a minute.

18.2 Where the data comes from and how it gets in

 Hugging Face Hub                     episodic-data-engine (rde)                         vla-finetune (this repo)
 ─────────────────                    ────────────────────────────                       ────────────────────────
 lerobot/svla_so100_pickplace ──┐     rde run --config ../episodic-data-engine/          mtrain profile  (read-only)
 lerobot/aloha_static_coffee  ──┼──▶      configs/so100_pickplace.json                   mtrain train:
                                │       "download, ingest, QC, version"  (README §4)        build_intervention_dataset
                                │       ingest  ─▶ raw/v1   (50 episodes, SO-100)            → (EpisodeSubsetFilter) clean/vK
                                │       transform ─▶ clean/v1 (48; dropped [9, 31])           → DatasetBuilder        training/vM
                                │       QC records per episode                              Trainer / Evaluator / FeedbackLoop
                                └──     catalog DB + Parquet + video under settings.paths    runs/… (git-ignored)

Verified from this repository:

  • Dataset identities are so100_pickplace and aloha_coffee (configs/base.yaml, aloha_coffee_baseline.yaml), each with input version clean/v1.
  • The SO-100 lineage chain, as printed in README §1 by rde version lineage so100_pickplace training/v6:
    • ingest [] -> raw/v1 episodes 0->50
    • transform ['raw/v1'] -> clean/v1 episodes 50->48 dropped=[9, 31]
    • build_training_dataset ['clean/v1'] -> training/v6 episodes 48->48

    Each step carries a run ID, a fingerprint and the software version. The two dropped episodes are recorded by ID.

  • Splits after QC: 48 episodes → 38/5/5 (SO-100); 40 → 32/4/4 (ALOHA) (README Table 1).
  • training/v6 means at least six training versions of SO-100 were built. That's consistent with Module 4's "every run mints a new version".

Inferred, not verified:

  • What the clean/v1 transform did beyond dropping episodes 9 and 31. The test fixture's clean_version applies NormalizeVector("state.joint"), NormalizeVector("action.joint") and then QC, which suggests the real clean/v1 is normalized. The README's "MSE is in normalized action units" agrees.
  • Why episodes 9 and 31 were dropped (presumably QC failures, perhaps missing or discontinuous frames). The engine's QC records hold the answer: rde obs quality, as profile.py's note suggests.
  • ALOHA's raw episode count before QC, and which ALOHA episodes (if any) were dropped. Not stated in this repo.
  • The engine ingest config's contents (../episodic-data-engine/configs/so100_pickplace.json).

The synthetic test data (tests/conftest.py) is generated by rde.testing.make_lerobot_dataset: 8 episodes × 20 frames at 10 fps, two task strings, then ingested, normalized, QC'd and built exactly like real data. No network, and no mocks of the source format (README §9). It's the only data this repository can produce on its own.

What to say in an interview: "Three engine changes were made for this project: real gradient fine-tuning with checkpointing, slice-level evaluation by episode ID, and the episode-filter transform interventions are built on. Everything else in the engine predates this layer." (Credit the engine's authorship per the Module 17 note.)

ArtifactIn this repository?Where it lives
Source code, configs, docs, results.svg, architecture SVGyesgit
Datasets (raw, clean, training versions)noengine data directory (settings.paths)
Run directories behind README Tables 2–4 (runs/2026-09-03/... etc.)no, git-ignored (runs)the experimenter's disk
Cross-run indexno, git-ignored (experiments/*.jsonl)the experimenter's disk
Checkpoints (selected step 1,800 SO-100 / step 800 ALOHA)noinside the run directories
Base model weightsnoHugging Face cache
Internal audit docs/CODE_REVIEW.mdno, git-ignored ("private record")local only

Consequence: every number in the README is backed by files that only exist on the machine that ran them. Before any demo, copy those run directories somewhere safe (a tarball and a second disk). Module 12 showed that one wrong command overwrites them, and they're the only copy.

18.5 The rehearsed demo script (about 8 minutes)

Pre-flight checklist (do this the day before, not on the call):

  1. cp -a runs/ runs.backup/ and cp -a experiments/ experiments.backup/. Make it a second copy, outside the repo.
  2. Activate ../.venv-vla (or ../.venv for steps that don't need torch). Run mtrain --help once to warm imports.
  3. Confirm the engine catalog still has so100_pickplace@clean/v1 and training/v6: mtrain inspect --dataset so100_pickplace --version training/v6.
  4. Know the three run paths by heart (or put them in shell variables): BASE=runs/<date>/baseline_full/seed_0, EXT=runs/<date>/baseline_full_extended/seed_0, ALOHA=runs/<date>/aloha_coffee_baseline/seed_0.
  5. Open docs/figures/results.svg and docs/diagrams/01-system-architecture.svg in browser tabs.
  6. Have a terminal font large enough to share.

The script. Each step lists what to run, what to point at, and what it proves.

#DoPoint atWhat it provesTime
1Show 01-system-architecture.svgthe seven runner stages, and the left column of engine servicesthe design in one picture: procedure here, mechanism in the engine45 s
2rde version lineage so100_pickplace training/v6dropped=[9, 31], three steps, fingerprintsa trained model traces back to raw episodes, with every exclusion recorded45 s
3mtrain profile --dataset so100_pickplace --version clean/v1 (scroll to tasks, episode_length, coverage.visual_diversity)1 task; 326–475 frames; "status": "unavailable"the imbalance variable came from the data, not a guess; unmeasurable things are marked unavailable60 s
4mtrain build-intervention --dataset so100_pickplace --version clean/v1 --kind imbalanced --variable episode_length_bucket --variable-value short --target-config /tmp/demo_imbalanced.yaml (no --apply)the printed block of episode IDs and reasoninterventions are resolved from the real catalog, dry-run by default45 s
5cat $BASE/split_integrity.json38/5/5, all overlaps [], "ok": trueepisode-level splits, re-checked before training (say it's a cheap, near-tautological check; Module 5)30 s
6Show results.svg panel (a), then head $EXT/metrics/validation.jsonlthe curve still falling at 1,000; resume point; selected step 1,800selection on validation only; continued training kept improving60 s
7mtrain compare --runs $BASE --runs $EXT --runs $ALOHAmse vs nearest_neighbor_mse columnsthe honest headline: 0.0290 → 0.0239 vs NN 0.0239; ALOHA 0.216 vs 0.10660 s
8jq '.summary' $EXT/evaluation/test_overall.json and jq '.temporal_error.mean_error_by_progress_bucket' $EXT/evaluation/test_overall.jsonmid-episode peak (≈ 0.144) vs ends (≈ 0.05)error decomposed by phase: the grasp and transport phase is hard45 s
9mtrain analyze-failures --run $EXT | head -40191/1,908 flagged, max episode 13.8%, "scattered"failures as evidence, not a verdict. Then say, before they ask: at p90 with 5 episodes, "scattered" is the only possible output (Module 8)60 s
10mtrain rollout --run /tmp/demo_rollout_run (an empty scratch directory, not a real run)"status": "unavailable"the system refuses to invent a success rate20 s
11Close with the slide-free summary: "the harness told me a nearest-neighbour lookup was as good as my VLA, and auditing it told me which of its own comparisons weren't valid yet"—the project's real value30 s

Optional live run (no GPU needed, about 1–3 minutes, unmeasured here): write a config with training: {model: mean_action} and a new experiment_name (for example demo_live_<time>), and run mtrain train --config /tmp/demo_live.yaml. It exercises intervention, split check, profile, baselines, report and index with no torch. That's exactly the path test_runner_completes_baseline_only_experiment covers. Show the new run directory tree, then reports/experiment_report.md with its "not run" and "unavailable" sections. Point out that the report's model_training git line says n/a, and explain the bug (Modules 3 and 10). Showing a known bug on purpose is a strong move.

Never do these during a demo

CommandWhy
mtrain test --run $EXT or mtrain evaluate --run …overwrites test_overall.json and stratified.json with new stochastic numbers and loses the length slices (Module 12)
mtrain evaluate --split val …writes validation metrics into test_overall.json
mtrain build-intervention … --apply --target-config configs/experiments/imbalanced.yamlerases the template's explanatory comments (Module 12)
mtrain rollout --run $EXTharmless but appends a duplicate record to a published run; use a scratch directory
mtrain train with an existing experiment name the same dayFileExistsError; looks like a crash
mtrain train --seed 1 "to show seeds"it doesn't change the training seed (Module 11)
two mtrain train at oncerun-directory, catalog and index races (Module 14)

18.6 Recovery table: what to say when things go wrong

What goes wrongWhat you seeWhat to sayWhat to do
Engine catalog missing or movedLookupError: unknown version"Data is addressed by catalog version, never by path. The catalog for this machine isn't mounted, which is exactly the failure this design makes loud."switch to the pre-recorded run directories (steps 5–9 need only files)
rde command not foundshell error"The engine is a sibling package in a separate environment."activate ../.venv-vla; skip step 2 and show README §1's printed lineage instead
import torch slow or CUDA init hangslong pause"This is the heavy environment; the light one doesn't load torch at all, which is why the CLI imports lazily."Ctrl-C; rerun in ../.venv for read-only steps
FileExistsError on the live runtraceback"Runs are write-once: it refuses to overwrite an existing run directory. A failed run blocks a same-day retry. That's a known gap, and the fix is a status file plus --force from the failed state."change experiment_name and rerun
JSONDecodeError on compare or validatetraceback"That's a partially written file. My writes aren't atomic yet, and the fix is temp-then-rename."restore from runs.backup/
compare columns look misalignednumbers under the wrong headers"The terminal table builds headers from the first run and places values by position; the CSV output is keyed correctly."rerun with --out /tmp/c.csv and column -s, -t /tmp/c.csv
The interviewer asks to run mtrain test—"I'd rather not on a published run. Re-scoring currently overwrites results, and SmolVLA is stochastic, so the numbers would move a few percent. That's the third fix on my list."offer to run it against a copy: cp -a $EXT /tmp/ext_copy && mtrain test --run /tmp/ext_copy
A number on screen differs from the READMEe.g. stratified slice MSE ≠ overall"Slices re-run the stochastic model, so they don't reconcile exactly with overall; re-scoring moves MSE up to 5%."cite README §2.3
The interviewer asks "so does it work on the robot?"—"Unknown. There's no robot or simulator here, and the system records rollout as unavailable instead of estimating it. Offline it ties a nearest-neighbour lookup. Closed loop is where I'd expect vision to matter, and that's the experiment I'd most like to run."—
Run directories were lostnothing to show"The artifacts are deliberately not in git because checkpoints are gigabytes; the figure in the README was generated from them by a script."show results.svg, the README tables and the code paths
GPU out-of-memory on any live trainingCUDA OOM"Two models resident at once, or another process on the GPU. The trainer frees its model before evaluation for this reason, and the crash marker records what happened."show crash.json in the run directory

18.7 Honest gaps to own before an interviewer finds them

  1. No artifact behind the README's numbers is in the repository. Consequence: results are reproducible only on the original machine, or by re-running. Back up before demos.
  2. Data licences aren't recorded anywhere in this repository, and I didn't check them. Consequence: don't redistribute exports or datasets without checking each Hugging Face dataset card.
  3. The clean/v1 transform contents and the reason for dropping episodes 9 and 31 aren't documented here. They're engine-side. Consequence: "why were those dropped?" has to be answered from the engine's QC records, not from memory.
  4. ALOHA's pre-QC episode count and lineage aren't shown anywhere in this repo. Consequence: SO-100 provenance is demonstrable; ALOHA's is asserted.
  5. The architecture diagram presents DeployedModel as an after-the-run step. Module 9 showed it has never been executed. Consequence: if you point at it, say so.
  6. Two CLI commands damage the very artifacts a demo shows (evaluate/test, --apply). Consequence: the demo script must avoid them, and you should say why. That turns a hazard into evidence of understanding.
  7. The live baseline-only run's duration is unmeasured here. Consequence: rehearse it once and time it before relying on it.

The shape to carry into Module 0

 HF Hub (lerobot/svla_so100_pickplace, lerobot/aloha_static_coffee)
   └─▶ rde run: download · ingest raw/v1 (50) · transform+QC clean/v1 (48; dropped 9, 31) · catalog
         └─▶ mtrain: intervention → clean/vK? → training/vN (38/5/5) → train → eval → feedback → reports
               └─▶ runs/ + experiments/  (git-ignored; only copy on the experimenter's disk → BACK UP)
 Demo: architecture ▸ lineage ▸ profile ▸ dry-run intervention ▸ split check ▸ val curve ▸ compare
       ▸ phase error ▸ failures (+ honest caveat) ▸ rollout "unavailable" ▸ one-sentence close
 Never: mtrain test/evaluate on published runs · --apply on templates · --seed "replicates" · concurrent runs

Drill — answer from memory, files closed

  1. Trace the SO-100 data from Hugging Face to training/v6, naming every version and the episodes dropped.
  2. Which three changes were made to the engine for this project?
  3. Why didn't you just commit the run directories so anyone can verify the README?
  4. What breaks in production: mid-demo, the interviewer says "run the test command on that run so I can see it live." What do you do?
  5. Interviewer framing: "Show me something."

Appendix A — Model answers to the drill

A1. The SO-100 data path. "It starts as the Hugging Face dataset lerobot/svla_so100_pickplace. The data engine's rde run downloads and ingests it as raw/v1, 50 episodes. A transform with quality control produces clean/v1, 48 episodes, with episodes 9 and 31 dropped and recorded by ID in the lineage. My experiment configs start from clean/v1. For the baseline, the intervention is 'full', so there's no extra filter, and the engine's builder produces training/v6 with 38/5/5 episode-level splits. rde version lineage on that version prints all three steps with run IDs and fingerprints, so the model I selected traces back to exact raw episodes. What I can't tell you from memory is why 9 and 31 failed QC. That's in the engine's QC records."

Why that answer lands: it's exact on versions, counts and IDs, shows the verification command, and draws a clean line around what you don't know.

A2. The three engine changes. "Real fine-tuning in SmolVLAPolicy.fit, with checkpoint save, load and verification, because the adapter previously had no training step. An episode_ids selector on Evaluator.evaluate, so slices can be scored by episode set. And the EpisodeSubsetFilter transform, which is what makes every data intervention a lineage-tracked engine operation instead of a local copy. They were additive, and the engine still imports nothing from this layer."

Why that answer lands: it's precise about scope and purpose, and it reinforces the one-way dependency.

A3. Why not commit the run directories? "Checkpoints are on the order of a gigabyte each, with several per run, so committing them to git is the wrong tool. The figure script regenerates the README figure from them, and the per-frame outcomes and metrics are small. The real gap is that nothing behind the README is published. I'd publish the small artifacts (resolved configs, dataset.json, split_integrity.json, metrics, evaluation JSON, per-frame outcomes, git and environment files) as a release asset or a dataset repository, and publish the selected checkpoint to the Hub with its model card. Then anyone could verify every table without my disk."

Why that answer lands: it explains why the default is right for checkpoints, admits the actual gap, and proposes a concrete publication split by artifact size.

A4. "Run the test command live." "I'd say: I'd rather not on that directory, because mtrain test currently overwrites the run's test results in place, and SmolVLA samples actions stochastically, so the published numbers would change by a few percent with no backup. That's one of the first fixes on my list. Then I'd offer to copy the run to a scratch directory and run it there, so we can watch it re-score and see the number move, which itself demonstrates the inference noise I mention in the README."

Why that answer lands: it turns a trap into a demonstration of deep knowledge of your own tool, and still satisfies the request safely.

A5. "Show me something." "Three minutes, three things. First, lineage: this command shows the model's training data back to raw episodes, including the two QC drops. Second, the honest result: this comparison table shows fine-tuning cut error to 2.6% of the mean-action baseline but exactly tied a nearest-neighbour lookup on robot state. Third, the refusal to fabricate: rollout is recorded as unavailable, and the profile marks visual diversity unavailable instead of inventing a score. Then I'd show you the one bug I always point out myself: the report prints 'n/a' for this repo's git commit because of a key mismatch."

Why that answer lands: it's short, structured, and ends with a self-identified bug, which builds trust faster than any feature.

Module 18 Summary — the one page to revise

What this module is. The data's provenance (Hugging Face → engine raw/v1 → clean/v1 → training/vN, with recorded drops), the exact boundary with the engine (three additive changes), what's on disk vs in git (no result artifacts in git), a rehearsed 8-minute demo built from read-only commands and pre-recorded runs, and a recovery table.

Five claims to make cold

  1. SO-100: lerobot/svla_so100_pickplace → raw/v1 (50) → clean/v1 (48, dropped 9 and 31) → training/v6 (38/5/5).
  2. Three engine changes: SmolVLAPolicy.fit with checkpoints; an episode_ids selector on Evaluator.evaluate; EpisodeSubsetFilter.
  3. Every README number is backed by git-ignored files on one machine. Back them up before demos.
  4. The demo is read-only: lineage, profile, dry-run intervention, split check, validation curve, compare, phase error, failures, rollout.
  5. Never run mtrain test/evaluate or --apply on published artifacts; offer a scratch copy.

Decisions

ChoseOverBecauseCost
Git-ignore runscommit artifactscheckpoints are GBsREADME not verifiable from the repo
Pre-recorded demolive trainingruns take 10–22 minless "live"
Read-only demo commandsfull CLI tourtwo commands damage artifactsmust explain why
Scratch copy for risky commandsrefuse outrightsatisfies the request safelyone extra step

Volunteer unprompted: the backup step; why mtrain test is excluded; data licences unchecked; ALOHA lineage not shown in this repo; the diagram's DeployedModel box.

Numbers and names: 50 → 48 (dropped [9, 31]) → 38/5/5; ALOHA 40 → 32/4/4; training/v6; failures 191/1,908 (max episode 13.8%), ALOHA 440/4,400 (22.6%, 12.1%); demo about 8 minutes, 11 steps; fixture 8 × 20 frames at 10 fps, 2 tasks.

Sentences worth memorizing

  • "This command traces the model back to raw episodes, including the two QC drops."
  • "I won't run test on a published run: it overwrites in place, and the model is stochastic. Let me show you on a copy."
  • "Every number in the README is backed by files that only live on the machine that ran them; publishing the small ones is on my list."
